Psychiatric Disorders, Chapter 1

Final Textbook 3, Psychiatric Disorders, full-length edition: chapter 1 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 1 Abnormality, Causes and the Classification of Mental Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 1.1 What Counts as Abnormal, and What Counts as Normal: apply the statistical, social-norm, distress and dysfunction criteria and the four Ds to real cases, state how DSM-5-TR, ICD-11, Wakefield and Indian law define a mental disorder, and describe Offer and Sabshin's four perspectives on normality.
  2. 1.2 The Vocabulary of Psychopathology: Onset, Course, Prognosis, Prevalence and Comorbidity: define and use the descriptive and epidemiological terms that every later chapter relies on, tell point, period and lifetime prevalence from incidence, and recognise what comorbidity is and is not.
  3. 1.3 Explaining Disorder: Models, Causes and the Diathesis-Stress Idea: summarise what each classic model blames for disorder, use the language of causation, and explain the diathesis-stress and biopsychosocial models and how this book applies them.
  4. 1.4 Genes and Mental Disorder: Family, Twin, Adoption and Molecular Studies: explain how family, twin and adoption studies estimate genetic influence, interpret concordance and heritability correctly, and describe how linkage, association and genome-wide studies, polygenic risk scores and endophenotypes search for the genes behind a disorder.
  5. 1.5 Genes Meet the World: Interaction, Correlation, Epigenetics and the Brain: tell gene-environment interaction from gene-environment correlation, explain epigenetics and the studies behind it, map the main neurotransmitter, hormonal and brain findings across the disorders, and judge any claim that a disorder is purely biological.
  6. 1.6 Approaches to Classification: Syndromes, Categories and Diagnostic Error: explain why and how disorders are classified, compare the syndromal and aetiological approaches and the categorical, dimensional and prototypical ones, and use reliability, validity, false positives and false negatives to judge a diagnosis.
  7. 1.7 The DSM from DSM-I to DSM-5-TR, and the Multiaxial System: trace the DSM edition by edition and say what each changed and why, record a diagnosis on the five DSM-IV-TR axes, and explain why DSM-5 abolished them.
  8. 1.8 Inside DSM-5-TR: Sections, Specifiers, Conditions for Further Study and Z Codes: find your way around DSM-5-TR's three sections and twenty disorder chapters, record a diagnosis with its subtypes, specifiers, severity and codes, choose between "other specified" and "unspecified", and name the conditions for further study and the other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention.
  9. 1.9 The ICD: From ICD-6 to ICD-11: trace the ICD's mental disorders chapter from ICD-6 to ICD-11, name every ICD-10 F block and the ICD-11 Chapter 6 groupings with their codes, list what changed between them, and compare the ICD with the DSM.
  10. 1.10 Culture and Diagnosis: Culture-Bound Syndromes and Cultural Concepts of Distress: explain how culture shapes distress, trace the move from culture-bound syndromes to cultural concepts of distress, use the Cultural Formulation Interview, and identify the main cultural syndromes, especially those of India, by region and core feature.

Weightage. 30 of the 334 questions mapped in this book (9.0%) test this chapter, 18 of them from real past papers. 1 of its 10 sections is High yield.

In the last days of October 1967 a strange fear spread through Singapore. Swine fever, a disease of pigs, had broken out on the island's farms that July, and the government had vaccinated the herds. Now a rumour was passing from mouth to mouth: eating pork from a pig that had been given the injection could make a man's penis shrink. On 29 October the newspapers reported the fear, and within days men began to arrive at the emergency departments of the city's hospitals in a state of terror. Most of them were ethnic Chinese. Each was sure that his penis was retracting into his abdomen, and that when it had disappeared completely he would die. Many came holding on to it with their fingers; others had tied it with string or rubber bands, or gripped it with clamps, clothes pegs or chopsticks, so that it could not slip away. At the height of the panic, in the first week of November, a single hospital was receiving dozens of these men a day.

The first official reassurance did not help. On 3 November the Ministry of National Development announced that pork from the government abattoir was perfectly safe and told the public to ignore the rumours. It did nothing to calm the city (some accounts say it made the panic worse), and pork sales, already low, collapsed as families and diners refused to buy the meat. The next day the Singapore Medical Association, through a panel of doctors led by the physician Gwee Ah Leng, declared that neither swine fever nor the vaccine nor the meat of vaccinated pigs could harm anyone, and at a televised press conference it told the city that the cause was fear and rumour. Over the following days the Ministry of Health repeated the message in the newspapers and on television. The effect was swift. At Singapore General Hospital the number of cases seen in a day fell from 38 on 5 November to 17 two days later, and within a month the epidemic was over and pork was back on the table. Gwee's team, who published their survey in 1969 under the collective name of the Koro Study Team, counted more than 450 cases in the hospitals alone, almost all of them men. Nobody knows how many more went only to private doctors or to practitioners of Chinese medicine.

The condition had a name and a history. The men called it suo yang (in Cantonese, shook yong), the "shrinking of the yang", the male principle, and it is described in Chinese medical books of the Qing dynasty. Doctors knew it by a word from the Malay and Indonesian world, koro, whose origin is disputed. Nor was it confined to Chinese men. From late June to mid-September 1982, through the monsoon months, a koro epidemic swept through four districts of lower Assam. Psychiatrists at Gauhati Medical College, D. Dutta, H. R. Phookan and P. D. Das, studied 83 of the people affected. Most were men under 30 from Hindu Assamese families, but 19 were women. Here too, hearing about a case or seeing one was often enough to set off an attack, and here too the epidemic ended, with no deaths, after the public was reassured through the mass media.

Stories like these force three questions on anyone who studies mental disorder, and this chapter is built around them. First, were these hundreds of frightened people mentally ill? Their belief was shared by their neighbours and printed in their newspapers, it made sense within their own medical tradition, and in almost every case the terror was gone within hours or a few days. If a disorder is something that has gone wrong inside a person, what exactly had gone wrong? Second, what caused it: a rumour, a culture's ideas about vital essences and the genitals, ordinary anxiety catching fire in a crowded city, or some vulnerability in the particular men and women who fell ill while their neighbours did not? Third, what should it be called, and who decides: a "culture-bound syndrome", an attack of anxiety, mass hysteria or, as DSM-5-TR now suggests, an example of an "other specified" obsessive-compulsive and related disorder? Section 1.1 takes up the first question, Section 1.3 the second and Section 1.10 the third. Remember the men of Singapore; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

1.1 What Counts as Abnormal, and What Counts as NormalMedium yield · 4 Qs

A physician who suspects diabetes sends a sample of blood to the laboratory and gets a number back. Even that number has to be interpreted: the level at which blood sugar counts as diabetic was fixed by expert committees weighing the risks of blindness, kidney failure and heart disease, and it has been revised more than once. A psychologist who suspects depression does not even have the number. There is no blood test for depression, no scan that confirms obsessive-compulsive disorder, no laboratory value that separates a worried mind from a disordered one. The line between normal and abnormal has to be drawn by people, using criteria they can defend in front of a patient, a family, a colleague and, sometimes, a court. This section sets out those criteria and puts them to work (1.1.2), examines the official definitions of a mental disorder that grew out of them (1.1.3), turns the question round to ask what "normal" itself means (1.1.4), and ends with the idea that normal and abnormal are two ends of a continuum (1.1.5).

Why the question comes first

Every chapter that follows describes disorders: what schizophrenia looks like from the inside, how a panic attack unfolds, how anorexia nervosa takes hold of a teenager. Each of those descriptions rests on a decision that has been made before the first symptom is listed, the decision that a particular pattern of experience and behaviour lies on the far side of a line. It is tempting to treat that decision as a technicality. It is not, because a great deal hangs on it. Where the line falls decides who is offered treatment and who is told to go home and rest. It decides who may be admitted to a psychiatric hospital without full consent under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017, and who keeps the right to refuse (Book 2, Section 1.10.7). It decides who receives a disability certificate, with the concessions and reservations that come with it (Book 2, Section 9.9). And it decides who carries a label that may follow them into marriage negotiations, job interviews and insurance offices (Section 1.6.8).

How, then, is the line drawn? Look closely at any serious attempt and you will find two kinds of judgement mixed together. One is a judgement of fact: is something inside the person failing to work as it should, a memory that no longer holds, a mood that no longer lifts, a perception that reports voices when nobody is speaking? The other is a judgement of value: does that failure harm the person or the people around them, and harm by whose standards? Medicine faces the same mixture. The blood-sugar line for diabetes rests on facts about damaged eyes and kidneys, but the decision about how much risk is too much is a value judgement. Psychopathology has the harder task, because it usually has no measurement to start from, and because harm is judged against what a family, a community and a culture expect of a person at a particular age and in a particular role.

The men of Singapore in the chapter opening show how stubborn the mixture can be. The facts were not in doubt: their terror was real and their belief about their bodies was false. Whether those facts added up to a disorder depended on values, on what counts as an expectable reaction in a city where almost everyone had heard the rumour and many believed it. Keep that difficulty in mind through the rest of this section. (Two terms used throughout this book, psychopathology and abnormal psychology, are defined in Section 1.2.1.)

The criteria of abnormality in brief

Over the last century psychologists and psychiatrists have proposed a handful of criteria for drawing the line. Book 2, Sections 1.6.1 to 1.6.5, examines each of them at length, with its history and its classic failures. What follows is a compact reminder, because every later chapter of this book leans on these ideas, and then a worked application, because the criteria only make sense when you push real people through them.

The statistical criterion equates abnormal with unusual. Behaviour is abnormal if it deviates from typical behavioural patterns, that is, if it is rare in the population. For anything that can be measured on a scale and that spreads out in the bell-shaped normal distribution, the criterion becomes a simple rule: choose a cut-off, often two standard deviations from the mean, and call everything beyond it abnormal (Book 2, Figure 1.9, draws the rule for intelligence). Its attractions are objectivity and simplicity. Its flaws are just as plain. It cannot tell admirable rarity from harmful rarity, so the perfect pitch of a gifted singer is as "abnormal" as a severe intellectual disability. It misses disorders that are common: depressive and anxiety disorders are among the most frequent conditions in every population that has been surveyed (Section 1.2.6), and being common makes them no less disabling. And its cut-off is arbitrary, a choice made by people rather than a boundary found in nature.

The social-norm criterion calls behaviour abnormal when it violates a society's norms: its explicit norms, the laws and written rules, and its implicit norms, the unwritten expectations about how close to stand to a stranger, how loudly to speak in a temple and what to wear to a funeral. Its strength is that it judges behaviour in context. Its weakness is cultural relativity: norms differ from one community to the next and change from one decade to the next. Thirty years ago a man walking along a Mumbai pavement, laughing and arguing with nobody, would have drawn anxious looks; today passers-by assume he is wearing a wireless earphone. Worse, norms can be enforced in the name of medicine. The American physician Samuel Cartwright invented a disease called "drapetomania" in 1851 to explain why enslaved people ran away; Soviet psychiatrists diagnosed political dissidents with "sluggish schizophrenia"; and homosexuality stayed in the American Psychiatric Association's diagnostic manual until the association's board of trustees voted to remove it in December 1973 (Book 2, Section 1.6.2, tells these stories).

Two further criteria look at consequences rather than appearances. On the maladaptiveness or dysfunction criterion, behaviour is abnormal when it interferes with the person's ability to manage daily life: to study or work, to look after themselves and to keep relationships going. On the personal distress criterion, behaviour is abnormal when it causes the person suffering: anxiety, sadness, guilt, mental pain. Both capture something important, and both fail in a revealing way. Some people who are plainly disordered are not distressed at all. A woman in a manic episode may feel better than she has ever felt while she spends her family's savings, and a man with a long record of cruelty and fraud may feel no remorse whatever; the people around them do the suffering. In the other direction, distress is often perfectly healthy. Grief after a death, fear before a viva and anger at an injustice all hurt, and none of them is a disorder (Book 2, Section 1.6.3). Marie Jahoda (1907 to 2001) turned the whole problem upside down in 1958 by defining positive mental health instead, through six criteria (positive attitudes towards the self, growth and self-actualisation, integration of the personality, autonomy, accurate perception of reality, and environmental mastery), so that on her ideal mental health criterion abnormality is a falling short of that ideal, a bar so high that few people clear it (Book 2, Section 1.6.4).

Because each criterion fails when used alone, textbooks combine them. The most widely taught combination is the four Ds of the American psychologist Ronald J. Comer. Deviance means thoughts, feelings and behaviour that differ markedly from a society's ideas of proper functioning, and it takes in both statistical rarity and the breaking of norms. Distress means that the experience is unpleasant and upsetting to the person. Dysfunction means that it interferes with the ability to conduct daily life in a constructive way. Danger means that it puts the person or others at risk. Many teachers add a fifth D, duration, because a pattern that persists is treated differently from a passing reaction, and you will find minimum durations written into most diagnostic criteria, from two weeks for a major depressive episode to six months for generalised anxiety disorder. Danger is the least common of the four. Most people with a mental disorder, including most people with a psychosis, are a danger to nobody, and the belief that they are violent is one of the oldest sources of stigma. The clinician must still ask about danger every time, because when it is present it decides what happens next (Book 2, Section 1.6.5).

None of these criteria settles a case on its own. The way to see why is to watch them at work on three people.

Aarti is 28 and works as a software engineer in Pune. She lives alone, and every night before bed she checks that the front door is locked, that the gas knob on the stove is turned off and that the geyser switch is down. She does not check once. She touches the lock and pulls the handle, walks away, comes back and checks again, and if she loses count she starts from the beginning. The routine takes about two hours. "I can see that the knob is off," she says, "but I do not feel that it is off." For two years she has slept about five hours a night, she now reaches the office an hour late on most days, and last month her manager gave her a written warning. She is ashamed and has told nobody. Her colleagues see a capable, slightly tired engineer.

Sameer is 19 and lives in Kolkata. He is one of the strongest junior chess players in the country and spends eight or nine hours a day on chess: playing online, studying openings, replaying the games of the great masters in his head on the metro. His aunts complain that this is "not normal" for a boy of his age. Sameer is cheerful, has a close circle of friends from his chess club, passes his college examinations after some last-minute cramming, and cannot imagine a better life.

Leela is 58 and lives in a village in Kerala. Twelve days ago her husband of 35 years died suddenly of a heart attack. Since then she has wept every day, eaten little, slept badly and dreamt of him almost every night. She has not left the house; relatives cook for her and sit with her, as her community expects them to during the days of mourning. When her daughter told the visiting doctor that her mother had said "I wish I had gone with him", the doctor asked Leela gently whether she had thought of ending her life. She looked shocked. "Never. Who would watch my grandchildren grow up?"

Now run each of them through the four Ds. Aarti shows distress (shame, frustration, tears at two in the morning) and dysfunction (lost sleep, late arrival, a written warning). Danger is absent. Deviance is the interesting one. Nothing she does breaks a norm, since everybody checks a lock, and nobody sees her do it; only the amount is unusual, and only she knows the amount. A clinician does not need visible deviance to take her seriously, and if she sought help the question would be whether her checking meets the criteria for obsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 6.5). The lesson is that many serious disorders are private, invisible to the people who work beside the sufferer every day.

Sameer is the reverse. He is deviant in the statistical sense, far out in the tail of any distribution of chess skill or hours of practice, but he shows no distress, no dysfunction and no danger. He is the statistical criterion's classic false alarm: rare talent, not disorder. His aunts' complaint is a judgement about norms, and a weak one, since devoting oneself to a demanding skill breaks no rule of their society.

Leela shows intense distress, and perhaps dysfunction in the narrow sense that she has stopped her usual work. But her withdrawal is exactly what her community expects of a new widow. The roles she has dropped are the ones that custom suspends during mourning, and the role of mourner she fills completely. She is not deviant; if anything, it would be deviant for her not to grieve. The doctor was right to ask about suicide, and her answer settles that question: no danger. She is the textbook case of distress without disorder, the expectable response to a loss that the official definitions explicitly exclude (Section 1.1.3). If, many months from now, her longing were still so intense that she could not take up her life again, the question would change (Section 7.4.3 teaches prolonged grief disorder, and Section 5.2.4 the boundary between grief and depression).

Figure 1.1 places the three of them on a map of the four Ds. Notice that the person who most needs help, Aarti, sits where two circles overlap, outside the circle of deviance altogether. A clinician who looked only for unusual or rule-breaking behaviour would never find her.

In practice a criterion is often recognised from a single phrase rather than from a whole case, and several of the phrases sound alike. Table 1.1 collects the phrases that stand for each criterion, together with the look-alike phrases that point somewhere else.

"Violates society's explicit and implicit norms"Deviance (the social-norm criterion)Norm-breaking is the social half of deviance"Distressful", "dysfunctional"
"Unhappiness", "suffering", "upsetting to the person"DistressThe person's own pain"Deviance", "dysfunction"
"Interferes with daily functioning", "maladaptive"DysfunctionJudged by what the behaviour costs in work, self-care and relationships"Distress"

Exam trap

"According to statistical approaches, what defines abnormal behaviour?" The answer is deviation from typical behavioural patterns, because the statistical approach cares only about frequency, about how far a behaviour lies from what most people do. The distractors are all real features of disorder, which is what makes them tempting. "Experiencing intense anxiety" is a symptom and belongs to distress; "loss of contact with reality" describes psychosis, and was once the everyday and legal meaning of "insanity"; "withdrawal and depression" are symptoms. None of them says anything about rarity.

Exam trap

The aspect of abnormality that "includes unhappiness" is distress: deviance is about being different from others, distress about the person's own suffering. When a stem speaks of "society's explicit and implicit norms", the answer is deviance, not dysfunction or distress. Deviance covers both kinds of difference, statistical rarity and norm-breaking, so a question about "the D that includes statistical and social abnormality" also wants deviance. The four Ds are deviance, distress, dysfunction and danger; "delusion", "disability", "diagnosis", "dependence" and "drug use" are invented Ds. If a fifth D is offered, it is duration.

Clinical link

Ask whose distress brought the person to the clinic. In Indian outpatient departments it is often the family's: parents bring a son who "talks too much and has stopped sleeping" and who insists that nothing is wrong with him. The four Ds explain why such a referral must be taken seriously even when the person denies any problem, since mania and psychosis can bring dysfunction and danger without personal distress. The opposite mistake is just as common. People like Aarti, whose disorder is private and shameful, may wait years before telling anyone, so a clinician who sees them for something else should ask directly about time-consuming habits, worries and rituals.

What a mental disorder is: the official definitions

The four Ds tell a clinician what to look for. They do not say what a mental disorder is. That question was taken up in the 1990s by a philosopher of psychiatry and by the committees that write the official manuals, and their answers now stand at the front of DSM-5-TR and ICD-11.

The most influential analysis came from Jerome C. Wakefield, an American professor of social work and philosopher of psychiatry, in a 1992 paper in American Psychologist. A mental disorder, he argued, is a harmful dysfunction, and the two words do different jobs. Dysfunction is the factual part: some mental mechanism is failing to perform the function that evolution designed it to perform. Harmful is the value part: the failure must damage the person, judged by the standards of their culture. Both are needed. Leela's grief is harm without dysfunction, because the mechanism that makes us mourn is doing exactly what it evolved to do. On Wakefield's analysis Aarti shows both: the inner signal that tells a person a job is finished seems to fail her, and the failure costs her sleep and perhaps her job. Book 2, Section 1.6.6, sets out the account and its main weakness, which is that we rarely know for certain what any mental mechanism was designed to do.

DSM-5-TR (2022) begins its discussion of key concepts with a definition that keeps the wording of DSM-5 (2013). In plain words, a mental disorder is a syndrome, a cluster of features that go together, marked by a clinically significant disturbance in a person's cognition, emotion regulation or behaviour that reflects a dysfunction in the psychological, biological or developmental processes underlying mental functioning. Such disorders are usually, though not always, accompanied by significant distress or disability in social, occupational or other important activities. Two things are then excluded. An expectable or culturally approved response to a common stressor or loss, such as the death of a loved one, is not a mental disorder. Nor is socially deviant behaviour, whether political, religious or sexual, or a conflict that lies mainly between the individual and society, unless the deviance or conflict results from a dysfunction in the individual. Read the definition again with Section 1.1.2 in mind and you can see where each piece came from: Wakefield's dysfunction, Comer's distress and dysfunction, and two exclusion clauses, one to keep ordinary grief out and one to stop the social-norm criterion being turned against rebels, believers and minorities.

The paragraph that follows the definition in the manual is quoted far less often, and it matters just as much. It makes four points. First, a diagnosis should have clinical utility: it should help the clinician to predict the course, to plan treatment and to anticipate the outcome. A label that does none of these things is not worth giving. Second, a diagnosis is not the same as a need for treatment. Whether to treat is a separate decision, which weighs how severe the symptoms are, how salient they are (suicidal thoughts outweigh most other symptoms), how much mental pain and disability they cause, the risks and benefits of the treatments on offer, and other factors such as psychiatric symptoms that complicate a physical illness. Third, the logic runs the other way as well: a person whose symptoms fall short of the full criteria may still clearly need care, and the manual warns that missing symptoms should not be used as a reason to deny someone appropriate help. Fourth, the definition was written for clinical, public health and research purposes. A court deciding criminal responsibility, a board deciding disability compensation or a judge deciding competency needs more information than a diagnosis can supply (the manual's Cautionary Statement for Forensic Use is described in Section 1.8.1).

The manual is also candid about the weak point in all of this. Under the heading of the clinical significance criterion it admits that, in the absence of clear biological markers or useful measures of severity for many disorders, it has not been possible to separate normal from pathological expressions of a symptom completely. Sadness, worry and suspicion all appear in diagnostic criteria, yet in their milder forms they are part of everyone's life. The manual's solution is a general requirement attached to most disorders, usually worded "the disturbance causes clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning". It describes deciding whether this is met as an inherently difficult clinical judgement, and it advises that information from family members and other informants is often needed. In effect, the distress and dysfunction of the four Ds have been written into the criteria themselves.

The World Health Organization's ICD-11, adopted by the World Health Assembly in 2019 and in effect from 1 January 2022, describes the conditions in its Chapter 6, "Mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders", in almost the same terms. They are syndromes marked by a clinically significant disturbance in a person's cognition, emotional regulation or behaviour that reflects a dysfunction in the psychological, biological or developmental processes that underlie mental and behavioural functioning, and they are usually associated with distress or impairment in personal, family, social, educational, occupational or other important areas of functioning. The overlap with DSM-5-TR is no accident; the two organisations worked to bring their systems closer together (Section 1.9). Notice that ICD-11 names more areas of life in which impairment may show, including the family and education, and that it handles the border with normality disorder by disorder in its clinical guidelines rather than through exclusion clauses attached to the general definition.

Textbooks condense the official wording into working definitions. The best known belongs to David H. Barlow and V. Mark Durand, whose textbook of abnormal psychology defines a psychological disorder by three components that must be present together: a psychological dysfunction, meaning a breakdown in cognitive, emotional or behavioural functioning; personal distress or impairment; and a response that is atypical or not culturally expected. Each component is insufficient alone. A dysfunction that causes no distress or impairment may be a harmless quirk; distress alone may be grief; and an atypical response may be talent or eccentricity, as Sameer's is. Apply the third component to the opening of this chapter and you see at once why koro is a hard case: in Singapore in November 1967, terror about pork and the genitals was, for a few weeks, exactly what a man in that city might be expected to feel.

Indian law needs a definition for a different purpose: to decide when the state may limit a person's choices and when special protections apply. It therefore draws the line in a different place. Section 2(1)(s) of the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 defines mental illness as "a substantial disorder of thinking, mood, perception, orientation or memory that grossly impairs judgment, behaviour, capacity to recognise reality or ability to meet the ordinary demands of life". The definition includes mental conditions associated with the abuse of alcohol and drugs, and it excludes mental retardation (the Act's term), which it describes as a condition of arrested or incomplete development of mind, specially characterised by subnormality of intelligence. Section 3(3) adds an Indian version of DSM's second exclusion clause: mental illness shall not be determined on the basis of a person's political, economic or social status, membership of a cultural, racial or religious group, or non-conformity with the moral, social, cultural, work or political values or religious beliefs prevailing in their community. The words "substantial" and "grossly" matter, because they set the legal bar well above the clinical one. Aarti has a condition that a clinician might well diagnose, yet it would be a stretch to call her checking a substantial disorder that grossly impairs her judgement or her capacity to recognise reality. The Act's definition is built for decisions about capacity, rights and admission, and its high threshold stops people being brought under those provisions lightly (Book 2, Section 1.10.3). Table 1.2 sets the five definitions side by side.

Table 1.2 Five definitions of mental disorder compared.
SourceYearKey wordsWhat it addsWhat it excludes
Wakefield, "harmful dysfunction"1992Dysfunction of an evolved mental mechanism that causes harmSeparates the factual part (dysfunction) from the value part (harm)Harm without dysfunction (normal grief and fear); dysfunction without harm
DSM-5-TR (same wording as DSM-5, 2013)2022Syndrome; clinically significant disturbance in cognition, emotion regulation or behaviour; dysfunction; usually distress or disabilityClinical utility; diagnosis is not need for treatment; not enough alone for legal decisionsExpectable or culturally approved responses to loss; socially deviant behaviour and individual-society conflict, unless they stem from a dysfunction
ICD-11, Chapter 6Adopted 2019; in effect 2022Syndromes; clinically significant disturbance in cognition, emotional regulation or behaviour; dysfunction; usually distress or impairmentNames personal, family, social, educational and occupational functioning; used worldwideBoundary with normality set out disorder by disorder in its guidelines
Barlow and DurandTextbook definitionPsychological dysfunction; distress or impairment; atypical or not culturally expectedThree tests that must be met togetherResponses that are culturally expected

Exam trap

Harmful dysfunction is Wakefield's phrase (1992). Szasz called mental illness a myth (1961), Jahoda defined positive mental health (1958) and Comer gave the four Ds; none of them wrote "harmful dysfunction". In the DSM-5-TR definition, distress or disability is usually present, not always, so an option saying that a disorder "must always cause distress" is wrong. Expectable grief and socially deviant behaviour (political, religious or sexual) are excluded unless they arise from a dysfunction in the person. And a diagnosis does not by itself mean that treatment is needed: that is a separate clinical judgement.

Four ways of being normal: Offer and Sabshin

Every definition so far is a definition of abnormality. Its mirror image is more awkward: what, exactly, is normal? It sounds as if the answer should be easy, the absence of abnormality, but two American psychiatrists showed in the 1960s that the word "normal" is used in at least four different senses, and that clinicians slide from one to another without noticing.

Daniel Offer (1929 to 2013) spent his career studying ordinary teenagers, an unusual subject for a psychiatrist trained to treat illness. Melvin Sabshin (1925 to 2011) later served for almost a quarter of a century, from 1974 to 1997, as medical director of the American Psychiatric Association. Together they pointed out that psychiatrists are trained to recognise the abnormal and are therefore poorly equipped to recognise the normal: the training that sharpens one skill dulls the other. In Normality: Theoretical and Clinical Concepts of Mental Health (New York: Basic Books, 1966) they reviewed what the behavioural and social sciences had meant by normality and found four "functional perspectives", each with its own definition, which they argued complement one another.

Normality as health is the traditional medical and psychiatric view. Behaviour is normal when no manifest psychopathology is present, when the person is free of grossly observable signs and symptoms. On this view normality covers most of human behaviour and abnormality is the small remainder, and health means a reasonable rather than an optimal level of functioning. Offer and Sabshin illustrated it with a simple definition from the psychiatric literature of their day: a healthy person is someone reasonably free of undue pain, discomfort and disability. It is the view of the general practitioner who examines you, finds nothing wrong and sends you home: you are "normal" because no disease has been found, not because you are flourishing.

Normality as utopia is its opposite. Here normality is an ideal, a harmonious and optimal blending of the parts of the mental apparatus that culminates in optimal functioning. This is the sense psychoanalysts use when they describe the ideal person or the goal of a completed analysis, and Offer and Sabshin traced it to Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), who wrote in his 1937 essay "Analysis terminable and interminable" that a normal ego, like normality in general, is "an ideal fiction". The same ideal appears among therapists of quite different schools, Carl Rogers (1902 to 1987) among them, and Jahoda's positive mental health and Abraham Maslow's (1908 to 1970) self-actualising person are its close cousins. Its weaknesses are that few people ever reach it and that the ideal itself shifts with the culture and the century: the self-reliant individualist admired in one society is the selfish son in another.

Normality as average borrows the mathematics of the bell-shaped curve. The middle range is normal and both extremes are deviant, so the unusually well-adjusted person is as far from normal as the unusually disturbed one. This is the logic of the statistical criterion of Section 1.1.2, and it is the perspective behind every test norm, an IQ of 100 or a personality score near the mean. Offer used it in the study for which he is best remembered.

Key study: Offer's modal adolescents (from the early 1960s)

In the mid-twentieth century it was widely believed, especially among psychoanalysts, that turmoil is a normal and even necessary part of adolescence. Offer wanted to know what typical teenagers were actually like. He gave a specially constructed Self-Image Questionnaire to the boys entering two high schools in middle-class suburbs of Chicago, and selected as "modal" those whose scores fell within one standard deviation of the mean on almost all of its scales. This was normality as average put into practice: the method deliberately removed both the disturbed teenager and the one who functioned at a superior level. The 73 modal boys were then interviewed and followed for eight years, through high school and beyond. They had almost no history of gross psychopathology, had mastered earlier developmental tasks without serious setbacks, coped reasonably well with anxiety, shame, guilt and anger, and related well both to friends and to adults. Some had experimented with minor delinquency in their early teens and then left it behind. What the researchers did not find was serious "adolescent turmoil".

The study's limits are the limits of its perspective. It described only what was average in one group, middle-class suburban American boys of one era, and "average" is not the same as "healthy"; a definition that labels the superior teenager deviant has an obvious blind spot. But the project showed that normality could be studied rather than assumed, and it challenged the belief that storm and stress are an inevitable part of growing up.

Normality as process insists that normality cannot be judged from a single snapshot. Normal behaviour is the end result of interacting systems that change over time, so change across time is part of the definition. Offer and Sabshin pointed to Erik Erikson's (1902 to 1994) developmental stages and to general system theory as examples of this way of thinking, and as their example of process research they chose the work of Futterman, Hoffman and their colleagues at the University of Illinois, who followed parents of children with leukaemia from diagnosis onwards and asked how their ways of coping changed as the illness ran its course. The practical consequence is that the same behaviour can be normal at one age and abnormal at another. An imaginary friend at four, intense self-consciousness at fifteen and slower recall of names at seventy-five are all expected; none of them is expected at forty.

Some later accounts of the scheme add a fifth view, normality as transactional systems, in which normality is defined within an ongoing relationship, between a parent and a child for instance, each partner helping to define what is normal for the other. Offer and Sabshin returned to the theme in an edited collection, The Diversity of Normal Behavior (1991). Figure 1.2 draws the four original perspectives.

Indian MPhil teaching and psychiatry notes often present a slightly different list, the "models of normality". Each model names a different yardstick. The subjective model uses the person's own experience: you are normal if you feel no distress, suffer no disability and seek no help. The medical or health model uses the clinician's examination: you are normal if no psychiatric disorder or psychopathology can be found. The ideal or utopian model uses an image of optimal functioning, the statistical model uses the average, and the social-norm model uses the standards of the community, so that you are normal if your behaviour is socially acceptable. Three of these map straight onto Offer and Sabshin (ideal onto utopia, statistical onto average, medical onto health), and the social-norm model is the criterion of Section 1.1.2 turned into a definition of normality. The subjective model is normality as health seen from the patient's side of the desk, close to that simple definition of the healthy person as reasonably free of pain, discomfort and disability.

The two health models can disagree, and the disagreement matters clinically. A man in a manic episode feels wonderful and asks for no help, so on the subjective model he is normal; the clinician who finds a manic syndrome disagrees. A conscientious student who comes to the college counselling centre with ordinary examination nerves is seeking help, so on the subjective model she is not normal; a careful assessment finds no disorder at all. People who lack insight, and people who seek help readily, are exactly where a purely subjective yardstick goes wrong. Table 1.3 brings the views together.

Table 1.3 The views of normality, with the wording examiners use.
View"Normal" meansExampleExam wordingSource
Normality as health (medical or health model)No manifest signs or symptoms of psychopathology; reasonable, not optimal, functioningNothing found after a full assessment"Absence of any psychiatric disorder or psychopathology"Offer and Sabshin (1966): the traditional medical view
Subjective modelNo distress, no disability, no help-seeking, judged by the personSomeone who feels well, copes and asks for nothing"Absence of distress, disability or help-seeking behaviour"Indian MPhil notes; overlaps normality as health
Normality as utopia (ideal model)Harmonious, optimal functioningFreud's "ideal fiction"; Jahoda; Maslow"Optimal functioning", "ideal mental health"Offer and Sabshin (1966); psychoanalysts and humanists
Normality as average (statistical model)The middle of the distribution; both extremes deviantOffer's modal adolescents"Average", "middle range", "within one standard deviation"Offer and Sabshin (1966); the statistical criterion
Normality as processThe outcome of interacting systems over time; varies by stageA tantrum at 3 but not at 23"Changes across the life span", "interacting systems"Offer and Sabshin (1966); Erikson; system theory
Social-norm modelSocially acceptable behaviourDress and conduct expected at a wedding"Socially acceptable behaviour", "conformity"The social-norm criterion (Section 1.1.2)
Exam trap

"How is normality defined in the subjective model?" The answer is absence of distress, disability or help-seeking behaviour: "subjective" means judged by the person's own experience. The other options are the other models. "Optimal functioning" is the ideal or utopian model; "absence of any psychiatric disorder or psychopathology" is the medical or health model, judged by the clinician; "socially acceptable behaviour" is the social-norm model. If a question asks instead for Offer and Sabshin's four perspectives, the answer is health, utopia, average and process; "subjective" and "social norm" are not among their four.

Normal and abnormal as a continuum

The criteria and definitions in this section can leave the impression that people fall into two boxes, the normal and the disordered. Most of the evidence points the other way. Almost every symptom in this book is continuous with ordinary experience. Everyone feels low after a setback, worries before an important event, wonders now and then whether colleagues are talking about them, and holds a belief or two that others find odd. Many people in the general population report experiences that resemble hallucinations, such as hearing their name called when nobody has spoken (Chapter 2 describes them). Barlow and Durand put the point plainly: most psychological disorders are "simply extreme expressions of otherwise normal emotions, behaviors, and cognitive processes". The Indian notes that many students learn from say the same thing in a single line: abnormality depends on the intensity and frequency of behaviour.

What turns an ordinary experience into a symptom? Four things, which the criteria in later chapters measure again and again. Intensity: how strong is it? Frequency: how often does it come? Duration: how long has it lasted? Impairment: what has it cost? Worry becomes part of generalised anxiety disorder only when it is excessive, occurs on more days than not and has lasted at least six months. Low mood becomes part of a major depressive episode only when it, or a loss of interest and pleasure, has been present for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks, together with other symptoms. Aarti's checking differs from yours or mine not in kind (we all check a lock now and then) but in amount and in cost. Figure 1.3 draws the idea. Each experience runs from left to right along the same scale, and the clinical significance criterion of Section 1.1.3 marks the point at which a clinician begins to speak of disorder.

Exactly where the threshold belongs is a live argument, and grief is its best-known battleground. DSM-IV told clinicians not to diagnose a major depressive episode in the first two months after a death unless certain severe features were present, a rule known as the bereavement exclusion. DSM-5 (2013) removed the exclusion and replaced it with explanatory notes to help clinicians tell grief from depression (Section 5.2.4), and DSM-5-TR (2022) went further by adding a new diagnosis, prolonged grief disorder, for grief that remains intense and disabling long after a loss (Section 7.4.3). Supporters saw both changes as recognising real suffering; critics saw a universal human experience being turned into a diagnosis.

Normality also depends on who you are. It depends on developmental stage: a three-year-old's tantrum on the floor of a sweet shop is ordinary, and the same tantrum at twenty-three is not, which is Offer and Sabshin's normality as process at work. It depends on sex and gender, because societies expect different emotional displays from women and men; a man who weeps openly at his desk may be judged more harshly than a woman who does the same, and a clinician must not mistake a breach of a gender expectation for a symptom. And it depends on culture, which shapes not only what is expected of a person but how distress itself is felt and expressed, the subject of Section 1.10.

The continuum has a danger built into it. If there is no natural break between ordinary unhappiness and disorder, then every revision of a manual can move the line, and critics argue that it usually moves in one direction. The most prominent of them is Allen Frances (born 1942), the psychiatrist who chaired the task force that produced DSM-IV. In Saving Normal (2013) he argued that DSM-5 would medicalise ordinary life, turning everyday grief, the overeating of a world full of tempting food and other common experiences into mental disorders, so that more people would be labelled and more would be given medicines they did not need; his name for the process was diagnostic inflation. The manuals' main defence is the clinical significance criterion: an experience counts as a symptom of disorder only when it causes clinically significant distress or impairment. Whether that defence holds depends on how carefully clinicians apply it, one patient at a time.

Clinical link

When a family asks "Is this normal?", four questions do most of the work: how intense is it, how often does it happen, how long has it lasted, and what has it cost? Add a fifth: what is expected of a person of this age, this gender and this community? Remember too the DSM-5-TR point from Section 1.1.3, that need for care and diagnosis are separate questions. Leela has no disorder, but a widow who lives alone may still benefit from a follow-up visit, from practical help and from someone who checks, in a few months, that her grief is easing.

Exam trap

The bereavement exclusion was removed in DSM-5 (2013); prolonged grief disorder was added in DSM-5-TR (2022), not in DSM-5. The general definition of a mental disorder still excludes an expectable response to the death of a loved one. What changed is that bereavement no longer automatically rules out a diagnosis of major depression when the full criteria for it are met.

Critics, labels and culture

Some critics reject the enterprise itself rather than the position of the line. The psychiatrist Thomas Szasz (1920 to 2012) argued in The Myth of Mental Illness (1961) that, apart from demonstrable disease of the brain, mental illness is a metaphor for problems in living, and that medical labels disguise moral and social judgements. David L. Rosenhan's 1973 study, in which people with no psychiatric history were admitted to psychiatric hospitals as pseudopatients and diagnosed with schizophrenia, was taken for decades as proof that a label changes what clinicians see; the journalist Susannah Cahalan's The Great Pretender (2019) has since raised serious doubts about how the study was carried out. These critiques, and the labelling theory that grew up beside them, are told in full in Book 2, Sections 1.6.7 and 2.10.7, and are not retold here. This chapter returns to what a diagnostic label costs, and what it gives, in Section 1.6.8.

Culture has run through every part of this section: whose norms, whose expectations of a woman or a man, whose idea of a proper response to a death. Section 1.10 takes it up directly, with the cultural concepts of distress, the Cultural Formulation Interview and the syndromes themselves. For now, carry one conclusion forward. Whether a person is disordered cannot be read off them the way a blood group can be typed; it is decided, and the decision blends evidence about what is failing with judgements about what matters. The rest of this chapter is about making that decision well: first by naming carefully what we see (Section 1.2), then by asking why it happens (Sections 1.3 to 1.5), and finally by sorting it into the categories of the DSM and the ICD (Sections 1.6 to 1.9).

Scan Your Brain
