1.2 The Vocabulary of Psychopathology: Onset, Course, Prognosis, Prevalence and ComorbidityMedium yield · 3 Qs

Open any chapter of this book after this one and you will meet sentences like these: "The onset is usually insidious, in late adolescence." "The course is episodic, with full recovery between episodes in most cases." "The prognosis is guarded." "The 12-month prevalence is about 1 percent, and comorbidity with depression is the rule." Each of these sentences is packed with technical words, and each word has a precise meaning that examiners test directly. A student who half-knows them reads every later chapter through a fog. This section is therefore the glossary for the rest of the book. It defines the words that clinicians use to describe a disorder (what it looks like, how it starts, how it unfolds and how it ends) and the words that epidemiologists use to count it (how many people have it, how many new cases appear each year, and how often it comes in company with other disorders). Section 1.1 asked where the line between normal and abnormal should be drawn; this section gives you the language for describing what lies on the far side of the line.

Describing, explaining, treating

Start with the name of the field. The word psychopathology means the scientific study of mental disorders: their nature, their causes, how they develop and change over time, and how they can be treated. The word joins the Greek psyche (mind) and pathos (suffering) to logos (study), so it means, literally, the study of the mind's sufferings. Clinicians also use the word more loosely, for the abnormal experiences and behaviour themselves. When a report ends "no evidence of psychopathology", it means that the examiner found no symptoms, not that no science was done. The term abnormal psychology names the branch of psychology that studies abnormal behaviour and experience, and in practice the two terms overlap so much that most textbooks treat them as synonyms; the difference is one of flavour. "Abnormal psychology" is the name of the undergraduate course and of the textbooks that psychology departments use, while "psychopathology" is the word preferred in psychiatry and in clinical reports. Indian revision notes such as Psycho Didi's put the difference neatly: abnormality is atypicality in general, and psychopathology is its medical angle, with its concern for aetiology, diagnosis and prognosis.

What does the science of psychopathology actually do? David H. Barlow and V. Mark Durand, whose Abnormal Psychology: An Integrative Approach is one of the most widely used textbooks in the field, divide the work into three tasks. The first is clinical description, which they define as the unique combination of behaviours, thoughts and feelings that make up a specific disorder. A good clinical description says what makes the disorder different from ordinary experience and from neighbouring disorders, and it also gathers the facts about who gets it, when it starts and how it runs. The second task is aetiology, the study of causes: why the disorder begins, at the biological, psychological and social levels (Section 1.3 is about the language of cause). The third is treatment and outcome: what helps, and what becomes of people with and without help. The three are linked. A treatment that works can hint at a cause, although, as Barlow and Durand warn, the hint can mislead: a headache that lifts after an aspirin was not caused by a shortage of aspirin.

Description is the oldest of the three tasks and, in psychiatry, still the foundation of the others, because most mental disorders are still identified by what they look like rather than by what causes them. The careful, disciplined description of abnormal experiences, as the patient lives them, is called descriptive psychopathology, and its founding text is General Psychopathology (1913), by the German psychiatrist and philosopher Karl Jaspers (1883 to 1969). Jaspers taught clinicians to separate the form of an experience (is it a hallucination, a delusion, an obsession?) from its content (what the voice says, what the delusion is about), and Chapter 2 is built on his method. Two more words complete the picture. A nosology is a system for classifying diseases (the word also names the science of doing so), and nomenclature is the agreed set of names that a classification uses; DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 are both nosologies, and each provides a nomenclature (Book 2, Section 2.10.1 explains why clinicians classify at all, and Sections 1.6 to 1.9 of this chapter take the classifications apart).

Symptom, sign, syndrome, disorder, disease

Meet Pooja, who will help us through the rest of this section. She is 31, a mathematics teacher from Nagpur, married, with a son of six. Four months ago her husband was transferred to Pune, and she gave up her job to move with him into a flat in a city where she knows nobody. When she comes to the outpatient clinic, she says that for about two months she has felt "empty, as if someone switched off the lights inside". Nothing gives her pleasure, not even her son. She wakes at four every morning and cannot fall asleep again, she has no appetite, she is tired all day, she cannot follow the plot of a television serial, and she feels that she has become "a useless burden on everyone". Once or twice she has thought that it would not matter if she did not wake up, though she has made no plan. While she talks, the psychologist notices other things. Her speech is slow, with long pauses before each answer. She looks at the floor, her eyes fill with tears twice, and the clinic's scale shows that she weighs four kilograms less than at her last medical check-up.

Pooja's first list is made of symptoms, and her second of signs. A symptom is a subjective experience that the person reports and that the clinician can know only by asking: low mood, loss of pleasure, early waking, guilt, poor concentration, thoughts of death. A sign is an objective feature that the clinician observes or measures: slowed speech, tearfulness, poor eye contact, loss of weight. Some features can be both, as when a man complains of shaking hands and the doctor also sees the tremor. Psychiatry leans more heavily on symptoms than the rest of medicine does, which is one reason the interview matters so much in this field. The difference has a practical edge: a symptom can be concealed or exaggerated, while a sign is open to anyone who looks.

Signs and symptoms that tend to occur together and suggest a common underlying condition make up a syndrome, from the Greek for "running together". Pooja's low mood, loss of interest, sleep and appetite changes, fatigue, guilt, poor concentration and slowing hang together as the depressive syndrome, and the same cluster has been recognised for centuries. Whether the syndrome counts as a mental disorder depends on whether it meets the definition given in Section 1.1.3 (clinically significant disturbance, reflecting a dysfunction, usually with distress or impairment); Pooja's does, and she would be diagnosed with major depressive disorder, recurrent episode, for reasons that will become clear below. DSM chose the word "disorder" deliberately, because it makes no claim about cause. A disease, in strict medical usage, is a condition whose pathology and cause are known: general paresis, the dementia and paralysis caused by syphilis of the brain (Book 2, Section 1.3.6), Huntington's disease, caused by a single abnormal gene, or Alzheimer's disease, with its plaques and tangles. Most psychiatric conditions are not yet diseases in this sense. A third word, illness, is sometimes used for the person's own experience of being unwell. The psychiatrist and anthropologist Arthur Kleinman, writing with Leon Eisenberg and Byron Good in 1978 (Section 1.10.1 introduces all three), made the distinction famous: the doctor diagnoses and treats a disease, but the patient suffers an illness, which includes what the problem means to her, to her family and to her culture. Pooja's illness includes her conviction that she is failing as a wife and mother, and that meaning matters as much to her recovery as her sleep does.

One more word explains why psychiatric diagnosis works the way it does. A sign is pathognomonic when it is found in one condition only, so that seeing it settles the diagnosis; the small white Koplik spots inside the cheek in measles are the textbook medical example. Psychiatry has almost no pathognomonic signs. Even the first-rank symptoms of the German psychiatrist Kurt Schneider (1887 to 1967), which were once treated as nearly decisive for schizophrenia, turned out to occur in mania and other psychoses too (Section 4.1.6). Because no single feature settles a psychiatric diagnosis, the diagnostic manuals define disorders by syndromes: a required number of features from a list, lasting a required time, causing a required degree of trouble. Chapter 2 adds two further distinctions that you will need there (primary versus secondary symptoms, and the form versus the content of a symptom).

Exam trap

A symptom is reported by the patient (subjective); a sign is observed by the clinician (objective). A syndrome is a cluster of signs and symptoms that go together; it says nothing about cause. A disease has a known pathology or cause, which is why the manuals speak of disorders. Pathognomonic means "diagnostic of one condition only", and options claiming that a psychiatric symptom is pathognomonic are almost always wrong.

Onset

The first thing a clinician wants to know about a new problem is how it began. The onset is the beginning of a disorder, described by when it happened and how fast it developed. Barlow and Durand give the two basic patterns. An acute onset is sudden: the person passes from well to clearly ill in hours or days. An insidious onset is gradual: the disorder creeps in over months or years, so that the family can often say only that "he slowly changed". Delirium is the model of an acute onset; DSM-5-TR states that it develops over a short period, usually hours to a few days. Brief psychotic disorder usually begins abruptly too; its ICD-10 counterpart, acute and transient psychotic disorder, requires an acute onset by definition, and when the episode follows events that would be markedly stressful to almost anyone, DSM-5-TR adds the specifier "with marked stressors", the condition once called brief reactive psychosis. At the other extreme, the neurocognitive disorder due to Alzheimer's disease requires, as one of its criteria, an insidious onset and gradual progression, and DSM-5-TR says that most people with schizophrenia show a slow and gradual development of social withdrawal, emotional change and cognitive change before the first psychotic symptoms, although the onset may be abrupt. Pooja's depression sits in between, as many depressions do: it came on over about six weeks, which a clinician would call gradual but not insidious in the sense of the slow decline of years.

Two refinements are worth knowing. First, some classifications put numbers on these words. ICD-10, for its group of acute and transient psychotic disorders, defines an acute onset as a change from a non-psychotic to a clearly psychotic state within two weeks or less, and records an abrupt onset, within 48 hours or less, separately (Section 4.5.1). Second, the word "acute" is also used for duration rather than onset, which can trap the unwary. DSM-5-TR specifies delirium as acute when it lasts a few hours or days and persistent when it lasts weeks or months, and it specifies hypersomnolence disorder as acute (less than one month), subacute (one to three months) or persistent (more than three months). Read the stem carefully to see whether "acute" is describing how the problem began or how long it has lasted.

The age at onset is the second descriptor, and it differs greatly from disorder to disorder. Taking the figures in DSM-5-TR, specific phobias usually begin in childhood, with a mean age at onset of about 10; social anxiety disorder has a median onset of 13 in the United States; obsessive-compulsive disorder has a mean onset of 19.5; schizophrenia peaks in the early to mid-20s in men and the late 20s in women; and generalised anxiety disorder begins later than the other anxiety disorders, with a mean onset of 35 in North America. Taken together, mental disorders are mostly disorders of the young. In the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, a large American survey that you will meet in Section 1.2.6, half of all lifetime cases of mental disorder had begun by the age of 14 and three-quarters by the age of 24. That one finding explains why prevention and early intervention services aim at schools and colleges.

Three related terms describe the period around the onset. The prodrome (from the Greek prodromos, running before) is the period of early, non-specific change that comes before the full syndrome: in schizophrenia, months of withdrawal, falling marks, neglect of hygiene and odd ideas before any voice is heard (Section 4.6.4 follows one young man through it). A person's premorbid functioning is how well the person managed at work, study and relationships before the illness, and premorbid personality is what the person was like before it; both are taken in the case history (Book 2, Section 2.3) and both carry prognostic weight, as Section 1.2.5 shows. Finally, a precipitating event is a stressor that comes shortly before the onset and appears to trigger it. Pooja's move to Pune, the loss of her job and her sudden isolation are candidate precipitants. Notice the word "appears": a stressor before an onset is not proof of cause, as Section 1.3.3 explains.

Exam trap

Insidious onset means gradual, developing slowly over months or years. It does not mean dangerous, sly, hidden or deliberately concealed, which is what the everyday word suggests. Acute onset means sudden. Watch for the second meaning of "acute": short duration, as in acute versus persistent delirium. The prodrome comes before the full disorder; the residual phase (Section 1.2.4) comes after it.

Course

Once a disorder has begun, it unfolds in a pattern over time, and that pattern is its course. Barlow and Durand describe three basic patterns. A chronic course is one in which the disorder tends to last a long time, sometimes a lifetime; much schizophrenia follows such a course. An episodic course is one in which the person is likely to recover within a few months, only to suffer a recurrence later, a pattern that may repeat throughout life; the mood disorders are the classic example. A time-limited course is one in which the disorder improves, even without treatment, in a relatively short period. Brief psychotic disorder is time-limited by definition: DSM-5-TR requires that an episode last at least one day but less than one month, with an eventual full return to the person's previous level of functioning. Adjustment disorder is another, because by definition it must resolve within six months of the end of the stressor or its consequences.

Real disorders add some variations to Barlow's three. A waxing and waning course (also called fluctuating) is continuous but uneven: the disorder never fully lifts, yet it is better in some months and worse in others. DSM-5-TR uses these very words for generalised anxiety disorder, whose symptoms "tend to be chronic and wax and wane across the life span", and for untreated obsessive-compulsive disorder, whose course is usually chronic with waxing and waning symptoms (Sections 6.4 and 6.5). A progressive course, also called deteriorating, is one in which the person steadily gets worse; it is the course of the neurodegenerative neurocognitive disorders, such as Alzheimer's disease, while vascular neurocognitive disorder often declines in steps, each step following a fresh stroke. Two further words describe episodes. A single episode is the only one the person has had; a recurrent disorder has had two or more. DSM-5-TR codes major depressive disorder differently for a single episode and for a recurrent one, and Pooja's diagnosis is recurrent because she had an earlier episode at 22, after failing to win a government teaching post. That episode lasted about seven months and ended with a full recovery, after which she was well for nine years.

Figure 1.4 draws the five commonest shapes on the same time scale. Look at the difference between the second and third panels: both are episodic, but in the third the person never quite returns to her old self between episodes. That leftover, below the level needed for a diagnosis but above normal, is called residual symptoms, and in schizophrenia the period after an active episode, when such symptoms linger, is called the residual phase (Section 4.6.4). Whether recovery between episodes is full or partial matters a great deal, because people with residual symptoms relapse sooner.

Four more words describe what happens to an episode once treatment begins, and they are easy to muddle. A remission is a period in which the symptoms have fallen to a minimal level, so that the person is, for practical purposes, well again. A recovery is a remission that has lasted long enough (usually several months) for the episode to be considered over. A relapse is the return of the full syndrome during remission, before recovery, so it is the same episode coming back. A recurrence is a new episode after recovery. The distinction decides how long treatment continues, and Section 5.7.1 teaches all of these terms, with response, in the setting where they were defined, the treatment of depression.

Course is not only a description. It was the main tool that the German psychiatrist Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926) used, at the end of the nineteenth century, to divide the psychoses into a deteriorating dementia praecox and an episodic manic-depressive insanity, and Section 1.6.2 tells how course became the basis of modern classification. DSM-5-TR still builds course into its diagnoses through specifiers: after a year of illness, for example, schizophrenia can be specified as first episode or multiple episodes, each currently in acute episode, in partial remission or in full remission, or as continuous (Section 4.4.2).

Exam trap

Barlow's three courses: chronic (lasts a long time, sometimes a lifetime: schizophrenia), episodic (recovery within months, then recurrence: the mood disorders) and time-limited (improves without treatment in a relatively short time). Do not confuse acute, which describes onset, with episodic, which describes course. Relapse is the same episode returning before recovery; recurrence is a new episode after recovery. A disorder that is better and worse but never gone is waxing and waning, not episodic.

Prognosis and outcome

Onset and course describe what has happened so far. The clinician's next question looks ahead. The prognosis is the anticipated course and likely outcome of a disorder: a forecast of whether, how fully and how soon the person will recover, and of how likely the disorder is to return or to become chronic. The word comes from the Greek for "knowing beforehand". Barlow and Durand give the two phrases you will hear on every ward: "the prognosis is good", meaning that the person will probably recover, and "the prognosis is guarded", meaning that the probable outcome does not look good. Clinicians also speak of a fair or poor prognosis. A prognosis can be stated for a disorder in general ("untreated obsessive-compulsive disorder has a poor prognosis") or for one person at one moment ("Pooja's prognosis for this episode is good").

The easiest way to keep prognosis apart from its neighbours is to think about time. The history of an illness looks backwards: it is the account, taken from the patient and the family, of how the problem began and developed up to today (Book 2, Section 2.3 teaches how to take it). The presenting symptoms are the complaints of the present moment, the reasons the person has come now. The diagnosis identifies and names the condition, and it is reached by applying diagnostic criteria, the rules a classification sets out for deciding whether a disorder is present. Aetiology asks what caused it. Only prognosis looks forward. In one sentence: history and aetiology deal with the past, the presenting symptoms and diagnosis with the present, and prognosis with the future.

How can anyone forecast the future of a mental disorder? By studying large numbers of people over time and noting which features, present early, predict a better or worse course. Such features are called prognostic factors, and they should be kept apart from risk factors. A risk factor predicts who will develop a disorder in the first place; a prognostic factor predicts what will happen to someone who already has it. DSM-5-TR puts both under the single heading "Risk and Prognostic Factors" and adds a subsection on course modifiers, the factors that make the course worse or better. The best-studied set belongs to schizophrenia, and Section 4.6.7 teaches it in full. In outline, the outlook is better when the onset is acute rather than insidious, late rather than early, and follows a clear precipitating stressor; when the person functioned well before the illness; when mood symptoms and positive symptoms are prominent and negative symptoms are not; when the person is a woman, is married, and has relatives with mood disorder rather than schizophrenia; when the psychosis is treated quickly; and when the family atmosphere is calm and supportive. The logic is worth seeing. Good premorbid functioning means there is a life to return to, and an acute onset after a stress suggests an episode laid over a fairly intact brain, while an insidious, early onset with negative symptoms suggests a long-standing developmental problem.

Now apply the idea to Pooja. In favour of a good outcome for this episode are her full recovery last time, her good functioning for nine years in between, an identifiable precipitant, no alcohol or drug use, a supportive husband, and the fact that she has come for help within weeks rather than years. Against her is the fact that this is her second episode: in major depressive disorder, every episode makes another more likely, so her long-term prognosis, the risk of further recurrences, is more guarded (Sections 5.5.6 and 5.7 explain why and what can be done). Her passing thoughts of death also belong in the prognosis, because the risk of suicide is part of any forecast in depression (Section 5.8). Prognoses differ widely across disorders. Brief psychotic disorder has a favourable outcome for most people, although DSM-5-TR notes that more than half later relapse. Adjustment disorder, by definition, resolves within six months of the end of its stressor. Untreated obsessive-compulsive disorder, by contrast, usually runs a chronic course, and DSM-5-TR cites a remission rate of only about 20 percent among adults re-examined 40 years later.

Prognosis is the forecast; outcome is what actually happens. Researchers distinguish two kinds. A symptomatic remission means that the symptoms have gone or fallen to a minimal level. A functional recovery means that the person is back at work or study, in their relationships and in their ordinary roles. The two often part company. A man with schizophrenia may lose his voices on medication yet remain unable to hold a job because of the cognitive and negative symptoms that medication helps least, and a woman whose depression has lifted may take months to regain her old confidence at work. Service users have added a third meaning: personal recovery, living a satisfying and hopeful life with or without continuing symptoms.

Clinical link

Families nearly always ask, "Will she become normal again?" A good answer gives the forecast as odds, not destiny, and names the factors that can still be changed. For Pooja: "Most people recover from an episode like this one, as you did last time. Because this is the second episode, we will plan to keep treatment going after you are well, and we will agree on the early signs that should bring you back." For a young man after a first psychotic episode the modifiable factors are the ones in Section 4.6.7: taking treatment, avoiding cannabis, and a calm home. A prognosis delivered this way gives everyone a job to do.

Exam trap

"Which of the following best defines prognosis?" The answer is the likely outcome or course of the disorder, a forecast. The distractors each name something else: the history of the illness is the account of its past; the immediate symptoms are the presenting complaints; the diagnostic criteria are the rules for making the diagnosis. Also not prognosis: diagnosis (naming the condition) and aetiology (its cause). A prognostic factor predicts course in someone already ill; a risk factor predicts who becomes ill.

How common? Prevalence, incidence and the burden of disorder

So far the words have described a disorder in one person. The next set describes it in a population, and they belong to epidemiology, the study of how disorders are distributed in populations and of the factors that determine that distribution (the word comes from the Greek epi, upon, and demos, the people). Epidemiology asks two kinds of question. Descriptive questions ask how many people have a disorder, and who, where and when. Analytic questions ask why: which exposures raise or lower the risk. The answers matter to three different people. A health planner needs them to decide how many beds, clinics and psychologists a district needs. A researcher uses them to find causes, because a disorder that is commoner in one place, period or group may point to something operating there. And a patient's relative, asking "What are the chances that my son will get it too?", is asking an epidemiological question.

The most basic count is prevalence: the proportion of a population that has the disorder. It counts existing cases, new and old together, and it is usually given as a percentage or as so many per 1,000. Because "has the disorder" needs a time frame, there are three kinds. The point prevalence is the proportion who have the disorder at a single point in time, such as the day of the survey. The period prevalence is the proportion who have it at any time during a stated period, and the period used most often is 12 months; DSM-5-TR says that its Prevalence sections most often give 12-month figures, although point prevalence is given for some disorders. The lifetime prevalence is the proportion who have had the disorder at any time in their lives up to the survey, including people who have long since recovered. Imagine a survey of 1,000 adults in which 30 are depressed on the day of the interview, 70 have been depressed at some time in the past year, and 160 have been depressed at some time in their lives. The point prevalence is 3 percent, the 12-month prevalence 7 percent and the lifetime prevalence 16 percent. Notice the built-in rule: in any one survey, lifetime prevalence can never be smaller than 12-month prevalence, and 12-month prevalence can never be smaller than point prevalence, because each count includes the one before it.

Lifetime prevalence has a weakness. It depends on how old the people surveyed are, because a 20-year-old has lived through only a fraction of the years in which most disorders begin, and on memory, because people forget or play down episodes from long ago. Epidemiologists therefore also calculate the lifetime morbid risk: the projected proportion of people who will have developed the disorder by a given age, if they live that long, with an allowance made for the years of risk that the younger members of the sample have still to live. DSM-5-TR gives a lifetime morbid risk of 9.0 percent for generalised anxiety disorder in the United States. In the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, described below, the lifetime prevalence of any mental disorder was 46.4 percent, and the projected risk of having had one by the age of 75 was 50.8 percent.

Prevalence counts the people who are ill. The incidence counts the people who become ill: the number of new cases arising in a population at risk during a stated period, usually expressed as a rate, such as so many new cases per 100,000 people per year. The words "at risk" matter. People who already have the disorder cannot become new cases, so they are left out of the denominator. The careful reviews of the Australian epidemiologist John McGrath and his colleagues put the median incidence of schizophrenia at about 15 new cases per 100,000 people per year (Section 4.6.1). Incidence is the figure a researcher wants when hunting for causes, because a change in incidence means that the forces that start the disorder have changed; prevalence is the figure a planner wants, because it says how many people need care now.

How are the two related? The easiest way to see it is the bathtub in Figure 1.5. New cases flow in from the tap: that is incidence. The water in the tub is everyone who has the disorder at the moment: that is prevalence. Water leaves through two drains, recovery and death (and, in a district survey, people moving away). The level in the tub depends not only on how fast the tap runs but on how fast the drains empty it. When a population is in a steady state and the disorder is not too common, prevalence is roughly equal to incidence multiplied by the average duration of the disorder. A chronic disorder therefore has a high prevalence compared with its incidence, and a brief one has a low prevalence even if it strikes often.

Push a number through the tub. Take an imaginary district, Sundarpur, with one million adults. If schizophrenia arises there at about 15 new cases per 100,000 a year, the tap adds about 150 new cases each year. A systematic review of the world literature by Sukanta Saha, McGrath and their colleagues (2005) put the median point prevalence of schizophrenia at 4.6 per 1,000, which in Sundarpur means about 4,600 people living with the illness on any given day. Divide the level by the inflow and you get about 30 years: on average, a case of schizophrenia stays in the tub for decades, which is exactly what a chronic disorder means. Now imagine a different disorder with the same incidence that lasted, on average, one month. The same 150 cases a year would produce a point prevalence of only about 12 people. Same tap, different drains, a very different level. The tub also explains a paradox that puzzles students. A better treatment that keeps people with a chronic illness alive longer, without curing them, slows the death drain and so raises prevalence, even though nothing has got worse; a treatment that speeds recovery lowers it.

Barlow and Durand list two more descriptors that every clinical description includes. The sex ratio is the proportion of cases in men and in women. DSM-5-TR reports that women have roughly twice the rate of major depressive disorder that men do, especially between menarche and menopause, and that women and girls are at least twice as likely as men and boys to have generalised anxiety disorder, while substance use disorders run the other way. The second descriptor, age at onset, was covered in Section 1.2.3.

Epidemiologists also need words for the strength of a link between an exposure and a disorder. A risk factor is any characteristic or exposure associated with a higher probability of developing a disorder. Its strength is often expressed as the relative risk, the risk of the disorder in people who were exposed divided by the risk in people who were not. If 10 percent of people who lost a parent in childhood become depressed by 30, against 5 percent of everyone else, the relative risk is 2: the exposed group carries twice the risk. Case-control studies, which start from people who already have the disorder and look back at their exposures, report instead an odds ratio, which approximates the relative risk when the disorder is rare. The arithmetic of both belongs to the Research Methodology book. The point to hold on to here is that a risk factor is a statistical association, not necessarily a cause, and Section 1.3.3 explains why.

Where do the famous figures come from? Three generations of large surveys, each made possible by structured interviews that trained lay interviewers could give to thousands of people in their homes (the interviews themselves belong to the Assessment book). The first was the American Epidemiologic Catchment Area study of the early 1980s, which interviewed about 20,000 adults at five sites in the United States and was reported in a 1991 book edited by Lee N. Robins and Darrel A. Regier. The second was the National Comorbidity Survey, the first to give a structured psychiatric interview to a national probability sample of Americans (aged 15 to 54). In 1994 Ronald C. Kessler and his colleagues reported that nearly 50 percent of the respondents had had at least one DSM-III-R disorder in their lifetime and close to 30 percent in the past year. The repeat survey, the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, interviewed 9,282 adults between 2001 and 2003; published in 2005, it put the lifetime prevalence of any DSM-IV disorder at 46.4 percent and the 12-month prevalence at 26.2 percent, with anxiety disorders the commonest group (28.8 percent lifetime). The third generation is the World Health Organization's World Mental Health Survey Initiative, which has given the same interview in many countries so that their rates can be compared.

Indian context

India's own count is the National Mental Health Survey 2015 to 2016, carried out by NIMHANS in 12 states (Book 2, Section 1.10.11 describes how it was done). Excluding tobacco use disorder, it found that 10.6 percent of adults had a current mental disorder and 13.7 percent had had one at some time in their lives: current versus lifetime prevalence, the distinction this subsection has just taught. Its most sobering figure was the treatment gap, the proportion of affected people receiving no treatment, which ran from 70.4 percent for bipolar disorder to 86.3 percent for alcohol use disorder, and reached 91.8 percent for tobacco use (hence the survey's summary range of "70 to 92 percent"). Do not compare its lifetime figure directly with the American 46.4 percent: the surveys used different interviews, counted different disorders and asked about different spans of memory.

Two last terms measure the weight that disorders place on a population rather than their numbers (Table 1.4 then collects every term of this subsection). The disability-adjusted life year (DALY) is one year of healthy life lost, counted by adding the years of life lost to early death to the years lived with disability. The years lived with disability (YLDs) count time spent in ill health, weighted by how severe the condition is. Because most mental disorders disable far more often than they kill directly, their burden lies mainly in the YLDs. In the Global Burden of Disease study's analysis for 2019, mental disorders accounted for 125.3 million DALYs and 14.6 percent of all the world's YLDs, and they remained among the ten leading causes of burden worldwide. The World Health Organization's World Mental Health Report of 2022 put the human scale plainly: in 2019 nearly a billion people, including 14 percent of the world's adolescents, were living with a mental disorder.

Table 1.4 The epidemiological terms.
TermDefinitionExampleExam wording
PrevalenceProportion of a population that has the disorder (all existing cases)About 7 percent of US adults had major depressive disorder in the past year (DSM-5-TR)"How many have", "the frequency of mental illness"
Point prevalenceHas the disorder at one point in timeSchizophrenia, median 4.6 per 1,000 (Saha and colleagues, 2005)"On a given day", "at the time of the survey"
Period (12-month) prevalenceHas it at any time during a stated period, usually a year26.2 percent of US adults, any disorder (NCS-R, 2005)"In the past year"
Lifetime prevalenceHas ever had it, up to the time of the survey46.4 percent (NCS-R); 13.7 percent (NMHS India)"Ever in their lives"
Lifetime morbid riskProjected proportion who will have had it by a given ageGeneralised anxiety disorder, 9.0 percent in the US (DSM-5-TR)"Risk of developing it over a lifetime"
IncidenceNew cases in the population at risk during a period, as a rateSchizophrenia, about 15 per 100,000 per year"New cases", "rate of onset"
Sex ratioProportion of cases in men and womenMajor depression about twice as common in women"More common in"
Age at onsetTypical age when the disorder beginsHalf of all lifetime cases begin by 14 (NCS-R)"Typical age of onset"
Treatment gapProportion of affected people who receive no treatmentAbout 70 to 92 percent across disorder groups, the top figure for tobacco (NMHS India)"Untreated", "unmet need"

Exam trap

"The frequency of mental illness" is prevalence (or incidence, if only new cases are meant), never comorbidity. Incidence counts only new cases; prevalence counts all existing cases, new and old. In a single survey, lifetime prevalence can never be lower than 12-month prevalence, which can never be lower than point prevalence. DSM-5-TR usually reports 12-month prevalence. A chronic disorder has a high prevalence relative to its incidence, because prevalence is roughly incidence multiplied by duration.

Comorbidity

Gaurav is 28 and works as an accountant for a firm in Hyderabad. His wife brought him to the clinic after he missed a second audit deadline. From about the age of 13 he dreaded being looked at: when he had to read aloud in class he blushed, his voice shook and his mind went blank, and he invented stomach aches to avoid school functions. In college he made a discovery. Two beers before a party made him, as he puts it, "normal for one evening". After he joined the firm he began to drink before client meetings and office celebrations, then most evenings at home, alone. He now needs far more than he used to for the same effect, his hands shake on Monday mornings, and his attempts to cut down have failed. For the past two months he has also felt hopeless and flat, and he wakes at three in the morning. The clinician's working list has three entries: social anxiety disorder, alcohol use disorder, and a depressive syndrome whose nature is still uncertain. If the low mood clears within about a month of stopping drinking, it was probably alcohol-induced; if it persists well beyond that, DSM-5-TR's guidance points to an independent depressive disorder (Chapter 10 explains the rule).

Gaurav illustrates comorbidity: the co-occurrence of two or more differently defined disorders in the same person. The disorders may be present together now, which is concurrent comorbidity (Gaurav's social anxiety and his alcohol use disorder), or at different times over the person's life, which is lifetime comorbidity (his social anxiety began fifteen years before his drinking became a disorder). Comorbidity is about one person carrying more than one diagnosis; it has nothing to do with how common a disorder is in the population, and nothing to do with how much a person suffers.

The word was coined not in psychiatry but in general medicine. In 1970 the American physician and epidemiologist Alvan R. Feinstein, in a paper in the Journal of Chronic Diseases on the classification of "co-morbidity" in chronic disease, defined it as any additional co-existing ailment in a patient with a particular index disease. His concern was statistical. A trial that compares two treatments for, say, diabetes will mislead if one group happens to have more heart disease than the other, because the extra illness alters the course, the choice of treatment and the outcome. Psychiatry took up the word in the 1980s and 1990s, once the manuals began to allow people to carry more than one diagnosis at a time (the hierarchy and exclusion rules that once prevented this are taught in Section 1.6.5), and it found that in mental disorders co-occurrence is not the exception but the rule. DSM-5-TR itself lists "high rates of comorbidity among disorders" first among the structural problems of a purely categorical system.

Key study: the National Comorbidity Survey (Kessler and colleagues, 1994)

The survey that gave comorbidity its public name was designed, as its title says, to study it. Kessler's team interviewed a national probability sample of Americans aged 15 to 54 with a structured diagnostic interview given by lay interviewers. Beyond the headline prevalence figures, the most striking finding concerned concentration. More than half of all the lifetime disorders found occurred in the 14 percent of the population who had a history of three or more disorders, and this highly comorbid group also included the vast majority of the people with severe disorders. Mental illness, in other words, was not spread thinly across the population; much of it was piled up in about one person in seven (the 14 percent). Even in this group, fewer than half had ever received specialist mental health treatment. The replication survey (2005) found the same pattern a decade later: of the people with a disorder in the past year, 55 percent had one diagnosis, 22 percent had two and 23 percent had three or more.

Some pairings are so common that every chapter of this book will mention them. Depression goes with the anxiety disorders so often that some researchers wonder whether they are separate disorders at all (Section 6.1.7 gives the figures). Substance use disorders go with post-traumatic stress disorder, with depression, and with antisocial personality disorder. Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder goes with oppositional and conduct problems in children. Eating disorders go with depression and anxiety. And mental disorders go with physical illness: depression is more common after a heart attack and in diabetes, and it worsens the outcome of both. Services in India, as elsewhere, use a special phrase for one of these pairings. The phrase dual diagnosis means a mental disorder together with a substance use disorder, and such patients have long tended to fall between two services, with psychiatric wards reluctant to manage their drinking and de-addiction centres reluctant to manage their psychosis or depression.

Why do disorders keep company? There are at least five reasons, and in any one person several may be at work.

  • Shared causes. The same genes can raise the risk of several disorders, a phenomenon called pleiotropy (Section 1.4.6), and the same temperament, especially neuroticism, the tendency to react to life with anxiety and low mood, predisposes to both anxiety and depression.
  • One disorder leading to another. Gaurav drank to quieten his social fear. DSM-5-TR notes that social anxiety disorder usually begins before the disorders that accompany it, that people may use substances as self-medication for social fears, and that the isolation of social anxiety may in time lead to depression. The causal arrow can also run the other way, as when heavy drinking produces low mood.
  • Overlapping criteria. Poor sleep, poor concentration, restlessness and fatigue appear in the criteria for major depressive disorder, generalised anxiety disorder and post-traumatic stress disorder, so one set of complaints can count towards several diagnoses at once.
  • An artefact of classification. If a single continuous problem, such as a general tendency to distress, is cut up into many narrow categories, one person will inevitably meet several of them. This is one argument for dimensional classification (Section 1.6.4).
  • Referral bias. People with two disorders are more likely to seek or be sent for treatment than people with one, so clinic samples overstate how often disorders occur together. The American statistician Joseph Berkson showed in 1946, in a paper on the limits of analysing hospital data, that studying only hospital patients can create an apparent association between two conditions that does not exist in the general population. The distortion is now called Berkson's bias, and it is one reason why community surveys such as the National Comorbidity Survey were needed.

Why does comorbidity matter? Because it changes almost everything about a case. People with comorbid disorders are usually more severely ill, more impaired and at higher risk of suicide than people with one disorder, and their prognosis is worse, exactly as Feinstein argued for physical illness. Treatment has to be planned for the combination: treating Gaurav's depression while ignoring his drinking, or his drinking while ignoring the social fear that drives it, is likely to fail, and integrated treatment of both is preferred. Research is affected too, since a trial that admits only "pure" cases studies people unlike most of those seen in clinics. A clinician who finds one disorder should therefore always ask about the commonest companions, and should take a time line, as Gaurav's clinician did, to see which disorder came first. Table 1.5 sorts out the options that examiners set beside the definition.

Table 1.5 What the options in comorbidity questions really describe.
OptionWhat it really describesWhere taught
"The occurrence of two or more disorders at the same time"Comorbidity (concurrent): the correct answerSection 1.2.7
"Differentially defined disorders can co-occur"Comorbidity: the correct answerSection 1.2.7
"The frequency of mental illness"Prevalence (or incidence)Section 1.2.6
"Two or more causes of death indicated for patients"Multiple causes recorded on a death certificate: mortality coding, unrelatedNot a psychiatric term
"The degree of suffering of mentally ill patients"Distress, or the severity of a disorderSection 1.1.2; severity specifiers, Section 1.8.3
"People with the same psychopathology die at the same time"A nonsense distractorNone
"Sufferers exhibit a chronic fear of death"Thanatophobia, death anxietySection 6.3.5
"Depression always happens in the winter"A garbled version of the seasonal pattern specifier, which needs a regular seasonal link, usually but not always winterSection 5.3.11
Exam trap

Comorbidity means that two or more differently defined disorders co-occur in the same person, at the same time or over the lifetime. It is not the frequency of mental illness (prevalence), not two causes of death, not the degree of suffering, not a fear of death and not dying at the same time. The word came from general medicine (Feinstein, 1970). In psychiatry comorbidity is the rule, not the exception. "Dual diagnosis" is the special case of a mental disorder plus a substance use disorder.

Reading a disorder's description

You now have the vocabulary, and the best way to see it at work is to open DSM-5-TR at any disorder. After the diagnostic criteria comes a text that describes the disorder under a fixed set of headings, and almost every word taught in this section has a heading of its own. Table 1.6 lists them in the manual's order with what each tells you. When a section has little information to give, the manual simply leaves it out, so not every disorder has every heading. The disorder chapters of this book follow roughly the same order: what the disorder looks like, how it is diagnosed, who develops it and what becomes of them, what causes it, how it is told apart from its neighbours, and how it is treated. Once the headings are familiar, a new disorder is no longer a page of unfamiliar facts but a set of answers to questions you already know how to ask. (How the manual as a whole is organised, into sections and chapters, is the business of Section 1.8.1.)

Table 1.6 The headings under which DSM-5-TR describes each disorder.

Associated FeaturesFeatures not in the criteria that occur more often in people with the disorder
