1.3 Explaining Disorder: Models, Causes and the Diathesis-Stress IdeaHigh yield · 7 Qs

Section 1.2 gave you the words for describing a disorder. This section turns to the second of the field's three tasks, explaining it. Why did Pooja become depressed after moving to Pune when her husband, who made the same move, did not? Why did Gaurav's shyness become a disorder and then pull two more disorders in behind it? Every school of psychology has offered its own answer, and for most of the twentieth century the schools argued as rivals. The modern view is that each caught part of the truth, and that disorders arise from many causes acting together. This section first shows what that claim means in a single case, then recaps the classic models briefly (Book 2 teaches them one by one), then gives you the precise vocabulary of causation, and finally explains the two frameworks that tie the causes together, the diathesis-stress model and the biopsychosocial model. Together they are the scaffolding on which every "causes" section in Chapters 3 to 11 is built.

One cause or many

For most of history, explanations of abnormal behaviour have been one-dimensional. Barlow and Durand describe three great traditions, each of which looked for the cause: the supernatural tradition (demons, spirits, the stars, divine punishment), the biological tradition (a disease of the body or brain) and the psychological tradition (something gone wrong in the mind, in upbringing or in learning). They call any explanation that traces a disorder to a single cause a one-dimensional model, or linear model. A linear model might hold that a phobia or schizophrenia is caused by a chemical imbalance in the brain, or that it is caused by growing up surrounded by overwhelming family conflict. Such explanations are attractive because they are simple, and because a single cause suggests a single cure. They are almost always wrong.

The alternative is a multidimensional integrative model, which treats the causes of a disorder as a system. A system has many inputs, but once an input becomes part of the system it can no longer be considered on its own, because every part affects every other part through feedback loops. Barlow and Durand call this way of thinking systemic: any single influence on a disorder, whether a gene, a belief or a family argument, has to be understood in the context of all the others, biological and behavioural, cognitive and emotional, social and cultural, and the person's stage of development. The best way to understand the idea is to watch it work.

Irfan is 20, in the final year of a commerce degree at a college in Delhi. One afternoon in May, standing in a packed bus in the heat, he suddenly felt his heart pounding so hard that he thought it would burst. He could not breathe, his hands tingled, and he was certain he was dying, as his father's elder brother had died of a heart attack at 52 only four months earlier. He got off at the next stop and took an auto-rickshaw to a hospital. The electrocardiogram was normal. In the next three months he had five more attacks and two more normal electrocardiograms. He now checks his pulse many times a day, avoids buses and the metro, and has asked his mother to come with him to the college for his examinations. He has been drinking three or four cans of an energy drink a day to stay awake for revision. The diagnosis is panic disorder (Section 6.2). What caused it? Look at it strand by strand, as Figure 1.6 does.

  • Biological influences. Irfan's mother is a lifelong worrier, and DSM-5-TR notes that the children of parents with anxiety and depressive disorders are at greater risk of panic disorder. He may have inherited a nervous system that sounds the alarm easily. The caffeine in his energy drinks, on top of the heat and the crush, pushed his heart rate up on the day of the first attack.
  • Behavioural influences. The first attack happened on a bus, and buses now trigger fear by classical conditioning. His own racing heart has become a conditioned signal too, so that a flight of stairs can set off an attack. Every time he avoids a bus his anxiety falls, and that relief rewards the avoidance and keeps the fear alive.
  • Emotional influences. Irfan now fears the fear itself. He lives in a state of anxious apprehension about the next attack, which keeps his body on alert and makes the next attack more likely.
  • Cognitive influences. The key event in each attack is a thought: a racing heart is read as "I am having a heart attack". That catastrophic misinterpretation turns an ordinary bodily sensation into terror (Section 6.8.5 tells the cognitive theory of panic).
  • Social and cultural influences. His uncle's sudden death made heart disease the obvious meaning of a pounding heart, and DSM-5-TR notes that most people with panic disorder report identifiable stressors, including illness or death in the family, in the months before their first attack. His family's alarm, the rushed trips to hospital and his mother's escort to college all confirm that he is in danger. The final-year examinations add strain.
  • Developmental influences. The timing is typical. Panic disorder most often begins in early adulthood (DSM-5-TR gives a median age at onset of 20 to 24 in the United States), and Irfan's came at a point of transition, at the end of college and the start of adult responsibility.

No single strand explains Irfan's disorder. His brother, with the same parents, uncle and bus routes, has no panic attacks. Thousands of people drink energy drinks without panicking, and many young men lose uncles to heart disease without developing a disorder. A pounding heart after a fast run means nothing at all to a cricketer, because he reads it as exertion. What made the disorder was the combination: a sensitive alarm, a frightening meaning, a conditioned cue, an emotional state of dread and a family that, out of love, kept confirming the danger. Notice also the feedback loops. Fear raises the heart rate, which confirms the catastrophic thought, which raises the fear; avoidance lowers anxiety today and raises it tomorrow. Once a loop like this is running, the original cause hardly matters, which is why treatment can work even when the cause is unknown.

Remember

A one-dimensional (linear) model traces a disorder to a single cause, such as "a chemical imbalance" or "family conflict". A multidimensional integrative model treats causes as a system with feedback, so that biological, behavioural, emotional, cognitive, social and cultural, and developmental influences can be understood only in relation to one another.

The classic models at a glance

Before the integrative view, each major school offered its own model of psychopathology: a set of assumptions about what goes wrong in a disorder and therefore what should be done about it. Each model is a lens that brings one level of the person into sharp focus and blurs the rest. Table 1.7 sets them side by side. Book 2, Sections 1.7.1 to 1.7.8 teach each one fully, with its history, its key concepts and its evidence; here the point is the comparison, because examination questions ask you to match a model to the cause it blames, and the wrong options are always the causes blamed by the other models.

Table 1.7 The classic models of psychopathology: what each blames, and what follows.
Model and key figuresWhat causes disorderTreatment that followsMain criticismBook 2
Supernatural: ancient and folk traditions worldwideSpirits, possession, sin, sorcery, the evil eyeExorcism, prayer, ritual, amuletsUntestable; can delay effective care1.7.1
Biological (medical): Hippocrates; Wilhelm Griesinger (1817 to 1868); KraepelinGenes, brain structure, neurotransmitters, hormones, infectionDrugs, electroconvulsive therapy and other physical treatmentsReductionist; a brain correlate is not proof of cause1.7.2
Psychodynamic: Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939)Unconscious conflict rooted in emotional trauma in childhood; fixation; defencesInsight-oriented therapy (Chapter 3)Unfalsifiable; built on case studies1.7.3
Behavioural: Ivan Pavlov (1849 to 1936), John B. Watson (1878 to 1958), B. F. Skinner (1904 to 1990), Albert Bandura (1925 to 2021)Maladaptive or inappropriate learning: conditioning, reinforcement, modellingBehaviour therapy (Chapter 4)Neglects thought, feeling and biology1.7.4
Cognitive: Albert Ellis (1913 to 2007), Aaron T. Beck (1921 to 2021)Disturbed thought patterns: irrational beliefs, negative schemas, distortionsCognitive therapy and rational emotive behaviour therapy (Chapter 5)Are the thoughts a cause or a consequence?1.7.5
Humanistic-existential: Carl Rogers (1902 to 1987), Abraham Maslow (1908 to 1970), Rollo May (1909 to 1994), Viktor Frankl (1905 to 1997)Failure to live up to one's full potential: conditions of worth, incongruence, loss of meaningClient-centred and existential therapy (Chapter 6)Vague; hard to test; little to offer severe disorder1.7.6
Sociocultural: social psychiatry; labelling theory (Thomas J. Scheff, 1966)Poverty, gender, discrimination, culture, stigma, labelling, weak supportSocial and community change, culturally adapted care (Chapter 9)Correlational: does poverty cause disorder, or disorder cause a drift into poverty?1.7.7
Family systems: Gregory Bateson (1904 to 1980), Salvador Minuchin (1921 to 2017)Disturbed family interaction, roles and communication; high expressed emotionFamily therapy (Chapter 7)Has blamed families; hard to test1.7.8

Read down the second column and you can see why the models clashed. Each locates the cause at a different level: the spirit world, the body, the unconscious, the history of learning, the content of thought, the blocked growth of the self, the society and the family. Read across a row and you can see why each mattered: each cause implied a treatment, and most of the treatments in Book 2 began as the practical arm of one of these models. Read down the fourth column and you can see why none of them won outright: each was criticised for explaining too little or for being impossible to test. The modern view keeps every one of the levels and stops treating them as rivals, which is where Sections 1.3.4 and 1.3.5 lead.

Remember

The therapies that grew from each model (psychoanalysis and its descendants, behaviour therapy, cognitive therapy, client-centred and existential therapy, family therapy) are taught in Book 2, Chapters 3 to 7. This book names the treatment and its evidence for each disorder and points there.

Exam trap

"Psychodynamic therapists believe that disorder stems from" emotional trauma experienced in childhood (unresolved, unconscious conflict from early life). The other options are other models' answers: disturbed thought patterns is the cognitive model; a failure to live up to one's full potential is the humanistic model; inappropriate learning patterns is the behavioural model. In the same way, a statement that disorders arise when "a biological predisposition is set off by a stressful situation" is not humanistic-existential, sociocultural or cognitive: it is the diathesis-stress model (Section 1.3.4).

The language of cause

Clinicians and researchers who argue about causes need precise words, and the words come mostly from medicine and philosophy. Aetiology, defined in Section 1.2.1, is the cause or set of causes of a disorder, and also the study of them. The most basic distinction is between three kinds of cause. A necessary cause must be present for the disorder to occur: without it, there is no disorder. The spirochaete Treponema pallidum is a necessary cause of general paresis, since nobody develops general paresis without syphilis (Book 2, Section 1.3.6). In psychiatry, exposure to a traumatic event is a necessary cause of post-traumatic stress disorder by definition, because the diagnosis cannot be made without one (Section 7.2). A sufficient cause guarantees the disorder: if it is present, the disorder follows. Sufficient causes are rare in psychiatry. One of the few clear examples is genetic: DSM-5-TR states that a repeat length of 40 or more in the huntingtin gene is invariably associated with Huntington's disease. Notice that the two ideas are independent. Syphilis is necessary for general paresis but not sufficient, because only a minority of people with untreated syphilis ever develop it; the Huntington's mutation is sufficient, given a long enough life. Most causes in psychiatry are neither. A contributory cause raises the probability of a disorder without being either necessary or sufficient. Childhood adversity is a contributory cause of depression: many people who suffered it never become depressed, and many depressed people never suffered it, yet it clearly increases the risk.

Causes also differ in their distance from the outcome. A distal cause is remote, in time or in the chain of events: genes, a difficult birth, the loss of a parent in childhood. A proximal cause is close at hand, the immediate trigger: last month's humiliation, a week without sleep, the move to a strange city. Distal causes usually act through proximal ones. Pooja's mother's history of depression is part of the distal story; her lonely months in Pune are part of the proximal one.

A warning belongs here, because it is the commonest error in reasoning about mental disorders. A risk factor, as Section 1.2.6 defined it, is a characteristic associated with a higher probability of developing a disorder, and an association is not yet a cause. There are three other ways the association can arise. The disorder may cause the supposed risk factor, which is reverse causation: schizophrenia is commoner in the poorest parts of cities partly because the illness itself pushes people down the social ladder (the social drift explanation of Book 2, Section 1.7.7). A third factor may cause both, which is confounding: if heavy cannabis users are also more often poor, traumatised or genetically vulnerable, those shared factors may explain part of the link between cannabis and psychosis, which is why researchers work so hard to control for them (Section 4.7.8). Or the association may be chance or an artefact of how the sample was chosen, like the referral bias of Section 1.2.7. A treatment effect can mislead in the same way. That a drug relieves a disorder does not show that the disorder was caused by a lack of that drug, any more than a headache is caused by a lack of aspirin.

Clinicians who explain a particular person's problem use a related set of words, the four Ps of case formulation, which Book 2, Section 2.9.2 teaches as the 5 Ps (adding the presenting problem). The predisposing factors are the long-standing vulnerabilities that explain why this person was at risk; precipitating factors are the recent events that explain why the problem began now; perpetuating factors are the processes that keep it going; and protective factors are the strengths that reduce the risk or help recovery. DSM-5-TR uses the same four words in its own introduction, saying that diagnosis requires clinical training to recognise when the combination of predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating and protective factors has produced a psychopathological condition. For Pooja: a family history of depression and a self-critical style (predisposing), the move and the loss of her job (precipitating), her isolation and her habit of lying in bed brooding (perpetuating), and a loving husband and a good recovery last time (protective).

Causes also loop back on themselves. In reciprocal causation (bidirectional causation), the cause and the effect influence each other. Stress can cause depression, but depression can also cause stress. In 1991 the American psychologist Constance Hammen followed women with unipolar depression for a year and compared them with women who had bipolar disorder, chronic medical illness or no illness. The women with depression met more stressful events than the healthy women, significantly more interpersonal stress than everyone else, and tended to have more dependent events, the kind that a person's own behaviour helps to bring about (a quarrel, a broken friendship), as opposed to independent events such as a relative's death. Hammen called this stress generation: through their symptoms, behaviour and circumstances, depressed people create some of the stress that then keeps them depressed. Gaurav's drinking, which wrecked his audits and so gave him new reasons to drink, is the same kind of loop.

Table 1.8 collects the whole vocabulary of causation. The last pair of terms comes from developmental psychopathology, the study of how disorders arise and change across the lifespan (the Developmental book has more). The term equifinality names the principle that many different pathways can lead to the same outcome. Barlow and Durand compare it to fever, which can have many causes. A delusional syndrome may be part of schizophrenia or the result of amphetamine use; delirium may follow surgery in an older person or come from thiamine deficiency or kidney disease. Depression may be reached through a loss, through hypothyroidism (Section 5.3.4) or through heavy genetic loading with very little stress at all. Its partner, multifinality, is the reverse principle: the same starting point can lead to many different outcomes. Children who suffer maltreatment may grow up to develop depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, a personality disorder or a substance use disorder, and some, the resilient ones of Section 1.3.6, develop no disorder at all. The two terms were set side by side in a short, much-cited paper by Dante Cicchetti and Fred A. Rogosch in Development and Psychopathology in 1996. Their practical message is humbling. Knowing someone's disorder does not tell you the path that led to it, and knowing someone's history does not tell you where it will lead.

Table 1.8 The vocabulary of causation.
TermMeaningExample
AetiologyThe cause or causes of a disorder, and their studyThe aetiology of general paresis is syphilis of the brain
Necessary causeMust be present for the disorder to occurTreponema pallidum for general paresis; a traumatic event for PTSD
Sufficient causeGuarantees the disorder when present40 or more CAG repeats in the huntingtin gene for Huntington's disease
Contributory causeRaises the probability; neither necessary nor sufficientChildhood adversity for depression
Distal and proximal causesRemote in time or in the causal chain; close at hand, the triggerGenes, early loss; a recent humiliation, sleep loss
Risk factorAssociated with higher risk; may be a cause, a consequence or a confoundUrban poverty and schizophrenia (cause, or social drift?)
Predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating, protectiveWhy this person, why now, why it continues, what helpsPooja: family history; the move; isolation; her husband
Reciprocal causationCause and effect influence each otherDepression generating stressful events (Hammen, 1991)
EquifinalityDifferent pathways lead to the same outcomeDepression after a loss, from hypothyroidism, or from genetic loading
MultifinalityOne starting point leads to different outcomesChildhood maltreatment followed by depression, PTSD, a personality disorder, or resilience
Exam trap

Necessary is not sufficient. A necessary cause must be present (no syphilis, no general paresis) but need not produce the disorder; a sufficient cause always produces it. Most psychiatric causes are contributory. A risk factor is not a cause until reverse causation, confounding and chance have been ruled out, and a drug that helps does not reveal the cause. Equifinality: many roads to one disorder. Multifinality: one starting point, many outcomes. Do not swap them.

The diathesis-stress model

Return to Pooja and her husband. They made the same move, lost the same friends and faced the same strange city, and only Pooja became depressed. The single-cause models cannot explain that, but the most widely used framework in psychopathology can. The diathesis-stress model holds that a disorder develops when a predisposition is set off by a stressor. The diathesis (from the Greek for disposition or arrangement) is the vulnerability, which may be genetic, biological or psychological. The stress is the environmental event or circumstance that triggers it. Neither alone usually produces the disorder: a vulnerable person who meets little stress may never fall ill, and most people who meet hardship cope. The two also trade off, so that the more vulnerable a person is, the less stress it takes to tip them over. The model goes under other names, the stress-vulnerability model and the vulnerability-stress model, and they are the same idea. Its history belongs to schizophrenia research. In 1962 the American psychologist Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003) proposed that what is inherited is not schizophrenia but a subtle neural defect he called schizotaxia, which becomes clinical schizophrenia only in some people, under adverse experience; and in 1977 Joseph Zubin (1900 to 1990) and Bonnie Spring recast the idea as a vulnerability model in which an episode occurs whenever stress exceeds a threshold set by the person's vulnerability. Book 2, Section 1.7.10 tells both stories and draws Zubin and Spring's threshold as its Figure 1.11, and Book 1, Section 11.9.8 introduces the model.

Four further points turn this summary into a working tool. First, consider the range of things that can serve as a diathesis. A diathesis may be genetic, as in Pooja's family history (her mother was treated for depression after a miscarriage). It may be biological without being genetic: an obstetric complication or prenatal infection that disturbs brain development (Section 4.7.6), or an inborn temperament such as behavioural inhibition, the shy, easily alarmed style that Jerome Kagan (1929 to 2021) found in some infants (Section 6.7.2). It may be psychological: a dormant negative schema ("I am worthless unless I succeed"), a pessimistic attributional style that explains bad events as permanent and personal, or the anxiety sensitivity that made Irfan's heartbeat so frightening (Sections 5.6.4, 5.6.5 and 6.8.5). Pooja's lifelong belief that she must never fail is a diathesis of this kind. And it may be social. George W. Brown and Tirril Harris's study of women in Camberwell, London (1978), found that a severe event was far more likely to lead to depression in women with certain vulnerability factors, such as having no confiding relationship or no employment outside the home (Section 5.6.8). Since the move, Pooja has no job, although she does have a confiding husband.

Second, consider the range of things that count as stress. Acute life events are the most studied: a death, a separation, a job loss, a move, each of which Thomas Holmes and Richard Rahe weighted on their Social Readjustment Rating Scale of 1967 (Book 1, Section 11.9.1). Chronic strain is the long-running difficulty, such as bad housing, a sick child or an alcoholic husband; Brown and Harris counted as major difficulties only problems that had lasted at least two years. Daily hassles are the small, repeated irritations of ordinary life (the crowded commute, the quarrelling neighbours), which Allen D. Kanner, Richard S. Lazarus (1922 to 2002) and their colleagues argued in 1981 predict psychological symptoms at least as well as major events. Trauma is the extreme end: events that threaten death, serious injury or sexual violence (Section 7.2). And not all stressors are unpleasant. In bipolar disorder, a promotion or a new romance, a goal-attainment event, can set off mania (Section 5.6.9).

Third, the model comes in more than one version, and the difference matters to researchers. In an additive version the two simply add up, so that a large enough dose of either can push a person over the threshold: even someone with very little vulnerability may break down under an extreme stress, and a highly vulnerable person may need almost none. Zubin and Spring's picture, in which everyone has some degree of vulnerability and the two trade off along a threshold curve, works this way. In an interactive version stress has an effect only, or mainly, in people who carry the diathesis: for those without it, the risk barely rises however much stress they meet, while for those with it, the risk climbs steeply with each stressor. The famous gene-environment studies of the early 2000s were tests of an interactive model (Section 1.5.1). The American psychologists Scott M. Monroe and Anne D. Simons, reviewing the life-stress research in Psychological Bulletin in 1991, added two further cautions. Researchers must be precise about which kinds of stress are supposed to interact with which diatheses, and they must remember that the diathesis can itself shape the stress a person meets. A self-critical, irritable or impulsive person does not meet a random sample of life events. Hammen's stress generation (Section 1.3.3) and gene-environment correlation (Section 1.5.2) are two forms of this point.

Fourth, the balance between diathesis and stress can change over a person's life. Clinicians have long noticed that first episodes of depression often follow a major blow, while later episodes need less and less provocation. The American psychiatrist Robert M. Post explained this in 1992 with the idea of kindling, borrowed from epilepsy research: each episode leaves a lasting trace that lowers the threshold for the next, a process also called stress sensitisation (Section 5.5.6). Pooja fits the pattern. Her first episode followed a severe disappointment, the loss of the government post she had worked for; her second has followed a stress that is real but smaller. In diathesis-stress terms, her first episode has become part of her diathesis.

Finally, the model has a version at the level of genes. When a particular gene raises the risk of a disorder only in people exposed to a particular environment, the result is called gene-environment interaction, and it is the diathesis-stress model written in molecular terms (Book 2, Section 1.7.10 tells the Dunedin study of 2003 that made the idea famous, and Section 1.5.1 of this chapter teaches the concept and its troubled history). Two other names sometimes appear beside diathesis-stress in examination options. The neurodevelopmental model is a real but narrower theory, proposed for schizophrenia in 1987 by Daniel Weinberger and, separately, by Robin Murray and Shôn Lewis: that the disorder begins with a disturbance of early brain development that stays silent until adolescence (Sections 1.5.4 and 4.7.6). "Clinical-reductionist model" and "syndrome-sign model" are not established models at all; each borrows a real word (reductionism, syndrome, sign) to sound plausible (Book 2, Table 1.16).

How does this book use the model? Each disorder chapter teaches the biological causes first and the psychological and social causes second, and then puts them together in a diathesis-stress form: the vulnerability-stress account of schizophrenia (Section 4.9.5), the integration of the causes of depression (Section 5.6.10), David Barlow's triple vulnerability theory of anxiety (Section 6.8.11), and the before, during and after risk factors for post-traumatic stress disorder (Section 7.3.4). Table 1.9 gives a preview. Notice that even the most heritable disorders have stressors in the table. Schizophrenia and bipolar disorder carry the heaviest genetic loading in psychiatry, yet life events, sleep loss, cannabis and a critical family still time their episodes; Section 1.5.6 takes up a past-paper question built on the opposite claim.

Table 1.9 Diathesis and stress across the disorders of this book.
DisorderTypical diathesisTypical stressorWhere taught
SchizophreniaGenetic loading; obstetric complications and prenatal infectionLife events; a critical, hostile family (high expressed emotion); heavy cannabis useSections 4.7, 4.9.4, 4.9.5
Major depressive disorderGenetic loading; neuroticism; negative schemas; early loss of the motherSevere life events, especially loss; long-running major difficultiesSections 5.5, 5.6
Bipolar disorderThe highest heritability of the mood disordersSleep loss; goal-attainment events; disrupted routines; stopping medicationSection 5.6.9
Panic disorderNeuroticism; anxiety sensitivity; behavioural inhibitionInterpersonal stress, illness or death in the family in the months before the first attackSections 6.7, 6.8
Post-traumatic stress disorderEarlier trauma; childhood adversity; earlier mental disorder; neuroticismThe traumatic event itself; afterwards, poor support and continuing stressSection 7.3.4
Anorexia and bulimia nervosaAnxious or obsessional traits in childhood; weight concerns, low self-esteem, childhood obesity, early puberty; familial riskDieting; leaving home; settings that prize thinnessChapter 8
Alcohol use disorderGenetic influences (40 to 60 percent of the variance); impulsivity; a low response to alcoholAvailability and price; heavy peer use; stress; poverty and unemploymentChapter 10
Exam trap

Five examination stems ask the same thing in different words: "a predisposition towards and risk triggers for a mental disorder", "a biological predisposition set off by a stressful situation", "a combination of genetic predisposition and environmental stressors", "a combination of vulnerability and stress", "an interaction between a predisposing vulnerability and environmental stressors". The answer every time is the diathesis-stress model. Every wrong option names a single factor: genetic factors only, environmental factors only, neurobiological abnormalities only, purely biological causes, learned experiences alone, stress alone regardless of constitution, genetics alone, unconscious conflicts only, supernatural influences. The neurodevelopmental model is real but is a narrower theory of how schizophrenia begins; "clinical-reductionist" and "syndrome-sign" are not models. The diathesis is the vulnerability and the stress is the trigger, not the reverse, and the diathesis need not be genetic.

The biopsychosocial model

The diathesis-stress model says how causes combine. A broader framework says which kinds of cause a clinician must look for. In 1977 the American internist and psychiatrist George Libman Engel (1913 to 1999), of the University of Rochester, published in Science a paper titled "The need for a new medical model: A challenge for biomedicine". The dominant biomedical model, he argued, explains illness entirely through measurable biological variables, which makes it reductionist, and it separates the body from the mind, which makes it dualist. He proposed instead the biopsychosocial model: illness, physical or mental, always results from interacting biological, psychological and social factors, which form linked levels of one system, so that a change at any level spreads to the others. Book 2, Section 1.7.9 tells the story of the paper and of the patient Engel used to show what the biomedical model misses. What a clinician needs from this chapter is how the model is used.

The model becomes a working tool when its three levels are crossed with the four Ps of Section 1.3.3, giving a grid of twelve questions: what are the biological predisposing factors, the psychological precipitants, the social perpetuating factors, and so on. Consider Bhavna. She is 19, from Nashik, a first-year arts student living in a college hostel in Mumbai. For six months, about three times a week, she has eaten very large amounts of food in secret (a packet of biscuits, two plates of leftover rice, a bag of chips, all within an hour) with a feeling that she cannot stop, and then made herself vomit. Between binges she skips breakfast and lunch. Her weight is in the healthy range, but she weighs herself several times a day and feels that her worth depends on the number. Her roommate found her vomiting and brought her to the college counsellor. The diagnosis is bulimia nervosa (Chapter 8). Table 1.10 is her formulation grid.

Table 1.10 Bhavna's biopsychosocial formulation grid.
FactorBiologicalPsychologicalSocial
PredisposingOverweight as a child; early puberty, at 10; an aunt treated for an eating disorderPerfectionism; self-worth tied to weight and shapeTeased as "moti" at school; a mother who is always dieting and comments on weight
PrecipitatingA crash diet with long fasts before a cousin's weddingShame at an aunt's remark that she had "become healthy"Leaving home for a hostel in a new city
PerpetuatingHunger after fasting drives the binges; vomiting (potassium to be checked)The cycle of strict rules, binge, shame, vomiting and stricter rules; secrecyFitness videos on her phone; fixed hostel meal times; examination stress
ProtectiveHealthy weight; no medical complications so farInsight; wants to stop; a good studentA roommate who noticed and came with her; parents willing to help

The grid earns its place in three ways. It stops the clinician from missing a level: a counsellor focused on Bhavna's self-esteem might forget to check her potassium, and a physician focused on her potassium might never ask about the teasing or the aunt's remark (in much of India "you have become healthy" is a polite way of saying "you have put on weight", which is exactly how Bhavna heard it). It also shows how the levels interact. Dieting is a behaviour with a social origin, but the hunger it produces is biological, and the binge that hunger drives produces the shame that drives the next diet. DSM-5-TR notes that binge eating frequently begins during or after a diet, and Bhavna's did. Finally, it points to treatment, because perpetuating and protective factors are the ones that can still be changed: regular meals to break the cycle of fasting and bingeing, a medical check, cognitive behaviour therapy aimed at the eating disorder (Book 2, Chapter 5; Chapter 8 gives the evidence), and the help of her roommate and her parents.

The biopsychosocial model has become the official philosophy of modern psychiatry and clinical psychology, and it has its critics. The psychiatrist S. Nassir Ghaemi argued in The Rise and Fall of the Biopsychosocial Model (2010) that it is so broad that it predicts nothing: it tells the clinician to consider everything but gives no rule for deciding which level matters most in a given case, and so it can be used to justify almost any treatment. That criticism points to the right relationship between the two models of this section. The biopsychosocial model is a framework, a reminder of what must not be left out. The diathesis-stress model is a more specific mechanism that operates inside the framework, saying how a vulnerability at one level and a trigger at another combine to produce a disorder.

Exam trap

The biopsychosocial model is George L. Engel's, published in Science in 1977 as an alternative to the biomedical model. The distractors are all famous for something else: Emil Kraepelin classified the psychoses by their course, separating dementia praecox from manic-depressive insanity (Section 1.6.2); Aaron T. Beck built the cognitive model and the cognitive triad of depression (Book 2, Section 5.3); Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939) coined the word schizophrenia (Section 4.1.4). Do not confuse the biopsychosocial model (which levels) with the diathesis-stress model (how vulnerability and trigger combine).

Vulnerability, resilience and protective factors

The diathesis-stress model has a gap. Two people with the same vulnerability may meet the same stress and still end up differently, and the difference lies in a third ingredient. Protective factors, introduced with the four Ps in Section 1.3.3, are characteristics of the person or the environment that lower the probability of a disorder in someone who is at risk; they are not merely the absence of risk, but something that works against it. In 1984 Keith H. Nuechterlein and Michael E. Dawson built them into a vulnerability-stress model of schizophrenic episodes, alongside personal vulnerabilities and environmental stressors (Book 2, Section 1.7.11). Their partner concept is resilience, positive adaptation in spite of significant adversity. Its classic demonstration is the study that Emmy Werner (1929 to 2017) and Ruth Smith began on the Hawaiian island of Kauai, following every child born there in 1955 into adulthood: about one in three of the children at highest risk grew into competent, caring adults, protected by ordinary things such as an easy temperament, at least one stable relationship with a caring adult and a sense of purpose. Ann Masten later summed up the field by calling resilience "ordinary magic", and Jay Belsky and Michael Pluess's differential susceptibility hypothesis added a twist: some of the traits counted as vulnerabilities are better seen as sensitivity to the environment, making the same child do worst in a harsh home and best in a kind one (Book 2, Section 1.7.11 tells all three stories). How genes help to choose the environments people meet, gene-environment correlation, is the subject of Section 1.5.2.

Clinical link

Put the four people of this section side by side and you have the clinician's whole task. For each, the predisposing factors (Pooja's family history, Gaurav's shyness since 13, Irfan's anxious temperament, Bhavna's perfectionism) explain but cannot be undone, and the precipitants are already in the past. Treatment acts on what keeps the disorder going and on what protects against it: Pooja's isolation and brooding, Gaurav's drinking, Irfan's avoidance and catastrophic thoughts, Bhavna's cycle of fasting and bingeing, and on the other side a husband, a wife, a mother and a roommate. Telling a patient "you have a vulnerability, and episodes come when stress outruns your protection" is accurate, removes blame and gives everyone something to do.

Scan Your Brain

1.4 Genes and Mental Disorder: Family, Twin, Adoption and Molecular StudiesLower yield · 1 Q

Rakesh is 29, an accounts officer in a cooperative bank in Kanpur, and he lives in the house his grandfather built: his parents, his father's two brothers, their wives and children and his grandmother, fourteen people and one kitchen. Three years ago his elder brother, then 31, stopped sleeping, talked without pause about a business scheme, spent two lakh rupees of the family's savings in a single week and was admitted to hospital in a manic state. The diagnosis was bipolar I disorder, and on lithium he has been well since. Now Rakesh's own marriage is being arranged, and over tea the young woman's father asked the question that is asked in every psychiatric outpatient department in India: "Is it hereditary?"

Behind that polite question are three harder ones. Will Rakesh himself fall ill? Will his children? And when an illness does run through a family, is it the genes the family shares, or the house, the worries, the habits and the ways of reacting to trouble that it shares? This section explains how researchers answer those questions. It is a section about methods: how family, twin and adoption studies estimate how much genes matter, what the much-misread numbers called concordance and heritability really mean, and how molecular genetics hunts for the genes themselves. The findings for particular disorders are taught in their own chapters (schizophrenia in Section 4.7, the mood disorders in Section 5.5.1, the anxiety disorders and OCD in Section 6.7.1); here they appear as examples of the methods at work.

Genes, genotype and phenotype

Start with the vocabulary, because every later idea depends on it. Almost every cell in your body carries, in its nucleus, 46 chromosomes arranged in 23 pairs, one member of each pair inherited from your mother and one from your father. A chromosome is a single, enormously long molecule of deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) wound around supporting proteins. A gene is a stretch of that DNA that carries the instructions for building a particular protein (or for regulating other genes), and your complete set of genes, more than 20,000 of them, is your genome. Because chromosomes come in pairs, you carry two copies of most genes, and the copies need not be identical. The alternative versions of a gene are called alleles. A dominant gene is an allele that shows its effect even when only one copy is present; a recessive gene shows its effect only when both copies are of that kind. Brown eyes are dominant over blue: a child who inherits one "brown" allele and one "blue" allele has brown eyes, and blue eyes appear only when both alleles are "blue". When a trait depends on a single gene of this kind, the laws Gregor Mendel worked out with pea plants in the nineteenth century let you predict how many children of a given couple will show it.

Two more words are needed. Your genotype is your genetic make-up: the particular alleles you carry. Your phenotype is what can be observed: your height, the colour of your eyes, your temperament, your blood pressure, and, if you develop one, a psychiatric disorder. The phenotype is never simply the genotype printed out. It is what the genotype produces in interaction with everything that happens to the person, from the womb onwards. Two people with the same genotype (identical twins) can have different phenotypes, and, as you will see, one twin may develop depression while the other does not.

A handful of conditions with psychiatric features do follow Mendel's rules, and they are worth knowing because they show what a single-gene, or Mendelian, disorder looks like. Huntington's disease is caused by a single dominant gene on chromosome 4: every child of an affected parent has a 50 percent chance of inheriting it, and almost everyone who inherits it develops, usually in middle life, the characteristic movements, together with depression, irritability, sometimes psychosis, and finally dementia (Chapter 10). Phenylketonuria is recessive: a child must inherit the faulty allele from both parents, and without a special diet the result is intellectual disability (Chapter 3). Fragile X syndrome, carried on the X chromosome, is the commonest inherited cause of intellectual disability (Chapter 3). In conditions like these, one gene of large effect is enough.

The common psychiatric disorders are not like this at all. They are polygenic: influenced by many genes, each contributing only a tiny effect, all of which may in turn be influenced by the environment. DSM-5-TR puts it precisely for schizophrenia: "Liability is conferred by a spectrum of risk alleles, common and rare, with each allele contributing only a small fraction to the total population variance." For bipolar disorder it says that the mechanism of heritability "is not Mendelian" and involves multiple genes of small effect. There is no "schizophrenia gene" and no "depression gene", just as there is no single gene for height. This is why the question Rakesh's future father-in-law asked has no yes-or-no answer.

But how can hundreds of tiny genetic nudges produce an illness that a person either has or does not have? The answer is the liability-threshold model, which the Scottish geneticist Douglas Scott Falconer (1913 to 2004) put into its standard quantitative form in 1965, building on earlier ideas of a "threshold" for congenital malformations. Imagine adding up, for each person, every influence that pushes towards a particular disorder: every risk allele, every complication at birth, every severe loss, every night without sleep. The total is the person's liability, and like height or blood pressure it varies continuously across the population, with most people in the middle and few at the extremes, in the shape of a bell curve. The disorder appears only in people whose liability passes a threshold. Now consider the first-degree relatives of someone who has the disorder. They share some of that person's risk alleles (and some of his environment), so their whole curve of liability is shifted towards the threshold, and a larger share of them lies beyond it. Figure 1.7 draws the idea. In the general population 1 percent cross the threshold; shift the relatives' curve by a little less than one standard deviation and about 8 percent of them cross it, which is roughly the risk DSM-5-TR gives for the first-degree relatives of a person with bipolar disorder (5 to 10 percent).

The model explains four facts that would otherwise be puzzling. First, most people with a polygenic disorder have no affected parent: the risk alleles are individually common and are carried, in smaller doses, by many people who stay well, so two unaffected parents can pass on a heavy load. Second, risk falls smoothly, not in Mendelian steps, as the relationship becomes more distant, because more distant relatives share fewer of the alleles. Third, relatives of severely ill people, or of families with several affected members, are at higher risk than relatives of a single mild case, because such families carry more liability. Fourth, an identical twin of an affected person can stay well, because genes are only part of the liability. The American psychologist Irving Gottesman (1930 to 2016) and the British psychiatric geneticist James Shields (1918 to 1978) applied the model to schizophrenia in 1967 (Section 4.7.2), and it is now the standard way of thinking about almost every disorder in this book.

How, then, do researchers find out how much of the liability comes from genes, and which genes they are? The psychiatric geneticist Kenneth S. Kendler described the field in 2005 as a sequence of four research strategies, which Barlow and Durand use to organise their account (Table 1.11). The first two use statistics on relatives and need no DNA at all; the last two work with DNA itself. The rest of this section follows the table from top to bottom. (Book 1, Section 9.7, applies the same family, twin and adoption designs to intelligence.)

Table 1.11 Kendler's four strategies for studying genes in psychological disorders (2005, as presented by Barlow and Durand), used as the map of this section.
StrategyMethodQuestion it answersTaught in
Basic genetic epidemiologyStatistical analysis of family, twin and adoption studiesIs the disorder inherited, and how much of it is attributable to genes (heritability)?1.4.2 to 1.4.5
Advanced genetic epidemiologyThe same studies, analysed furtherWhat shapes the genetic influence: age, sex, development, the environment?1.4.5, 1.5.1, 1.5.2
Gene findingLinkage and association studies in families and individualsWhere are the genes?1.4.6, 1.4.7
Molecular geneticsBiological analysis of individual DNA samplesWhat do the genes do to produce the symptoms?1.4.6, 1.4.8
Exam trap

The genotype is the genetic make-up; the phenotype is the observable result, and a psychiatric disorder is a phenotype. Huntington's disease (dominant), phenylketonuria (recessive) and fragile X syndrome (X-linked) are single-gene conditions; schizophrenia, bipolar disorder and depression are polygenic, with no single causal gene. In the liability-threshold model it is liability that is continuous; the disorder appears only beyond the threshold.

Family studies

The oldest and simplest question is whether a disorder runs in families. A family study begins with an affected person, called the proband (or index case), and counts how often the disorder appears among his relatives, compared with the relatives of unaffected control probands or with the rate in the general population. Relatives are grouped by how many genes they share with the proband on average. First-degree relatives (parents, brothers and sisters, and children) share about half. Second-degree relatives (grandparents, grandchildren, uncles and aunts, nephews and nieces, and half-siblings) share about a quarter. Third-degree relatives such as first cousins share about an eighth. If genes matter, the disorder should be commonest in first-degree relatives, less common in second-degree relatives, and less common again, though still above the population rate, in more distant ones. The tendency of a disorder to cluster in families is called familial aggregation.

Counting is harder than it sounds. A 20-year-old brother who is well today may fall ill at 30, so a raw count of affected relatives underestimates their lifetime risk. Researchers therefore calculate the morbid risk: the proportion of relatives expected to develop the disorder if they all lived through the whole age of risk, with younger relatives counted as only part of a person according to how much of the risk period they have already passed. They must also decide how to find out who is ill. In the family study method every available relative is interviewed directly, ideally by an interviewer who does not know whether that person is related to a patient or to a control. In the cheaper family history method the proband, or one well-informed relative, is asked about everyone else. The family history method misses a good deal, especially milder illness and illness that families keep quiet about, so it underestimates familial rates.

The results are clear for many disorders. Against a general-population risk of about 1 percent, the first-degree relatives of a person with bipolar disorder have a risk of 5 to 10 percent (DSM-5-TR). The first-degree relatives of a person with major depressive disorder have two to four times the general population's risk, and the relative risk is higher still when the proband's depression began early or keeps recurring. Alcohol use disorder is three to four times commoner in close relatives of affected people, and the rate climbs with the number of affected relatives, with closeness of relationship and with the severity of their problems. Barlow and Durand report that familial aggregation for blood-injection-injury phobia, the phobia in which people faint at the sight of blood or a needle, is as high as 60 percent: six in every ten first-degree relatives share the reaction to some degree, one of the highest rates for any psychological disorder. For schizophrenia, the pooled figures compiled by Irving Gottesman show risk rising steadily with genetic closeness, from 1 percent in the general population to about 9 percent in siblings and about 48 percent in identical twins (Section 4.7.1 gives the whole gradient).

Return to Rakesh. As the brother of a man with bipolar I disorder he is a first-degree relative, and his lifetime risk of bipolar disorder is somewhere around 5 to 10 percent, against 1 percent in the population. That is a large relative increase, five to ten times the usual risk, but a modest absolute one: of every hundred men in his position, about ninety or more will never develop the illness. His future children would be second-degree relatives of his brother, with a smaller increase still. Notice that these are averages for people in his position; they say nothing certain about Rakesh himself, and he has already lived through some of the years in which bipolar disorder usually begins.

Now notice the weakness of every family study. Rakesh's family shares far more than genes. Fourteen people share one kitchen, one television, one set of money worries, the same neighbourhood, the same water tank and the grandmother's firm ideas about how to react to illness. Barlow and Durand give the example of a mother who developed a strong reaction to blood after witnessing a serious accident as a girl; her children watch her turn pale and faint every time someone cuts a finger, learn the same reaction, and pass it on to their own children. Nothing genetic need be involved. Hepatitis A can run through a household that shares a contaminated water tank, and speaking Bhojpuri runs in families too; nobody calls either of them genetic. "Familial" means only that a disorder clusters in families. To separate genes from the shared household, researchers need families in which genes and environment come apart, and nature supplies two kinds: twins and adopted children.

Exam trap

Familial aggregation alone cannot separate genes from environment, because families share homes as well as genes. A family study begins from the proband. First-degree relatives (parents, siblings, children) share about 50 percent of their genes; second-degree relatives (grandparents, uncles, aunts, nephews, nieces, half-siblings) about 25 percent. A relative risk of five to ten times can still mean that 90 percent or more of relatives stay well.

Twin studies

Meenal and Divya are identical twins, now 27, who grew up in the same two-room flat in Nashik, went to the same school, wore the same uniforms and were, as their mother likes to say, "never apart for a single night until college". Meenal teaches mathematics in a girls' school; Divya is a pharmacist. At 22, in the year after their father died, Meenal had a major depressive episode, and at 25 she had another. Divya grieved for her father too, but she has never been depressed. Here are two women with the same genes and, by any ordinary measure, the same upbringing, and only one of them has the disorder. Twin studies are built on pairs like this.

Twins come in two kinds. Monozygotic (MZ) twins, identical twins, develop from a single fertilised egg that splits in two, so they carry virtually the same genes. Dizygotic (DZ) twins, fraternal twins, develop from two eggs fertilised by two sperm, so they share on average about half of the genes that vary between people, exactly as ordinary brothers and sisters do; they may be of the same sex or of different sexes. Both kinds share a womb, a birthday and usually a home. The logic of the twin method follows. If MZ pairs resemble each other in a trait more than DZ pairs do, the extra resemblance is attributed to their extra genetic similarity, because (the method assumes) their environments are equally similar.

For a disorder, which a person either has or does not have, resemblance is measured as concordance: the rate at which both members of a twin pair have the disorder. A pair in which both are affected is concordant; a pair like Meenal and Divya, in which one is affected and the other is not, is discordant. There are two ways of calculating the rate. Pairwise concordance is the proportion of pairs, among all pairs with at least one affected twin, in which both twins are affected. Probandwise concordance is the proportion of affected twins whose co-twin is also affected, counting a pair twice when both twins were independently found by the researchers; it gives higher figures and is the one that can be compared with the risks of other relatives. Section 4.7.2 works through both with schizophrenia's numbers. The essential result is always the comparison: Kendler and his colleagues (1991) found, among more than 2,000 female twins they interviewed, that both twins had bulimia nervosa in 23 percent of MZ pairs but in only 9 percent of DZ pairs (Barlow and Durand's figures), so genes matter, but most MZ co-twins of women with bulimia did not have it.

For traits that vary continuously, such as height or a score on a scale of anxiety, resemblance is measured as a correlation. The behaviour geneticist Robert Plomin pointed out that the correlation for height is about 0.90 between MZ twins and about 0.45 both between DZ twins and between other first-degree relatives. From two such correlations Falconer derived a simple estimate of heritability, the proportion of the variation in a trait within a population that is associated with genetic differences between its members. Falconer's formula doubles the difference between the MZ and DZ correlations: h2 = 2 × (rMZ minus rDZ). The reasoning is that MZ twins share all their varying genes and DZ twins half, so the difference between their correlations reflects half the genetic effect. Push the height figures through it: 2 × (0.90 minus 0.45) = 2 × 0.45 = 0.90. About 90 percent of the variation in height in such populations is associated with genetic variation, and about 10 percent with environment. (Book 1, Section 9.7.3, applies the same formula to IQ.) For disorders, which are yes-or-no, Falconer's 1965 paper also showed how to turn the rates of illness among relatives into a correlation in the underlying liability, so the same reasoning can be applied.

Modern twin studies go one step further and divide all the variation in a trait into three parts, known by their initials as the ACE model. A is additive genetic influence, the summed effects of many genes. C is the shared environment (or common environment): everything that makes twins reared together alike, such as the family's income, the neighbourhood, the school and the parents' general style. E is the non-shared environment: everything that makes members of the same family different, such as different friends, a different teacher, an accident, an illness, a different position in the womb, and also, unavoidably, errors of measurement. The three parts can be estimated from MZ and DZ correlations. On the height figures, the shared environment comes out at about zero (C = 2 × 0.45 minus 0.90 = 0) and the non-shared environment at about 0.10 (E = 1 minus 0.90). A real psychiatric example shows the typical pattern. In a study of more than 31,000 Swedish twins published in 2006, Cynthia Bulik and her colleagues estimated the heritability of narrowly defined anorexia nervosa at 0.56, the shared environment at only 0.05 and the non-shared environment at 0.38. Again and again, for disorder after disorder, twin studies find substantial A, substantial E and little C. The experiences that matter most seem to be the ones that siblings do not share. That is the most surprising lesson twin research has taught psychiatry.

The twin method rests on the equal environments assumption: that MZ twins are treated no more alike than DZ twins in ways that matter for the trait. Critics point out that identical twins are sometimes dressed alike and even given rhyming names, are more often mistaken for each other, and influence each other more than fraternal twins do, so part of their greater resemblance could be environmental. Meenal and Divya were dressed alike until the age of ten. Two answers have been developed. One is to study identical twins reared apart, who share genes but not homes (Book 1, Section 9.7.1, tells the Minnesota study). The other is to combine twin and adoption methods, which is the subject of the next subsection.

Twins have also allowed one of the most elegant tests in psychiatric genetics. If an identical twin stays well while her co-twin develops schizophrenia, has she escaped the genes, or merely failed to express them? Gottesman and the Danish psychiatrist Aksel Bertelsen answered by following up the children of Danish twin pairs discordant for schizophrenia, a design built on the earlier work of Margit Fischer. In 1989 they reported that the children of the healthy identical co-twins had about the same risk of schizophrenia and related disorders as the children of the affected twins, about 17 percent in each group (Section 4.7.2 gives the figures). The healthy twin carried, and passed on, the same genetic liability; it simply had not been expressed in her. This is called the offspring-of-discordant-twins design, and its message is the message of the whole section: a genotype is a liability, not a destiny.

Even identical twins are not perfectly identical. Barlow and Durand note that chemical markers on the DNA, called epigenetic markers, begin to differ between MZ twins even in the womb, which explains some of the subtle differences between them; how such marks work is taught in Section 1.5.3. Between that, the non-shared environment and chance, it is no surprise that MZ concordance for every psychiatric disorder ever studied is well below 100 percent.

Exam trap

Concordance is the rate at which both twins are affected; heritability is a population statistic estimated from the comparison of MZ and DZ resemblance. They are different numbers: schizophrenia's heritability is about 80 percent but its MZ concordance only about 33 to 48 percent. An MZ concordance below 100 percent is proof that something other than DNA sequence contributes. DZ twins share about half their varying genes, like ordinary siblings, not a quarter. In the ACE model, C is the environment twins share and E the environment that makes them different.

Adoption studies

Twin studies cannot escape the suspicion that identical twins share a more similar environment. Adoption breaks the link between genes and upbringing completely, because a child adopted in infancy receives genes from one family and rearing from another. Barlow and Durand give the simplest version. Suppose a young man has a disorder, and researchers discover that his brother was adopted as a baby and brought up in a different home. They examine the brother. If enough such pairs are found, and the adopted-away brothers have the disorder more often than chance would predict, genes are probably at work, since the brothers shared genes but not homes. Researchers have built four more systematic designs on this idea (Figure 1.8).

The adoptee study (also called the adopted-away offspring design) starts from parents who have the disorder and follows up their children who were adopted away early in life, comparing them with control adoptees whose biological parents were well. The classic example is the study by the American psychiatrist Leonard Heston, published in 1966, of children born to mothers with schizophrenia in an Oregon state hospital and removed from them within days of birth: schizophrenia appeared among these children, raised by others, but not among the controls (Section 4.7.3 tells the study with its numbers). The same design has been used for alcohol: DSM-5-TR reports a three- to fourfold increase in the risk of alcohol use disorder in the children of affected parents, even when the children were given up for adoption at birth and raised by adoptive parents who did not have the disorder.

The adoptees' family study works in the opposite direction. It starts from adoptees who have developed the disorder and examines both sets of their relatives: the biological relatives, who gave them their genes, and the adoptive relatives, who raised them. If genes matter, the disorder should be concentrated among the biological relatives. The definitive studies were done in Denmark, whose national registers of adoptions and psychiatric admissions made it possible to trace every adoptee and every relative, by the American team of Seymour Kety (1915 to 2000), David Rosenthal and Paul Wender, with the Danish psychiatrist Fini Schulsinger; the team began reporting in 1968. Schizophrenia and milder related conditions were concentrated among the biological relatives of adoptees with schizophrenia, while the adoptive relatives did not differ from those of controls (Section 4.7.3). The same design was used for bipolar disorder by Julien Mendlewicz and John Rainer, who reported in 1977 that mood disorders were far commoner among the biological parents of adoptees with bipolar disorder than among the parents who had raised them (Section 5.5.1).

The cross-fostering design asks the most pointed question of all: what happens to a child with no genetic risk who is raised by an ill parent? Wender and his colleagues found, among Danish adoptees, children whose biological parents were well but one of whose adoptive parents later developed schizophrenia, and compared them with adoptees who had an ill biological parent and well adoptive parents. In their 1974 report, psychopathology was commoner among the adopted-away children of ill biological parents, but there was no such increase among the cross-fostered children. Being raised by a parent with schizophrenia did not, on its own, produce schizophrenia.

The fourth design adds what the Danish studies left out, a direct look at the adoptive home. In the Finnish Adoptive Family Study of Schizophrenia, Pekka Tienari and his colleagues traced children of mothers with schizophrenia who had been adopted away and compared them with control adoptees, but they also visited and rated the functioning of every adoptive family. The children at high genetic risk did worst when they were placed in disturbed adoptive families and much better in healthy ones, while for children at low genetic risk the adoptive family made little difference. That result is not simply "genes" or "environment"; it is the two working together, and it is taught as gene-environment interaction in Section 1.5.1 (with the details in Section 4.7.3).

Adoption studies have their own weaknesses, and examiners like to ask about them. The first is selective placement: agencies often try to match a child to adoptive parents who resemble the biological parents in background or education, which partly reunites genes and environment. The second is the prenatal environment. An adopted child spent nine months in the biological mother's womb, sharing her nutrition, her stress hormones, her infections and perhaps her alcohol, all of which are environmental influences that an adoption study wrongly counts as genetic. The third is late adoption: a child who lived with the biological parent for months or years before placement has shared that parent's environment too. The fourth is that adoptive families are screened by agencies, so they are, on average, more stable and better off than other families; the range of rearing environments is narrowed, and the effect of environment is underestimated. Finally, the parents who give up children for adoption, and the children themselves, may differ from other people in ways that limit how far the results apply to everyone else. Such studies need national registers of adoptions and of hospital admissions, which is why almost all of them come from Scandinavia and the United States.

Exam trap

Match the design to its starting point. Adoptee study: start from ill biological parents and follow their adopted-away children (Heston, Oregon, 1966). Adoptees' family study: start from ill adoptees and compare biological with adoptive relatives (Kety, Rosenthal and Wender, Denmark, from 1968). Cross-fostering: children of healthy parents raised by an ill adoptive parent (Wender and colleagues, 1974: no increase). Tienari (Finland) rated the adoptive families and found gene-environment interaction. The main weaknesses are selective placement, the shared prenatal environment, late adoption and screened adoptive homes.

Heritability: what the number means

Family, twin and adoption studies come together in a single number, heritability, and Book 1, Section 9.7.3, explains carefully what it is and what it is not. In brief: heritability (h2) is the proportion of the variation in a trait, within a particular population at a particular time, that is associated with genetic variation in that population. It describes differences among people, not the make-up of any one person. It is not fixed: it changes when the range of environments changes. And a highly heritable trait can still be changed by the environment, as height (which has risen over generations with better nutrition) and phenylketonuria (whose effects a diet prevents) both show. Hold those three points in mind, because every one of them is violated daily in conversations about mental illness.

Table 1.12 collects the estimates for the disorders of this book, taken only from DSM-5-TR, Barlow and Durand, and the studies reported in the disorder chapters. Read it as a whole before you memorise any line of it.

Table 1.12 Heritability and twin findings across the disorders (high yield). Estimates come from twin studies unless stated; ranges reflect different studies and populations.
DisorderHeritability estimateTwin or family findingSource
Bipolar disorderAbout 90 percent in some twin studies; about 85 percent (McGuffin, 2003)MZ concordance only 40 to 70 percentDSM-5-TR; Section 5.5.1
SchizophreniaAbout 80 percent (81 percent, Sullivan and colleagues, 2003; 79 percent, Danish twins, 2018)MZ concordance about 48 percent (Gottesman, 1991) or 33 percent (probandwise, 2018)Section 4.7.2
Autism spectrum disorder37 to more than 90 percent from twin concordance; 80 percent in a five-country cohortAbout 15 percent of cases linked to a known mutationDSM-5-TR
ADHDAbout 74 percentNo single geneDSM-5-TR
Separation anxiety disorder73 percent in 6-year-old twinsHigher in girlsDSM-5-TR
