Four lessons stand out. The first is that every disorder in the table is partly heritable, and none is wholly genetic. Even at the top, where bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, autism and ADHD carry heritabilities of 70 to 90 percent, the concordance of identical twins is far below 100 percent: DSM-5-TR itself says of bipolar disorder that monozygotic concordance of 40 to 70 percent indicates that "much risk is left unexplained by genes alone". The second is that a high heritability is perfectly compatible with a modest concordance. The two numbers measure different things: heritability is about the share of variation in liability that tracks genetic differences, while concordance is about how often a co-twin actually crosses the threshold. The third is that for the common disorders in the lower half of the table, depression, generalised anxiety and the phobias, more than half of the variation lies with the environment, and the twin studies say it is mostly the non-shared environment. The fourth is that heritability depends on exposure. Alcohol use disorder has a heritability of 40 to 60 percent, yet nobody develops it without drinking; in a community where nobody drank, its heritability could not even be estimated. Heritability is always a statement about a particular population living in a particular range of environments, which is also why figures from Scandinavian, American and Australian twin registers cannot simply be assumed to hold in Kanpur or Nashik.

Whether these figures mean that some disorders are "caused by biology" and have "little to no" psychosocial cause, as one examination question claims for bipolar disorder and schizophrenia, is taken up in Section 1.5.6, once gene-environment interplay has been explained.

Clinical link

When Rakesh asks "Will I get it?", the answer comes from empiric risk figures for relatives (about 5 to 10 percent for the sibling of a person with bipolar disorder), not from heritability. Telling a family that bipolar disorder is "90 percent genetic" invites them to hear "a 90 percent chance", which is false and frightening. Good genetic counselling gives the absolute risk, explains that most relatives never fall ill, describes the early warning signs (in bipolar disorder, reduced need for sleep and rising energy), and points to what is changeable: regular sleep, avoiding cannabis and other drugs, and early help.

Exam trap

A heritability of 80 percent does not mean that 80 percent of a person's illness comes from genes, nor an 80 percent chance of illness in a relative. Heritability belongs to a population, changes with the environment, and says nothing about whether a disorder can be treated. The most heritable disorders (bipolar disorder about 90 percent in some studies, schizophrenia about 80) still have MZ concordance far below 100 percent. Major depression is moderately heritable, about 40 percent.

Finding the genes: linkage, association and genome-wide studies

Family, twin and adoption studies can tell you that genes matter and roughly how much, but not which genes, or where they lie. Finding them needed new tools, and the first was genetic linkage analysis. Its principle is simple. Genes that lie close together on the same chromosome tend to be inherited together, because the shuffling of chromosomes that happens when eggs and sperm are made rarely separates near neighbours. So researchers choose genetic markers, stretches of DNA whose position on a chromosome is already known and which vary between people, and trace them through a family with many affected members. If one version of a marker travels with the disorder, turning up in the affected members far more often than chance allows, a gene for the disorder probably lies near that marker. The strength of the evidence is expressed as a LOD score (the logarithm of the odds that the marker and the disorder are linked); a score of 3 or more, odds of a thousand to one in favour, is the traditional threshold. Linkage was a triumph for single-gene diseases: in 1983 the gene for Huntington's disease was located on chromosome 4 in this way, with the help of a huge Venezuelan family.

For the psychiatric disorders, linkage produced one of the most instructive disappointments in the history of the field. In 1987 Janice A. Egeland and her colleagues reported in Nature on a large Old Order Amish family in Pennsylvania. The Amish suited the method perfectly: large families, careful genealogies, little migration, and little alcohol or drug use to blur diagnosis. Two markers at the tip of the short arm of chromosome 11, the gene for insulin and a known cancer gene, appeared to travel with bipolar disorder through the pedigree, and the authors concluded that a dominant gene conferring a strong predisposition to manic-depressive illness lay there. Two years later John Kelsoe and colleagues, including Egeland herself, re-analysed the family. Several new members had been added to the pedigree and the clinical status of two members had changed; with those changes, and with a larger extension of the pedigree, the linkage disappeared and could be excluded. Barlow and Durand draw the lesson: the failure to replicate casts doubt on any claim that a single gene is responsible for such complex disorders, so be wary the next time a newspaper reports that "the gene" for a disorder has been found.

Linkage works well for genes of large effect, and the psychiatric disorders turned out not to have many. The second tool, the association study, is better at finding genes of small effect. Instead of following markers through families, it compares a large group of people with the disorder with a group without it: if a particular allele is significantly commoner among the cases, the allele, or a gene very close to it, is assumed to be involved. For two decades association studies were mostly candidate gene studies. Researchers chose a gene whose function seemed relevant (a dopamine receptor gene for schizophrenia, the serotonin transporter gene for depression, the gene for the enzyme that breaks down dopamine) and tested it in a few hundred people. Hundreds of positive findings were published, and almost none survived when much larger samples were examined. Section 1.5.1 tells the most famous case, the serotonin transporter gene in depression, and Section 4.7.4 the candidate genes of schizophrenia.

The breakthrough was the genome-wide association study (GWAS). The commonest form of genetic variation is the single-nucleotide polymorphism (SNP), a single position in the DNA where people differ by one "letter" (one base). A GWAS measures hundreds of thousands to millions of SNPs spread across the entire genome in every participant, without any prior guess about which genes matter, and compares cases with controls at every one of them. Because so many comparisons are made, chance "hits" are inevitable, so a very strict criterion of genome-wide significance is used, conventionally a probability below 5 × 10-8 (five in a hundred million), roughly the ordinary 0.05 divided by a million tests. Effects so small can be detected only in enormous samples, tens or hundreds of thousands of people, which no single research group can collect. Hence the international consortia, above all the Psychiatric Genomics Consortium, whose schizophrenia analyses have identified hundreds of associated loci (Section 4.7.4 gives the counts and what the genes do). For other disorders DSM-5-TR notes, for example, that large GWAS have identified a number of loci for ADHD, and that there is no single gene for it.

The genome-wide era brought a surprise that matters for classification. The same variants turn up in more than one disorder. DSM-5-TR says that the risk alleles identified for schizophrenia "are also associated with other mental disorders, including bipolar disorder, depression, and autism spectrum disorder." One gene influencing several apparently different traits is called pleiotropy. In 2013 the Cross-Disorder Group of the Psychiatric Genomics Consortium analysed 33,332 cases and 27,888 controls of European ancestry with five disorders, autism spectrum disorder, ADHD, bipolar disorder, major depressive disorder and schizophrenia, and found four loci with effects across disorders, among them variants in two genes for subunits of L-type calcium channels (CACNA1C and CACNB2). Shared genes are one reason why disorders co-occur so often (comorbidity, Section 1.2.7), and they challenge the assumption that the categories of the manuals are separate natural kinds (Section 1.6.4).

Common SNPs of tiny effect are not the whole story. There are also rare variants of large effect. Copy number variants (CNVs) are stretches of DNA that are deleted or duplicated; the best known, the 22q11.2 deletion, carries a greatly raised risk of psychosis (Section 4.7.4). Some variants are de novo mutations, new changes present in the child but in neither parent; they become more frequent with the father's age. For autism, DSM-5-TR reports that as many as 15 percent of cases appear to be associated with a known genetic mutation, with different de novo copy number variants or mutations in different families.

And yet a gap remains, known as missing heritability. Twin studies put the heritability of schizophrenia at about 80 percent, but in 2014 the Psychiatric Genomics Consortium found that all the common variants it could score together explained only about 7 percent of the variation in liability, and the genome-wide significant loci about half of that. Where is the rest? Probably in thousands of further variants too weak to detect yet, in rare variants that SNP chips do not capture, in interactions among genes and between genes and environments, and partly, perhaps, in twin estimates that run a little high. The gap shrinks as samples grow, but it is the reason no genetic test can yet tell a person whether he will develop a common psychiatric disorder.

Exam trap

Linkage analysis traces markers of known position through families and finds genes of large effect; association studies compare cases with controls and find genes of small effect; a GWAS is an association study across the whole genome, with significance set at 5 × 10-8. The Amish chromosome 11 linkage for bipolar disorder (Egeland, 1987) collapsed on re-analysis (Kelsoe, 1989). Pleiotropy: one gene, several disorders. Missing heritability: found variants explain far less than twin studies estimate.

Polygenic risk scores

If a disorder depends on thousands of variants of tiny effect, no single one is worth testing. But they can be added up. A polygenic risk score is a weighted sum of a person's risk alleles across thousands or even millions of variants: for each SNP, the number of risk alleles the person carries (none, one or two) is multiplied by the size of that allele's effect as estimated in a GWAS, and the products are summed. Scores calculated this way are spread across the population in a bell curve, just as the liability-threshold model predicts. People in the top tenth of the distribution have a clearly higher average risk than people in the bottom tenth.

Polygenic risk scores have become one of the most useful tools in psychiatric research. They allow researchers to compare groups (are scores for schizophrenia raised in people with bipolar disorder? they are), to test whether the genetic risk of one disorder overlaps with another or with a trait such as educational attainment, and to ask whether genetic risk and an environmental exposure combine (Section 1.5.1). The 2013 cross-disorder study used them to show genetic overlap especially among the disorders that begin in adulthood.

What they cannot do, at least not yet, is tell an individual whether he will fall ill. A score that explains about 7 percent of the variation in liability to schizophrenia leaves 93 percent unexplained; most people with a high score never develop the disorder, and many people who develop it have ordinary scores (Sections 4.7.4 and 5.5.1). There is a second limitation, and it matters directly for Indian readers. Scores are only as good as the GWAS from which their weights come, and those studies have been done overwhelmingly in people of European ancestry: in 2019 Alicia Martin and her colleagues reported that about 79 percent of all GWAS participants were of European descent, although Europeans make up about 16 percent of the world's population. When they applied European-derived scores to people of other ancestries in the UK Biobank, prediction accuracy for the 17 body-measurement and blood-test traits they studied (not psychiatric disorders) fell, by 1.7-fold on average in South Asians and 4.9-fold in Africans. Until large genetic studies are done in South Asian populations, a polygenic score calculated for Rakesh would be even less informative than one calculated for a European.

The ethical questions arrive before the science is ready. Commercial companies already offer polygenic scores directly to the public and even to couples choosing among embryos, practices most psychiatric geneticists regard as premature. Scores could be misused by insurers and employers, and in a country where marriages are still often arranged with horoscopes and family histories in hand, a "genetic score" could easily become one more reason to reject a bride or a groom, or one more source of stigma for a family already affected.

Remember

A polygenic risk score adds up thousands of small genetic effects, each weighted by its GWAS effect size. It is a research tool for groups, not a diagnostic test for individuals: for schizophrenia it explains only about 7 percent of the variation in liability (2014), and it predicts less well in people of non-European ancestry, including South Asians, because most GWAS samples are European. There is no genetic test that can diagnose or predict schizophrenia, bipolar disorder or depression in an individual.

Endophenotypes: the links between genes and symptoms

Consider how long the road is from a gene to a symptom. A variant in a stretch of DNA alters, very slightly, how much of some protein a neuron makes. That changes, very slightly, how certain synapses form during development, which changes how a circuit in the prefrontal cortex handles information, which makes it harder to hold a thought in mind for a few seconds, which, together with a hundred other influences, years later and under stress, may contribute to disorganised thinking or a delusion. Every step of that road is shaped by other genes and by experience. And the diagnosis at the end of the road is itself a mixture: two people with schizophrenia may share hardly a symptom. No wonder the search for a single "schizophrenia gene" failed. What if researchers stopped looking for the genes of the diagnosis, and looked instead for the genes of something nearer to the DNA?

That something is an endophenotype: a measurable, heritable characteristic, which may be neurophysiological, biochemical, endocrine, neuroanatomical, cognitive or neuropsychological, that lies on the pathway between the genotype and the disorder, closer to the genes than the symptoms are. The prefix "endo" means within: an endophenotype is an internal phenotype, invisible to the naked eye, that has to be measured with an instrument or a test. Because it sits nearer the genes, it should be influenced by fewer of them, and so their effects should be easier to find. Barlow and Durand define endophenotypes as "the genetic mechanisms that ultimately contribute to the underlying problems causing the symptoms and difficulties experienced by people with psychological disorders". Their example makes the idea concrete: in schizophrenia, researchers are not looking for a "schizophrenia gene" (a genotype); they are searching for the gene or genes responsible for the working memory problems characteristic of people with the disorder (an endophenotype), and for the genes behind its other problems.

The word has an unexpected history. It was coined in 1966 by two geneticists, Bernard John and Kenneth R. Lewis, who were trying to explain the geographical distribution of grasshoppers: the insects' distribution, they argued, depended less on their visible features than on an internal, microscopic one, the structure of their chromosomes, which they called the endophenotype. Irving Gottesman and James Shields, the London psychiatric geneticist who maintained the Maudsley Hospital twin register, borrowed the term for psychiatry in the early 1970s, in their work on the genetics of schizophrenia. Three decades later Gottesman and the younger American researcher Todd Gould revived and popularised it, in 2003, in a paper in the American Journal of Psychiatry on "the endophenotype concept in psychiatry". They also set out criteria that a good endophenotype should meet:

  • It is associated with the illness in the population: people with the disorder show it more often than people without.
  • It is heritable.
  • It is largely state-independent: it is present whether or not the illness is active at the time, so it is not simply a symptom or a side effect of being ill or of treatment.
  • Within families, the endophenotype and the illness co-segregate, that is, they tend to be inherited together.
  • It is found in the unaffected relatives of affected people at a higher rate than in the general population, which shows that it reflects the genetic liability rather than the illness itself.

The best-studied candidates come from schizophrenia, and Section 4.7.5 teaches them with their history. The oldest is smooth-pursuit eye movement dysfunction: asked to follow a swinging pendulum with their eyes, many people with schizophrenia, and many of their unaffected relatives, cannot keep their gaze gliding smoothly and fall back on jerky catch-up movements. P50 sensory gating is the failure to damp down the brain's electrical response to the second of two identical clicks; prepulse inhibition is the normal reduction of a startle blink when a weak sound comes just before a loud one, which is weaker in schizophrenia; and working memory deficits appear, in milder form, in relatives too. Other disorders have candidates of their own. In ADHD, Barlow and Durand describe research suggesting that poor inhibitory control, the ability to stop a response when signalled to, is common both in children with the disorder and in their unaffected siblings and parents, and may be a marker of the genetic liability (Goos and colleagues, 2009). For alcohol use disorder, DSM-5-TR describes "intermediate characteristics (or phenotypes)" that alter risk, among them a low level of response to alcohol, which goes with a high risk of the disorder, and the alcohol-related flushing reaction commoner in people of Asian descent, which goes with a low one. In antisocial personality disorder, Barlow and Durand note, researchers look for genes behind specific traits such as fearlessness and impulsivity rather than for genes of the disorder as a whole (Chapter 11).

Two related terms are easily confused with endophenotypes. A biomarker is any measurable biological indicator of a disorder, and biomarkers come in two kinds. A state marker is present only while the person is ill and disappears on recovery, as raised cortisol may in a depressive episode; a trait marker is present whether the person is ill or well. Endophenotypes are trait markers that are also heritable and run in families. None of the candidates is specific enough to diagnose anyone; their value is as research tools that cut a heterogeneous diagnosis into pieces closer to biology, an approach that also underlies the Research Domain Criteria (Section 1.6.4; Book 2, Section 2.10.6).

Table 1.13 sets the endophenotype beside the three other terms that examiners offer as distractors, so that you can see why each is wrong.

Table 1.13 Genotype, endophenotype, phenotype and molecular genetics.
TermWhat it isExampleIs it the intermediate mechanism between genes and symptoms?
GenotypeA person's genetic make-up: the alleles he carriesCarrying two copies of a particular risk alleleWrong: the DNA itself, at the start of the road, not the link to symptoms
EndophenotypeA measurable, heritable internal trait on the path from genes to disorderWorking memory or eye-tracking deficits in schizophrenia; poor inhibitory control in ADHDRight: it is the intermediate mechanism (Barlow and Durand's definition)
PhenotypeThe observable characteristics, including the disorder and its symptomsHallucinations, a diagnosis of schizophrenia, heightWrong: the symptoms themselves, at the end of the road
Molecular geneticsA research method: the biological analysis of DNA samples to see what genes doStudying how a risk gene changes a proteinWrong: a way of studying genes, not a mechanism in the person
Exam trap

"The genetic mechanisms that ultimately contribute to the symptoms and difficulties experienced by people with psychological disorders" are endophenotypes (Amity 2023, copying Barlow and Durand). Not phenotypes (the observable symptoms and disorder), not genotypes (the genetic make-up), not molecular genetics (a research method). The term came from insect genetics (John and Lewis, 1966), entered psychiatry with Gottesman and Shields in the early 1970s, and was revived by Gottesman and Gould in 2003. A good endophenotype is heritable, state-independent and found more often in unaffected relatives. Smooth-pursuit eye movement dysfunction is the classic example.

1.5 Genes Meet the World: Interaction, Correlation, Epigenetics and the BrainLower yield · 1 Q

Go back to Meenal and Divya, the identical twins of Section 1.4.3. They carry the same genes, they grew up in the same flat, and only one of them has had a depressive illness. Section 1.4 established two facts that seem to pull in opposite directions: almost every psychiatric disorder is substantially heritable, and no disorder is wholly genetic. This section shows how the two facts fit together. Genes and environments do not simply add their effects like two weights on one side of a scale. A gene can change how strongly a person reacts to an experience (gene-environment interaction). Genes can change which experiences a person meets in the first place (gene-environment correlation). And experiences can change which genes are switched on (epigenetics). After those three ideas, the section maps what is known about the brain's chemistry, hormones and structure across the disorders, sweeps through the environmental risks from the womb to the city, and ends with a question that examiners like to set: are some disorders simply biological?

Gene-environment interaction

A gene-environment interaction (often written G × E) exists when the effect of a genotype depends on the environment, or, saying the same thing the other way round, when the effect of an environment depends on the genotype. The word "interaction" is meant in its statistical sense: the effect is not genes plus environment but something more like genes times environment. The simplest example is phenylketonuria. The faulty genotype does harm only in an environment that contains the amino acid phenylalanine; on a low-phenylalanine diet it does almost none, and phenylalanine, harmless to everybody else, is damaging only to people with the genotype. Neither the gene nor the diet alone predicts the outcome; the combination does. This is the diathesis-stress model of Section 1.3.4 written at the level of genes: the gene is the diathesis, the environment is the stress, and illness requires both.

Key study: Caspi, Moffitt and the MAOA gene (2002)

Question. Children who are maltreated are at higher risk of becoming violent and antisocial adults, but most of them do not. Why? Avshalom Caspi, Terrie Moffitt and their colleagues asked whether a gene could protect some children. Method. They used the Dunedin Multidisciplinary Health and Development Study, a cohort of 1,037 children born in Dunedin, New Zealand, in the early 1970s and assessed at regular intervals from the age of 3 into adulthood. Between the ages of 3 and 11, 8 percent of the children had experienced "severe" maltreatment, 28 percent "probable" maltreatment and 64 percent none. The gene they chose codes for monoamine oxidase A (MAOA), an enzyme that breaks down the neurotransmitters serotonin, noradrenaline and dopamine; a common variant in the gene's promoter makes it work at low or at high activity. Because the gene lies on the X chromosome, males carry only one copy, so the analysis was done in the boys. Antisocial outcomes were measured four ways: conduct disorder in adolescence, convictions for violent crime, a personality disposition towards violence, and symptoms of antisocial personality disorder reported by people who knew the men well. Result. The genotype on its own predicted nothing, and maltreatment on its own predicted antisocial behaviour; but the effect of maltreatment was much stronger in boys with the low-activity genotype than in boys with the high-activity genotype. Men who combined low MAOA activity with maltreatment made up only 12 percent of the male cohort but accounted for 44 percent of its convictions for violent crime, and 85 percent of the severely maltreated boys with the low-activity genotype developed some form of antisocial behaviour. The maltreated and non-maltreated groups did not differ in genotype, so the gene was not causing the maltreatment. Conclusion. Genotype can moderate a child's sensitivity to an environmental insult; a gene can protect as well as endanger. Criticism. The authors themselves warned that the finding needed replication before anyone spoke of clinical use. Individual replications were mixed, but meta-analyses (Julia Kim-Cohen and colleagues, 2006; Amy Byrd and Stephen Manuck, 2014, pooling 20 male cohorts) supported the interaction in males, which makes it one of the more durable gene-environment findings.

A year later the same Dunedin team published an even more famous result, on depression. Book 2, Section 1.7.10, tells it with its numbers: people with one or two short versions of the serotonin transporter gene (5-HTTLPR) seemed to become depressed after stressful life events and childhood maltreatment far more often than people with two long versions. The paper became one of the most cited in psychiatry, and then it fell apart. A meta-analysis of 14 studies by Neil Risch and his colleagues in 2009 found no evidence of the interaction. In 2019 Richard Border and his colleagues tested the 18 candidate genes for depression that had each been studied ten or more times, in samples of between about 62,000 and 443,000 people, and found no clear evidence for any of them, alone or in interaction with childhood abuse or socioeconomic adversity; most of the published candidate-gene findings, they concluded, were probably false positives (Section 5.5.1). The lesson is not that gene-environment interaction is a myth. It is that a single common variant of tiny effect cannot be studied reliably in a few hundred people, however tidy the result looks. The principle survives, now tested with polygenic scores in large samples, even where the particular gene did not.

Two other interactions recur in this book. In 2005 Caspi and colleagues reported from the Dunedin cohort that adolescent cannabis use raised the risk of adult psychosis mainly in people carrying one version of a common variant of the COMT gene, which codes for an enzyme that breaks down dopamine; later studies have been less consistent (Section 4.7.8). And the Finnish adoption study of Pekka Tienari, met in Section 1.4.4, is an interaction study in all but name. Children of mothers with schizophrenia who were adopted into disturbed, poorly functioning families developed schizophrenia-spectrum disorders far more often than those adopted into healthy families, while for children of healthy mothers the quality of the adoptive family made little difference (Section 4.7.3). The genetic risk mattered chiefly in a harmful environment; the harmful environment mattered chiefly for the genetically vulnerable.

One twist on the idea deserves a line. Jay Belsky and Michael Pluess's differential susceptibility hypothesis (Book 2, Section 1.7.11) suggests that some "vulnerability" genes are really sensitivity genes: their carriers do worse than others in bad environments and better than others in good ones.

Exam trap

Gene-environment interaction: the effect of a gene depends on the environment (and the reverse). Caspi and colleagues 2002: the MAOA gene, childhood maltreatment and antisocial behaviour in Dunedin boys (low-activity MAOA plus maltreatment = most risk); Caspi and colleagues 2003: the serotonin transporter gene (5-HTTLPR), stress and depression, not replicated (Risch 2009; Border 2019). Do not swap the genes or the outcomes. Tienari's Finnish adoption study is also an interaction: genetic risk plus a disturbed adoptive family.

Gene-environment correlation

Interaction assumes that genes and environments are dealt out independently and then meet. Often they are not independent at all. In gene-environment correlation (rGE), a person's genes influence the environments he is exposed to. Book 2, Section 1.7.11, teaches the three kinds that Robert Plomin, John DeFries and John Loehlin distinguished in 1977 (Book 1, Section 9.7.4, gives Sandra Scarr and Kathleen McCartney's 1983 version), so a fresh example of each is enough here. In passive gene-environment correlation, parents give a child both genes and a home that reflect the same tendencies: a girl whose mother has an anxiety disorder may inherit an anxious temperament and also grow up watching her mother check the locks, avoid crowds and warn of dangers, so that genes and upbringing push the same way. In evocative gene-environment correlation (also called reactive), a child's inherited characteristics draw particular responses from other people: a restless, impulsive, easily frustrated boy is shouted at and punished more by parents and teachers than his calmer brother. In active gene-environment correlation, people seek out environments that suit their dispositions: a thrill-seeking adolescent chooses risky friends, fast vehicles and drugs. Passive correlation matters most in early childhood; active correlation grows as young people gain control over their own lives.

Barlow and Durand call the same idea the reciprocal gene-environment model, and their example is worth retelling because it shows how a genetic vulnerability can manufacture its own trigger. Imagine a person with an inherited tendency to faint at the sight of blood, who also has an inherited impulsive temperament. Always rushing, careless of physical safety, she has more minor accidents than other people, and so she sees blood more often, and each fright strengthens the phobia. A person with neither tendency rarely meets the trigger at all. The genes, in other words, help to create the very environmental risk that then sets off the genetic vulnerability.

Salim shows the same pattern in an Indian setting. He is 24 and works as a food-delivery rider in Hyderabad, and he has always been the boy who climbed highest, rode fastest and answered back first; his father, he says, was "exactly the same at my age". In two years he has had three road accidents, lost two jobs after quarrels with supervisors, broken an engagement and been in a fight outside a tea stall that ended at a police station. He comes to the clinic, at his mother's insistence, because he is drinking every night and cannot sleep. A life-events checklist shows a heavy burden of stress, and a diathesis-stress account might say that stress has tipped a vulnerable young man into a disorder. But look at the events. Almost all of them are what researchers call dependent events, ones his own behaviour helped to produce; only one, his grandfather's death, was independent of him. His temperament, partly inherited, is generating his stressors.

How large is this effect? Kenneth Kendler and his colleagues asked 2,315 pairs of twins from the Virginia Twin Registry about the stressful events of the past year and reported in 1993 that, for total life events, genetic factors and the family environment each accounted for about 20 percent of the variation. The kind of event mattered. For "network" events, which happen to people in one's social circle (a relative's illness, say), twin resemblance came entirely from the family environment; for "personal" events, which happen directly to the respondent (being robbed, losing a job, a quarrel), most of the twin resemblance was genetic. In 2007 Kendler and Baker reviewed 55 independent studies of the heritability of environments that matter to psychiatry: stressful life events, parenting, the family environment, social support, peer relationships and marital quality. The weighted average heritability was 27 percent, with most measures between 15 and 35 percent; it was smaller but still present (14 percent) when the environment was rated by direct observation rather than by the person, so it reflects actual behaviour and not only perception. Barlow and Durand add a striking example from Matt McGue and David Lykken (1992): the probability that you will divorce roughly doubles if your fraternal twin has divorced, and rises about sixfold if your identical twin has. Nobody inherits a gene for divorce; people inherit traits, such as being impulsive, short-tempered or drawn to incompatible partners, that make a divorce more likely.

Gene-environment correlation has two practical consequences. For research, it means that an "environmental" risk factor may carry genetic information. If harsh parenting goes with conduct problems in children, the harshness may be causing the problems, or the parents and children may share genes that produce both, or the children's difficult temperament may be provoking the harshness. Only genetically informative designs (adoption studies, twin studies, studies of the children of twins) can tell these apart, which is one reason Caspi's team checked that genotype did not predict maltreatment. For the clinician, it means something hopeful. If a temperament keeps steering a person into harmful situations, then helping him to recognise and change those choices (a different job, a safer route, different friends, learning to walk away from a quarrel) cuts the chain that links his genes to his stressors. Genetic influence on an environment does not make the environment unchangeable. Table 1.14 sets interaction and the three kinds of correlation side by side.

Table 1.14 Gene-environment interaction and the three kinds of gene-environment correlation.
ProcessWhat happensExampleWhy it matters
Interaction (G × E)The effect of an environment depends on the genotypeMaltreatment leads to antisocial behaviour mainly in boys with low-activity MAOA (Caspi, 2002)Explains why the same adversity harms some people far more than others
Passive correlationParents supply both genes and a matching homeAnxious mother: anxious temperament plus an anxious household"Parenting effects" may partly be genetic
Evocative (reactive) correlationA person's inherited traits draw responses from othersAn impulsive, irritable boy is punished more oftenThe child partly shapes the treatment he receives
Active correlationPeople choose environments that fit their dispositionsSalim, a sensation seeker, rides fast and picks fightsStressors may be "dependent" events produced by the person
Exam trap

In interaction, genes change how strongly an environment affects you; in correlation, genes change which environments you meet. Barlow and Durand's "reciprocal gene-environment model" is gene-environment correlation: an impulsive person with a genetic vulnerability to blood-injection-injury phobia has more accidents and so sees more blood. Passive (parents provide genes and environment), evocative or reactive (traits draw responses), active (people seek niches). Life events themselves are modestly heritable (about 20 percent for total events, Kendler 1993; 27 percent across environmental measures, Kendler and Baker 2007).

Epigenetics: experience written on the genome

Nearly every cell in your body carries the same DNA, yet a neuron and a liver cell could hardly be more different. The difference lies in which genes each cell actually uses. Genes can be switched on or off, turned up or down, without any change in the DNA sequence itself, and the study of these changes is epigenetics. The prefix "epi" means on or around, and the name fits: the changes are made by chemical material sitting on or around the genome, collectively called the epigenome. Two mechanisms are best understood. In DNA methylation, small chemical tags called methyl groups are attached to the DNA, often in the region at the start of a gene that controls its activity (the promoter), and heavy methylation there usually silences the gene. In histone modification, chemical changes are made to the histones, the proteins around which DNA is wound like thread on a spool; some changes loosen the winding so that a gene can be read, others tighten it so that it cannot. A useful analogy is a play. The DNA sequence is the script, identical in every copy; the epigenome is the director's pencil marks, which decide which lines are spoken loudly, which softly and which are cut. What made epigenetics revolutionary for psychopathology is the discovery that experience, especially early experience, can make those pencil marks.

Key study: Meaney's mothers and their pups (1999 to 2005)

Question. Some rat mothers spend a great deal of time licking and grooming their pups and nursing them in a protective, arched-back posture; others do much less. Their offspring differ too: the pups of attentive mothers grow up less fearful and better able to tolerate stress. Is that inherited through the mothers' genes or through their mothering? Method. The neuroscientist Michael Meaney and his colleagues at McGill University in Montreal used cross-fostering: newborn pups of one mother are given to another mother to rear (Figure 1.9). Darlene Francis, Josie Diorio, Dong Liu and Meaney (1999) took newborn pups of fearful, easily stressed mothers and placed them with calm, attentive mothers, while other pups stayed with their own mothers. Result. Pups born to easily stressed mothers but reared by calm ones grew up calm, and, as mothers themselves, they were calm and attentive with their own pups. The style of mothering was passed down the generations independently of the genes. Cameron and colleagues (2005), as Barlow and Durand report, showed that the maternal care had lastingly altered the pups' hormonal response to stress by affecting gene expression, but only if the care was given in the pups' first week of life; after that it no longer mattered. Ian Weaver and colleagues (2004) found the mechanism: pups of high-licking mothers had different DNA methylation at the promoter of the glucocorticoid receptor gene in the hippocampus, the receptor that lets the brain switch off the stress response. The differences emerged over the first week of life, were reversed by cross-fostering and persisted into adulthood, and a drug that altered histone chemistry, infused into the brain, removed them. Conclusion. Early experience can set the epigenetic state of a stress-related gene, and that state is potentially reversible. Criticism. These are rats; human brains cannot be sampled in the same way, and how far the mechanism carries over to people is still being worked out.

Primates show the same thing. The American primatologist Stephen Suomi used cross-fostering with rhesus monkeys and reported in 1999 that genetically reactive, emotional infants reared by calm mothers for their first six months behaved in later life as if they had never been reactive at all; and when they became mothers, they too were calm and supportive with their young. The early environment had overridden, and even reversed across a generation, an inherited tendency. A third study made the point from the opposite direction. John Crabbe, Douglas Wahlsten and Bruce Dudek (1999) tested several genetically distinct strains of mice (Barlow and Durand simplify them to three types) at the same time in three laboratories, at their three universities, and went to extraordinary lengths to make the conditions identical: the same sawdust bedding changed on the same day of the week, the same gloves, even the same kind of pen for marking tails. If genes alone determined behaviour, each strain should have behaved identically at all three sites. On some tests it did; on others it did not, and for some tests even the size of the differences between strains depended on which laboratory did the testing. Small, uncontrolled differences in environment still mattered. The neuroscientist Robert Sapolsky drew the conclusion that Barlow and Durand quote: genetic influences "are often a lot less powerful than is commonly believed".

Is there human evidence? Experiments are impossible, but history has provided some terrible natural ones. In the winter of 1944 to 1945, a German blockade of the western Netherlands caused a sharp, time-limited famine, the Dutch Hunger Winter, in which average daily rations fell below about 4,200 kilojoules, roughly 1,000 kilocalories. Because the famine began and ended abruptly and the Dutch kept excellent records, researchers could identify exactly who had been conceived or carried during it. Ezra Susser and Shang Lin reported in 1992 that people whose first trimester in the womb fell during the severe famine had a raised risk of later being admitted to hospital with schizophrenia: about twice the usual risk in women (a relative risk of about 2.2), though not in men; a more precise study in 1996 found that people conceived at the height of the famine, men and women alike, had double the risk (a relative risk of 2.0). Was the famine written on their genes? In 2008 Bastiaan Heijmans and colleagues compared people who had been exposed to the famine around the time of conception with their own unexposed brothers and sisters of the same sex. Six decades later, the exposed siblings had less methylation of IGF2, a gene involved in growth: the first direct evidence that early-life conditions can cause epigenetic changes in humans that persist throughout life. (IGF2 is not a gene for schizophrenia; the study showed that the mechanism exists in people.)

The closest human parallel to Meaney's rats came from his own institute. Patrick McGowan, Meaney and their colleagues examined brain tissue from the hippocampus of people who had died by suicide, some with and some without a history of childhood abuse, and of control subjects. Published in 2009, the study found that the suicide victims who had been abused in childhood had increased methylation of the promoter of the glucocorticoid receptor gene and lower expression of the receptor: the same gene, the same direction of change and the same brain region as in the poorly mothered rat pups. DSM-5-TR now mentions epigenetic factors among the influences on susceptibility to PTSD, and Section 5.5.1 notes several epigenetic mechanisms that have been implicated in depression.

Three cautions keep the excitement in proportion. First, almost all the human evidence is correlational, and the methylation measured in blood or saliva, which is all that can be sampled in living people, may not match what is happening in the brain. Second, the transmission across generations in Meaney's and Suomi's animals ran through behaviour: calm mothers raised calm daughters who became calm mothers. Whether epigenetic marks themselves pass from human parents to grandchildren through eggs and sperm is far less certain than some popular accounts suggest. Third, epigenetic marks are not destiny either; in the rats they were reversible. What epigenetics does settle is a question of logic. Heritability estimates assume that the contributions of genes and environment can be separated and added up. If experience alters the activity of genes, the separation is artificial. Barlow and Durand draw the conclusion: it is probably too simplistic to say that the genetic contribution to a personality trait or a psychological disorder is about 50 percent; a heritable contribution can be spoken of only in the context of the individual's past and present environment. The differences between identical twins such as Meenal and Divya, which begin in the womb and widen with every year of separate experience, are part of the same story.

Exam trap

Epigenetics changes whether and how much a gene is expressed, not the DNA sequence; the main mechanisms are DNA methylation (usually silencing) and histone modification. Meaney's rats: pups of fearful mothers cross-fostered to calm mothers grew up calm (Francis and colleagues, 1999), the effect needed the first week of life (Cameron, 2005), and it worked through methylation of the glucocorticoid receptor gene (Weaver, 2004). Suomi: rhesus monkeys. Crabbe: identical mice in three laboratories. Human evidence: the Dutch Hunger Winter (Susser; Heijmans) and McGowan's suicide victims abused in childhood.

The brain: messengers, hormones and structures

Genes and experiences can only cause a disorder by changing the brain, and the biological chapters of this book each describe what has been found in the brains of people with particular disorders. This subsection is a map of those findings, not a lesson in neuroscience: how neurons communicate, how drugs act at the synapse and how the brain is imaged belong to the Physiological Psychology book, and the disorder-specific details to Sections 4.8, 5.5 and 6.7. Neurons communicate by releasing chemical messengers, neurotransmitters, across the tiny gap of the synapse, where they act on receptors on the next neuron and are then broken down or taken back up into the neuron that released them (reuptake). Six transmitters dominate the psychopathology literature (Table 1.15).

Table 1.15 The neurotransmitters of psychopathology: a map with pointers (high yield).
TransmitterMain idea in psychopathologyDisorders linkedDrugs acting on itTaught in
DopamineReward, motivation and the salience of events; excess activity in psychosisSchizophrenia and other psychoses; addiction; ADHDAntipsychotics block D2 receptors; stimulants such as amphetamine raise dopamine4.8; Chapters 3 and 10
Serotonin (5-HT)Mood, anxiety and impulse controlDepression; anxiety disorders; OCD; impulsive aggression and suicideSelective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), such as fluoxetine5.5.2; 6.7.3; 6.7.6; 5.9.5
NoradrenalineArousal, alertness and alarmDepression; panic disorder; PTSDSerotonin-noradrenaline reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs); tricyclics5.5.2; 6.7.3
GABAThe main inhibitory transmitter, a brake on neural activityAnxiety disorders; alcohol withdrawalBenzodiazepines enhance its action; alcohol acts on it too6.7.3; Chapter 10
GlutamateThe main excitatory transmitter; reduced NMDA receptor function in schizophreniaSchizophrenia; depressionKetamine blocks NMDA receptors (psychosis-like effects; rapid antidepressant action)4.8.6; 5.7.8
AcetylcholineMemory and attentionAlzheimer's disease and other dementiasCholinesterase inhibitors, such as donepezilChapter 10

Most of these theories were born the same way: a drug that helped a disorder was found to act on a particular transmitter, and the disorder was then presumed to be caused by a fault in that transmitter. Antipsychotics block dopamine receptors, so schizophrenia must involve too much dopamine; antidepressants raise serotonin and noradrenaline, so depression must involve too little (Section 5.5.2 tells the monoamine story). Reasoning from what a treatment does to what caused the illness has a Latin name, ex juvantibus reasoning, and it is risky. A headache is not caused by a lack of aspirin; paracetamol relieves a fever without telling you whether the cause is malaria or influenza. The monoamine hypothesis of depression shows the danger: antidepressants change serotonin within hours but take weeks to help, and a 2022 umbrella review led by Joanna Moncrieff found no consistent evidence that depression is caused by low serotonin (Book 2, Section 1.7.2). The popular phrase "a chemical imbalance" claims far more than the evidence shows. A drug's effect tells you something about the brain systems that can relieve a disorder, not necessarily about the systems that caused it.

Hormones form the second strand. The body's main stress system is the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis: in response to stress the hypothalamus signals the pituitary gland, which signals the adrenal glands to release the stress hormone cortisol, and cortisol in turn feeds back on the brain, through glucocorticoid receptors like those Meaney studied, to switch the response off. Chronic stress and severe depression are often accompanied by an overactive axis (Book 1, Section 11.8.4; Section 5.5.3). The thyroid is the other gland every clinician must remember, because thyroid disease mimics psychiatric disorder: an underactive thyroid produces slowness, fatigue, low mood and weight gain that look like depression, and an overactive one produces tremor, palpitations and restlessness that look like anxiety. That is why a careful clinician checks thyroid function before diagnosing either (Sections 5.3.4 and 6.1.8).

Structure and function make the third strand, and here a sentence each is enough, with the full stories in their chapters. Many people with schizophrenia have enlarged lateral ventricles, the fluid-filled spaces of the brain, and reduced grey matter (Section 4.7.7). In the anxiety disorders, an overactive amygdala, the brain's alarm centre, appears to be poorly regulated by the prefrontal cortex (Section 6.7.4). In OCD, attention centres on a loop running from the orbitofrontal cortex through the striatum and thalamus and back, the cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical circuit (Section 6.7.6). In long-standing recurrent depression, the hippocampus, which is rich in glucocorticoid receptors and sensitive to prolonged stress, is often smaller (Sections 5.5.4 and 5.5.7). These findings come from four main imaging methods: computed tomography (CT), which builds pictures of brain structure from X-rays; magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), which gives far more detailed pictures of structure using magnetic fields; positron emission tomography (PET), which uses radioactive tracers to show metabolism or receptors at work; and functional MRI (fMRI), which tracks changes in blood oxygen as a sign of which regions are active during a task (the Assessment and Physiological Psychology books explain the methods).

One theory ties genes, the early environment and the brain together for schizophrenia. The neurodevelopmental model, proposed in 1987 by Daniel Weinberger and, independently, by Robin Murray and Shôn Lewis, holds that the disorder begins with a disturbance of brain development before or around birth, produced by risk genes, prenatal infection or malnutrition, or complications of delivery. The disturbance stays silent through childhood, apart from subtle differences, and declares itself only in adolescence or early adulthood, when the maturing brain, pruning its synapses and completing the wiring of the prefrontal cortex, comes to depend on the very circuits that were built wrongly (Section 4.7.6). It is a narrower theory than the diathesis-stress model, a theory of when and how the vulnerability arises, and the two are compatible (Section 1.3.4; Book 2, Table 1.16). It should not be confused with the neurodevelopmental disorders, such as autism and intellectual developmental disorder, which form their own DSM-5-TR chapter (Chapter 3). A newer strand of research looks at inflammation: a subset of people with depression show raised inflammatory markers in the blood (Section 5.5.8).

Finally, the warning that applies to every finding in this subsection: correlation is not cause. A brain difference found in people with a disorder may be a cause of the disorder, but it may equally be a consequence of the illness, of years of stress, poverty or poor sleep, of alcohol or cannabis, or of the medication used to treat it. The best studies examine people at their first episode, before treatment, or follow children at high risk before anyone falls ill, precisely to untangle these possibilities.

Exam trap

A drug's effect does not prove a disorder's cause (ex juvantibus reasoning): headache is not a lack of aspirin, and depression has not been shown to be a lack of serotonin. GABA is the main inhibitory transmitter (benzodiazepines), glutamate the main excitatory one (NMDA receptors, ketamine); antipsychotics block D2 dopamine receptors; acetylcholine is the transmitter of Alzheimer's disease (cholinesterase inhibitors). The neurodevelopmental model (Weinberger; Murray and Lewis, 1987) is a theory of schizophrenia, not the DSM chapter of neurodevelopmental disorders.

The environment: from the womb to the city

In psychiatric research "environment" means far more than upbringing. It means everything that is not written in the DNA sequence, and it begins before birth. This subsection sweeps through the environmental risks that recur in the disorder chapters, a sentence or three on each, so that you recognise them when they appear; each is taught in full where it matters most.

Before and around birth. For schizophrenia, DSM-5-TR names pregnancy and birth complications with hypoxia (a shortage of oxygen) and greater paternal age as risk factors, and links other prenatal and perinatal adversities, among them maternal stress, infection, malnutrition and maternal diabetes, with the disorder; then it adds at once that the vast majority of offspring with these risk factors do not develop schizophrenia. The Dutch Hunger Winter (Section 1.5.3) is the best-studied example of prenatal malnutrition. For autism spectrum disorder, DSM-5-TR lists advanced parental age, extreme prematurity and exposure in the womb to certain drugs, such as the anticonvulsant valproic acid. Alcohol drunk in pregnancy can damage the developing brain; DSM-5-TR proposes a "neurobehavioural disorder associated with prenatal alcohol exposure" as a condition for further study (Section 1.8.6; Chapter 3). Notice that every one of these influences is biological and every one is environmental.

Childhood adversity. The largest single demonstration of how early adversity echoes through adult life is the Adverse Childhood Experiences (ACE) study of Vincent Felitti, Robert Anda and their colleagues, published in 1998. They mailed a questionnaire to 13,494 adults who had had a standard medical check-up at Kaiser Permanente, a large health organisation in San Diego, and 9,508 replied. It asked about seven categories of adverse experience in childhood: psychological, physical or sexual abuse; violence against the mother; and living with household members who were substance abusers, mentally ill or suicidal, or ever imprisoned. More than half of the respondents reported at least one category. The striking finding was a graded, dose-response relationship: the more categories a person had experienced, the higher the risk of every adult health problem studied. People with four or more categories, compared with those with none, had four to twelve times the risk of alcoholism, drug abuse, depression and suicide attempts, and higher rates of heart disease, cancer, chronic lung disease and liver disease. The same adversity led to many different outcomes, which is multifinality (Section 1.3.3), and the adversities clustered: a child who suffered one usually suffered several.

Trauma, life events and chronic difficulties. Exposure to a traumatic event is a necessary cause of PTSD by definition (Section 1.3.3; Chapter 7). Severe life events, especially losses, and chronic difficulties such as a bad marriage or poverty precede many depressive episodes, as George Brown and Tirril Harris showed in their Camberwell study (Section 5.6.8; Book 1, Section 11.9.1, for Holmes and Rahe's life-event scale). Remember from Section 1.5.2 that many such events are partly produced by the person's own temperament.

Drugs. Heavy cannabis use in adolescence raises the risk of a later psychotic disorder, especially with high-potency forms and early, frequent use (Section 4.7.8). Alcohol and other drugs both cause and complicate many disorders (Chapter 10).

The city, migration and social defeat. People who are born or brought up in cities, and migrants and their children, have higher rates of schizophrenia than people raised in the countryside or in their own country of origin; one explanation, the social defeat hypothesis, proposes that the chronic experience of being an outsider and of being excluded sensitises the brain's stress and dopamine systems (Sections 4.6.3 and 4.9.6).

Poverty. Serious mental illness is commoner among the poor, and two explanations compete: social causation (the stresses of poverty cause illness) and social drift (illness drives people down the social ladder). Both operate, to different degrees in different disorders (Book 2, Section 1.7.7).

Gender. Women have about twice the rate of depression that men have, especially between menarche and menopause, and DSM-5-TR notes that women are disproportionately affected by major risk factors for depression across the lifespan, including interpersonal trauma; differences in rumination and in social roles add to the gap (Section 5.6.6).

Discrimination. DSM-5-TR states that there is evidence that racism can worsen many psychiatric disorders and contribute to poor outcomes, and that racial biases can affect diagnostic assessment. In India the same questions arise about caste, which DSM-5-TR lists among the aspects of cultural identity that a clinician should explore (Section 1.10.3).

Set against all these risks are the protective factors and the capacity for resilience described in Section 1.3.6: a stable relationship with at least one caring adult, an easy temperament, problem-solving ability and a sense of purpose. They are environmental too, and they are where much of prevention begins.

Clinical link

This list is also an assessment checklist. A complete history asks about the pregnancy and birth (complications, maternal alcohol or drug use), early development, adversity and abuse in childhood, trauma, substance use, migration, poverty, discrimination and the supports a person has. Asking about adverse childhood experiences is not prying: it identifies people at raised risk, explains symptoms that otherwise seem to come from nowhere, and guides treatment, since the same depression may need a different plan in someone who was abused as a child (Book 2, Section 2.3, on the case history).

How biological is a disorder?

It is often said that bipolar disorder and schizophrenia are "biological illnesses", with little or no psychological or social cause, whereas a disorder such as bulimia nervosa is "psychological" or "cultural". After Sections 1.4 and 1.5 you can test that claim properly. Part of it is right, and part of it is wrong, and the way it is wrong is the most important idea in this chapter.

Start with what is right. Bipolar disorder and schizophrenia are the two most heritable of the major adult disorders in Table 1.12: around 90 percent in some twin studies of bipolar disorder and about 80 percent for schizophrenia. DSM-5-TR says plainly that genetic processes "strongly affect predisposition to bipolar disorder". Both disorders respond more clearly to medication than to any other treatment: lithium and the other mood stabilisers prevent relapses of bipolar disorder (Section 5.7.5), antipsychotic drugs reduce the positive symptoms of schizophrenia and prevent relapse (Section 4.10), and in both disorders psychological treatment alone is not enough. Brain differences are well documented, especially in schizophrenia (Section 4.7.7). Bulimia nervosa looks different. DSM-5-TR lists the internalisation of a thin body ideal as a risk factor, and childhood sexual or physical abuse as another; binge eating frequently begins during or after an episode of dieting to lose weight; and the disorder is closely tied to the cultural pressure on young women to be thin (Chapter 8). On a forced choice between these three, the first two are the disorders with the strongest evidence of biological causation.

Now look at what is wrong, and begin with the bulimia comparison. Bulimia nervosa is not "non-biological" either: both twins are affected in 23 percent of identical pairs but only 9 percent of fraternal pairs, and the consensus of twin studies puts genes at about half of the causal equation for the eating disorders. The difference between the disorders is one of degree, not of kind.

More important is what is wrong about bipolar disorder and schizophrenia. The claim that there is "little to no" evidence of psychosocial causation is simply false. Consider bipolar disorder first. If genes were everything, an identical twin of a person with bipolar disorder would always have it; in fact monozygotic concordance is only 40 to 70 percent, which, in DSM-5-TR's words, indicates that "much risk is left unexplained by genes alone". The timing of episodes is strongly influenced by events. Sleep loss can precipitate mania; so can life events, and so, remarkably, can success: in Sheri Johnson's studies, goal attainment events, such as a promotion, an acceptance letter or falling in love, were followed by rises in manic symptoms (Section 5.6.9). Rakesh's brother is a case in point. Looking back, the family recalled that his manic episode had begun after a month in which he slept four hours a night, working late to set up the business, and his psychiatrist's plan for preventing the next one combines lithium with a regular sleep routine and a family that knows the warning signs.

Schizophrenia tells the same story. Identical twins of people with schizophrenia develop it only about 33 to 48 percent of the time (Section 4.7.2). In the families of people who have recovered from an episode, high expressed emotion, a climate of criticism, hostility and emotional over-involvement, predicts relapse, and family interventions that reduce it lower relapse rates (Section 4.9.4; Book 2, Sections 7.2.4 and 7.2.5). Being conceived during a famine doubled the risk (Section 1.5.3). Growing up in a city, migrating, heavy adolescent cannabis use and childhood adversity all raise it (Sections 4.6.3, 4.7.8 and 4.9.6). Tienari's Finnish adoptees at high genetic risk developed schizophrenia-spectrum disorders mainly when they were raised in disturbed families (Section 1.5.1). Every one of those findings is psychosocial or environmental evidence about the most "biological" disorder in the book.

The resolution is the one this section has been building towards. Genes set the diathesis; the environment, from the womb onwards, decides whether the diathesis is expressed, when the illness begins, how severe it is and how often it returns. The two kinds of cause are not rivals competing for a fixed share of the explanation, because they act through each other: genes alter sensitivity to experience (interaction), shape the experiences a person meets (correlation), and are themselves switched on and off by experience (epigenetics). Asking whether a disorder is "biological or psychosocial" is a little like asking whether the area of a rectangle comes from its length or its width.

Remember

Four clarifications to carry into every chapter. Biological is not the same as genetic: prenatal infection, famine, a head injury and heavy cannabis use are biological and environmental at once. Genetic is not the same as unchangeable: phenylketonuria is almost entirely genetic and almost entirely preventable by diet (Book 1, Section 9.7.3). A drug response does not prove a biological cause: headache is not a lack of aspirin (Section 1.5.4). Psychological causes and treatments act through the brain too: in Lewis Baxter's 1992 study, people with OCD who improved with behaviour therapy showed the same change in the activity of the caudate nucleus as those who improved with fluoxetine (Section 6.7.6).

Exam trap

Asked (RML 2020) which illness has "considerable evidence that it is caused by biological factors and little to no evidence that it is caused by psychosocial factors", with the options bipolar disorder, bulimia nervosa, schizophrenia, and "(a) and (c)", choose (a) and (c): bipolar disorder and schizophrenia have the highest heritabilities and the clearest response to drugs, while bulimia nervosa is the most strongly shaped by culture and dieting. But know that the premise is false. Identical-twin concordance is only 40 to 70 percent for bipolar disorder and about 33 to 48 percent for schizophrenia; sleep loss, life events and goal attainment trigger manic episodes; expressed emotion, prenatal famine, urban upbringing, migration, cannabis and childhood adversity all affect schizophrenia; and bulimia nervosa is itself about half heritable. Genes set the diathesis; the environment times and shapes the illness.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

1.6 Approaches to Classification: Syndromes, Categories and Diagnostic ErrorLower yield · 2 Qs

Spend one Monday morning in the psychiatry outpatient department of a large government hospital and you will see the problem this section is about. Before lunch a single junior doctor may see forty new patients: a farmer's wife who has stopped eating and cannot stop crying, a college student who hears his dead father's voice, a shopkeeper who washes his hands fifty times a day, a retired teacher who keeps getting lost on the way home from the market, a young man who has not slept for four nights and has spent his family's savings on a car he cannot drive. To help any of them she must do three things quickly. She must decide what kind of problem each person has, she must write it down in words that the next doctor, the pharmacist and the hospital's records office will all understand, and she must choose a treatment that has worked for other people with the same kind of problem. All three depend on one act: sorting the people in front of her into groups.

Sections 1.1 to 1.5 asked what a disorder is and what causes one. This section asks how disorders should be sorted. Should they be grouped by what you can see and hear, or by what caused them? Should a person be put in a box or placed on a scale? When two diagnoses seem to fit, which one wins? How can you tell whether a way of sorting is any good, and what happens when it sorts a person wrongly? Finally, what does the label cost the person who receives it? Sections 1.7 to 1.9 then follow the two great classifications in use today, the American Psychiatric Association's DSM and the World Health Organization's ICD, edition by edition.

Why classify

Consider Harish, a 35-year-old bank clerk from Lucknow. In ten years he has been admitted to hospital three times, each time after a week or two of racing thoughts, sleeplessness, grand plans and reckless spending, and each admission was followed by months of heavy, slowed-down depression. His old discharge slips describe him in three different ways: "nervous exhaustion", "acute psychosis" and "depression with excitement". Each phrase captures something, but none of them tells a new doctor what to expect next, which medicine has the best evidence, or which research applies to him, and none could be counted by the hospital's records office. Written instead as "bipolar I disorder", the same history tells every reader that further episodes are likely, that a mood stabiliser such as lithium is the usual first choice (Section 5.7.5), and that a large body of research on people like him is relevant. That is what classification buys.

The vocabulary is worth having straight. A nosology (from the Greek nosos, disease) is a classification of diseases or disorders; its nomenclature is its agreed list of names; taxonomy is the general science of classifying anything, from beetles to rocks, and the word is borrowed from biology. A diagnosis is the act of deciding which category of a nosology a particular person's problems belong to, and also the name that results. Clinicians classify for six overlapping reasons: to communicate, to predict the course, to choose a treatment, to make research possible, to run health services (records, insurance, certificates and the courts all work through diagnostic codes), and, in the long run, to understand causes, because grouping people who share a pattern is the first step towards finding out why they share it. Book 2, Section 2.10.1 takes each purpose in turn.

Two cautions belong at the very start. First, a diagnosis classifies a problem, not a person. Harish has bipolar disorder; he is not "a bipolar", any more than a man with diabetes is "a diabetic" in any sense that matters about him. Writing and speaking this way is called person-first language, and it is now the expected style in case notes, reports and research papers. Second, the DSM and the ICD are not tests, therapies or rating scales. They are classification and diagnostic systems: nosologies that list the disorders, define them and give each a code. That single fact is the whole answer to a recurring easy question that Section 1.9.7 takes up when it compares the two systems.

Early classifications: from Hippocrates to Kraepelin

The urge to sort madness into kinds is as old as medicine. The Greek physician Hippocrates (about 460 to 377 BCE) divided mental disorders into three broad groups: mania, states of excitement and overactivity; melancholia, states of sadness and despondency; and phrenitis, an acute feverish confusion that we would now call delirium. The word hysteria, from the Greek for womb, also comes from the Hippocratic tradition, along with the mistaken idea that a wandering womb caused the symptoms (Section 7.1.1 follows the word through history). Notice what kind of scheme this was. Hippocrates explained disorder by the balance of the four humours, but he sorted his patients by what they looked like. Book 2, Section 1.2.2 tells his story.

India has its own early nosology. The Charaka Samhita, the classical Ayurvedic text, describes five types of unmada, the Ayurvedic term for insanity: three arising from a disturbance of a single dosha (vataja, pittaja and kaphaja), a fourth from the disturbance of all three together (sannipatika), and a fifth, agantuja, from causes that come from outside the body, among which the classical texts include possessing spirits. This scheme is interesting for a reason that the next subsection will make clear: it sorts disorders by their supposed cause, not by their appearance. It is an aetiological classification two thousand years before the word existed (Book 2, Section 1.2.3).

The modern story begins in the asylums of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, where for the first time large numbers of disturbed people lived under medical observation for years. The French physician Philippe Pinel (1745 to 1826), remembered above all for striking the chains off patients in Paris (Book 2, Section 1.3.1), also simplified the bewildering lists of his predecessors. In his treatise on mental alienation of 1801 he recognised only a handful of kinds: melancholia, mania (with and without delirium), dementia (in his sense, a breakdown of coherent thinking, not the memory loss of old age) and idiocy. In Germany, Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899) described catatonia in 1874 and his younger colleague Ewald Hecker (1843 to 1909) described hebephrenia in 1871, and Kahlbaum insisted that a disease should be recognised by its whole course and not by a snapshot of its symptoms (Section 4.1.2).

That insistence found its master in Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926). The asylum doctors of his day noticed that the same symptoms, excitement or delusions or despair, appeared in patients whose futures turned out to be completely different. Kraepelin therefore classified descriptively, by the pattern of symptoms, but above all by course and outcome: how the illness began, how it unfolded over the years and how it ended. Following his patients for years, he drew the great line of modern psychiatry. On one side he placed dementia praecox, an illness beginning early in life and moving towards lasting deterioration; on the other, manic-depressive insanity, an illness of episodes from which the patient recovered in between. By the sixth edition of his textbook (1899) the division was complete. The split of the major psychoses into these two families is called the Kraepelinian dichotomy, and it still shapes the DSM and the ICD, which keep schizophrenia and bipolar disorder in separate chapters. Kraepelin also believed that each disease he described would turn out to have its own brain pathology, as general paresis had turned out to have syphilis; the very name dementia praecox (early dementia) carried that belief. His story, including the way he worked, is told in Book 2, Section 1.3.7 and in Section 4.1.3 of this book; dementia praecox was renamed schizophrenia by Eugen Bleuler in 1908 (Section 4.1.4).

Kraepelin's ideas had a second life in the United States half a century after his death. In 1978 the American psychiatrist Gerald L. Klerman (1928 to 1992) coined the term neo-Kraepelinian for a group of psychiatrists, centred on Washington University in St Louis, who held that psychiatry is a branch of medicine, that mental disorders are discrete conditions rather than points on a vague continuum of maladjustment, and that diagnosis should be made by explicit, testable rules. Their research criteria became the backbone of DSM-III in 1980 (Section 1.7.3).

Exam trap

Kraepelin is the name for classification by course and outcome and for the split between dementia praecox and manic-depressive insanity. Pinel is the name for humane reform (and a simple four-part scheme); Bleuler is the name for the word schizophrenia (1908). Dementia praecox is schizophrenia, not Alzheimer's disease or any dementia of old age, even though the word "dementia" is in it. Hippocrates' phrenitis was a feverish delirium, not schizophrenia. And Charaka's five types of unmada are classified by cause (the doshas and external agents), which makes them an early aetiological scheme.

Syndromal and aetiological approaches

Neelam is a 42-year-old schoolteacher in Pune. For four months she has felt low and heavy, has lost interest in her pupils, sleeps ten hours and wakes unrefreshed, has gained six kilograms, and cannot follow the plot of a television serial. Described by what anyone can observe and what she reports, this is a depressive syndrome, and it would comfortably meet the criteria for a major depressive episode. Then a routine blood test comes back: her thyroid gland is badly underactive. Now the same picture has a cause, and the cause changes everything. Her diagnosis becomes a depressive disorder due to another medical condition (hypothyroidism), and the first treatment is not an antidepressant but thyroid hormone. Neelam's case contains the whole argument of this subsection in miniature, because there are two fundamentally different ways of deciding what to call her.

The first is the syndromal approach, also called the descriptive approach. A syndrome (from the Greek for "running together") is a cluster of signs and symptoms that tend to occur together and to follow a recognisable course (Section 1.2.2 defines symptom, sign and syndrome). The syndromal approach defines each category by such a cluster, whatever its cause: if the right symptoms are present for the right length of time and cause enough distress or impairment, the category applies. The second is the aetiological approach: categories are defined by their cause (their aetiology), so that everyone whose illness has the same cause belongs to the same category, whatever their symptoms look like.

For the rest of medicine the aetiological approach is the ideal, and two stories show why. The first is general paresis (Book 2, Section 1.3.6). Asylum doctors first recognised it in the 1820s as a syndrome: grandiose delusions, a slow collapse of intellect, slurred speech and paralysis, then death within a few years. Once the syphilis spirochaete was found in the brains of paretic patients in 1913, the category was redefined by its cause. Today neurosyphilis is diagnosed by blood and spinal-fluid tests, includes patients with no grandiosity at all, and is treated with penicillin. The second is tuberculosis. For centuries "consumption" was a syndrome: cough, fever, night sweats and wasting. After the German physician Robert Koch (1843 to 1910) identified the tubercle bacillus in 1882, tuberculosis became a cause-defined disease. The new category lumped together illnesses that look nothing like consumption, such as tuberculosis of the spine, the lymph glands and the brain, and it split off coughing, wasting illnesses with other causes, such as lung cancer. That is the power of an aetiological classification: it sorts by what actually happened in the body, so it predicts course and points straight to treatment.

The two labels you are most likely to meet for these approaches are "syndromal" and "historical". The second is not a term the DSM or ICD use, and it is worth understanding rather than memorising. In some Indian study materials and mock papers the cause-based approach is called the historical approach. They do not explain the label, but the most sensible reading is that the cause-based approach is the older ideal: it is how Charaka sorted unmada, how nineteenth-century psychiatrists hoped to sort every form of madness once general paresis had shown the way, and how medicine still sorts diseases whenever their cause is known. Read "historical" in this context as "the older, aetiological, cause-based approach", and the contrast is simple: the syndromal approach rests on clinical signs and symptoms, the historical approach on known aetiology.

Why, then, are the DSM and ICD mainly syndromal? Because for most mental disorders the ideal is out of reach. Sections 1.3 to 1.5 showed that the causes of most disorders are unknown, or are many small causes acting together (dozens or hundreds of genes of small effect, early adversity, stress, the city, the family) with none of them necessary or sufficient. The same cause can lead to several different disorders: childhood maltreatment raises the risk of depression, posttraumatic stress disorder, substance use disorders and personality disorders. And the same disorder can be reached by different routes in different people. A classification built on causes would have to be built on theories of cause, and history shows what happens then. The "neuroses" of DSM-I and DSM-II were defined partly by a psychoanalytic theory of unconscious conflict, so clinicians who did not share the theory could not apply the categories consistently. DSM-III's answer in 1980 was to describe disorders by what can be observed and reported and to stay as neutral as possible about cause (Section 1.7.3). ICD-10 took the same descriptive line.

Aetiology has not vanished, however. It returns wherever a cause is secure enough to rely on, so the modern manuals are really hybrids: syndromal at their core, aetiological at their edges. You will meet the aetiological edges in five places.

  • Disorders due to another medical condition. Neelam's diagnosis is one; anxiety disorder due to hyperthyroidism and psychotic disorder due to another medical condition are others. The cause is part of the name.
