  • Substance/medication-induced disorders. An alcohol-induced depressive disorder or a psychotic disorder induced by a stimulant is defined by its cause (Chapter 10).
  • The organic block of ICD-10 and the secondary syndromes of ICD-11. ICD-10 groups delirium, dementia and other conditions with a demonstrable brain cause in its first block, F00 to F09; ICD-11 has a grouping of secondary mental or behavioural syndromes caused by a medical condition (Sections 1.9.4 and 1.9.5).
  • The trauma- and stressor-related disorders. Posttraumatic stress disorder cannot be diagnosed without exposure to a traumatic event, and adjustment disorder requires an identifiable stressor (Sections 7.2 and 7.4). Even here the event is necessary but not sufficient: most people exposed to trauma never develop the disorder.
  • The neurocognitive disorders. Major and mild neurocognitive disorder are first diagnosed as syndromes and then subtyped by aetiology: due to Alzheimer's disease, vascular, with Lewy bodies, due to traumatic brain injury, and so on (Chapter 10).

Three older divisions of disorder belong to the same debate and are worth one sentence each, because the terms are still in common use. The division between organic disorders (caused by a demonstrable physical disease of the brain or body) and functional disorders (no such cause found, also called "psychogenic") was used in the DSM up to DSM-III-R; DSM-IV dropped it in 1994 because it wrongly implied that "functional" disorders have no biology and that "organic" ones have no psychology, and replaced it with "due to a general medical condition", "substance-induced" and "primary" (DSM-5 later changed "primary" to "independent"). The division between psychosis and neurosis is taught in Section 6.1.3. The division of depression into endogenous (arising from within) and reactive (a response to events) is taught in Section 5.1.5. Table 1.16 brings the two main approaches together.

Table 1.16 The syndromal and aetiological approaches to classification compared.
FeatureSyndromal (descriptive) approachAetiological ("historical") approach
Basis of a categoryA cluster of signs and symptoms that occur together, with a typical courseA known cause, whatever the symptoms look like
Classic exampleMajor depressive episode; schizophrenia; generalised anxiety disorderNeurosyphilis (general paresis); tuberculosis; Down syndrome
Main strengthUsable now, without waiting for causes; can be made reliable with explicit criteriaPredicts course and points straight to treatment and prevention
Main weaknessOne category may hide several causes; boundaries between categories are blurredImpossible where causes are unknown or many; a theory of cause can be wrong
Where it appears todayMost of DSM-5-TR and ICD-10 and ICD-11Disorders due to another medical condition, substance-induced disorders, ICD-10 F00 to F09, ICD-11 secondary syndromes, PTSD's stressor, neurocognitive subtypes
Exam trap

The syndromal approach is based on clinical signs and symptoms; the historical (aetiological) approach is based on known aetiology. Option sets love to swap the two, so read every word. "The syndromal approach is not used in psychiatry" is the reverse of the truth: it is the approach the DSM and ICD mainly use. And neither approach belongs only to medical or only to psychiatric illness: medicine uses both (migraine and irritable bowel syndrome are still syndromal categories), and so does psychiatry (Neelam's depressive disorder due to hypothyroidism is aetiological). Finally, "organic versus functional" was dropped by DSM-IV, not by DSM-5.

Categories, dimensions and prototypes

Sorting by symptoms still leaves a second choice: boxes or scales. In categorical classification a person either has a disorder or does not, as a woman either is or is not pregnant. In its classical form, which goes back to Kraepelin, every member of a category shares the same defining features and, it is hoped, the same cause. In dimensional classification each person is placed on one or more continuous scales, as blood pressure and height are measured, so that a patient might be described as very anxious, moderately low in mood and only slightly suspicious. Between them sits prototypical classification, which is what the DSM and ICD actually do: a category is described by a typical case, and a person qualifies by matching enough of its features. Criteria of the "any five of these nine" kind are called polythetic criteria; criteria that require every listed feature are called monothetic criteria. The standard example is the major depressive episode, which needs at least five of nine symptoms over the same two weeks, one of them depressed mood or loss of interest. The consequence is startling when you first work it out. One man may have depressed mood, weight loss, broken sleep, slowed movements and exhaustion; another may have depressed mood, loss of interest, crushing guilt, poor concentration and thoughts of death. They share a single criterion, yet both have the same diagnosis. Even within one criterion the features can be opposites, since "insomnia or hypersomnia" and "weight loss or gain" each count once. Book 2, Section 2.10.2 compares the three approaches in its Table 2.33, and Book 1, Section 8.2.3 explains the psychology of prototypes.

The categorical approach dominates because clinicians must make yes-or-no decisions (treat or not, admit or not, certify or not) and because names are easier to communicate than profiles of numbers. But DSM-5-TR is unusually frank about the costs. In the chapter that introduces its dimensional measures it lists six problems with categorical diagnosis, and each deserves a sentence of explanation.

  1. No zones of rarity. If disorders were natural kinds, there should be gaps, "zones of rarity", between them and between each disorder and health, where few people fall, as there are between measles and chickenpox. The British psychiatrist Robert Evan Kendell (1935 to 2002) made this the test of a valid category. For most mental disorders the gaps are not there: depression shades into ordinary sadness and into generalised anxiety.
  2. The need for intermediate categories. Schizoaffective disorder exists because many patients fall between schizophrenia and bipolar disorder (Section 4.5.3); a classification that needs bridges between its boxes is admitting that the boxes overlap.
  3. High rates of comorbidity. Many people meet the criteria for two, three or more disorders at once (Section 1.2.7), which is what you would expect if the categories were slices of a few underlying dimensions.
  4. Frequent use of "other specified" and "unspecified" diagnoses. Large numbers of patients fit no category well and end up in the residual ones (Section 1.8.4).
  5. Few unique antecedent validators. The categories have not led to distinctive causes. Genes, brain findings and childhood risks turn out to be shared across many disorders (Section 1.4.6).
  6. Lack of treatment specificity. The same treatments work across categories: a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor helps depression, panic disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder, generalised anxiety disorder and bulimia nervosa.

DSM-5 (2013) therefore added dimensional elements to a categorical frame. Some categories became spectrum disorders, the clearest case being autism spectrum disorder, which absorbed several separate DSM-IV diagnoses (Section 1.7.6), and the schizophrenia spectrum chapter, which runs from schizotypal personality disorder to schizophrenia. Many disorders gained severity ratings (mild, moderate, severe), and Section III added cross-cutting symptom measures that rate problems such as anxiety, depression and poor sleep in every patient whatever the diagnosis (Section 1.8.5), and an alternative, trait-based model of personality disorder (Chapter 11). The core, however, is still a list of categories.

Is that core real? Can one ever find out whether a condition is truly a category or truly a dimension? The American psychologist Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003), whose theory of schizotaxia you met in Section 1.3.4, believed that one could, and with his colleagues he developed a family of statistical procedures called taxometrics. A taxon is a natural category: a group whose members really differ in kind from everyone else, so that their scores on several indicators are related within the whole sample only because the sample is a mixture of two latent groups. Taxometric procedures (with names such as MAXCOV and MAMBAC) look for the statistical fingerprints of such a hidden mixture. The results, pooled by the Melbourne psychologist Nick Haslam and his colleagues, are clear. A 2012 review of 177 articles and 311 findings estimated that only about 14 percent of findings were truly taxonic once weaker methods were allowed for, with little convincing evidence of taxa for mood, anxiety, eating or externalising problems or for most personality disorders, and more promising evidence for schizotypy, substance use disorders and autism. A 2020 meta-analysis of 317 findings found dimensional results outnumbering taxonic ones by about five to one, with alcohol use disorder, intermittent explosive disorder, problem gambling, autism, suicide risk and paedophilia as the most plausible candidate taxa. For most of psychopathology, in other words, the categories are cut-points placed on continua, which is exactly what Section 1.1.5 argued about the line between normal and abnormal.

If the underlying structure is dimensional, what are the dimensions? Two research programmes have tried to answer from the data upwards rather than from expert committees downwards. The Hierarchical Taxonomy of Psychopathology (HiTOP), set out by Roman Kotov and a large consortium of colleagues in 2017, groups individual signs and symptoms into narrow components and traits, these into syndromes, syndromes into subfactors, subfactors into broad spectra and spectra into superspectra, all according to which problems actually co-occur. Its six spectra so far are internalising, thought disorder, disinhibited externalising, antagonistic externalising, detachment and somatoform. In HiTOP, comorbidity stops being a nuisance and becomes information: depression and generalised anxiety co-occur because they are neighbouring parts of the internalising spectrum. At the very top of such a hierarchy sits the p factor. In 2014 Avshalom Caspi and his colleagues analysed 20 years of mental health data, from adolescence to midlife, in the Dunedin cohort in New Zealand. Three broad factors (internalising, externalising and thought disorder) explained the pattern of disorders well, but a single general factor of psychopathology explained it even better. They named it p, by analogy with g, the general factor of intelligence, and found that people higher on p had more impairment in life, more affected relatives, harder childhoods and more signs of compromised brain function early in life. Neither HiTOP nor the p factor is an official manual; they are research frameworks, like the Research Domain Criteria (RDoC) of the United States National Institute of Mental Health, which studies dimensions of functioning from genes and circuits up to behaviour (Book 2, Section 2.10.6).

Exam trap

DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR are categorical systems with polythetic (prototypical) criteria and some dimensional additions (spectra, severity ratings, cross-cutting measures, the Section III personality model). They are not dimensional systems. "Five of nine symptoms" is polythetic; "all of the following" is monothetic. A taxon is a natural category, and taxometrics (Meehl) tests for one; most findings favour dimensions. The p factor is Caspi and colleagues (2014), named by analogy with Spearman's g; HiTOP is Kotov and colleagues (2017); RDoC belongs to the US National Institute of Mental Health. None of the three is a diagnostic manual.

Hierarchy, exclusion and the "better explained" rule

Tanvir is a 30-year-old tailor from Bhopal with a ten-year history of schizophrenia. During his present relapse he hears voices commenting on him and is sure that his neighbours are poisoning his water. He is also tense and fearful most of the day, sleeps badly, has lost interest in his work and says he feels "finished". Counted mechanically against the criteria, his symptoms might seem to justify four diagnoses: schizophrenia, a generalised anxiety disorder, insomnia disorder and a depressive episode. No experienced clinician would write all four. The anxiety, the sleeplessness and the low mood are understood as part of the psychotic relapse, and they are expected to lift as the psychosis is treated. The rule being applied here is one of the oldest in clinical psychiatry.

The diagnostic hierarchy arranges disorders in an order of precedence. In its classical form the order is: organic states (brain disease, delirium, dementia, intoxication) at the top; then the psychoses such as schizophrenia; then the mood (affective) disorders; then the neurotic disorders, such as anxiety; and personality disorders at the bottom. A condition higher in the order can produce the symptoms of conditions below it, but not the other way round. A brain tumour or a delirium can produce delusions, low mood and anxiety; schizophrenia can produce low mood and anxiety; depression can produce anxiety; but an anxiety disorder does not produce delusions. So when a higher condition is present, it is diagnosed, and the lower symptoms are treated as part of it rather than given names of their own. There is a practical logic as well. The higher conditions are usually the more urgent and the more specifically treatable, so they must be looked for first: missing a brain tumour in a man with "anxiety" costs far more than missing his anxiety. The principle is usually traced to Karl Jaspers (1883 to 1969), whose General Psychopathology (1913) you will meet in Chapter 2. In Britain the psychologist Graham Foulds tested it with questionnaires. The Delusions-Symptoms-States Inventory, which he devised with his colleague Bedford, lists his levels in its very name, from delusions at the top, through neurotic symptoms, down to states of anxiety and depression at the bottom. Foulds and Bedford (1975) reported that the symptoms of about 93 percent of 480 psychiatric patients fitted the predicted pattern: people with symptoms at a higher level nearly always had symptoms at every lower level too. Foulds set out the full model in The Hierarchical Nature of Personal Illness (1976).

DSM-III (1980) built the hierarchy into its criteria as exclusion criteria: rules stating that a diagnosis may not be made if the symptoms are due to, or occur only during, another disorder. Many anxiety diagnoses, for example, could not be made if the anxiety arose during a depressive episode or during schizophrenia. The rules soon met an awkward fact.

Key study: Do excluded disorders really belong to the dominant ones? (Boyd and colleagues, 1984)

The question was whether DSM-III's exclusion rules matched the way symptoms actually cluster in the population. Boyd and his colleagues used the National Institute of Mental Health's Diagnostic Interview Schedule with a community sample of 11,519 adults in the United States and scored every syndrome without applying the exclusion rules. They found that when two disorders were linked by an exclusion rule, having the "dominant" one greatly raised the odds of also having the "excluded" one, as the hierarchy assumed. But they also found a broad tendency for disorders to co-occur in general: having almost any disorder raised the odds of having almost any other, including pairs that DSM-III treated as unrelated. The authors concluded that the assumptions behind the hierarchy should be tested empirically rather than taken on trust. A limitation is that a community survey using a structured interview given by lay interviewers may count mild syndromes that a clinician would not; even so, studies like this one helped persuade the next revision to loosen the rules.

DSM-III-R (1987) did loosen them. Many of the hierarchical exclusions were removed, so that, for example, panic disorder and major depression could now be diagnosed together when each met its own criteria. One consequence was a boom in recorded comorbidity, and with it the research on comorbidity described in Section 1.2.7. Some exclusions survived into DSM-IV: generalised anxiety disorder, for instance, could still not be diagnosed if it occurred only during a mood disorder.

Today's criteria carry the hierarchy in two standard clauses, and once you know them you will see them at the end of nearly every criteria set in Chapters 3 to 11.

  • "Not attributable to the physiological effects of a substance or another medical condition." This is the old organic tier at the top of the hierarchy. The generalised anxiety disorder criteria even name an example, hyperthyroidism, because an overactive thyroid can produce tremor, palpitations, restlessness and worry that look exactly like an anxiety disorder.
  • "Not better explained by another mental disorder." This replaces many of DSM-III's rigid rules with a clinical judgement. Worry that is only about having panic attacks is better explained by panic disorder (Section 6.2.3); worry that is only about contamination is better explained by obsessive-compulsive disorder; and schizophrenia is diagnosed only after schizoaffective disorder and depressive or bipolar disorder with psychotic features have been ruled out (Section 4.4.2).

Notice what the modern rule does and does not forbid. It forbids giving a separate name to symptoms that are simply part of another disorder, as with Tanvir's anxiety during his relapse. It does not forbid two diagnoses when each disorder is independently present. If Tanvir had also had, for years and between his psychotic episodes, intrusive fears of contamination with hours of washing, he could receive a diagnosis of obsessive-compulsive disorder as well, because his schizophrenia would not explain it.

The same logic decides the order in which a clinician works through the possibilities, a process called differential diagnosis. The American psychiatrist Michael B. First, the editor of DSM-5-TR, sets it out in six steps: first rule out feigning (malingering or factitious disorder); then rule out a substance or medication as the cause; then rule out another medical condition; only then decide which primary mental disorder or disorders are present; then decide whether an adjustment disorder or an "other specified" or "unspecified" category fits better; and finally decide whether the problem crosses the line into disorder at all (Book 2, Section 2.9.8 walks a case through the steps). The hierarchy and First's steps express one idea: look first for the causes that are most dangerous to miss and most specifically treatable. Neelam's thyroid test in Section 1.6.3 is step three in action.

Exam trap

The classical hierarchy runs organic, then psychotic, then mood, then neurotic, then personality: a higher condition explains the symptoms of a lower one, never the reverse. DSM-III (1980) enforced it with exclusion criteria; DSM-III-R (1987) relaxed many of them, which is one reason comorbidity rose. Today's two clauses are "not attributable to a substance or another medical condition" (the organic tier) and "not better explained by another mental disorder". The hierarchy does not forbid comorbidity when each disorder is independently present. The definition of comorbidity itself is in Section 1.2.7.

Reliability and validity

How do you judge whether a way of sorting people is any good? Two questions do most of the work. The first is whether different clinicians, using the same system on the same person, reach the same answer. That is the reliability of a diagnosis. It is tested in two main ways. One is inter-rater reliability: agreement between two or more clinicians who judge the same information, for example two psychologists who each rate the same recorded interview without consulting each other. The other is test-retest reliability: agreement between two separate assessments of the same person a short time apart, usually by different clinicians; it is the harder test, because the patient may tell a different story the second time. Book 2, Section 2.1.3 tells how badly psychiatry did on this test in the 1950s and 1960s: Aaron T. Beck (1921 to 2021) and his colleagues found in 1962 that pairs of experienced psychiatrists agreed on a specific diagnosis only about half the time, the US-UK Diagnostic Project showed that New York and London psychiatrists used "schizophrenia" for different patients, and the largest source of disagreement turned out to be criterion variance, clinicians applying different rules to the same information. Section 1.7.2 shows how that crisis drove the making of DSM-III.

Agreement is measured with Cohen's kappa, introduced by the American psychologist Jacob Cohen (1923 to 1998) in 1960. Raw percentage agreement flatters clinicians, because some agreement happens by chance alone, so kappa asks how far the observed agreement rises above chance: kappa = (observed agreement minus chance agreement) divided by (1 minus chance agreement). A kappa of 1 means perfect agreement and 0 means agreement no better than chance. Because chance agreement depends on how common the diagnosis is, kappa is sensitive to the base rate of the condition in the sample. Book 2, Section 2.10.5 works through a calculation step by step and gives the conventions for what counts as a good kappa. The headline from the DSM-5 field trials, which used a demanding design in which two clinicians interviewed each patient separately, is worth remembering: kappa was about 0.67 for posttraumatic stress disorder and 0.46 for schizophrenia, but only 0.28 for major depressive disorder and 0.20 for generalised anxiety disorder (Darrel Regier and colleagues, 2013). Two of the most common diagnoses in everyday practice were among the least reliable.

The second question is whether the category is real and useful: does it pick out a condition with its own features, causes, course and response to treatment? That is diagnostic validity. In 1970 Eli Robins and Samuel B. Guze of Washington University proposed that a diagnosis is validated in five phases: clinical description, laboratory studies, delimitation from other disorders, follow-up studies and family studies. In 1980 Kenneth S. Kendler sorted the evidence by timing into antecedent validators (family history, premorbid personality, precipitating factors), concurrent validators (psychological test results, biological markers) and predictive validators (stability of the diagnosis, course, response to treatment). The two qualities are linked in one direction only. Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity. A diagnosis that clinicians cannot agree on cannot be valid, because it does not consistently pick out anything; but perfect agreement guarantees nothing. Two record clerks who sorted every patient by month of birth would agree perfectly, and their twelve categories would predict nothing about anyone's illness.

By these standards most psychiatric categories are only partly validated, which led Kendell and Assen Jablensky in 2003 to urge a distinction between validity and clinical utility, the usefulness of a category for communication, prediction and choosing treatment. Many DSM and ICD categories, they argued, have not been shown to be distinct natural entities separated by zones of rarity, yet they are very useful; the danger lies only in forgetting the difference and treating a useful name as a discovered thing. Book 2, Section 2.10.6 develops the argument.

Exam trap

Reliability asks "do we agree?"; validity asks "is what we agree on real and meaningful?". A diagnosis can be reliable but invalid, never valid but unreliable. Kappa corrects agreement for chance (Cohen, 1960); 0 means chance-level agreement, not "no diagnoses made". The five phases of validation are Robins and Guze (1970); antecedent, concurrent and predictive validators are Kendler (1980); validity versus utility is Kendell and Jablensky (2003). Low reliability is a different problem from a high false-positive rate, which the next subsection explains.

When diagnosis goes wrong: false positives and false negatives

Reliability tells you whether clinicians agree with each other. It does not tell you whether they are right. To ask that, you need a standard of truth: some better way of knowing who really has the disorder, such as a careful interview by an experienced clinician, a structured research interview, or the course of the illness over the following years. Once you have such a standard, every diagnostic decision can be placed in one of four boxes, because the person either has the disorder or does not, and the clinician (or the questionnaire, or the screening test) either says so or does not.

  • A true positive is a person who has the disorder and is diagnosed with it: a case correctly found.
  • A false positive is a person who does not have the disorder but is diagnosed with it anyway: a well person labelled disordered. This is over-diagnosis.
  • A false negative is a person who has the disorder but is not diagnosed: a real case missed. This is under-diagnosis.
  • A true negative is a person without the disorder who is correctly cleared.

Two summary figures describe how good a test or a set of criteria is, and each is read down one column of the table in Figure 1.10. The first, sensitivity, is the proportion of people who truly have the disorder whom the test correctly identifies: true positives divided by everyone with the disorder. A sensitive test misses few cases. The second, specificity, is the proportion of people who truly do not have the disorder whom the test correctly clears: true negatives divided by everyone without the disorder. A specific test rarely raises a false alarm. A high false-positive rate therefore means low specificity; a high false-negative rate means low sensitivity.

Sensitivity and specificity describe the test. But the clinician facing one patient with a positive result wants to know something different: given this result, how likely is it that this person really has the disorder? That is the positive predictive value, the proportion of positive results that are true positives, read across the top row of the table. Its partner, the negative predictive value, is the proportion of negative results that are true negatives. Here is the surprise that every clinician has to learn, often the hard way: the predictive values depend not only on the test but on how common the disorder is in the group being tested, its base rate (the prevalence of Section 1.2.6).

Key study: Meehl and Rosen on base rates (1955)

The question that Paul Meehl and Albert Rosen asked in the Psychological Bulletin in 1955 sounds simple: when is a psychological test sign or cutting score actually worth using? Their method was logical and arithmetical rather than experimental. They showed, with worked cases, that the usefulness of any sign depends on the antecedent probability (what we now call the base rate) of the condition in the population where it is used. Their result was uncomfortable for clinicians. When a condition is rare, even a sign with good accuracy can produce more wrong decisions than simply predicting, for everyone, the more common outcome, because most of its positive results come from the large group of people who do not have the condition. Their conclusion was that test manuals should report base rates, that a cutting score chosen in one setting may be wrong in another, and that clinicians routinely neglect the base rate when they interpret a test result. The criticism, which they anticipated, is that counting errors is not enough: a missed suicide and a false alarm are not equally bad, so the costs of each kind of error have to be weighed as well. The habit of ignoring base rates is now called base-rate neglect, and it is studied by cognitive psychologists as well as clinicians.

Push a case through the arithmetic, because this idea only clicks when you do. A university counselling centre gives a depression questionnaire to all 1,000 first-year students. Suppose the questionnaire has a sensitivity of 90 percent and a specificity of 90 percent, which is good for a short screen, and suppose that 5 percent of the students truly have a depressive disorder.

  1. Split the population by the truth. Five percent of 1,000 is 50 students with depression; the other 950 do not have it.
  2. Apply the sensitivity to the 50. Ninety percent of 50 is 45 true positives; the remaining 5 are false negatives, students with depression whom the screen misses.
  3. Apply the specificity to the 950. Ninety percent of 950 is 855 true negatives; the remaining 10 percent, 95 students, are false positives.
  4. Count the positives. The screen flags 45 + 95 = 140 students.
  5. Work out the positive predictive value. Only 45 of the 140 flagged students are truly depressed: 45 divided by 140 is about 32 percent. Roughly two out of every three positive results are false alarms.
  6. Work out the negative predictive value. Of the 860 students the screen clears, 855 are truly well: about 99.4 percent.

Now notice two things. First, the screen is "90 percent accurate" in both directions, yet most of its positive results are wrong. That is not a flaw in the questionnaire; it is the arithmetic of a rare condition. Because the well students outnumber the depressed ones by 19 to 1, even a small error rate among the well produces a crowd of false positives. Second, the screen makes 100 errors in all (95 false positives and 5 false negatives), so it is right about 900 of the 1,000 students. A counsellor who simply declared every student well, without testing anyone, would be right about 950. This is exactly Meehl and Rosen's point. The screen is still worth using, because it finds 45 of the 50 students who need help, but only if everyone it flags is then properly assessed before anyone is told that they have a disorder. Table 1.17 shows the same questionnaire in three different settings.

Table 1.17 The same screen (90 percent sensitivity, 90 percent specificity) used on 1,000 people in three settings. The prevalence figures are illustrative.
SettingPrevalenceTrue positivesFalse positivesPositive predictive value
Community survey of adults1 percent (10 cases)9999 of 108, about 8 percent
First-year college students5 percent (50 cases)459545 of 140, about 32 percent
Psychiatry outpatient clinic30 percent (300 cases)27070270 of 340, about 79 percent

Where the cut-off is placed is a choice, and every choice trades one kind of error for the other. Lower the threshold of a questionnaire, or loosen the criteria for a diagnosis, and sensitivity rises: fewer cases are missed, but more well people are caught. Raise the threshold, or tighten the criteria, and specificity rises: fewer false alarms, but more real cases slip through. Loose criteria therefore breed false positives, over-diagnosis and the medicalisation of ordinary unhappiness that Section 1.1.5 warned about. Strict criteria breed false negatives: people with real and treatable disorders who receive no help, which in India adds to the very large treatment gap described in Section 1.2.6. Think back to Harish in Section 1.6.1: his first episode of mania, at 25, was put down to "examination stress" by the family doctor, and the false negative cost him three years without treatment.

Which error is worse depends on the setting. A suicide-risk screen in an emergency department should be set to tolerate many false positives, because a false alarm costs a careful conversation and a missed case can cost a life. A decision with heavy consequences for the person, such as an admission without consent under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (Book 2, Section 1.10.7) or a diagnosis that will be disclosed to a prospective employer, should demand high specificity, because the harm of a false positive is serious and hard to undo. That is why good practice runs in two stages: a sensitive, cheap screen that casts a wide net, followed by a careful clinical assessment of everyone it catches.

You may also meet the labels Type I and Type II error for these mistakes. They come from statistical hypothesis testing, where the null hypothesis is that nothing is there. If the null hypothesis is "this person has no disorder", then diagnosing a well person rejects a true null hypothesis, a Type I error (a false positive), and missing a real case fails to reject a false null hypothesis, a Type II error (a false negative). The Research Methodology book explains the statistical terms.

Clinical link

Short screening questionnaires for depression, anxiety and alcohol use are increasingly used in Indian primary health centres and college counselling services (the instruments themselves belong to the Assessment book). The arithmetic of this subsection tells you how to talk to a student who has "screened positive": not "you have depression", but "your answers suggest you may be going through a lot; let us talk about it properly". The screen opens the conversation; it does not make the diagnosis.

Exam trap

A high false-positive rate means that normal people are diagnosed as disordered. It reflects low specificity and criteria that are too loose, not too strict. "Disordered people go undetected" describes a high false-negative rate (low sensitivity, criteria too strict). "Low reliability" means that raters disagree with each other, which is a different problem: two clinicians can agree perfectly and both be wrong. Sensitivity is read among the truly ill, specificity among the truly well, and the predictive values among the test results; only the predictive values change with the base rate.

The costs of a label

Every diagnosis is also a label, and a label travels. It goes into the case file and the insurance form, into the family's conversations and the neighbours' gossip, and into the person's own picture of himself. Book 2 tells the story of the critics who made this point famous: the sociologists of labelling, Edwin Lemert, Thomas Scheff and Erving Goffman, the later and more modest modified labelling theory of Bruce Link and his colleagues, Thomas Szasz's attack on the very idea of mental illness, and the pseudopatient study and the doubts that have since engulfed it (Book 2, Sections 1.6.7 and 2.10.7). That story is not retold here. What matters for a student of the disorders is the set of harms and benefits that any label carries, and the habits that keep the harms small.

The harms come in three forms. The first, public stigma, is the prejudice and discrimination of the community: the assumption that a person with schizophrenia is dangerous, the landlord who will not rent to him, the family that breaks off a marriage negotiation when it hears of a sister's depression. The second, self-stigma, is what happens when the person comes to believe the stereotype about himself, loses self-esteem and stops trying ("who would give a job to someone like me?"). The third, diagnostic overshadowing, is the clinician's version: once a psychiatric label is on the file, new problems are put down to it. When Tanvir, whose schizophrenia you met in Section 1.6.5, came to the emergency department breathless and feverish, the first doctor wrote "anxious, known psychiatric case"; it took a second doctor and a chest X-ray to find his pneumonia. The term was introduced by Steven Reiss and his colleagues in 1982 for emotional problems missed in people with intellectual disability, and it is now used most often for physical illness missed in people with mental disorders.

A label also brings benefits, and a fair account must include them. It can bring relief ("this has a name; I am not simply weak or wicked"), it opens the door to treatment and to the research on what works, and it gives access to sick leave, insurance cover, disability certification (Book 2, Section 9.9) and the protections of the law. Three habits keep the balance on the right side. Use person-first language. Treat every diagnosis as a working hypothesis that can be revised when the evidence changes. And pair the diagnosis with a formulation that describes the person's story, context and strengths (Book 2, Section 2.9), so that the label is never the only thing written about him.

Indian context

The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 writes three protections against the misuse of labels into its very first rules on diagnosis. Section 3(2) forbids anyone to classify a person as a person with mental illness except for purposes directly related to treatment, or as the Act or another law provides. Section 3(4) says that past treatment or hospitalisation in a mental health establishment, though relevant, does not by itself justify any present or future determination that the person is mentally ill. Section 3(5) says that a determination of mental illness does not by itself mean that the person is of unsound mind, unless a competent court has declared so. In plain words: a diagnosis is for care, an old diagnosis is not a life sentence, and a diagnosis is not a declaration of incompetence. Section 3(1), which ties the determination of mental illness to the ICD, is discussed in Section 1.9.3.

Exam trap

Public stigma is the community's prejudice; self-stigma is the person applying it to himself; diagnostic overshadowing (Reiss and colleagues, 1982) is a clinician attributing new problems to an existing label. Labelling theory and its critics belong to Book 2 and the Social book; the DSM-5-TR rule that socially deviant behaviour is not in itself a disorder is taught in Section 1.1.3. Under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, past treatment alone cannot justify a present determination of mental illness (section 3(4)), and mental illness does not equal unsound mind without a court's declaration (section 3(5)).

1.7 The DSM from DSM-I to DSM-5-TR, and the Multiaxial SystemMedium yield · 3 Qs

Say the full name of the American manual slowly: the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders. Why "statistical"? A book of diagnostic criteria has no obvious need for statistics. The word is a fossil, and like all fossils it records a history. The manual did not grow out of a wish to understand mental disorders; it grew out of a government's wish to count them. This section follows that history from a nineteenth-century census to the edition on the desk of every clinician today, DSM-5-TR. Along the way it teaches the best-known structure the manual ever had, the five axes of DSM-III and DSM-IV, by building a complete five-axis record for a real-sounding patient, and it explains why DSM-5 threw the axes away. The ICD's parallel history is told in Section 1.9, and the inside of today's DSM-5-TR (its sections, chapters, specifiers and codes) in Section 1.8.

Before the DSM: counting the insane

The first official attempt to collect information about mental illness in the United States was the census of 1840, which recorded the frequency of a single category, "idiocy/insanity", household by household. One category is about the crudest classification imaginable: it lumps together what we would now call intellectual disability, psychosis, dementia and much else. But the census of 1840 is remembered for something worse than crudeness.

Key study: The census that found too much madness (Edward Jarvis, 1844)

When the 1840 figures were printed, they seemed to show that insanity and idiocy were far more common among free Black Americans in the northern states than among enslaved people in the South. Defenders of slavery seized on the numbers as proof that freedom drove Black people mad; in 1844 the Secretary of State, John C. Calhoun (1782 to 1850), cited the census in a letter to the British government defending slavery. The Massachusetts physician Edward Jarvis (1803 to 1884) went back to the returns town by town. He found that the 133 "coloured" insane recorded for Worcester, Massachusetts, were in fact the white patients of the state hospital, entered in the wrong column, and that towns with no Black inhabitants at all had been credited with insane Black residents. He published his analysis, "Insanity among the coloured population of the free states", in the American Journal of the Medical Sciences in 1844 and concluded that the figures were worthless. An official review nevertheless defended them, and they went on being quoted. Jarvis spent much of the rest of his life campaigning for better statistics (he was president of the American Statistical Association from 1852 until his death) and is remembered as a pioneer of American psychiatric epidemiology. The lesson has not dated: a classification applied carelessly by untrained counters produces numbers, and numbers, once printed, will be used for whatever purpose suits the user.

Forty years later, the census of 1880 distinguished seven categories of mental illness: mania, melancholia, monomania, paresis, dementia, dipsomania and epilepsy. Several of the terms need translation. Monomania was a nineteenth-century idea of madness confined to a single subject, a fixed false belief or obsession in an otherwise reasonable mind (Section 4.5.4 meets its descendant, delusional disorder). Paresis was general paresis, the brain syphilis of Section 1.6.3. Dipsomania meant bouts of uncontrollable craving for drink (Chapter 10). Epilepsy was still counted among the mental illnesses.

The next step came from the asylum doctors themselves. In 1917 the Committee on Statistics of the American Medico-Psychological Association (the asylum superintendents' professional body), working with the National Committee for Mental Hygiene (founded in 1909; the APA's own history of the DSM calls it the National Commission on Mental Hygiene), drew up a plan for collecting uniform statistics across the country's mental hospitals, and the Bureau of the Census adopted it. It was published in 1918 as the Statistical Manual for the Use of Institutions for the Insane, with 22 diagnostic categories, and it is usually regarded as the first official psychiatric nosology in the United States. It paid more attention to clinical usefulness than the census lists had, but it was still above all a scheme for counting hospital patients. In 1921 the American Medico-Psychological Association changed its name to the American Psychiatric Association (APA), the name that appears on every DSM.

Between the wars the APA also worked with the New York Academy of Medicine on a psychiatric nomenclature for the American Medical Association's Standard Classified Nomenclature of Disease, but it too was designed mainly for inpatients with severe psychiatric and neurological disorders. The Second World War exposed the limits of such schemes. The army's psychiatrists found that a nomenclature built for long-stay inpatients had no good place for many of the soldiers they saw. They were dealing with acute breakdowns under combat stress, bodily symptoms with emotional causes, and personality problems that surfaced under military discipline. A committee led by the American psychiatrist William C. Menninger (1899 to 1966) produced a new nomenclature for the army, the War Department Technical Bulletin known as Medical 203 (it is usually dated 1943, although some accounts date its release to 1945); one historian has called it the first psychodynamic nomenclature. The Veterans Administration modified it for its own clinics. These broader wartime systems strongly influenced the sixth revision of the World Health Organization's International Classification of Diseases, ICD-6, adopted in 1948, the first ICD to contain a section on mental disorders (Section 1.9.1). The APA's Committee on Nomenclature and Statistics then prepared a variant of the ICD-6 section for American use, and published it in 1952 as the first Diagnostic and Statistical Manual. Figure 1.11 lays out the whole sequence.

Exam trap

The "S" in DSM is for statistical, because American classification began as a way of counting patients: the 1840 census had one category, "idiocy/insanity"; the 1880 census had seven; the 1918 Statistical Manual for the Use of Institutions for the Insane was the first official American nosology. The American Medico-Psychological Association became the American Psychiatric Association in 1921. The first ICD with a section on mental disorders was ICD-6 (1948), and DSM-I (1952) began as an American variant of it. The DSM is published by the American Psychiatric Association, not the American Psychological Association.

DSM-I (1952) and DSM-II (1968)

DSM-I was a slim book, by the usual count 106 categories in about 130 pages. Its claim to a place in history is that it was the first official manual of mental disorders to contain a glossary describing each diagnostic category, so that a clinician could read what "schizophrenic reaction" was supposed to mean instead of guessing. That phrase shows its most distinctive feature: the word reaction ran right through the classification (schizophrenic reaction, depressive reaction, anxiety reaction). The word reflected the influence of the Swiss-born American psychiatrist Adolf Meyer (1866 to 1950), whose psychobiological view held that mental disorders are reactions of the whole personality to psychological, social and biological factors, rather than diseases that strike a person from outside. It was a humane idea and, in the post-war years, a psychoanalytically coloured one.

DSM-II, which appeared in 1968, was built on a different decision: to base the American classification on the mental disorders section of the eighth revision of the ICD, ICD-8, for which APA representatives had acted as consultants, and both went into effect in 1968. By the usual count it was slightly longer, 182 categories in 134 pages. It dropped most of the "reactions" but kept the psychoanalytic vocabulary, above all the neuroses (anxiety neurosis, hysterical neurosis, depressive neurosis and others), which were understood as the outward signs of unconscious conflict and the defences against it (Section 6.1.4 follows the neuroses through every edition). The history of homosexuality's place in these manuals, from DSM-I's "sociopathic personality disturbance" to the vote of the APA's trustees in December 1973 that removed it from DSM-II, is told in Book 2, Section 2.10.3.

Both manuals shared a weakness that mattered more than any single category: neither gave rules for deciding when a disorder was present. Each glossary entry was a short paragraph sketching a typical case, and each clinician decided how close a patient had to come to the sketch. The result, documented through the 1960s and early 1970s, was a crisis of reliability: the low agreement of experienced psychiatrists, the different meanings of "schizophrenia" in New York and London, and the discovery that most disagreement came from clinicians applying different rules to the same information (Section 1.6.6 and Book 2, Section 2.1.3). The remedy came from the neo-Kraepelinians of Section 1.6.2. In 1972 John Feighner, Eli Robins, Samuel Guze and their colleagues at Washington University published explicit diagnostic criteria for use in research on the main psychiatric disorders, now called the Feighner criteria, and a few years later Robert Spitzer, Jean Endicott and Robins developed them into the Research Diagnostic Criteria. For the first time, a diagnosis could be defined by a rule that two strangers could apply in the same way. The next edition of the DSM would apply that idea to the whole of psychiatry.

Exam trap

The dates are DSM-I 1952 and DSM-II 1968; ICD-6, the first ICD with a section on mental disorders, is 1948. Some widely circulated Indian notes give DSM-II as 1962 and ICD-6 as 1952, and claim that DSM-5 "introduced Roman numerals". All three are wrong: 1962 is the year of Beck's reliability study, 1952 is DSM-I, and DSM-5 did the opposite, replacing Roman numerals with an Arabic one. DSM-I is the manual of "reactions" (Adolf Meyer); DSM-II kept the neuroses and was based on ICD-8. Neither had explicit criteria.

DSM-III (1980): the revolution

Work on the third edition began in 1974 under the direction of the American psychiatrist Robert L. Spitzer (1932 to 2015), who had helped write the Research Diagnostic Criteria, and the manual appeared in 1980. Its development, like that of its predecessors, was coordinated with the ICD, this time with ICD-9. (DSM-5-TR says ICD-9 was published in 1975 and implemented in 1978; other sources give 1979. The dates differ because an ICD revision is adopted in one year and comes into use later, a pattern you will meet again in Section 1.9.) DSM-III is the most important edition in the series, and it is worth knowing exactly what it changed.

  1. Explicit diagnostic criteria. For almost every disorder the glossary paragraph was replaced by a list of rules: which symptoms must be present, how many, for how long, and what must be ruled out. The Feighner and Research Diagnostic Criteria had done this for a handful of research diagnoses; DSM-III did it for the whole of clinical psychiatry.
  2. A descriptive approach neutral about causes. Disorders were defined by what could be observed or reported, not by presumed psychoanalytic or biological causes. The stance was called atheoretical. Its most visible casualty was the neurosis. Phobias, for example, were no longer classed as a "phobic neurosis" defined by unconscious conflict; they were placed in a new, broader group of anxiety disorders defined by their symptoms (Section 6.1.4).
  3. The multiaxial system. Each patient was described on five axes, so that the clinician looked beyond the main disorder to the personality, the body, the stresses and the level of functioning (Section 1.7.5).
  4. Fuller descriptions. Each disorder came with far more descriptive text than before, covering such matters as its associated features, age at onset, course, prevalence, sex ratio, familial pattern and differential diagnosis. Today's DSM-5-TR text headings (Section 1.2.8) are its descendants.
  5. Field trials. Draft criteria were tested in clinical settings before publication to check that clinicians could apply them reliably, a step that DSM-IV and DSM-5 repeated (the text revisions had none).

DSM-III also introduced new categories, of which the most consequential was posttraumatic stress disorder, first named in DSM-III in 1980 after years of pressure from clinicians who worked with Vietnam veterans (Section 7.2). The book was much bigger than its predecessors, by the usual count 265 categories in 494 pages. And it spread far beyond the United States. When Maser, Kaelber and Weise surveyed the international use of diagnostic systems in 1991, they found that DSM-III had become popular because of its precise descriptive format, its neutrality about the causes of disorder and its multiaxial view of the whole person, and that more clinicians around the world were using DSM-III-R at the beginning of the 1990s than the ICD, the system designed for international use.

The revolution had its critics, and their points are still worth making in an interview. First, an "atheoretical" manual is not free of theory: choosing to define disorders by observable symptoms and to treat them as discrete medical conditions is itself a theoretical choice, the neo-Kraepelinian one, and psychoanalysts protested that it threw away their way of understanding patients. Second, many of the cut-offs were committee decisions. Why five symptoms of nine, or two weeks rather than ten days? Barlow and Durand list "arbitrary decisions on criteria for many disorders" among DSM-III's shortcomings, along with low reliability for some diagnoses even with the new rules. Third, reliability was bought partly at the cost of validity: criteria were written to be easy to agree on, and agreement is not truth (Section 1.6.6). None of this undoes DSM-III's achievement. Before 1980, two psychiatrists could not be sure they meant the same thing by a diagnosis; after 1980 they could at least check.

Exam trap

DSM-III (1980), directed by Robert L. Spitzer, introduced explicit diagnostic criteria, the "atheoretical" descriptive approach and the multiaxial system; none of these began with DSM-IV. Posttraumatic stress disorder was first named in DSM-III. "Atheoretical" means neutral about causes, not free of all assumptions. DSM-III's companion ICD was ICD-9, not ICD-10. Spitzer is also the best-known critic of the pseudopatient study (Book 2, Section 2.10.7); do not confuse him with Kraepelin, Kupfer or Frances.

DSM-III-R (1987), DSM-IV (1994) and DSM-IV-TR (2000)

Experience with DSM-III soon revealed inconsistencies and criteria that did not work as intended, so the APA appointed a work group to revise it, and its corrections were published in 1987 as DSM-III-R (the R stands for "revised"). Three changes matter for this chapter. Many of DSM-III's hierarchical exclusion rules were removed, so that co-occurring disorders could be diagnosed together (Section 1.6.5). DSM-III's category of ego-dystonic homosexuality was dropped (Book 2, Section 2.10.3). And Axis V became the Global Assessment of Functioning scale that Section 1.7.5 explains. By the usual count DSM-III-R had 292 categories in 567 pages.

DSM-IV, published in 1994, was the culmination of a six-year effort that involved more than 1,000 people and numerous professional organisations. Its task force, chaired by the American psychiatrist Allen Frances (born 1942), set itself a rule: no change without empirical support, relying as little as possible on expert consensus. Every proposed change therefore went through three stages: systematic reviews of the published literature, reanalyses of large data sets that had been collected for other purposes, and 12 field trials testing the reliability and validity of alternative criteria. The developers worked closely with the WHO team preparing ICD-10 (published in 1992), and the result was increased congruence between the two systems. Five of DSM-IV's changes matter most for this book.

  • The end of "organic" versus "functional". The old split between organically and psychologically based disorders was eliminated, for the reasons given in Section 1.6.3.
  • The clinical significance criterion. To reduce false positives (Section 1.6.7), almost half the criteria sets gained a requirement that the symptoms cause clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning. The clinical significance criterion is still part of most DSM-5-TR criteria sets.
  • New axes. Axis II was restricted to personality disorders and mental retardation (developmental disorders that DSM-III-R had placed on Axis II moved to Axis I), and Axis IV changed from a rating of stress to a list of psychosocial and environmental problems (Section 1.7.5).
  • Culture. Appendix I added an Outline for Cultural Formulation and a Glossary of Culture-Bound Syndromes, the first serious attempt by the manual to deal with culture (Section 1.10.2).
  • Criteria sets for further study. Appendix B collected proposed disorders that were not yet official, the ancestor of DSM-5's conditions for further study (Section 1.8.6).

DSM-IV-TR (2000) was a text revision: the descriptive text accompanying each disorder was updated with the research published since 1994, and the diagnostic criteria were left virtually unchanged. Its multiaxial form, which the next subsection teaches, was the version in use around the world for thirteen years, which is why it still dominates older textbooks, case files and question banks. Table 1.18 summarises the editions.

Table 1.18 The DSM editions at a glance. Category and page counts are the usual published counts and vary slightly with what is counted.
EditionYearLeadership or basisWhat was new
DSM-I1952APA Committee on Nomenclature and Statistics; a variant of ICD-6First official manual with a glossary of descriptions; "reactions" (Meyer); about 106 categories
DSM-II1968Based on the mental disorders section of ICD-8Most "reactions" dropped; neuroses and psychoanalytic terms kept; about 182 categories
DSM-III1980Robert L. Spitzer; coordinated with ICD-9Explicit criteria; atheoretical descriptive approach; multiaxial system; field trials; PTSD; about 265 categories
DSM-III-R1987APA work group to revise DSM-IIIInconsistencies corrected; many exclusion rules dropped; GAF on Axis V; about 292 categories
DSM-IV1994Allen Frances; coordinated with ICD-10Literature reviews, data reanalyses and 12 field trials; organic versus functional dropped; clinical significance criterion; cultural appendix
DSM-IV-TR2000Text revision of DSM-IVText updated, criteria virtually unchanged; five axes as in DSM-IV
DSM-52013David J. Kupfer (chair) and Darrel A. Regier (vice-chair)Arabic numeral; axes abolished; lifespan chapter order; dimensional measures; Section III
DSM-5-TR2022Michael B. First (editor); text revision of DSM-5Text updated, criteria clarified for more than 70 disorders; prolonged grief disorder; codes for suicidal behaviour and non-suicidal self-injury
Exam trap

The task force chairs are a favourite source of wrong options: Spitzer led DSM-III, Frances chaired DSM-IV, Kupfer chaired DSM-5 (with Regier as vice-chair), and First edited DSM-5-TR. DSM-III-R (1987) dropped many exclusion rules and brought in the GAF; DSM-IV (1994) dropped "organic versus functional", added the clinical significance criterion to almost half the criteria sets and added the cultural appendix; DSM-IV-TR (2000) is a text revision, with the criteria virtually unchanged, not a new edition.

The multiaxial system

A diagnosis on its own says very little about a person. "Generalised anxiety disorder" fits a contented retired professor who worries too much about his grandchildren and also a young man who is failing his examinations, sleeping four hours a night, borrowing money and hiding a physical illness. The multiaxial system was designed to stop clinicians from writing the name and moving on. Introduced in DSM-III (1980) and used, with changes, through DSM-III-R, DSM-IV and DSM-IV-TR, it required every patient to be described on five separate axes, each a different domain of information: the clinical disorder, the personality, the body, the circumstances and the overall level of functioning. Its purpose, in a phrase, was to make the clinician look at the whole person. In its final DSM-IV-TR form the axes were these.

  • Axis I: Clinical disorders, and other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention. All the mental disorders except the personality disorders and mental retardation went here: schizophrenia, mood disorders, anxiety disorders, substance use disorders, eating disorders, the childhood disorders and the rest. So did the "other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention", problems such as bereavement or a relational problem that bring someone to a clinician without being disorders, recorded with codes that began with V (the V codes; DSM-5-TR's equivalents are the Z codes of Section 1.8.7).
  • Axis II: Personality disorders and mental retardation. The ten personality disorders and mental retardation (now called intellectual developmental disorder, Chapter 3) were recorded here. The clinician could also note prominent maladaptive personality features that fell short of a disorder, and habitual defence mechanisms. If there was nothing to record, the entry read "V71.09 No diagnosis on Axis II".
  • Axis III: General medical conditions. Physical illnesses potentially relevant to understanding or managing the person's mental disorder: a thyroid disorder in a depressed patient, epilepsy in a patient with psychosis, diabetes in someone taking a medicine that raises weight.
  • Axis IV: Psychosocial and environmental problems. The stresses and disadvantages that might affect diagnosis, treatment or outcome, generally those of the past year, listed under nine headings: problems with the primary support group (the family), problems related to the social environment, educational problems, occupational problems, housing problems, economic problems, problems with access to health care services, problems related to interaction with the legal system or crime, and other psychosocial and environmental problems.
  • Axis V: Global Assessment of Functioning. A single number from 0 to 100 summarising the clinician's judgement of the person's overall psychological, social and occupational functioning on what the manual called a hypothetical continuum of mental health and illness.

The Global Assessment of Functioning (GAF) scale deserves a closer look, because it is the part of the system that most needs practice. It descends from a 0 to 100 Health-Sickness Rating Scale published by Lester Luborsky and colleagues in 1962 and from Jean Endicott and colleagues' Global Assessment Scale of 1976; DSM-III-R turned it into Axis V. The scale is divided into bands of ten points, each with an anchor description and examples (Table 1.19). The clinician finds the band that best fits, using one rule that students often miss: symptoms and functioning are judged separately, and if they point to different bands, the final rating always reflects the worse of the two. A man with mild symptoms who has stopped going to work is rated by his work; a woman who still works but has persistent thoughts of suicide is rated by her symptoms. The rating usually describes the present, though it could also be given for the highest level in the past year or at discharge.

Table 1.19 The Global Assessment of Functioning scale of DSM-IV-TR (Axis V), with the band descriptions paraphrased.
ScoreWhat the band describesThe kind of example the manual gave
91 to 100Superior functioning in many activities; problems never get out of hand; sought out by others for positive qualities; no symptomsNo example given
81 to 90Absent or minimal symptoms; good functioning in all areas; involved and socially effective; only everyday worriesMild nerves before an examination
71 to 80Any symptoms are brief, expectable reactions to stress; no more than slight impairmentTrouble concentrating after a family quarrel
61 to 70Some mild symptoms, or some difficulty in social, work or school functioning; generally functioning fairly well, with meaningful relationshipsLow mood with mild insomnia
51 to 60Moderate symptoms, or moderate difficulty in social, work or school functioningOccasional panic attacks; few friends; conflicts with co-workers
41 to 50Serious symptoms, or any serious impairment in social, work or school functioningSuicidal ideation; severe rituals; unable to keep a job
31 to 40Some impairment in reality testing or communication, or major impairment in several areas (work, family, judgement, thinking or mood)Speech at times illogical; neglects family and cannot work
21 to 30Behaviour considerably influenced by delusions or hallucinations, or serious impairment in communication or judgement, or unable to function in almost all areasStays in bed all day; no job, home or friends
11 to 20Some danger of hurting self or others, or occasional failure to keep minimal hygiene, or gross impairment in communicationLargely incoherent or mute
1 to 10Persistent danger of severely hurting self or others, or persistent inability to keep minimal hygiene, or a serious suicidal act with clear expectation of deathRecurrent violence
0Inadequate informationNo example given

The axes were not born in their final form. In DSM-III, Axis IV was a rating of the severity of the psychosocial stressors the person had faced, on a numbered scale, and Axis V was a rating of the highest level of adaptive functioning the person had reached in the past year, on a seven-point scale. Clinicians in the field trials found the stress rating hard to apply, not least because the same event (a divorce, a move, a loss of job) is crushing for one person and a relief for another. DSM-III-R replaced the old Axis V with the GAF, and DSM-IV replaced the stress rating with the list of problems you have just read. Axis II also changed: DSM-III-R had placed the developmental disorders there as well, and DSM-IV moved them to Axis I, leaving only personality disorders and mental retardation. Why give those two a separate axis at all? Because they are lifelong, background conditions, and the designers feared that they would be overlooked whenever a florid Axis I disorder such as a psychotic episode or a severe depression held the clinician's attention. A personality disorder changes how a depression should be treated and how quickly it will lift; mental retardation changes how a psychosis presents and how its treatment must be explained. Separate axes forced the question to be asked. The multiaxial idea was not only American: in 1997 the WHO published a three-axis presentation of ICD-10 for adult psychiatry (clinical diagnoses, disabilities and contextual factors), which Section 1.9 places in the ICD's history.

The axes are easiest to understand by filling them in for one person. Meet Dinesh, whom you will follow through the next three sections of this chapter: Section 1.8.3 records his diagnosis in DSM-5-TR, Section 1.9.7 codes it in ICD-10 and ICD-11, and Section 1.10.3 shows his Cultural Formulation Interview.

The running case: Dinesh

Dinesh is 24 and unmarried, the elder son of a farming family from a village in eastern Uttar Pradesh. He holds a BA degree. For two years he has lived in a rented room in Delhi, preparing for a government recruitment examination; his coaching fees are paid from money his father borrowed. Four months ago his engagement was fixed, and the wedding is planned for after the examination results, about five months away. He comes to the psychiatry outpatient clinic of a Delhi general hospital on referral from a physician whom he has seen three times for "weakness".

For about nine months he has worried, on most days, about many things: his health, the examination, the family's debt and the marriage. He cannot switch the worry off. He is restless, tires easily and cannot concentrate ("I read a page and nothing stays"); he is irritable, his neck and shoulders ache, and his sleep is broken. He has had no panic attacks, no obsessions or compulsions and no psychotic symptoms. His mood is "low at times", but he still enjoys watching cricket on his phone and the Sunday meals he shares with his elder cousin, and he has had no thoughts of suicide. He takes no alcohol, tobacco or other drugs.

Dinesh has his own explanation. He is sure that his weakness comes from the loss of dhat, a whitish discharge he believes he sees in his urine, and from night emissions, and he fears that he is losing his strength and will not be able to satisfy his wife or father children. He has spent several thousand rupees at a roadside clinic that advertises cures for "gupt rog" (secret diseases) and has taken herbal powders. He has told no one in his family.

The physician found a mild iron-deficiency anaemia, for which Dinesh is taking iron tablets; his thyroid function and urine tests were normal. He attends his coaching classes irregularly, failed the preliminary examination three months ago and avoids phone calls from his fiancée's family, but he still cooks, bathes and meets his cousin. He describes himself as conscientious and "a worrier since school". He has no past psychiatric history. A maternal uncle "had nerves"; nothing more is known.

Now build his record in the DSM-IV-TR form, one axis at a time, and notice how each axis makes the clinician ask a different question.

Axis I: what clinical disorder is present? The clinician works through the criteria for generalised anxiety disorder as DSM-IV-TR wrote them. Dinesh has excessive worry on more days than not for at least six months (nine, in fact) about a number of events and activities, and he finds it hard to control. He has not merely three but all six of the associated symptoms: restlessness, being easily fatigued, difficulty concentrating, irritability, muscle tension (the aching neck and shoulders) and disturbed sleep. The worry is not confined to the features of another disorder: it is not only about having panic attacks (he has none), not only about contamination (he has no obsessions), and not only about having a serious illness, since his health is one worry among several. It causes clinically significant distress and impairment. Then come the hierarchy's questions from Section 1.6.5. Is a substance responsible? He uses none. Is a general medical condition responsible? His anaemia may add to his tiredness, but it cannot produce nine months of uncontrollable worry about debt and marriage, and his normal thyroid tests exclude a classic medical mimic of anxiety. Is the anxiety occurring only during a mood disorder, which DSM-IV-TR would have treated as excluding the diagnosis? His mood is low at times, but he still enjoys cricket and his Sunday meals and has no thoughts of suicide, so there is no major depressive episode. The diagnosis is 300.02 generalised anxiety disorder. His belief about dhat is not given a separate diagnosis here; the clinician treats it as his own cultural explanation of his weakness, to be recorded in the cultural formulation (DSM-IV-TR's Appendix I provided an outline for one and listed dhat in its glossary). Section 1.10.3 interviews him about it.

Axis II: is there a personality disorder or mental retardation? There is no evidence of either. Dinesh is anxious and conscientious and has been "a worrier since school", but these are traits, not a pervasive, inflexible pattern that has damaged his life since adolescence. The entry is V71.09 no diagnosis on Axis II, with the anxious, conscientious traits noted in words, because they help explain why examination pressure hit him so hard and they will shape how he responds to treatment.

Axis III: what physical illness is relevant? Iron-deficiency anaemia. It belongs on the record because it contributes to the tiredness and "weakness" that brought him to a doctor in the first place, and because it had to be considered and treated before his symptoms could be blamed on anxiety alone. Normal test results are not conditions and are not listed.

Axis IV: what psychosocial and environmental problems are there? Three of the nine headings apply. Educational problems: he failed the preliminary examination three months ago. Economic problems: the family is in debt for his coaching. Problems with the primary support group: the pressure surrounding the coming marriage, the fiancée's family whose calls he avoids, and a family who do not know what he is going through.

Axis V: how well is he functioning overall? Look at symptoms and functioning separately and take the worse. His symptoms are moderate: constant worry with bodily tension and poor sleep, but no suicidal ideation and no symptom that a band in the 40s would require. His functioning shows moderate difficulty: irregular attendance at coaching, a failed examination and avoidance of his future in-laws, yet he still looks after himself, sees his cousin and enjoys his cricket. Both point to the 51 to 60 band, "moderate symptoms or moderate difficulty", and the parts of his life that still work place him towards its upper end. The rating is GAF = 58 (current). It is not in the 61 to 70 band, because his difficulties are more than "some"; it is not in the 41 to 50 band, because there is no serious symptom such as suicidal thinking and no serious impairment such as being unable to function at all. Figure 1.12 shows the finished record.

Clinical link

The axes have gone from the manual, but the five questions they asked have not gone from good practice. A well-written case summary in any Indian clinic still covers the disorder or disorders, the personality, the physical health, the current stresses and the level of functioning, because each changes the plan. For Dinesh, the anaemia needs treating, the examination and marriage pressures shape what can be done before his results, his conscientiousness will make him a diligent patient, and his functioning tells you how urgent things are. Section 1.8.3 shows how DSM-5-TR records the same information without axes.

Exam trap

Axis III is general medical conditions. Axis I is clinical disorders (the "clinical psychiatric diagnosis", plus the V codes); Axis II is personality disorders and mental retardation; Axis IV is psychosocial and environmental problems; Axis V is the Global Assessment of Functioning (0 to 100; rate the worse of symptoms and functioning). The axes arrived with DSM-III in 1980, not with DSM-IV, and lasted through DSM-IV-TR; DSM-5 (2013) abolished them. In DSM-III, Axis IV rated the severity of stressors and Axis V the highest functioning in the past year; the GAF came with DSM-III-R (1987) and the list of problems on Axis IV with DSM-IV (1994).

DSM-5 (2013): the axes go

The fifth edition took longer to make than any before it. In 1999 the APA began an evaluation of the strengths and weaknesses of the DSM, working with the WHO, the World Psychiatric Association and the United States National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH); the first fruit was a 2002 monograph called A Research Agenda for DSM-V, and between 2003 and 2008 thirteen international research planning conferences brought together about 400 participants from 39 countries to review the evidence for both the DSM and the coming ICD-11. In 2006 the APA named David J. Kupfer as chair and Darrel A. Regier as vice-chair of the DSM-5 Task Force, which worked through 13 diagnostic work groups. For the first time, every task force and work group member had to disclose income from industry and keep it under a cap, and draft criteria were posted on a website for public comment before field trials tested them. The manual was published in May 2013. Its name used an Arabic numeral, "5", rather than the Roman "V" of the planning documents, so that later updates could be numbered as software is (5.1, 5.2 and so on); in the event, the next revision was called DSM-5-TR.

The most visible structural change was the end of the axes. DSM-5 moved to a non-axial record of diagnosis. Disorders and conditions formerly listed on Axis I (clinical disorders), Axis II (personality disorders and intellectual disability) and Axis III (other medical conditions) are now listed together, without formal differentiation, usually in order of clinical importance. The psychosocial and contextual factors of the old Axis IV are listed alongside them, using the Z codes of the chapter on other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention (Section 1.8.7). The GAF of Axis V was replaced by the WHO Disability Assessment Schedule (WHODAS), a measure of disability based on the WHO's International Classification of Functioning, Disability and Health and usable across all of medicine; it is printed in Section III of the manual (Section 1.8.5, and the Assessment book for the instrument itself).

Three reasons are usually given for the change. First, separating Axis I from Axis II implied that personality disorders differ in kind from other mental disorders, as if they were fixed and untreatable, and research did not support so sharp a line: some personality disorders change considerably over time, while some "Axis I" disorders, such as the chronic depressions, are as enduring as any personality disorder. Second, a separate axis for "general medical conditions" suggested that mental disorders are not medical conditions. DSM-5-TR now says the opposite in so many words: "mental disorder" and "another medical condition" are merely terms of convenience and do not imply any fundamental distinction between them. (This is why DSM-5 replaced the phrase "general medical condition" with "another medical condition".) Third, the GAF was judged conceptually muddled, because one number mixed symptoms, suicide risk and disability, and its reliability in routine practice was questionable. A rating of 45 might mean a man who is suicidal but still at work, or a man with no thoughts of suicide who has lost his job and all his friends, and two clinicians could give the same patient quite different numbers.

DSM-5 also rearranged its chapters. They now follow a developmental and lifespan order, beginning with the disorders thought to reflect processes that show themselves early in life (neurodevelopmental disorders, then the schizophrenia spectrum), moving through those that usually begin in adolescence and young adulthood (bipolar, depressive and anxiety disorders) and ending with those of later life (neurocognitive disorders). After the neurodevelopmental disorders, the chapters are grouped broadly into internalising disorders (with prominent anxiety, depressive and somatic symptoms), externalising disorders (with prominent impulsive, disruptive conduct and substance use symptoms), neurocognitive disorders and others, an arrangement meant to encourage research into what neighbouring disorders share. Section 1.8.2 lists the chapters in order. Table 1.20 summarises the main changes from DSM-IV-TR.
