DSM-5 was greeted with fierce public criticism. The loudest voice belonged to Allen Frances, who had chaired the DSM-IV task force. In his book Saving Normal (2013) and many articles he argued that DSM-5 would turn ordinary suffering into disorder: temper tantrums into disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, normal grief into major depression once the bereavement exclusion went, and the forgetfulness of old age into mild neurocognitive disorder. These are, in the language of Section 1.6.7, predictions of a rise in false positives. A different criticism came from the research side. On 29 April 2013, a few weeks before publication, the director of the NIMH, Thomas Insel, wrote that the institute would be "re-orienting its research away from DSM categories" towards its Research Domain Criteria (RDoC), because the DSM's categories lacked validity. Within weeks the NIMH and the APA issued a joint statement describing the two as complementary, the DSM for clinical diagnosis today and RDoC for the research that might change it tomorrow (Book 2, Section 2.10.6).

Exam trap

Compared with DSM-IV-TR, DSM-5 abolished the multiaxial system. It did not add a sixth axis, it did not keep the five axes, and it did not replace diagnoses with ICD codes: it still gives named diagnoses with criteria, and prints ICD-10-CM codes beside them for recording (Section 1.8.3). Former Axes I, II and III are listed together; Axis IV problems become Z codes; the GAF is replaced by the WHODAS. DSM-5 also changed "general medical condition" to "another medical condition", switched to an Arabic numeral, and ordered its chapters along the lifespan.

DSM-5-TR (2022): the edition in use

Nine years after DSM-5, in March 2022, the APA published DSM-5-TR, the first published revision of DSM-5. Its editor was Michael B. First, who co-chaired the Revision Subcommittee with Philip Wang, and more than 200 experts did most of the work. The manual is the product of three overlapping processes: the original criteria and text written by the DSM-5 Task Force for 2013; the online updates that the APA's DSM Steering Committee had issued since 2013 in response to clinical experience, new research and changes in the United States coding system; and a full rewrite of the descriptive text by the Revision Subcommittee. Four changes define it.

  • Clarified criteria. The criteria sets of more than 70 disorders were modified, mostly for clarity. The conceptual content of the criteria was not changed, which is why criteria that originated in 2013 are still called "DSM-5 criteria".
  • Updated text. The descriptive text of almost every disorder was revised in light of the research since 2013, most extensively the sections on prevalence, risk and prognostic factors, culture-related and sex- and gender-related diagnostic issues, association with suicidal thoughts or behaviour, and comorbidity (Section 1.2.8 explains these headings).
  • One new diagnosis and new codes. Prolonged grief disorder became an official diagnosis in the chapter on trauma- and stressor-related disorders (its history as a condition for further study is told in Section 1.8.6 and the disorder itself in Section 7.4.3), and symptom codes were added for recording suicidal behaviour and non-suicidal self-injury (Section 1.8.7).
  • Language and equity. For the first time the whole text was reviewed by a Work Group on Ethnoracial Equity and Inclusion, to give proper attention to risk factors such as the experience of racism and discrimination and to use non-stigmatising language. Some names changed: intellectual disability became intellectual developmental disorder (with "intellectual disability" kept in parentheses), to match ICD-11's "disorders of intellectual development" (Chapter 3), and conversion disorder became functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder), with the two names now in that order (Section 7.7). A residual category, unspecified mood disorder, was added for presentations in which it is hard to choose between an unspecified bipolar and an unspecified depressive disorder, for example in acute agitation.

Compare that with the previous text revision. DSM-IV-TR (2000) confined its updates almost entirely to the text and left the criteria virtually unchanged; DSM-5-TR went further, clarifying criteria and adding a disorder. Both, however, are text revisions within an edition, not new editions. There is no DSM-6, and the planning documents of the early 2000s that spoke of "DSM-V" described the manual that was eventually published as DSM-5.

Remember

The DSM string: 1952, 1968, 1980, 1987, 1994, 2000, 2013, 2022 for DSM-I, DSM-II, DSM-III, DSM-III-R, DSM-IV, DSM-IV-TR, DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR. The turning points are DSM-III (explicit criteria and the five axes) and DSM-5 (axes abolished, Arabic numeral). Book 2, Section 2.10.3 drills the same string.

Exam trap

Asked which DSM is "currently in use", and offered DSM-IV, DSM-V, DSM-III and DSM-6, choose DSM-V: examiners often write the fifth edition with a Roman numeral, and "DSM-V" means DSM-5. Know, though, that the text now in use is DSM-5-TR (2022), the text revision of DSM-5, and pick it if it is offered. DSM-IV and DSM-III are superseded, and there is no DSM-6. Do not confuse DSM-5-TR (APA, March 2022) with ICD-11 (WHO, adopted 2019, in effect from 1 January 2022), which happens to share the year (Section 1.9.5).

Scan Your Brain

  3. Criteria that require "five or more of nine" symptoms, rather than all of them, are called criteria.

1.8 Inside DSM-5-TR: Sections, Specifiers, Conditions for Further Study and Z CodesLower yield · 1 Q

Section 1.7 followed the manual through seventy years of editions. Now open the edition you will actually use. DSM-5-TR runs to more than a thousand pages, and it rewards a reader who knows its geography. A student who starts at page one wades through a long introduction and a classification list dozens of pages long before the first disorder appears. A student who jumps straight to the criteria for generalised anxiety disorder may never notice that half of what is needed to use those criteria properly lives somewhere else: in two pages on specifiers, in a coding rule, in a chapter of problems that are not disorders at all. This section is a guided tour. It shows how the book is built, why its disorder chapters come in the order they do, how a clinician turns a criteria set into a written diagnosis with its subtypes, specifiers and codes, what to do when a patient fits no category exactly, and what lives in the less-read parts of the manual: the rating scales, the alternative model of personality disorder, the proposed conditions for further study and the long list of "other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention". Section 1.9 then does the same for the World Health Organization's classification.

Three sections and an appendix

Before any numbered section comes the DSM-5-TR Classification, a list of every diagnosis in the manual in chapter order, each with its code and the page on which its criteria begin. Clinicians use it the way a traveller uses the index of a railway timetable: to find the right entry fast and to check a code. Then come three numbered sections and an appendix, stacked as Figure 1.13 shows.

Section I, "DSM-5 Basics", is the instruction booklet, and it has three parts. The Introduction tells how DSM-5 and its text revision were produced, explains the order of the chapters and the effort to harmonise them with ICD-11, and sets out the manual's key ideas: the definition of a mental disorder (Section 1.1.3), the uneasy marriage of categories and dimensions (Section 1.6.4), and the influence of culture, sex and gender on how disorders appear. Use of the Manual is the practical part: how to move from criteria to a diagnosis, what subtypes and specifiers are, when to use the "other specified" and "unspecified" categories, why clinical judgement matters and how to code and record what you have decided. Most of Sections 1.8.3 and 1.8.4 comes from these few pages, which students skip and examiners do not. The section closes with the Cautionary Statement for Forensic Use of DSM-5. Its message is simple. The manual's definition of mental disorder was written for clinicians, public health workers and researchers, not for the technical needs of courts and lawyers, so a DSM diagnosis does not by itself show that a person meets a legal standard: that he was not criminally responsible for an act, is not competent to make a decision, or is entitled to disability compensation. Those questions need more than a diagnosis, above all evidence about how this person's particular impairments affect the particular abilities the law cares about. The statement adds that the manual is not a treatment guide and should not be used to diagnose by people without clinical training. Indian law draws a similar line between a clinical diagnosis and a legal finding, as Book 2, Section 1.10 explains.

Section II, "Diagnostic Criteria and Codes", is the heart of the book and the only part whose disorders are officially recognised for clinical use. It contains 22 chapters, which Section 1.8.2 takes in order. Every disorder in it appears in the same format: the code and name, the criteria set, and then the descriptive text under standard headings such as Diagnostic Features, Prevalence, Development and Course, Risk and Prognostic Factors, Culture-Related Diagnostic Issues and Differential Diagnosis (Section 1.2.8 explains how to read those headings).

Section III, "Emerging Measures and Models", holds material the American Psychiatric Association considers promising but not yet established enough for routine use. It has four chapters: Assessment Measures, rating scales that add dimensions to the categories; Culture and Psychiatric Diagnosis, with the Cultural Formulation Interview and a glossary of cultural concepts of distress; the Alternative DSM-5 Model for Personality Disorders; and Conditions for Further Study, proposed disorders with research criteria. The manual is frank about the difference in status. The Section II criteria are described as well established and extensively reviewed; the Section III tools and proposals are those for which more scientific evidence may be needed before they can be widely used in the clinic. Sections 1.8.5 and 1.8.6 explain what each contains.

The Appendix lists every diagnosis twice, once alphabetically and once in numerical order of its ICD-10-CM code, and ends with the names of the advisors and contributors and the index. The numerical list is the one to reach for when a referral letter gives you only a code ("F41.1") and you need to know what it means.

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR has three sections, not five, and they are not the old five axes under a new name. Section I is the basics, Section II the official criteria and codes, Section III the emerging measures and models. If a question asks where the Cultural Formulation Interview, the WHODAS 2.0, the cross-cutting symptom measures, the alternative personality disorder model or the conditions for further study are found, the answer is Section III. The ten familiar personality disorders are in Section II; only the alternative model is in Section III. The Cautionary Statement for Forensic Use closes Section I.

The twenty disorder chapters and why they come in this order

Section II has 22 chapters, but only 20 of them are chapters of mental disorders. The twentieth, "Other Mental Disorders and Additional Codes", is a small chapter of leftover categories for presentations that fit nowhere else. The last two chapters are different in kind. "Medication-Induced Movement Disorders and Other Adverse Effects of Medication" and "Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention" are printed in Section II because clinicians meet them every day, but the manual says plainly that their contents do not have to meet the definition of a mental disorder and are not mental disorders. Table 1.21 lists all 22 with two examples each and the chapter of this book where you will meet them.

Why this order and not, say, alphabetical? DSM-5's designers, working alongside the WHO group revising the ICD, wanted the arrangement itself to carry information, and they used three principles that the manual spells out. The first is development across the life span. The book opens with disorders thought to reflect developmental processes that show themselves early in life (the neurodevelopmental disorders, then the schizophrenia spectrum disorders, which many researchers trace to early brain development, Section 1.5.4), moves on to disorders that more often begin in adolescence and young adulthood (bipolar, depressive and anxiety disorders) and ends with those of adulthood and later life, above all the neurocognitive disorders. Where possible the same order is used inside each chapter, which is why separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism, disorders of childhood, open the anxiety chapter.

The second principle is kinship. After the neurodevelopmental disorders the chapters are grouped into broad families that research on comorbidity and shared risk factors keeps finding. Internalising disorders are those whose prominent symptoms are anxiety, depression and bodily complaints, the suffering turned inwards; externalising disorders are those whose prominent symptoms are impulsive, disruptive conduct and substance use, the trouble turned outwards against other people and rules. These are followed by the neurocognitive disorders and then the others. Inside the families, neighbours are relatives. Bipolar disorders sit between the schizophrenia spectrum and the depressive disorders because, in their symptoms, family histories and genetics, they form a bridge between the two. Obsessive-compulsive and related disorders follow the anxiety disorders because of their close relationship to them, and the trauma- and stressor-related disorders are placed between those chapters and the dissociative disorders, because traumatic stress so often produces anxiety and dissociative symptoms. The hope was that this arrangement would encourage research into the shared causes that produce so much comorbidity (Section 1.2.7).

The third principle is clinical usefulness. A manual whose clusters mirror what clinicians actually see should help non-specialists, such as a general practitioner in a primary health centre, find the right diagnosis faster. When the evidence pulled in two directions, the designers had to choose. Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) is the manual's own example: its pattern of symptoms, comorbidity and shared risk factors argued for a place among the neurodevelopmental disorders, but the same data also made a strong case for the chapter on disruptive, impulse-control and conduct disorders. The preponderance of evidence won, and ADHD went into the neurodevelopmental chapter. One last point matters for Section 1.9: this structure was deliberately made to correspond with the arrangement then being planned for ICD-11, so the two manuals now list their families of disorders in much the same order.

Exam trap

Count carefully: 22 chapters in Section II, of which 20 hold mental disorders and 2 hold conditions that are not. The order runs from neurodevelopmental (first) to neurocognitive (seventeenth), and the personality disorders come after the neurocognitive disorders, followed by the paraphilic disorders; do not assume personality disorders come early because they were once on Axis II. ADHD is a neurodevelopmental disorder, not a disruptive behaviour disorder, though DSM-5-TR admits the decision was close. Gambling disorder sits in the substance-related and addictive chapter. The last chapter lists problems, not disorders.

Making and recording a diagnosis: subtypes, specifiers, severity and codes

A diagnosis in DSM-5-TR is not made by counting ticks. The manual says so in as many words: it is not enough to check off the symptoms in a criteria set, because the criteria are offered as guidelines to be informed by clinical judgement and are "not meant to be used in a rigid cookbook fashion". Judgement cuts both ways. It may justify giving a diagnosis to someone whose presentation falls just short of the full criteria, as long as the symptoms that are present are persistent and severe; but a clinician who applies the criteria loosely and idiosyncratically destroys the very thing the manual exists to provide, a common language. The diagnosis is also meant to sit inside a case formulation: a careful history and a concise summary of the biological, psychological and social factors that predispose, precipitate, perpetuate and protect (Book 2, Section 2.9 teaches formulation in full).

Almost every criteria set ends with three kinds of clause, and each has a job. The first is the clinical significance criterion (Section 1.1.3), usually worded as a requirement that the disturbance cause clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning. It exists because, without biological tests, the manual cannot otherwise separate a disorder from the ordinary troubles of life. Sadness, worry, a sleepless night or a ritual before an examination are not pathological in themselves, and many people have them in mild forms without needing a diagnosis; the distress-or-impairment clause draws the threshold, and judging whether it is met (especially for work and family roles) often needs information from relatives as well as the patient. The second and third are the exclusion clauses: the disturbance must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance or another medical condition, and it must not be better explained by another mental disorder. Section 1.6.5 explains the logic of these rules and the old diagnostic hierarchy behind them.

Once the criteria are met, the clinician considers the extra descriptors the manual provides, which come in two kinds. A subtype divides a diagnosis into mutually exclusive and jointly exhaustive subgroups: every person with the disorder belongs to exactly one subtype. The criteria flag subtypes with the instruction "Specify whether". Anorexia nervosa is the manual's example: a patient has either the restricting type or the binge-eating/purging type, never both and never neither. A specifier, by contrast, is neither exclusive nor exhaustive, so a diagnosis may carry several or none; specifiers are flagged with "Specify" or "Specify if", as in social anxiety disorder, "performance only". Specifiers do two things. They pick out a more homogeneous subgroup (major depressive disorder "with mixed features") and they convey information that matters for management (a sleep-wake disorder "with other medical comorbidity").

Specifiers also differ in the time they describe, and this distinction is easy to miss. Most describe the current presentation: they can change as the disorder changes, and they may be given only if full criteria are met now. "With good or fair insight" in obsessive-compulsive disorder, "predominantly inattentive presentation" in ADHD and "in a controlled environment" in a substance use disorder are examples. A few describe the lifetime course and may be given whatever the person's current state, such as "with seasonal pattern" for recurrent mood episodes or "bipolar type" in schizoaffective disorder.

Two families of specifiers deserve their own names. Severity specifiers grade how severe the disorder is now, usually as mild, moderate or severe, but the rule for grading differs from chapter to chapter, and examiners like the differences. In major depressive disorder, severity rests on the number of symptoms, how intense they are and how much they disable. In a substance use disorder, it is a simple count of the eleven criteria: two or three is mild, four or five moderate, six or more severe. In intellectual developmental disorder, the levels are defined by adaptive functioning, not by IQ, because it is adaptive functioning that decides how much support a person needs, and IQ tests are less valid at the low end of the range (Chapter 3). And some disorders have no severity specifier at all: generalised anxiety disorder is one. Course specifiers describe where the person is in the life of the disorder. For mood disorders, "in partial remission" means symptoms are still present but full criteria are no longer met (or a symptom-free stretch has lasted less than two months), and "in full remission" means no significant symptoms for the past two months. For substance use disorders, "in early remission" means that none of the criteria (except craving) has been met for at least three but less than twelve months, and "in sustained remission" for twelve months or longer.

Watch two clinicians write these out. Jyoti, a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Lucknow, has had a depressive episode once before, three years ago, and is now in her second: low mood, poor sleep and appetite, guilt and fatigue, enough symptoms and impairment for the clinician to call it moderate; she is also tense, restless and convinced that something awful is about to happen. Her diagnosis is recorded as F33.1 major depressive disorder, recurrent episode, moderate, with anxious distress. The manual prescribes the order: the disorder, then single or recurrent, then the severity, psychotic or remission specifier, then any specifiers without codes. Suresh, a 45-year-old bus conductor in Pune, met five criteria for alcohol use disorder last year (a moderate disorder) and has not met any criterion except craving for the four months since his de-addiction admission. His record reads F10.21 alcohol use disorder, moderate, in early remission. Had he still been on a locked ward without access to alcohol, the clinician would have added "in a controlled environment".

Look at what the codes do and do not carry. Jyoti's code changes with recurrence and severity (a single moderate episode would have been F32.1, a recurrent mild one F33.0), but her anxious distress lives only in the words after the name. Suresh's code changes with his remission (an active moderate disorder is F10.20), but "in a controlled environment" would not change it. That is the general rule. DSM-5-TR has no codes of its own: in the United States the official coding system since 1 October 2015 has been the ICD-10-CM, the clinical modification of the WHO's ICD-10 prepared by the US National Center for Health Statistics, and the manual prints its codes in front of each diagnosis (Section 1.9.3). Most are F codes from the mental disorders chapter, but you will also meet G codes (tardive dyskinesia, G24.01), R codes (borderline intellectual functioning, R41.83), T codes (current suicidal behaviour, T14.91A) and many Z codes (Section 1.8.7). A few subtypes and specifiers are built into the code, usually in its fifth character: F02.80 is major neurocognitive disorder due to Alzheimer's disease without behavioural disturbance and F02.81 the same disorder with it. Most are not, and are written in words after the name, like "social anxiety disorder, performance only".

Three more recording rules complete the picture. First, DSM-5-TR allows as many diagnoses as the person meets criteria for. The one chiefly responsible for an admission is the principal diagnosis; in an outpatient clinic the one chiefly responsible for the visit is the reason for visit. It is listed first and may be marked "(principal diagnosis)" or "(reason for visit)", and the rest follow in order of their importance for attention and treatment. The choice is sometimes arbitrary: in a man admitted with both schizophrenia and alcohol use disorder, both may have contributed equally. When the main problem is a mental disorder due to another medical condition, ICD rules require the medical condition to be listed first, as in "G30.9 Alzheimer's disease, F02.80 major neurocognitive disorder due to probable Alzheimer's disease, without behavioural disturbance, mild". Second, a provisional diagnosis is recorded by adding "(provisional)" when the clinician strongly expects the criteria to be met but lacks the information to be sure. It has two uses. One is a patient who cannot yet give an adequate history, whose diagnosis waits for an informant or old records. The other is a diagnosis that depends on an upper time limit: schizophreniform disorder requires symptoms for at least one month but less than six, so a patient whose illness is still running is diagnosed "schizophreniform disorder (provisional)" until the six months are up, when the label is either confirmed or changed to schizophrenia (Section 4.5.1). Third, there are no axes any more. Disorders that DSM-IV split across Axes I, II and III are listed together, and the psychosocial problems once listed on Axis IV are coded with the Z codes of Section 1.8.7 (Section 1.7.6 tells why the axes went).

Running case: recording Dinesh in DSM-5-TR

Section 1.7.5 told Dinesh's story in full and built his five-axis DSM-IV-TR record. A clinician in the same Delhi hospital clinic, writing him up in DSM-5-TR today, would record:

F41.1 generalised anxiety disorder (reason for visit)
D50.9 iron-deficiency anaemia
Z55.9 other problems related to education and literacy (failed the preliminary recruitment examination; irregular coaching)
Z59.9 other economic problem (family debt taken on for his coaching fees)
Z60.0 phase of life problem (the approaching marriage)

Notice what became of each axis. The Axis I disorder heads the list. Nothing replaces "no diagnosis" on Axis II: his anxious, conscientious traits are described in the formulation, not coded. The anaemia, once on Axis III, sits in the same list with its own ICD-10-CM code, because DSM-5-TR rejects any real distinction between mental disorders and other medical conditions. The three Axis IV problems become three Z codes; the clinician chose Z55.9 rather than Z55.2 ("failed school examinations") because his was a recruitment examination, not a school one. The GAF of 58 has no successor in the list, although a WHODAS 2.0 could be given (Section 1.8.5). Nothing follows "generalised anxiety disorder", because its criteria set offers no subtype, specifier or severity grade (the general "with panic attacks" specifier, which can be added to any anxiety disorder, would apply only if he had panic attacks, and he has none). And his conviction that he is losing dhat does not appear among the diagnoses at all: it is his explanation of his illness, and it belongs in the cultural formulation (Section 1.10.3).

Exam trap

Subtype = mutually exclusive and jointly exhaustive, flagged "Specify whether" (anorexia nervosa, restricting or binge-eating/purging type). Specifier = can be combined or omitted, flagged "Specify" or "Specify if" (social anxiety disorder, performance only). Most specifiers describe the current state and need full criteria now; a few, like "with seasonal pattern", describe the lifetime course. Severity in intellectual developmental disorder is graded by adaptive functioning, not IQ; in substance use disorders by the number of criteria (2 to 3 mild, 4 to 5 moderate, 6 or more severe); generalised anxiety disorder has no severity specifier. DSM-5-TR has no codes of its own: it prints ICD-10-CM codes, and most specifiers are not in the code. "Provisional" means the information is incomplete; it is not the same as "unspecified" (Section 1.8.4).

When nothing fits: other specified and unspecified disorders

However carefully criteria sets are drawn, real patients spill over their edges. Some have a recognisable cluster of symptoms that falls just outside every specific disorder; some are seen in settings where there is no time to find out more. The manual's designers accept this openly: decades of research have not produced a set of categories that covers the full range of mental disorders people bring to clinics around the world. So almost every chapter of Section II ends with two escape hatches, to be used when the symptoms typical of that chapter predominate and cause clinically significant distress or impairment but do not meet the full criteria for any specific disorder in it.

The first is the other specified disorder. The clinician writes the name of the category followed by the specific reason the presentation does not fit. The manual's own example is a person with persistent hallucinations and no other psychotic symptoms, recorded as "other specified schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorder, with persistent auditory hallucinations" (F28). In the anxiety chapter, a woman who worries excessively about many things, with all the accompanying tension and fatigue, but on fewer days than the "more days than not" that generalised anxiety disorder demands, would be recorded as "other specified anxiety disorder, generalised anxiety occurring less often than more days than not" (F41.8). The second is the unspecified disorder, used when the clinician chooses not to give a reason, or cannot. Its natural home is the emergency department at two in the morning, where an agitated young man brought in by his family is clearly hallucinating but nobody yet knows for how long, or whether a drug or an illness is responsible; "unspecified schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorder" (F29) is a placeholder until a proper differential diagnosis is possible.

Read that again, because it is the whole distinction: the difference between the two is nothing but the clinician's choice to record, or not to record, why the presentation falls short. Both require clinically significant distress or impairment, both may be used in any setting, and the manual calls the choice "entirely a matter of clinical judgment". For presentations that fit no chapter at all, the twentieth chapter offers other specified mental disorder and unspecified mental disorder (both coded F99), and their counterparts due to another medical condition (F06.8 and F09).

Why two categories instead of one? Because the single catch-all of earlier editions had become a problem. DSM-IV closed most chapters with a "not otherwise specified" (NOS) category, and in some areas it swallowed the specific diagnoses. The eating disorders were the notorious case: in a 2005 paper the Oxford psychiatrist Christopher G. Fairburn and his colleague Kristin Bohn called "eating disorder not otherwise specified" an example of the "troublesome" NOS category, because it was the commonest eating disorder diagnosis in routine clinical practice, more common than anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa, and yet it had barely been studied and its treatment not at all. A diagnosis that tells you only that someone has an eating disorder of some kind says very little. DSM-5 replaced NOS with the two new options, so that a clinician who knows why a patient does not fit can say so, and it promoted some of the commonest leftover patterns to full disorders of their own, binge-eating disorder among them.

The "other specified" lists in each chapter are worth reading, because they show where the manual expects its boundaries to leak. By long convention they include the proposals in the conditions for further study (Section 1.8.6) as examples, without any endorsement that these proposals are valid: "other specified bipolar and related disorder" lists short-duration hypomania (Section 5.4.6), for instance. Other examples come from culture. "Other specified anxiety disorder" lists khyâl cap ("wind attacks") and ataque de nervios ("attack of nerves"), two of the cultural concepts of distress you will meet in Section 1.10 and Section 6.1.8, and the dissociative chapter's own list includes identity disturbance after intense coercive persuasion (Section 7.5.2).

Exam trap

The only difference between "other specified" and "unspecified" is whether the clinician records the reason the presentation does not meet full criteria. "Unspecified" does not mean mild, provisional or doubtful, and "other specified" does not mean more severe. Both need clinically significant distress or impairment; without it there is no disorder to name. Both replaced DSM-IV's "not otherwise specified" (NOS). Do not confuse "other specified" with the specifiers of Section 1.8.3, which describe a disorder whose full criteria are met.

Section III: measures, culture and the alternative personality model

Section III opens with a short argument that you have already met in Section 1.6.4: pure categories fit mental disorders badly, and a growing body of evidence favours dimensions. The manual's proposal is not to abandon its categories but to combine them with dimensional measurement. A combined approach lets a clinician say how severe each symptom is (its intensity, duration and effect on functioning), including symptoms outside the criteria set, and it picks out people who meet no criteria yet are severely troubled and need treatment. Until biological measures arrive, these dimensions rest on what the patient reports and the clinician observes. The chapter "Assessment Measures" supplies the tools, all of which belong properly to the Assessment book; here, one line each.

The cross-cutting symptom measures are modelled on general medicine's review of systems, the doctor's routine run through every organ system in case something has been missed. The Level 1 measure asks one to three questions about each of 13 symptom domains for adults, who rate themselves, and 12 domains for children aged 6 to 17, rated by a parent or guardian (a self-rated version exists for adolescents aged 11 to 17). A worrying answer leads to a Level 2 measure that explores that domain, such as depression, anxiety, mania, anger, irritability or somatic symptoms, in more depth. Repeated at follow-up visits, the measures support what the manual calls measurement-based care. Disorder-specific severity measures, available online, grade particular diagnoses (generalised anxiety disorder, social anxiety disorder, posttraumatic stress disorder and autism spectrum disorder among them), and the printed book includes the Clinician-Rated Dimensions of Psychosis Symptom Severity (Section 4.4). Finally, the WHODAS 2.0, the World Health Organization Disability Assessment Schedule, version 2.0, measures disability rather than symptoms: the adult self-administered version has 36 items, asks about the past 30 days and covers six domains (understanding and communicating, getting around, self-care, getting along with people, life activities at home, work or school, and participation in society). It is based on the WHO's International Classification of Functioning, Disability and Health, can be used for any medical condition, and is DSM-5's replacement for the GAF of the old Axis V (Section 1.7.6).

The chapter Culture and Psychiatric Diagnosis defines the manual's key cultural terms, presents the cultural formulation and the Cultural Formulation Interview with its informant version, and contains the glossary of cultural concepts of distress; Section 1.10 teaches all of it.

The Alternative DSM-5 Model for Personality Disorders is the manual's experiment with a different way of thinking about personality pathology. The Section II personality disorders are the ten categorical diagnoses carried over almost unchanged from DSM-IV, and they have well-known weaknesses: a patient who meets criteria for one usually meets criteria for others, and "other specified" or "unspecified personality disorder" is often the correct but uninformative answer. The alternative is a hybrid dimensional-categorical model. Criterion A rates the level of impairment in personality functioning, in the self (identity, self-direction) and in relationships (empathy, intimacy), on a five-level scale from little or no impairment (level 0) to extreme impairment (level 4); at least moderate impairment is needed for a diagnosis. Criterion B identifies pathological personality traits in five broad domains, negative affectivity, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition and psychoticism, which contain 25 trait facets. From these the model derives six specific personality disorders (antisocial, avoidant, borderline, narcissistic, obsessive-compulsive and schizotypal) and a diagnosis of personality disorder, trait specified, for everyone else. Chapter 11 teaches the model; Section 1.9.6 shows how ICD-11 went further and made a dimensional model its official one.

Exam trap

The WHODAS 2.0 replaced the GAF; it is a 36-item measure of disability in six domains, not of symptoms, and it is not "Axis V" under a new name, because DSM-5 has no axes. Level 1 cross-cutting measure: 13 domains for adults, 12 for children. The alternative model's five trait domains are negative affectivity, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition and psychoticism; ICD-11's five are negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition and anankastia (Section 1.9.6). The alternative model is in Section III; the ten categorical personality disorders remain the official ones in Section II.

Conditions for further study

Every revision of the manual faces a stream of proposals for new disorders, from researchers who have studied a pattern for years and from clinicians who meet it every week. Some are ready; many are not. The conditions for further study are DSM-5-TR's waiting room: proposed conditions for which there is not yet enough scientific evidence to support widespread clinical use. Each comes with proposed criteria and a supporting text, so that researchers everywhere can study the same thing in the same way and future editions can decide, on the evidence, whether to admit it. The thresholds and durations in them were set by expert consensus, informed by literature reviews, reanalyses of data and field trials where these existed, and the manual is emphatic that these criteria sets are not intended for clinical use: only Section II disorders are officially recognised. A clinician who sees a patient who fits one records a Section II diagnosis instead, usually an "other specified" one, or one of the codes of Section 1.8.7.

The waiting room has a working exit. DSM-5 (2013) placed persistent complex bereavement disorder, a severe and long-lasting grief, among its conditions for further study. In the years that followed, thorough reviews found enough evidence of its validity, reliability and clinical usefulness, and DSM-5-TR moved it into Section II, in the trauma- and stressor-related chapter, with reformulated criteria and a new name, prolonged grief disorder (Section 7.4.3). That leaves seven conditions in the current manual, listed in Table 1.22.

Table 1.22 The seven conditions for further study in DSM-5-TR Section III.
Proposed conditionCore of the proposed criteriaRelated teaching
Attenuated psychosis syndromeAttenuated (milder, below-threshold) delusions, hallucinations or disorganised speech, present at least once a week for the past month, begun or worsened in the past year, distressing and disabling enough to need attention; criteria for a psychotic disorder never metSection 4.5.8
Depressive episodes with short-duration hypomaniaAt least one lifetime major depressive episode, plus at least two lifetime hypomanic periods that last at least 2 but less than 4 consecutive days, too short for a hypomanic episodeSection 5.4.6
Caffeine use disorderA problematic pattern of caffeine use meeting at least the first three of nine criteria within 12 months: failure to cut down, continued use despite caffeine-related problems, and withdrawalChapter 10
Internet gaming disorderPersistent, recurrent gaming on the internet, often with other players, with 5 or more of 9 criteria in 12 months (preoccupation, withdrawal when gaming is taken away, tolerance, failed attempts to control it, loss of other interests, continued excessive use despite problems, deceiving others about it, gaming to escape a bad mood, jeopardising a relationship, job or education); gambling excludedChapter 10
Neurobehavioural disorder associated with prenatal alcohol exposureMore than minimal exposure to alcohol in the womb, with impaired neurocognitive functioning, impaired self-regulation and impaired adaptive functioningChapter 3
Suicidal behaviour disorderA suicide attempt within the last 24 months; not non-suicidal self-injury; not applied to suicidal ideation or preparatory acts; not begun in delirium or confusion; not done solely for a political or religious objective. Specify current (within 12 months) or in early remission (12 to 24 months)Section 5.8
Non-suicidal self-injury disorderOn 5 or more days in the last year, intentional damage to the surface of one's own body likely to cause bleeding, bruising or pain (cutting, burning, stabbing, hitting, excessive rubbing), expecting only minor or moderate harm and without suicidal intent; done to relieve a negative feeling, resolve an interpersonal difficulty or produce a positive feeling; not socially sanctionedSection 5.8.2

A few of these deserve a sentence more. Attenuated psychosis syndrome describes the young person whose suspicions, odd perceptions or loosened speech are real and worsening but not yet fixed: doubt can still be raised, scepticism about the strange perception can still be induced, and insight is reasonably preserved. It was proposed because such people are at raised risk of a first psychotic episode. The arguments over it (would the label bring early help, or frighten and medicate young people, most of whom never become psychotic?) show exactly why a proposal waits in Section III until the evidence settles the question. Caffeine use disorder has an unusual rule: the first three criteria are compulsory, a stricter algorithm than other substance use disorders use, so that only cases with real clinical significance are picked up. Internet gaming disorder is the only proposed behavioural addiction; gambling disorder, its nearest relative, is already a Section II disorder, and DSM-5-TR notes that compulsive internet gaming has been called an addiction by the Chinese government and treated as a public health threat in South Korea. Sexual internet use, work use and gambling online are all excluded. ICD-11 has already taken the step DSM has not and lists gaming disorder as an official diagnosis (Section 1.9.6).

The last two proposals are a pair, and they explain question 8 in the past papers. Suicidal behaviour disorder defines a suicide attempt as a self-initiated sequence of behaviours that the person expected, at the moment of starting it, to lead to death. Non-suicidal self-injury disorder is the opposite intent with similar hands: the person damages the surface of the body expecting only minor or moderate harm, and does it for what it does to feelings. Take Ananya, a 19-year-old engineering student in a Hyderabad hostel. On seven or eight evenings in the past year, after rows with her parents on the phone, she has scratched her forearm with the point of a compass until it bled; the tension, she says, "drains out", and she has never wanted to die. Her pattern fits the proposed criteria for non-suicidal self-injury disorder exactly. But because that disorder is in Section III, her clinician cannot diagnose it. What DSM-5-TR offers instead, new in the text revision, are codes in Section II: R45.88 current non-suicidal self-injury (and Z91.52 for a past history), with T14.91A and Z91.51 for current and past suicidal behaviour. These codes can be attached to any diagnosis, such as a depressive disorder if Ananya has one, or used with no mental disorder diagnosis at all. The behaviour is recorded and treated (Section 5.8.2); only the label of a disorder waits for the evidence.

Exam trap

Question 8 asks for the proposed criteria set that involves cutting, burning or otherwise hurting one's own body without the intent to die. The answer is non-suicidal self-injury (disorder). The other options are traps because they too were Section III conditions: internet gaming disorder is excessive gaming, attenuated psychosis syndrome is sub-threshold psychosis, and persistent complex bereavement disorder was DSM-5's proposal for severe, lasting grief. That last one is no longer a condition for further study at all: DSM-5-TR (2022) made it an official Section II diagnosis, prolonged grief disorder. Also remember: suicidal behaviour disorder (an attempt with intent to die, within 24 months) is a separate proposal, and conditions for further study are not for clinical diagnosis, though DSM-5-TR's Section II codes (R45.88, T14.91A) let the behaviour itself be recorded.

Other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention

Much of what brings people to a clinic is not a mental disorder. A couple whose marriage is collapsing, a student who has failed, a family drowning in debt, a widow in the first weeks of mourning, a man who wants a certificate of illness to escape a court date: none of them has a disorder, yet each is a legitimate focus of a clinician's attention, and each problem may shape whatever disorder is also present. The last chapter of Section II, "Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention", gives these problems names and codes. Its contents are, in the manual's words, not mental disorders; they are listed so that clinicians can document the full range of issues they meet. A condition from this chapter may be coded, with or without a mental disorder, for any of four reasons: it is the reason for the current visit; it explains why a test, procedure or treatment is needed; it played a part in starting or worsening a mental disorder; or it is a problem the overall management plan must take into account.

Because almost all of them borrow their codes from the Z section of ICD-10-CM, these are known informally as Z codes, the successors of DSM-IV's "V codes". The nickname is not quite accurate. Current suicidal behaviour is T14.91A at an initial encounter (T14.91D afterwards), current non-suicidal self-injury R45.88, overweight or obesity E66.9, age-related cognitive decline R41.81 and borderline intellectual functioning R41.83. And because the names are DSM's but the codes are borrowed, one code can carry two DSM names: Z59.9 serves as both "other housing problem" and "other economic problem", because in ICD-10-CM it means "problem related to housing and economic circumstances, unspecified". The chapter has fourteen groups, shown with examples in Table 1.23.

Table 1.23 Other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention: the groups, with examples and their codes.
GroupExamples (code)
Suicidal behaviour and non-suicidal self-injuryCurrent suicidal behaviour, initial encounter (T14.91A); history of suicidal behaviour (Z91.51); current non-suicidal self-injury (R45.88); history of non-suicidal self-injury (Z91.52)
Abuse and neglectChild physical abuse, confirmed, initial encounter (T74.12XA); separate codes for suspected abuse, for services to a victim or perpetrator, and for a past history
Relational problemsRelationship distress with spouse or intimate partner (Z63.0); high expressed emotion level within family (Z63.8)
Educational problemsFailed school examinations (Z55.2); other problems related to education and literacy (Z55.9)
Occupational problemsUnemployment (Z56.0)
Housing problemsInadequate housing (Z59.1)
Economic problemsLow income (Z59.6); other economic problem (Z59.9)
Social environmentAcculturation difficulty (Z60.3); social exclusion or rejection (Z60.4)

Other psychosocial, personal and environmental circumstancesVictim of crime (Z65.4)
Access to medical and other health careUnavailability or inaccessibility of health care facilities (Z75.3)
Circumstances of personal historyPersonal history of psychological trauma (Z91.49)
Counselling and medical adviceSex counselling (Z70.9)
Additional conditions or problemsWandering associated with a mental disorder (Z91.83); uncomplicated bereavement (Z63.4); phase of life problem (Z60.0); religious or spiritual problem (Z65.8); adult antisocial behaviour (Z72.811); child or adolescent antisocial behaviour (Z72.810); nonadherence to medical treatment (Z91.19); overweight or obesity (E66.9); malingering (Z76.5); age-related cognitive decline (R41.81); borderline intellectual functioning (R41.83)

Four entries in the "additional conditions" group appear in examinations again and again. Malingering is the intentional production of false or grossly exaggerated physical or psychological symptoms, motivated by an external incentive: avoiding military duty or work, obtaining financial compensation, evading criminal prosecution or obtaining drugs. It is not always a vice; a prisoner of war who feigns illness may be behaving entirely sensibly. The manual tells clinicians to consider it strongly when any combination of four signs is present: a medicolegal context (the person is sent by a lawyer, or comes while litigation or charges are pending), a marked gap between the disability claimed and what is objectively found, poor cooperation with assessment and treatment, and antisocial personality disorder. The decisive contrast is with factitious disorder, in which symptoms are also produced deliberately but with no external incentive; the person seeks the sick role itself. Clear proof of feigning, such as a paralysed arm that works perfectly at home, therefore points to factitious disorder if the aim seems to be to be a patient and to malingering if it is to get money or escape a duty. Because its motive is ordinary gain, malingering is a Z code, while factitious disorder is a mental disorder in the somatic symptom chapter (Section 7.9.6).

Borderline intellectual functioning is used when a person's borderline intellectual level is the focus of attention or affects treatment or prognosis. DSM-IV-TR defined it by an IQ in the 71 to 84 range; DSM-5 dropped the number and now says only that telling it apart from mild intellectual developmental disorder needs a careful assessment of both intellectual and adaptive functioning and of the gap between them, especially when another disorder, such as schizophrenia or ADHD with severe impulsivity, may be spoiling test performance. Either way, it is not intellectual disability (Chapter 3). Uncomplicated bereavement is a normal reaction to the death of a loved one that becomes a focus of attention; the bereaved person may have symptoms that look like a major depressive episode (sadness, insomnia, poor appetite, weight loss) yet regard the low mood as normal and seek help only for the sleeplessness or the loss of appetite, and the manual notes that normal grief varies a great deal between cultural groups (Section 7.4.3 separates it from prolonged grief disorder and depression). The phase of life problem covers difficulty adjusting to a transition in the life cycle: entering or finishing school, leaving parental control, getting married, starting a career, becoming a parent, the "empty nest" or retirement. It is Dinesh's third Z code, for the marriage fixed for after his results. Two others have obvious uses in India: the religious or spiritual problem, for distress over loss or questioning of faith or conversion to a new one, and nonadherence to medical treatment, which the manual allows only when the problem is serious enough to need attention in its own right, whatever its cause (side effects, cost, beliefs about the treatment, the illness itself).

The neighbouring chapter, medication-induced movement disorders and other adverse effects of medication, lists medication-induced parkinsonism, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, medication-induced acute dystonia, medication-induced acute akathisia, tardive dyskinesia, tardive dystonia and tardive akathisia, medication-induced postural tremor and antidepressant discontinuation syndrome, with a category for other adverse effects. They are not mental disorders either, and the manual gives two reasons for printing them in Section II: they matter constantly in the management of patients on medication, and they must be told apart from mental disorders in differential diagnosis. The manual's own example is the restless patient on an antipsychotic: is this an anxiety disorder, or acute akathisia caused by the drug? Sections 4.10.4 and 4.10.5 teach the antipsychotic side effects and Section 5.7.3 the antidepressant discontinuation syndrome.

Exam trap

The "other conditions" codes record problems, not mental disorders; the chapter is the home of DSM-IV's old Axis IV psychosocial and environmental problems (Section 1.7.6). Malingering is a Z code (Z76.5) with an external motive; factitious disorder is a mental disorder with no external motive. Borderline intellectual functioning (R41.83) is not intellectual disability. Uncomplicated bereavement (Z63.4) is not prolonged grief disorder, which is a Section II disorder. Not every "Z code" begins with Z: suicidal behaviour is T14.91A, non-suicidal self-injury R45.88.

1.9 The ICD: From ICD-6 to ICD-11Lower yield · 2 Qs

The DSM is the manual of one national professional body. The International Classification of Diseases (ICD) is the world's. It is published by the World Health Organization (WHO), it covers every disease, injury and reason for contact with health services, from cholera to a fractured wrist, and member countries use it to record deaths and illnesses and to report them in a form that can be compared across the globe. For a clinical psychologist in India it matters even more than the DSM. The diagnosis on a government hospital case sheet, the categories in national mental health statistics and the legal test for mental illness in the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 all point to the ICD. Its mental disorders chapter, however, grew out of something much older and much grimmer than psychiatry: the counting of the dead.

From a list of causes of death to ICD-6

In 1837 England and Wales set up a General Register Office to record every birth, marriage and death, and it found in William Farr (1807 to 1883), its first medical statistician, a man determined to make those records mean something. The causes of death were written down in a chaos of names: one disease went by three or four terms, and one term was applied to several diseases. In the Registrar General's first annual report Farr argued that a uniform statistical nomenclature, however imperfect, mattered as much to his kind of inquiry "as weights and measures in the physical sciences". The idea spread. In 1853 the first International Statistical Congress, meeting in Brussels, asked Farr and Marc d'Espine of Geneva to prepare a single classification of causes of death that every country could use. At the next Congress, in Paris in 1855, they arrived with two lists built on completely different principles. Farr grouped diseases into epidemic diseases, constitutional (general) diseases, local diseases arranged by the part of the body affected, developmental diseases and the results of violence; d'Espine grouped them by their supposed nature (gouty, herpetic, haematic and so on). The Congress settled for a compromise list of 139 headings. It was never universally accepted, but Farr's principle of classifying many diseases by anatomical site survived into every later revision.

The next step came from Paris. In 1891 the International Statistical Institute, successor to the Congress, asked a committee chaired by Jacques Bertillon (1851 to 1922), head of the statistical services of the City of Paris, to prepare a classification of causes of death. His committee's report was adopted at the Institute's meeting in Chicago in 1893, and the Bertillon Classification of Causes of Death, as it was first called, was soon taken up by several countries and many cities. In 1898 the American Public Health Association recommended it to Canada, Mexico and the United States and proposed that it be revised every ten years. In August 1900 the French government called the first International Conference for the Revision of the Bertillon or International List of Causes of Death; delegates from 26 countries adopted a detailed list of 179 groups and an abridged list of 35. That 1900 list is counted as the first ICD, and further revision conferences followed in 1909, 1920, 1929 and 1938. These were lists for counting deaths. They had no section devoted to mental disorders, which began to matter to the classifiers only when illness in the living, and not just death, was to be counted.

The Second World War changed the owner and the purpose of the list. In 1946 the International Health Conference in New York gave the interim commission of the newly founded WHO two tasks: to prepare the next ten-yearly revision of the lists of causes of death, and to establish international lists of causes of illness. The Sixth Revision Conference met in Paris from 26 to 30 April 1948, and later that year the First World Health Assembly adopted its work. The result, published as the Manual of the International Statistical Classification of Diseases, Injuries, and Causes of Death, was ICD-6, and it broke with the past in two ways. It was the first revision to classify morbidity (illness in the living) as well as mortality, a comprehensive list for both; and, because illness and not only death was now being counted, it was the first to include a section on mental disorders.

That first section did not have much influence. The American Psychiatric Association published DSM-I in 1952, four years later, and the epidemiologist Morton Kramer of the US National Institute of Mental Health later explained, in the introduction to DSM-II, why it had needed a manual of its own. ICD-6 had no headings for many of the diagnoses American psychiatrists used, such as most of the chronic brain syndromes and the transient situational personality disorders, so a working party had to invent extra categories to fit DSM-I into it. Worse, the mental disorders section was not self-contained: general paralysis of the insane, the madness of late syphilis, was classified under syphilis, and post-encephalitic psychosis under the late effects of encephalitis. The seventh revision, agreed in Paris in February 1955, was deliberately limited to essential changes and the correction of errors, and it did not revise the mental disorders section at all.

Key study: Stengel's survey for the WHO (1959)

The United States was not alone in its frustration. The WHO asked Erwin Stengel (1902 to 1973), a psychiatrist from Vienna who had settled in Britain and became professor of psychiatry at Sheffield, to survey the classifications of mental disorders in use around the world. His report, "Classification of mental disorders", filled more than sixty pages of the Bulletin of the World Health Organization in 1959. It found dissatisfaction with the ICD's mental disorders section in every member country, and it put its finger on the root of the trouble: psychiatrists did not agree about the concepts on which a classification should be built, diagnoses could rarely be verified objectively, and the same condition went by a confusing variety of names in different countries. Stengel's remedy was the operational definition: since the pathology and causes of most mental disorders were unknown, each category should be defined by what a clinician can observe and record, not by a theory of its cause. The report led the WHO to urge its member states to work together on a classification that all of them could accept, and its central idea (define the category by observable features, and let the arguments about cause wait) can be traced through the glossaries of ICD-8 and ICD-9 to the explicit criteria of DSM-III and of ICD-10's research criteria.

Exam trap

ICD-6, adopted in 1948 by the First World Health Assembly, was the first ICD with a section on mental disorders and the first to classify illness as well as death. It was the first revision prepared by the WHO; the five before it (1900 to 1938) were convened by the French government. Do not write 1952 for ICD-6: 1952 is DSM-I. The ICD's ancestors are lists of causes of death (Farr and d'Espine from 1853; Bertillon's list adopted in 1893; the first International List in 1900), not psychiatric textbooks.

ICD-8 and ICD-9: glossaries and a shared language

Stengel's report started a decade of international work, and its product was the eighth revision. National committees met, proposals were traded (the American recommendations, presented in Geneva by Henry Brill, argued for a single, comprehensive section for all mental disorders), and the draft passed through the WHO's expert committee in October 1964. The Eighth Revision Conference in Geneva approved it in July 1965, the Nineteenth World Health Assembly adopted it in May 1966, and ICD-8 came into effect in member states in 1968. ICD-8 gave the mental disorders a single, comprehensive section, added categories that ICD-6 had lacked (non-psychotic mental disorders associated with physical conditions, physical disorders of presumably psychogenic origin, transient situational disturbances) and gave mental retardation a much fuller classification. Kramer called it "a compromise which will fully satisfy psychiatrists neither in the U.S. nor in any other country", and that was its strength: for the first time, the American Psychiatric Association decided to base its manual on the ICD, and DSM-II and ICD-8 went into effect in the same year (Section 1.7.2).

A list of names, however well agreed, does not tell two psychiatrists in different countries what each name means. So in November 1961 a WHO expert subcommittee had recommended an international glossary of operational definitions to go with the revised classification. It took time: the WHO's Glossary of Mental Disorders and Guide to Their Classification, for use with ICD-8, appeared in 1974, and a successor for the ninth revision followed in 1978. A glossary is not a set of diagnostic criteria. It describes each category in a paragraph, as a portrait of the typical case, and leaves the clinician to decide how close a patient must come to the portrait. But it gave the ICD's psychiatric terms an internationally agreed meaning, which is what Stengel had asked for.

The ninth revision kept the ICD's basic structure but added much more detail, with four-digit subcategories and some optional fifth digits, and it introduced the "dagger and asterisk" system that lets one diagnosis be coded twice, once for the underlying disease and once for its manifestation in a particular organ. The Ninth Revision Conference met in Geneva from 30 September to 6 October 1975, the Twenty-ninth World Health Assembly approved it in May 1976, the WHO published its first volume in 1977, and ICD-9 came into effect on 1 January 1979. In it the mental disorders occupied the numeric codes 290 to 319. The United States, needing more detail for hospital records and insurance claims, produced its own clinical modification, the ICD-9-CM, and from DSM-III (1980) to DSM-IV-TR the DSM printed ICD-9-CM codes beside its diagnoses: the familiar three digits and a decimal, such as 300.02 for generalised anxiety disorder on Dinesh's old Axis I (Section 1.7.5). DSM-5 (2013) printed both ICD-9-CM and ICD-10-CM codes, because the United States switched to the latter in October 2015.

For all this progress, the systems of the 1960s and 1970s had a serious weakness. They lacked precision, they often differed substantially from one another, they leaned on unproven theories of cause that not every school of psychiatry accepted, and their reliability was low: two clinicians looking at the same patient often reached different diagnoses, and as late as the 1970s countries such as France and Russia still used national systems of their own. The American answer, explicit diagnostic criteria, arrived with DSM-III in 1980 (Section 1.7.3). The ICD's answer took another decade. Figure 1.14 sets the whole journey, from Farr's first list to ICD-11, on one line.

Remember: why ICD dates differ

Each revision passes through several events: a revision conference agrees it, the World Health Assembly adopts it, the WHO publishes it, and it comes into force some years later. ICD-8: conference 1965, Assembly 1966, in force 1968 (Barlow gives 1969 for its publication). ICD-9: conference 1975, Assembly 1976, published 1977, in force 1979 (DSM-5-TR says "published in 1975 and implemented in 1978"). ICD-10: endorsed 1990, mental disorders guidelines 1992, formally in force 1 January 1993, research criteria 1993, in use by member states from 1994. ICD-11: adopted 2019, in force 2022. When an option gives a year, ask which event it means.

ICD-10: Chapter V (F)

The tenth revision took longer than any before it. The ICD was now used for so many purposes that the WHO concluded the old ten-year interval was too short to evaluate one revision before starting the next, and member states agreed to postpone the revision conference from 1985 to 1989. The Forty-third World Health Assembly endorsed ICD-10 in May 1990; it formally came into effect on 1 January 1993, and member states began to use it from 1994 (which is why both years appear in books). Its full name, the International Statistical Classification of Diseases and Related Health Problems, signals its wider reach. In the current WHO version it has 22 chapters, numbered in Roman numerals, and mental disorders form Chapter V, "Mental and behavioural disorders", with codes F00 to F99. Its developers worked closely with those of DSM-IV, so that the two systems of the 1990s were far more alike than any before them (Section 1.7.4).

The F codes are alphanumeric, and their structure is worth learning because examiners and case sheets assume it. Every code starts with a letter, F for this chapter, followed by two digits; this three-character code is a category. The first digit after the F places the category in one of the chapter's ten numbered blocks (F0 to F9, plus F99), so F4 means the block F40 to F48, the neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders. After a decimal point, a fourth character divides the category into as many as ten subcategories. So F41 is "other anxiety disorders" and F41.1 is generalised anxiety disorder; F32 is "depressive episode" and F32.1 a moderate depressive episode; F20 is schizophrenia and F20.0 paranoid schizophrenia. Figure 1.15, in Section 1.9.7, takes one code apart. The structure has a price. Because the second character is a single decimal digit, the chapter can hold at most ten main blocks, and the WHO itself later admitted that this artificial limit forced together disorders that had little in common, as you will see in Section 1.9.4.

Chapter V was never meant to be used on its own, so the WHO published a family of companion books. The Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Guidelines (CDDG, 1992), known from its cover as the "blue book", is the clinician's version: for each disorder it describes the main clinical features and then gives diagnostic guidelines, deliberately flexible, saying how many symptoms and how long a duration are usually needed for a confident diagnosis and leaving room for judgement. The Diagnostic Criteria for Research (DCR-10, 1993), the "green book", is the researcher's version, with explicit, DSM-style criteria so that research samples in different countries are comparable. A primary care version (1996) cut the chapter down to a short list of the conditions most often met in general practice, grouping some disorders together because they need similar management, and a multiaxial presentation of ICD-10 for adult psychiatry followed in 1997; the main ICD itself has never used axes.

One more chapter of ICD-10 matters to a clinician. Chapter XXI, "Factors influencing health status and contact with health services", holds the Z codes Z00 to Z99, and its block Z55 to Z65 covers potential health hazards related to socioeconomic and psychosocial circumstances: problems related to education and literacy (Z55), employment (Z56), housing and economic circumstances (Z59), the social environment (Z60) and the primary support group (Z63), among others. These are the ancestors of DSM-5-TR's other-conditions codes (Section 1.8.7), and because the United States built its ICD-10-CM on ICD-10, the code numbers are often the same. ICD-10-CM adds detail (its codes may run to seven characters, as in T74.12XA, "child physical abuse, confirmed, initial encounter"), and it has been the official US coding system since 1 October 2015.

Indian context

ICD-10 is still the working language of Indian psychiatry. Hospital case records, medical-college teaching and official health statistics are built around its F codes and the blue book's guidelines, and the move to ICD-11 is gradual. The National Mental Health Survey of India, 2015-16, made its diagnoses with the Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview, which gives ICD-10 diagnoses (Book 2, Section 1.10.11). The law points the same way. Section 3(1) of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 says that mental illness shall be determined in accordance with nationally or internationally accepted medical standards, "including the latest edition of the International Classification of Disease of the World Health Organisation", as notified by the Central Government (Book 2, Section 2.10.4). The DSM dominates textbooks, research papers and entrance examinations; the ICD dominates records and the law. An Indian clinician needs both.

Exam trap

ICD-10's mental disorders chapter is Chapter V (Roman five), codes F00 to F99. Its dates: endorsed by the World Health Assembly in 1990, the clinical guidelines ("blue book", CDDG) in 1992, the research criteria ("green book", DCR-10) in 1993, in use from 1994 (its formal date of entry into force is 1 January 1993). The blue book gives flexible guidelines for clinicians; the green book gives explicit criteria for research. ICD-10-CM is the United States' clinical modification (the source of DSM-5-TR's codes), not a WHO edition. The psychosocial Z codes are in Chapter XXI, not Chapter V.

The eleven blocks of ICD-10 Chapter V

Chapter V is divided into eleven blocks, one for each first digit from F0 to F9 plus the single code F99. Learn them as a sequence with a logic, not as a list. The chapter opens with the two blocks defined by a known cause, a disease of the brain or body (F00 to F09) and a psychoactive substance (F10 to F19), which is the aetiological approach of Section 1.6.3 surviving inside a mostly descriptive classification. Then come the great descriptive groups of adult psychiatry, roughly from the most to the least severe: the psychoses (F2), the mood disorders (F3), the neurotic disorders (F4), the disorders tied to bodily functions such as eating, sleep and sex (F5) and the disorders of personality (F6). The last three numbered blocks belong to development and childhood: mental retardation (F7), disorders of psychological development (F8) and behavioural and emotional disorders beginning in childhood (F9). A sentence to carry the order into the examination hall: Only Some Students Memorise Notes; Bright People Remember Details Clearly (Organic, Substance, Schizophrenia, Mood, Neurotic, Behavioural-physiological, Personality, Retardation, Development, Childhood). Table 1.24 gives each block's exact title and examples, all checked against the WHO's ICD-10 browser.

A few details in the table repay a second look. In the substance block the third character names the substance (F10 alcohol, F15 other stimulants including caffeine, F17 tobacco) and the fourth names the clinical state, so F10.2 is the alcohol dependence syndrome and F17.2 dependence on tobacco; Chapter 10 teaches the scheme. The F4 block is the one the WHO later singled out as a casualty of the ten-block limit: it holds phobias, panic, generalised anxiety, obsessive-compulsive disorder, the reactions to stress (including post-traumatic stress disorder), the dissociative and conversion disorders, the somatoform disorders and neurasthenia, a heterogeneous crowd held together mainly by the old idea of neurosis (Section 6.1.3). Its residual category F48.8, "other specified neurotic disorders", lists dhat syndrome among its inclusions, which is where an Indian clinician using ICD-10 codes a primary complaint of semen loss with weakness and anxiety when no more specific diagnosis fits (Section 1.10.4). The F5 block was a similar compromise: eating, sleep and sexual problems have little in common except that they involve the body. The F6 block uses the ICD's own names for the personality disorders, which differ from the DSM's: "dissocial" for antisocial, "emotionally unstable" for borderline, "anankastic" for obsessive-compulsive, "anxious [avoidant]" for avoidant (Chapter 11).

Exam trap

Question 20 asks which ICD-10 block contains delirium and dementia. The answer is F00-F09, organic, including symptomatic, mental disorders, because both have a known physical cause in the brain or body. F10-F19 is the substance block (a delirium caused by a substance, such as alcohol withdrawal with delirium, F10.4, is coded there, but delirium as such is organic); F20-F29 is schizophrenia, schizotypal and delusional disorders; and the option "F31-F39" is a garbled version of the mood block, which is F30-F39 (F31 is bipolar affective disorder, one category inside it). Remember too that ICD-10 keeps obsessive-compulsive disorder (F42) and post-traumatic stress disorder (F43.1) inside F40-F48, whereas DSM-5 gives each its own chapter, and that ICD-10's "mental retardation" (F70-F79) is now called intellectual developmental disorder (DSM-5-TR) or disorders of intellectual development (ICD-11).

ICD-11: Chapter 6

Work on the eleventh revision of the mental disorders chapter began in earnest when the WHO appointed an international advisory group in 2007, with working groups that reviewed the evidence for each family of disorders and kept in close contact with the team preparing DSM-5. The Seventy-second World Health Assembly adopted ICD-11 in May 2019 (the WHO announced the member states' agreement on 25 May, the date usually cited; the formal resolution, WHA72.15, is dated 28 May), and it came into effect on 1 January 2022, although each country decides when to switch from ICD-10 and many, India and the United States among them, are moving slowly. It is the first ICD designed to be used digitally: it is free to use online, in many languages, with an online coding tool.

ICD-11 has 28 chapters: 26 numbered chapters of health conditions and related topics (the 26th is a supplementary chapter on conditions of traditional medicine), plus two supplementary sections, one for functioning assessment (V) and one for extension codes (X), which add detail to a main code, such as the part of the body affected or whether a condition was present on admission. The chapters now carry Arabic numerals, and the mental disorders are Chapter 6 (written "06"), "Mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders", with codes from 6A00 to 6E8Z. Sleep-wake disorders, which ICD-10 split between the mental disorders and nervous system chapters, now have a chapter of their own, Chapter 07, and sexual dysfunctions and gender incongruence have moved to Chapter 17, "Conditions related to sexual health".

The codes look different because they are built differently. An ICD-11 stem code runs from 1A00.00 to ZZ9Z.ZZ. Its first character always gives the chapter, a digit for chapters 01 to 09 and a letter for chapters 10 to 26, so every mental disorder code begins with 6. The second character is always a letter, so that no ICD-11 code can be mistaken for an ICD-10 code; the third is always a digit, which stops codes from spelling unfortunate words; and the letters O and I are never used, to avoid confusion with 0 and 1. A final Y marks an "other specified" residual category and a final Z an "unspecified" one, which is why so many groupings end in Y and Z codes. Generalised anxiety disorder is 6B00; single episode depressive disorder, moderate, without psychotic symptoms, is 6A70.1.

Freed from the decimal straitjacket, the designers could give the chapter as many groupings as the evidence suggested. One of their guiding principles was that the classification should reflect current evidence about how disorders relate to one another, not what the WHO's own reference guide calls "antiquated concepts such as 'neurosis'", with poor construct and predictive validity. Another was clinical utility: a classification that clinicians find awkward will be used carelessly, and careless use produces worthless statistics. So, unusually, the WHO asked clinicians how they themselves group disorders. In one formative study, 1,371 psychiatrists and psychologists from 64 countries took part over the internet; in another, 517 mental health professionals in eight countries, working in five languages, sorted disorder names into groups face to face. Their mental maps of the territory were rational and strikingly similar across professions, languages and rich and poor countries, and the proposed ICD-11 structure matched those maps better than the structure of either ICD-10 or DSM-IV. The result is the 21 groupings, plus two residual codes, of Table 1.25.
