The ICD-11 chapter, like ICD-10's, needed a clinical companion, and it took until 8 March 2024 for the WHO to publish the Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Requirements for ICD-11 mental, behavioural and neurodevelopmental disorders (CDDR), an 852-page manual written for mental health professionals and also for non-specialists such as primary care doctors. Its style sits between the old blue book and the DSM. For each disorder it sets out the essential features, the things that must be present, in prose, and then describes the boundary with normality and with other disorders, course features, developmental presentations from childhood to old age, and culture-related and sex- and gender-related features. It deliberately avoids precise symptom counts and cut-offs where the evidence does not support them, trusting the clinician's judgement and leaving room for cultural variation. Book 2, Section 2.10.4 compares this style with the DSM's criteria.

Key study: testing ICD-11 in the clinic (Reed and colleagues, 2018)

Would clinicians around the world actually agree when they used the new guidelines? The WHO tested them before publication in routine clinics. In a study led by Geoffrey M. Reed and published in World Psychiatry in 2018, 339 clinicians at 28 centres in 13 countries assessed 1,806 adult patients in their own languages, using a joint-rater design: two clinicians, given exactly the same clinical information, each applied the draft ICD-11 guidelines independently to diagnose the high-burden groups (primary psychotic, mood, anxiety or fear-related and stress-associated disorders). Agreement, measured by kappa (Section 1.6.6), ranged from 0.45 for dysthymic disorder to 0.88 for social anxiety disorder, moderate to almost perfect for every diagnosis and better overall than had been reported for the equivalent ICD-10 guidelines. India took part: the paper's second author, Pratap Sharan, was at the All India Institute of Medical Sciences in New Delhi, and other co-authors came from medical colleges in Chandigarh and Raipur. The limitation is the design itself: two raters hearing the same interview test agreement in applying the guidelines, not whether two separate interviews would elicit the same information.

Exam trap

ICD-11's mental disorders chapter is Chapter 6 (06), codes 6A00 to 6E8Z; it is not Chapter V and its codes do not begin with F. Adopted by the 72nd World Health Assembly in May 2019, in force from 1 January 2022. ICD-11 has 28 chapters (26 plus the supplementary sections V and X); ICD-10 has 22. Chapter 6 has 21 groupings; ICD-10's Chapter V had 11 blocks. Sleep-wake disorders are Chapter 7, and sexual dysfunctions and gender incongruence are Chapter 17. ICD-11 has no separate grouping for "disorders of childhood": the neurodevelopmental grouping replaces three ICD-10 blocks, and the other childhood disorders join their adult relatives (separation anxiety with the anxiety disorders, enuresis with the elimination disorders, conduct disorder with the disruptive behaviour disorders). Its clinical companion is the CDDR (2024), which gives essential features rather than DSM-style counts.

What changed from ICD-10 to ICD-11

The WHO's own comparison maps each ICD-10 block onto its ICD-11 successor, and it reads like the break-up of a joint family. The organic block (F00 to F09) became the neurocognitive disorders, with the other organic syndromes moved to the new grouping of secondary mental or behavioural syndromes. The substance block became part of a wider grouping that also holds the addictive behaviours. The psychoses and mood disorders survived as recognisable groupings. The neurotic block was split into five. The physiological block was shared out among the feeding and eating disorders, the pregnancy-related disorders and the new chapters for sleep and sexual health. The personality block became the personality disorders and related traits. And the three developmental and childhood blocks were merged into the neurodevelopmental disorders, their other members joining their adult relatives. Table 1.26 sets out the changes most often examined.

Table 1.26 The main changes from ICD-10 Chapter V to ICD-11 Chapter 6.
ChangeICD-10ICD-11Taught in

Personality disorder made dimensionalSpecific types (F60.0 to F60.9: paranoid, dissocial, emotionally unstable, anankastic and others)One personality disorder graded mild, moderate or severe (6D10), with optional trait qualifiers (negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, anankastia) and a borderline pattern qualifier (6D11); personality difficulty (QE50.7) recorded outside the chapter as not a disorderChapter 11

Sexual health moved outSexual dysfunction (F52); gender identity disorders (F64)Chapter 17, Conditions related to sexual health (sexual dysfunctions; gender incongruence, HA60 and HA61), not mental disordersChapter 9
Acute stress reaction moved outAcute stress reaction (F43.0), a mental disorderAcute stress reaction (QE84) in Chapter 24, a normal response to an extreme eventSection 7.2.8
Hazardous use recognisedHarmful use (F1x.1) and dependence (F1x.2)Single episode of harmful use, harmful pattern of use and dependence in Chapter 6; hazardous alcohol use (QE10) and hazardous gaming (QE22) in Chapter 24Chapter 10

Three of these changes deserve a sentence of explanation, because interviewers ask "why?" as often as examiners ask "what?". The personality disorder change is the most radical. ICD-10's separate types overlapped so much, and were used so unevenly, that ICD-11 replaced them with a single question (is there a personality disorder, and how severe is it?) and a set of optional descriptions of its style; the five trait domains resemble the five of DSM-5-TR's alternative model (Section 1.8.5), with dissociality and anankastia in place of antagonism and psychoticism, and the borderline pattern qualifier was added late in the revision, largely so that clinicians could keep the link to a large body of research on treating borderline personality disorder (Book 2, Section 2.10.2; Chapter 11). The move of gender incongruence to Chapter 17 reflects the view that being transgender is not a mental disorder, while keeping the condition in the classification so that people who want gender-affirming health care can still be coded and served (Chapter 9). And the acute stress reaction left the mental disorders chapter because ICD-11 regards transient, intense distress in the hours and days after an extremely threatening event as a normal response, given the severity of the stressor; ICD-11 records it as a reason for contact with health services, not as an illness (Section 7.2.8). Notice, too, what ICD-11 kept that DSM-5-TR does not have: mixed depressive and anxiety disorder (6A73), which the DSM has never made an official diagnosis (DSM-5 offers an "anxious distress" specifier instead), and complex post traumatic stress disorder.

Exam trap

In ICD-11, gender incongruence is not a mental disorder; it sits in Chapter 17 with the sexual dysfunctions. Complex PTSD (6B41) and gaming disorder (6C51) are official ICD-11 diagnoses; DSM-5-TR has no complex PTSD and keeps internet gaming disorder in Section III as a condition for further study (Section 1.8.6). Prolonged grief disorder is official in both (6B42; the new diagnosis added by DSM-5-TR). ICD-11 has one personality disorder graded by severity, not ten types. Bodily distress disorder (ICD-11) and somatic symptom disorder (DSM-5-TR) are cousins, not the same thing. Neurasthenia, a staple of ICD-10 (F48.0), has no ICD-11 category.

The DSM and the ICD compared

You now know both manuals well enough to compare them properly, and the differences come down to their different jobs. The ICD must serve every member state, including countries where most mental health care is given by general doctors and health workers, and it must produce statistics that can be added up across the world, so it covers all of medicine, is free, and writes flexible guidelines. The DSM serves clinicians and researchers, above all in the United States, and its explicit criteria are designed for reliability in research and in insured practice. Book 2, Section 2.10.4 set DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 side by side; Table 1.27 extends that comparison to ICD-10, the version an Indian case sheet still most often uses.

Table 1.27 DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11 compared.
FeatureDSM-5-TRICD-10ICD-11

Date2022 (DSM-5, 2013)Endorsed 1990; in use from 1994Adopted 2019; in force 2022

The two families are closer now than they have been for decades, and not by accident. The groups revising DSM-5 and ICD-11 set out to harmonise the two classifications as far as possible, because two systems complicate national statistics, international drug trials and the replication of research across borders, and because even DSM-IV and ICD-10 diagnoses meant for the same patients did not always agree. The harmonisation succeeded mainly in the structure: DSM-5's chapters were deliberately arranged to correspond with the grouping planned for ICD-11, which is why the two lists of families now run in nearly the same order. The criteria themselves could not be fully harmonised, because DSM-5's development ran several years ahead of ICD-11's: its criteria were finalised just as the ICD-11 working groups were beginning to write their guidelines, though those groups were told to stay as close to DSM-5 as possible unless they had a considered reason to differ. A review comparing the two pairs of manuals concluded that DSM-5 and ICD-11 are closer than at any time since DSM-II and ICD-8, and that their remaining differences come largely from their different priorities and uses and from different readings of the evidence.

Those remaining differences are worth having at your fingertips. ICD-11 has complex post traumatic stress disorder and an official gaming disorder; DSM-5-TR has neither as an official diagnosis. ICD-11 diagnoses personality disorder by severity with optional traits; DSM-5-TR keeps ten types in Section II and puts its dimensional model in Section III. ICD-11 treats the acute stress reaction as a normal response, while DSM-5-TR's acute stress disorder is a mental disorder. ICD-11's bodily distress disorder and DSM-5-TR's somatic symptom disorder overlap without coinciding. ICD-11 keeps conversion symptoms with the dissociative disorders (dissociative neurological symptom disorder, 6B60), as ICD-10 did (F44), while DSM-5-TR places functional neurological symptom disorder in its somatic symptom chapter. ICD-11 keeps schizotypal disorder among the psychotic disorders (6A22), as ICD-10 did (F21), while DSM-5-TR lists schizotypal personality disorder in both its schizophrenia spectrum and personality chapters. ICD-11 places gender incongruence outside the mental disorders, while DSM-5-TR keeps gender dysphoria, the distress rather than the identity, as one of its disorder chapters.

Running case: coding Dinesh in ICD-10 and ICD-11

Section 1.7.5 told Dinesh's story and Section 1.8.3 recorded him in DSM-5-TR. Here is the same assessment in the WHO's two classifications.

ICD-10: F41.1 generalised anxiety disorder; D50.9 iron deficiency anaemia, unspecified (Chapter III); Z55.9 problem related to education and literacy, unspecified; Z59.9 problem related to housing and economic circumstances, unspecified; Z60.0 problems of adjustment to life-cycle transitions. His belief that he is losing dhat is written in words in the case record. ICD-10 does list dhat syndrome among the inclusions of F48.8, other specified neurotic disorders, but it is not coded here, because the generalised anxiety disorder accounts for his worry, tension and weakness, and the dhat belief is his explanation of that illness rather than a second one.

ICD-11: 6B00 generalised anxiety disorder; 3A00.Z iron deficiency anaemia, unspecified (Chapter 03); QD9Z problems associated with education, unspecified; QD5Z problems associated with finances, unspecified (both Chapter 24). ICD-11 has no code for a life-cycle transition as such, so the pressure surrounding the marriage is recorded in words, and it has no dhat category at all.

Line the three records up and the lesson is plain. The F41.1 of ICD-10 and the F41.1 printed in DSM-5-TR are the same code, and even the Z codes share numbers, though not always names (ICD-10-CM's "problems of adjustment to life-cycle transitions" is DSM-5-TR's "phase of life problem"). ICD-11's codes look entirely different, but the clinical judgement behind all three records is identical.

Exam trap

Question 12 asks what DSM-5 and ICD-11 are. They are classification and diagnostic systems for mental disorders, nosologies (Section 1.6.1): they name and define disorders and give the rules for diagnosing them. They are not personality tests (instruments such as the MMPI-2 or the Millon Clinical Multiaxial Inventory, whose MCMI-III version by Theodore Millon has 175 true-false items organised to match DSM-IV syndromes; despite the word "multiaxial" in its name, it is a test, not a classification: see the Assessment book). They are not types of psychotherapy (cognitive behaviour therapy, psychoanalysis and the rest belong to Book 2), and they are not intelligence scales (the Wechsler and Binet tests measure ability; the classifications only say where an intellectual disorder is coded). The DSM comes from the American Psychiatric Association, the ICD from the World Health Organization.

Scan Your Brain

  4. When a clinician chooses not to record why a presentation falls short of full criteria, the diagnosis is the disorder of that chapter.
  5. DSM-5's condition for further study that became prolonged grief disorder in DSM-5-TR was disorder.

1.10 Culture and Diagnosis: Culture-Bound Syndromes and Cultural Concepts of DistressMedium yield · 6 Qs

Think back to the frightened men who crowded into Singapore's hospitals at the start of this chapter. A psychiatrist armed only with a checklist could have examined each of them, counted racing hearts, sweating and terror, and written "panic attack" on every case sheet. The label would not have been wrong, exactly, but it would have missed almost everything that mattered: why these men, in this city, in these few weeks, all feared the same impossible thing, and why a few public statements from the city's doctors and its Ministry of Health did more to end the epidemic than any medicine. Every diagnosis in this book is made by one person about another, and both of them bring a culture into the room. The patient's culture shapes what he feels, what he calls it, what he thinks caused it and whom he goes to for help; the clinician's culture, including the culture of psychiatry itself, shapes what she notices and what she counts as a symptom. This section asks how much of a disorder is culture, how psychiatry has tried to describe the disorders that seem to belong to one place, and how a clinician in an Indian clinic can make a diagnosis that is accurate without being blind to the person's world.

Culture shapes distress

Book 2 opened this subject in two places. Its Section 1.6.8 argued that culture shapes the way distress is felt and expressed, not merely the yardstick by which conduct is measured, and it named the three cultural concepts of distress and gave dhat syndrome a brief first look, and its Section 1.7.1 described Arthur Kleinman's idea that every patient brings an explanatory model of the illness (Book 2, Section 2.2.9, adds the skills of interviewing across cultures and through interpreters). Here you need the fuller picture: what psychiatry now means by culture, the ways culture works on every stage of an illness, and the language you will use for the rest of this section.

Start with a woman in a clinic. Rekha, 38, a domestic worker in Pune who grew up in a village in Odisha, comes to a municipal health post saying she has "full tension", "gas" in her stomach, "weakness" in her body and a feeling that her "nerves have become weak". Asked what is going on at home, she explains that her son's school fees are due, her employer has cut her hours and she quarrelled with her sister-in-law last week. Her sleep is a little broken, but she still enjoys her evenings with her children, works every day and has no hopelessness. Rekha does not have a mental disorder. She is using the shared words of her community to say that life is pressing on her, much as a Delhi office worker might say "I'm so stressed" or an American student "I'm totally burned out". Words of this kind are called idioms of distress, and their first lesson is that they are a way of communicating, not a symptom count. A clinician who heard "tension, gas, weakness" and reached for a diagnosis would be making a mistake; so would one who heard the same words from a woman with severe depression and sent her home with an antacid.

What, then, is culture? The DSM-5-TR chapter "Culture and Psychiatric Diagnosis", in Section III of the manual, defines culture as the systems of knowledge, concepts, values, norms and practices that people learn and pass on across generations. It includes language, religion and spirituality, family structures, the stages of the life cycle, rituals and customs, and ways of understanding health and illness, together with moral, political, economic and legal systems. Three features of the definition matter for diagnosis. First, cultures are open and always changing, and most people today live in several at once: a young woman from a Malayali Christian family studying engineering in Bengaluru draws on her family's tradition, her college's youth culture and the global culture of the internet, and she builds her identity from all of them. Second, much of culture is implicit, background knowledge and assumptions so taken for granted that people find them hard to describe. Third, and following from the first two, DSM-5-TR warns clinicians not to overgeneralise cultural information or to stereotype groups in terms of fixed cultural traits. Knowing that a patient is a Tamil Brahmin, a Punjabi Sikh or a Gond tribal does not tell you what he believes; only he can tell you that. The manual then makes its central claim: all forms of illness and distress, including the DSM disorders themselves, are shaped by cultural contexts.

DSM-5-TR defines two related terms. It describes race as "a social, not a biological, construct", a way of dividing humanity by superficial physical traits such as skin colour that have been falsely taken to indicate inborn capacities; the construct matters to psychiatry because racism and discrimination harm mental health, worsen many disorders and bias diagnosis. And it defines ethnicity as a culturally constructed group identity, rooted in a shared history, ancestry, geography, language or religion, which may be claimed by the group or assigned to it by outsiders. In India the same logic applies to caste, religion, region and language, which shape access to care, exposure to discrimination and the way people present their suffering, and DSM-5-TR names caste explicitly among the aspects of identity a cultural assessment should consider (Section 1.10.3).

The most influential thinker in this field is Arthur Kleinman (born 1941), an American psychiatrist and medical anthropologist who did his early fieldwork in Taiwan and China. In 1978, with the psychiatrist Leon Eisenberg and the anthropologist Byron Good, he set out the distinction between disease, a malfunction of biological or psychological processes as the practitioner sees it, and illness, the person's own experience of being unwell (Section 1.2.2 uses the distinction). The year before, in a paper on depression and somatisation in Social Science and Medicine (1977), he had named a mistake that cross-cultural psychiatry kept making. The category fallacy is taking a diagnostic category that was developed in one culture and applying it to people of another culture, for whom it does not hang together and whose validity has never been shown there. Imagine an Indian psychiatrist arriving at a London university with a checklist for dhat syndrome. A few students would admit to tiredness and to worry about masturbation, and a "prevalence of dhat" could be calculated to two decimal places; the number would be meaningless, because for those students the idea of strength draining away with semen organises nothing about their distress. Kleinman's point was that Western psychiatrists did exactly this when they went abroad with Western categories. The most famous study of the problem is his own work with patients diagnosed with neurasthenia in Hunan, which is told in Section 5.2.7; there you will also see that critics accused him of committing the very fallacy he had named.

How does culture actually reach into a disorder? A useful starting point is the distinction, made by Karl Jaspers early in the twentieth century and taught in Chapter 2, between the form of a symptom and its content. The form (a delusion, a hallucination, an obsession) appears in every society that has been studied; the content is borrowed from the person's world. A persecuted man in a Bihar village fears that a neighbour has used black magic on him, his counterpart in a Mumbai high-rise fears hidden cameras, and a third man in Ohio fears the government; all three have persecutory delusions, but each delusion speaks the idiom of its place. DSM-5-TR notes that ideas which look delusional in one setting, such as the evil eye, illness caused by curses or the influence of spirits, may be commonly held in another; that hearing God's voice can be an ordinary part of religious experience in some communities; and that although auditory hallucinations are more common than visual ones everywhere, the proportion of visual hallucinations is higher in some regions than in others. Obsessions follow the same rule. Barlow and Durand report that obsessions in Saudi Arabia and Egypt centre on religious practice and cleanliness, while contamination themes are especially common in India, yet the forms and proportions of obsessions and compulsions are similar wherever they have been counted (Section 6.5).

Culture also decides where people go for help. In much of India the first stop for a troubled mind is not a clinic but a temple, a dargah, a church, a faith healer, an astrologer or an elder, and families often move between these and doctors, or use several at once (Book 2, Section 1.7.1, explains why these pathways make sense from inside the supernatural model). It decides how distress is presented to a doctor. Many Indian and Chinese patients with depression or anxiety lead with their bodies, with pain, burning, heaviness, weakness and "gas", rather than with sadness or fear (Sections 5.2.7 and 7.7 take this up). Barlow and Durand add a warning: researchers long assumed that expressing distress through the body was a feature of non-Western or developing societies, but closer study has shown that it is fairly common, and fairly uniform, throughout the world, and that the old impression may have come from the way the early studies were done. Finally, culture decides what counts as pathological in the first place. A woman whom the goddess "enters" at a village festival, who speaks in the deity's voice and afterwards remembers little, is taking part in an accepted collective practice; DSM-5-TR is explicit that a dissociative trance is a disorder only when it is "not a normal part of a broadly accepted collective cultural or religious practice" (Section 7.6.7 draws the line in detail).

Indian context

Traditional healing in India is neither all good nor all harmful. Many healers offer meaning, hope, ritual and the support of a community, and families value them for it. But on 6 August 2001, 28 people with mental illness (some reports give 25), kept in chains in a privately run home near the dargah at Erwadi in Tamil Nadu's Ramanathapuram district, died in a fire because they could not escape. The Supreme Court took up the case on its own motion, and the National Human Rights Commission later directed every state to certify that no person with mental illness was kept in chains; the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 now forbids chaining a person with mental illness "in any manner or form whatsoever" (Section 95). The clinician's task, as Section 1.10.6 explains, is to work alongside healers who do no harm and to make sure that no one is denied treatment in their name.

Indian patients use a rich vocabulary of distress, and you should learn to hear it. "Tension" is both an idiom and an explanation all over South Asia; DSM-5-TR lists it as the South Asian relative of the Latin American nervios. "Weakness" (kamzori), "my nerves are weak", "gas", "heat in the body", "burning in the hands and feet", ghabrahat (an anxious, restless agitation) and "thinking too much" are heard daily in Indian clinics. DSM-5-TR names the Indian idiom ashaktapanna, a feeling of weakness or lost strength, among the neurasthenia-like conditions of many cultures. Among Punjabi migrants in Bedford, England, the anthropologist Inga-Britt Krause described in 1989 the complaint of a "sinking heart", physical sensations in the heart or chest that sufferers attributed to excessive heat, exhaustion, worry or social failure, and she concluded that the Punjabi model corresponded exactly to no Western category, though it came closest to the Western idea of stress. None of these idioms is a diagnosis. Each is an invitation to ask more.

Exam trap

Keep Kleinman's three ideas apart. The category fallacy (1977) is imposing one culture's diagnostic category on another culture where it lacks coherence. The disease versus illness distinction (Kleinman, Eisenberg and Good, 1978) separates the practitioner's view of a malfunction from the patient's experience. The explanatory model (most fully in his 1980 book) is the patient's own account of what the illness is, what caused it and what should be done. Two more traps: an idiom of distress such as "tension" does not by itself indicate a disorder; and in the form versus content distinction it is the content of a symptom that culture supplies, while the form is broadly universal.

From culture-bound syndromes to cultural concepts of distress

Long before anyone spoke of cultural concepts, European doctors in the colonies were writing home about disorders they had never seen in Europe: men in Malaya who brooded over an insult and then ran murderously "amok", women in Java who, when startled, imitated every word and gesture around them. Emil Kraepelin, whose classification you met in Section 1.6.2, travelled to Java in 1904 to find out whether the disorders of his Munich clinic existed there too, and his journey is often taken as the start of what he called comparative psychiatry. For half a century afterwards the strange conditions of distant places were filed under headings such as the "exotic psychoses": curiosities from the edge of the map, described by outsiders and rarely understood from inside.

The psychiatrist who did most to bring order to them was Pow Meng Yap (1921 to 1971), who arrived in Hong Kong in 1948 as the colony's first qualified psychiatrist. In 1951 he published a survey of "mental diseases peculiar to certain cultures", and over the next twenty years he wrote on latah (1952), on the possession syndrome (1960) and on koro, which in 1965 he called "a culture-bound depersonalization syndrome". In 1967 he proposed a classification of what he named the culture-bound reactive syndromes. His argument had two halves. These conditions, he held, were reactive: psychogenic responses to stress, shaped by local beliefs about the body, the spirits and the social world. And precisely because they were reactions of a familiar kind, they were not new diseases at all but local forms of disorders psychiatry already knew, which should be fitted into the standard classification as variants. Yap died suddenly in November 1971 while attending the World Congress of Psychiatry in Mexico City; his book Comparative Psychiatry: A Theoretical Framework was published after his death, in 1974. The shorter phrase, culture-bound syndrome, is the one that stuck: a recurrent, locally recognised pattern of disturbed behaviour or experience, found in a particular society or region and understood there as an illness or an affliction.

By the 1980s the idea had a textbook of its own, The Culture-Bound Syndromes (1985), edited by the psychiatrist Ronald C. Simons and the anthropologist Charles C. Hughes, and in 1994 it entered the official manuals. DSM-IV placed in its Appendix I two new tools, an Outline for Cultural Formulation (Section 1.10.3) and a Glossary of Culture-Bound Syndromes with brief descriptions of 25 conditions, among them amok, ataque de nervios, brain fag, dhat, koro, latah, pibloktoq, susto, taijin kyofusho and zar; DSM-IV-TR (2000) kept both (Section 1.7.4 places the appendix in the history of the DSM). The ICD took a lighter step. ICD-10 itself lists dhat syndrome under F48.8, other specified neurotic disorders, and the ICD-10 Diagnostic Criteria for Research (1993) added an annex on culture-specific disorders that described the best-known conditions and suggested where in Chapter V each might be coded, while warning that these placements were tentative and giving no detailed criteria.

The term was useful, because it made generations of trainees aware that culture matters, but it drew four lasting criticisms. First, it was lopsided. The syndromes on every list came from Asia, Africa, Latin America and the Arctic, as if Western psychiatry described nature while everyone else had culture. Yet the West has syndromes of its own: anorexia nervosa, with its pursuit of thinness in societies that idealise slim bodies, has often been called a Western culture-bound syndrome (Chapter 8 weighs the argument), and DSM-5-TR itself now concedes that many DSM diagnoses "started out as cultural syndromes" and became widely accepted because they proved useful in clinics and research. Second, it exaggerated how local these conditions are. When Athula Sumathipala and his colleagues searched the historical literature in 2004, they found the anxiety about semen loss that defines dhat described in nineteenth-century Britain, the United States and Australia, and concluded that dhat "is not as culture-bound as previously thought". Third, it pointed attention at unusual clusters of symptoms when many clinically important cultural differences lie elsewhere, in how people explain and talk about their distress. Fourth, it carried Yap's assumption that each syndrome is really one Western disorder in local dress, waiting to be translated. That assumption rarely holds: a single cultural syndrome may include presentations that DSM would scatter across several disorders, and a single DSM disorder may appear under several local names.

DSM-5 (2013) answered these criticisms by retiring the construct. In its place, and retained in DSM-5-TR, are three cultural concepts of distress, which describe the different jobs a cultural term can do:

  1. A cultural idiom of distress is a behaviour, word, phrase or way of talking about symptoms, problems or suffering that people of similar cultural background share, used to express or communicate distress. DSM-5-TR's example is saying "I feel so depressed" to express low mood or discouragement that falls short of major depressive disorder. An idiom need not be tied to particular symptoms, syndromes or causes; it may cover everyday worries, problems below the threshold of a disorder or suffering caused by social circumstances, and most cultures have bodily idioms that express a wide range of troubles.
  2. A cultural explanation or perceived cause is a label, attribution or feature of an explanatory model that offers a culturally coherent account of why the symptoms, illness or distress happened, such as "stress", spirits, sorcery or a failure to follow prescribed practices. Such explanations are often the backbone of the local classifications that lay people and healers use.
  3. A cultural syndrome is a cluster of co-occurring, distinctive symptoms found in a particular cultural group, community or context, such as ataque de nervios. The syndrome may or may not be recognised as an illness locally (it might be described in non-medical terms), but an outside observer can still recognise the pattern.

The three are not boxes into which every term must fall exactly once. DSM-5-TR stresses that the same term often serves more than one function, and that usage changes over time. "Depression" is its own example: it names a syndrome (major depressive disorder), it has become an everyday idiom ("I'm so depressed about the result"), and it can be offered as an explanation ("the baby was born with emotional problems because his mother was depressed in pregnancy"). Dhat shows the same movement, as Figure 1.16 shows. Wig named it a syndrome in 1960, Indian patients use it as an explanation for weakness and worry, and it also serves as an idiom for genital infections; DSM-5-TR now describes it as "not a discrete syndrome but rather a cultural explanation of distress".

Why did DSM-5's authors think this vocabulary was better? Their own text gives the reasons. The term culture-bound syndrome, they wrote, ignores the fact that clinically important cultural differences often involve explanations or experiences of distress rather than unique symptom clusters, and it overstates how far cultural concepts are restricted to particular places; the new formulation accepts that all forms of distress are locally shaped, the DSM disorders included. DSM-5-TR then lists four features of cultural concepts that every clinician should keep in mind. There is seldom a one-to-one match between a cultural concept and a DSM disorder; the correspondence is more often one-to-many in both directions. Cultural concepts span the whole range of severity, so that a grieving widow with a social predicament and a man with severe psychopathology may use the same idiom or show the same cultural syndrome. The same term often names more than one kind of concept. And like culture and like the DSM itself, cultural concepts change over time. Add a fifth point from the same chapter: cultural concepts of distress are not endorsed uniformly by everyone in a culture, so a clinician must never assume that a Punjabi patient believes in the sinking heart or that every young man from Uttar Pradesh worries about dhat.

Knowing these concepts, the manual argues, improves practice in seven ways: it helps clinicians recognise people's concerns and detect disorders, because people often know their own cultural terms better than professional ones; it prevents misdiagnosis, for instance mistaking socially warranted suspicion for paranoia or an unfamiliar presentation for psychosis; it yields clinical information about risk, resilience and outcome; it improves rapport, because "speaking the language of the patient" improves communication, retention and adherence; it improves treatment, since culturally specific catastrophic thoughts can drive a panic attack and must be addressed; it guides clinical research; and it clarifies cultural epidemiology. It also feeds back into classification, because cultural syndromes may point to variants of a disorder that the manual had missed. DSM-5-TR's own example: in a change from DSM-IV, the experience of possession was written into the DSM-5 criteria for dissociative identity disorder (Section 7.6).

What happened to the glossary? DSM-5-TR's Section III chapter "Culture and Psychiatric Diagnosis" ends with ten examples of cultural concepts of distress, chosen because they are well studied and illustrate how cultural concepts can affect diagnosis: ataque de nervios, dhat syndrome, hikikomori, khyâl cap, kufungisisa, maladi dyab, nervios, shenjing shuairuo, susto and taijin kyofusho. Each entry ends with two lists: "related conditions in other cultural contexts", and "related conditions in DSM-5-TR", which includes both the disorders that overlap with the concept in their symptoms and the disorders that people commonly attribute to the explanation or express through the idiom. The manual adds that the principles these ten illustrate apply to the countless other cultural concepts found around the world, which is why the older syndromes still appear in textbooks and examination papers (Sections 1.10.4 and 1.10.5). The ICD has moved the same way. ICD-11's clinical descriptions and diagnostic requirements, released by the WHO in 2024, do not keep a list of exotic syndromes; instead, each main disorder carries a section on culture-related features.

Exam trap

Since DSM-5 (2013) the term culture-bound syndrome has been replaced by cultural concepts of distress, of exactly three kinds: cultural idioms of distress, cultural explanations (perceived causes) and cultural syndromes. The older term came from Pow Meng Yap (culture-bound reactive syndromes, 1967) and the old glossary of 25 conditions sat in DSM-IV's Appendix I; DSM-5-TR's ten examples sit in the Section III chapter "Culture and Psychiatric Diagnosis", not in Section II. DSM-5-TR calls dhat "not a discrete syndrome but rather a cultural explanation of distress", and stresses that there is "no one-to-one correspondence at the categorical level between DSM disorders and cultural concepts of distress".

The cultural formulation and the Cultural Formulation Interview

A diagnosis answers the question "what does this person have?" A formulation answers the harder questions "why this person, why now, and what will help?" (Book 2, Section 2.9, teaches formulation as a clinical skill). The cultural formulation is the part of that story that asks how culture enters it: who the person is, how he understands his trouble, what in his world makes it worse or better, and what stands between him and the clinician. DSM-IV's Outline for Cultural Formulation gave clinicians the first framework for this in 1994. DSM-5 expanded the Outline and added an interview to carry it out, and DSM-5-TR updated both. Notice one sentence in the manual's introduction to the Outline: the assessment is useful for every individual, "not only those whose cultural background may be unfamiliar to the clinician". A Hindi-speaking psychiatrist in Delhi interviewing a Hindi-speaking student has as much need of it as a London psychiatrist interviewing a refugee.

The Outline asks for a systematic assessment under five headings.

  1. Cultural identity of the individual. The clinician describes the person's age, gender and ethnic or racialised background, and the other socially defined characteristics that may shape relationships, access to resources and current predicaments: religion and spirituality, socioeconomic class, caste, where the person and the family were born and grew up, migrant status, occupation and sexual orientation, among others. The clinician notes which aspects of identity the person treats as most important and how they interact (DSM-5-TR uses the word intersectionality), how far a migrant is involved with the culture of origin and the new one, and which languages the person speaks and prefers, since this decides whether an interpreter is needed.
  2. Cultural concepts of distress. The idioms, explanations and syndromes through which the person experiences, understands and communicates the problem. The severity and meaning of the distress are judged against the norms of the person's own cultural background, together with which symptoms matter most to the person, how serious the illness seems, how much stigma it carries and what outcome is expected. This heading also covers how the person and the family cope, whom they have consulted and whom they expect to consult, including traditional, alternative and complementary healers as well as professionals.
  3. Psychosocial stressors and cultural features of vulnerability and resilience. The key stressors and supports in the person's world: social determinants of mental health such as housing, transport, education and employment; exposure to racism, discrimination and social exclusion (DSM-5-TR calls the last "structural violence"); and the part that religion, family, friends, neighbours, co-workers and online groups play in causing stress or giving support. Functioning, disability and resilience are judged in the light of the person's background.
  4. Cultural features of the relationship between the individual and the clinician, treatment team and institution. Differences in background, language, education and social status between the person and the clinician, and how each perceives the other, may make it hard to elicit symptoms, lead to misreading their cultural and clinical significance, and block the trust that accurate assessment needs. Experiences of racism and discrimination in the wider society may follow the person into the consulting room.
  5. Overall cultural assessment. The clinician draws the threads together: what the first four headings imply for the differential diagnosis, for other problems that need attention, and for management and treatment.

The Outline tells you what to find out; the Cultural Formulation Interview (CFI) tells you how to ask. DSM-5-TR describes it as a set of protocols with three components: the core CFI, 16 questions that can be used for an initial assessment of any individual; an informant version of the core CFI for collateral information; and a set of supplementary modules that expand the evaluation where needed. The core CFI is a brief semistructured interview. It is person-centred: it asks the person for his own views and those of the people around him, rather than assuming what someone of his background must believe, and this is the manual's defence against stereotyping. Because the questions are about personal views, there are no right or wrong answers, and the interviewer says so at the start. The printed form has two columns, instructions for the interviewer on the left and suggested questions on the right, and the questions may be rephrased, followed up or used selectively so that the interview keeps a natural flow. The CFI can be used at any age, in any setting and whatever the backgrounds of patient and clinician; DSM-5-TR points out that people who appear to share a background may still differ in ways that matter for care. It is especially helpful when the clinician and the person differ markedly in cultural, religious or socioeconomic background; when it is unclear whether culturally distinctive symptoms fit the diagnostic criteria; when severity or impairment is hard to judge; when the person's expectations come from other systems of healing; when patient and clinician disagree about the course of care; when the person may mistrust mainstream services because of a collective history of trauma or oppression; and when engagement and adherence are poor.

The 16 core questions are grouped into four domains, which run in a natural order from the person's view of the problem to the help he wants now (Figure 1.17).

Here is what each domain asks, in paraphrase. The interview opens with a short introduction: the clinician wants to understand the problems that bring the person in, wants to hear his own experience and ideas, and reminds him that there are no right or wrong answers. The first domain, the cultural definition of the problem (questions 1 to 3), asks what brings the person today, how he would describe the problem in his own words (which may differ from the way doctors describe it), how he would describe it to family, friends or others in his community, and what troubles him most about it. The clinician then uses the person's own term, "your weakness" or "your trouble with your son", in every later question. The second domain, cultural perceptions of cause, context and support (questions 4 to 10), asks why the person thinks this is happening and what he thinks the causes are (people often name several: bad events, problems with others, a physical illness, a spiritual reason), and what family, friends and community think the cause is; what kinds of support make the problem better and what stresses, such as money worries or family problems, make it worse; and which aspects of his background or identity matter most to him (his communities, languages, origins, ethnic background, gender, sexual orientation or faith), whether any of them make the problem better or worse, and whether any cause other difficulties, such as problems related to migration or conflict between generations or over gender roles. The third domain, cultural factors affecting self-coping and past help seeking (questions 11 to 13), asks what the person has done on his own to cope, what help, advice or healing he has sought from doctors, helpers or healers of any kind and what was or was not useful, and whether anything, such as money, work or family commitments, stigma, discrimination or services that do not understand his language or background, has kept him from the help he needs. The fourth domain, cultural factors affecting current help seeking (questions 14 to 16), asks what help would be most useful now, what other kinds of help family and friends have suggested, and finally, because doctors and patients sometimes misunderstand each other when they come from different backgrounds or have different expectations, whether the person has been concerned about this and what the clinic can do to give him the care he needs.

The informant version asks the same things of someone who knows the person's problems and circumstances well, usually a relative or carer. It can supplement the core CFI or replace it when the person cannot give the information himself, as with young children, people in florid psychosis and people with cognitive impairment. The twelve supplementary modules named in DSM-5-TR go deeper. Eight expand the core domains (explanatory model; level of functioning; social network; psychosocial stressors; religion and spirituality; cultural identity; coping and help seeking; and the clinician-patient relationship), and four serve particular groups (children and adolescents, older adults, immigrants and refugees, and caregivers). DSM-5-TR prints the core CFI and its informant version in Section III; the modules are available online.

Key study: Does the CFI work in real clinics? (Lewis-Fernández and colleagues, 2017)

Before the CFI went into DSM-5, a 14-question pilot version was field-tested in routine practice at eleven sites in six countries: Canada, India (Pune and New Delhi), Kenya, the Netherlands, Peru and the United States. Roberto Lewis-Fernández and his colleagues had clinicians administer it to diagnostically varied psychiatric outpatients as part of a diagnostic interview; afterwards patients and clinicians were interviewed about the experience and completed rating scales, and the time taken was recorded. Across 318 patients and 75 clinicians, the CFI was rated feasible, acceptable and useful. One finding is worth remembering: clinicians rated its feasibility lower than patients did, but after administering a single CFI their ratings rose significantly and later interviews took less time. These results, with other field-trial data, shaped the final 16-question version. The trial measured how practical and welcome the interview was; it was not designed to show that patients assessed with the CFI receive more accurate diagnoses or recover better, which later research must test.

To see the interview at work, return to a young man you have already met. Dinesh, the 24-year-old examination candidate from eastern Uttar Pradesh whose multiaxial record was built in Section 1.7.5, was sent to the psychiatry clinic by a physician he had consulted three times for "weakness".

Running case: Dinesh's Cultural Formulation Interview

The psychiatrist works through the four domains in Dinesh's own words.

  • Cultural definition of the problem (questions 1 to 3). He calls his trouble kamzori, weakness, and dhat jaana, losing dhat. What worries him most is the marriage: he fears he will not be able to satisfy his wife or father children.
  • Cultural perceptions of cause, context and support (questions 4 to 10). The cause, he says, is dhat lost through "bad habits" in his teens, by which he means masturbation; he grants only partly that the examination and the family's debt "give tension". His support is his elder cousin; his stresses are the debt and the fiancée's family. He identifies himself as the son of a Hindu farming family and the first in his family to try for a government job.
  • Self-coping and past help seeking (questions 11 to 13). He has taken herbal powders, prayed and fasted on Tuesdays, spent several thousand rupees at a roadside clinic advertising cures for gupt rog, "secret diseases", and seen the physician. He has told no one in his family.
  • Current help seeking (questions 14 to 16). He wants "a tonic" to restore his strength, fears that a "mental" label would end the engagement, and would prefer a doctor who speaks his own dialect.

Notice what the CFI has added to the diagnosis of generalised anxiety disorder: the meaning he gives his symptoms, the secrecy around them, the stake that makes the label dangerous to him, and the kind of explanation he will accept. Section 1.10.6 shows what the clinician does with it.

Clinical link

Kleinman's explanatory-model questions, published with Eisenberg and Good in 1978, and the CFI do the same job: what do you call the problem, what do you think caused it, why did it start when it did, what does it do to you, how severe is it, what do you fear most, and what treatment do you hope for? In a crowded Indian outpatient department you may have fifteen minutes, not an hour. Fit the essentials into the first interview: ask what the person calls the problem and what he thinks caused it (questions 1 and 4), ask what the family thinks (question 5), ask whom he has already consulted (question 12), and ask what help he expects and what he fears about coming here (questions 14 and 16). The remaining questions can follow at the next visit or from the informant version with a relative. Use his word for the problem throughout, and see him alone for part of the time, because some answers (about sex, a husband's drinking or thoughts of death) will not be given in front of the family.

Exam trap

The core CFI has 16 questions in four domains: cultural definition of the problem (1 to 3); cultural perceptions of cause, context and support (4 to 10); cultural factors affecting self-coping and past help seeking (11 to 13); and cultural factors affecting current help seeking (14 to 16). Do not confuse it with the Outline for Cultural Formulation, which has five headings and dates from DSM-IV (1994); the CFI arrived with DSM-5 (2013) and lives in Section III. The CFI's three components are the core CFI, the informant version and the supplementary modules. It is meant for every patient, not only immigrants or minorities.

Cultural syndromes of the Indian subcontinent

For an Indian clinician the cultural concepts of distress are not exotic entries in an appendix; several of them walk into the outpatient department every week. Indian examination papers know this, and the question they set most often is a simple one: which of these syndromes is reported in India, and which is not? This subsection teaches the Indian syndromes one by one, beginning with the most important.

Mohan, 20, a commerce student in Rohtak, sits in a skin and sexually transmitted diseases clinic with his eyes on the floor. For a year he has noticed a whitish thread in his urine after straining at stool, and he has night emissions two or three times a month. He feels tired, his face looks "dull" to him, he cannot study as he used to, and he is sure that his body is being drained. A friend told him it was because of masturbation, which he gave up six months ago with great effort, and an advertisement painted on a wall near the bus stand promised a cure. His real fear comes out only when the doctor asks what troubles him most: his marriage is being discussed at home, and he is afraid he will fail as a husband. Physical examination and urine tests are normal. He does not meet the criteria for a depressive or anxiety disorder, a sexual dysfunction or illness anxiety disorder; his functioning is intact apart from his worry. What he has is the most studied cultural concept of distress in South Asia.

The name dhat syndrome comes from dhat, the word that Mohan, and many patients and families like his, use for semen. It was named in 1960 by Narendra Nath Wig (1930 to 2018), who in 1963 founded the department of psychiatry at the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research (PGIMER) in Chandigarh and later headed psychiatry at the All India Institute of Medical Sciences in New Delhi; the name appeared in his paper on the problems of mental health in India. In 1975 H. K. Malhotra and Wig introduced it to an international readership in the Archives of Sexual Behavior as "a culture-bound sex neurosis of the Orient". DSM-5-TR describes dhat as a term coined in South Asia "little more than half a century ago" to account for common presentations of young men who attributed their various symptoms to semen loss. The symptoms they attribute to it are diverse: anxiety, fatigue, weakness, weight loss, erectile dysfunction, many other bodily complaints and depressed mood. The cardinal feature is anxiety and distress about the loss of dhat in the absence of any identifiable physiological dysfunction. Patients describe dhat as a white discharge they notice on urination or defecation, and sometimes they link it to night emissions or masturbation.

Why should losing semen frighten anyone? The answer lies in an old and widely shared idea of the body. In Ayurveda, the Hindu system of medicine, dhatu refers to the seven essential bodily substances whose balance maintains health, and semen is the last of the seven. Patients often explain the danger with a folk arithmetic; in the version that Sumathipala and his colleagues (2004) quote, it takes forty days for forty drops of food to become one drop of blood, and the same ratio holds at each later step, as blood becomes flesh, flesh becomes marrow and marrow at last becomes semen. If semen is the concentrated essence of the whole body, its loss must drain strength, beauty, memory and manhood, and the weakness, the dull face and the poor concentration all make sense. DSM-5-TR adds that such ideas about the harm of semen loss are widespread in the general population, not only among patients, which suggests a cultural disposition to explain health problems in terms of dhat. Research in health care settings has given very different prevalence figures: 64 percent of men attending psychiatric clinics in India for sexual complaints in one study, 30 percent of men attending general medical clinics in Pakistan in another. Dhat is most often identified in young men from lower socioeconomic backgrounds, but middle-aged men may also be affected, and similar worries about white vaginal discharge (leukorrhoea) have been described as a variant in women. The word may also serve as an idiom and causal explanation for sexually transmitted infections such as gonorrhoea and chlamydia, without any psychological distress. The concept is found across South Asia, in India, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Nepal and Bangladesh. The DSM-IV glossary named jiryan in India and sukra prameha in Sri Lanka as similar conditions; DSM-5-TR names koro in Southeast Asia, particularly Singapore, and shen-k'uei, "kidney deficiency", in China as related conditions in other cultures. And as Sumathipala and his colleagues showed (Section 1.10.2), semen-loss anxiety was once common in Western medicine too.

What is dhat in DSM-5-TR's terms? Not a disorder, and not even a discrete syndrome: "a cultural explanation of distress". The person who explains his suffering by dhat may have no disorder at all, like Mohan, or he may have one of the disorders DSM-5-TR lists as related: major depressive disorder, persistent depressive disorder, generalised anxiety disorder, somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder, erectile disorder, early (premature) ejaculation, other specified or unspecified sexual dysfunction, or, in a reminder that some of these men simply lack accurate information, educational problems. Management follows from this. Every patient needs a respectful explanation of sexual physiology within his own frame of reference (that night emissions and masturbation are normal and do not drain the body), reassurance based on examination and tests, and the involvement of a trusted relative where he agrees; any depression, anxiety disorder or sexual dysfunction underneath is treated in its own right (Chapter 9 discusses the sexual complaints, and Dinesh's outcome in Section 1.10.6 shows the approach). Mohan, given twenty unhurried minutes and a diagram, went home visibly lighter.

The second great Indian syndrome is koro, the acute fear that the genitals are shrinking and retracting into the body and that death will follow. The opening of this chapter told the story of the Singapore epidemic of 1967 and glanced at the Assam epidemic of 1982; here the Indian story is told in full. India has had several koro epidemics, and they form a clear series. A review of the Indian epidemics by Sayanti Ghosh, Arabinda N. Chowdhury and their colleagues (2013) lists the first in North Bengal in 1968, the second in 1982 in West Bengal and Assam, a third small outbreak in a village in South 24 Parganas district of West Bengal in 1988, a fourth in Tripura in 1998, and a fifth in 2010, which began in Assam and spread to labour camps in Mumbai, to West Bengal and to Tripura. In a Mumbai labour camp that October, where most of the men affected were migrants from Odisha and West Bengal, one worker described sitting for three or four hours in a tub of iced water to stop his genitals retracting; in West Bengal, where sitting in cold water up to the neck was the commonest home remedy, the feverish illness that went with the 2010 attacks was nicknamed "disco fever".

Key study: The koro epidemic in lower Assam (Dutta, Phookan and Das, 1982)

The opening of this chapter summarised this study; here are its details. From late June to the middle of September 1982, a koro epidemic swept through four districts of lower Assam. D. Dutta, H. R. Phookan and P. D. Das of the psychiatry department at Gauhati Medical College surveyed 83 cases, 19 of them women. Most of those affected were young men under 30 from Hindu Assamese middle-class families, and their complaints were mainly genital; attacks usually came at night, indoors. Hearing about koro or seeing a case was an important trigger of an attack. Marriage, education and occupational skill gave no protection, relapses were few, and the epidemic subsided without a single death after massive public reassurance through the mass media. Locally the illness was called jinjinia, Assamese for tingling. At almost the same time P. S. Sachdev and A. Shukla announced an "epidemic koro syndrome in India" in a letter to the Lancet (1982), and Sachdev's later analysis of 31 cases from north-east India found most of them poorly educated, poor and aged 20 to 40, with many women among them and little evidence of premorbid or sexual psychopathology. Ajita Chakraborty, reporting the simultaneous epidemic in West Bengal, tied it to social tensions of the time. Two lessons stand out: koro spreads by rumour and fear, and it is stopped by authoritative information.

Indian koro epidemics have differed from the classical Chinese picture in one striking way: many women have been affected, fearing that their breasts or nipples are retracting or, more rarely, the vulva; Psycho Didi's notes record koro in north-east India as "seen in both the sexes", and two of the women in the 2010 West Bengal series described their nipples drawing in. Barlow and Durand note that typical Chinese sufferers feel guilty about masturbation, unsatisfactory intercourse or promiscuity, worries that may focus attention on the genitals and feed the anxiety, much as in the anxiety disorders. In DSM-5-TR, koro appears in three places. In the glossary it is a related condition of dhat. In Section II it is one of the named examples of other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder: an episode of sudden and intense anxiety that the penis in males, or the vulva and nipples in females, will recede into the body, possibly leading to death. And the text on body dysmorphic disorder separates it from that disorder by its focus on death rather than on perceived ugliness (Sections 6.6.3 and 6.6.6).

The Assamese name for koro, jinjinia, leads to a different condition with almost the same name. The condition called jhinjhinia (jhin-jhini, Bengali for tingling and numbness) was described by D. N. Nandi and his colleagues (1992), who traced every victim of an outbreak that struck the village of Arkhali, about 50 kilometres north of Calcutta in North 24 Parganas, in March 1982. A rumour that the disease had broken out in neighbouring villages created an atmosphere of terror. The illness struck suddenly with tingling and numbness in the legs that spread upwards through the body; within seconds the person was seized with horror of impending death and cried for help before becoming speechless and motionless, and would fall to the ground unless "rescued" by a squad of villagers with a local remedy, recovering within half an hour to two hours. Eighteen people fell ill within two months, as many women as men, most of them under 40. The villagers blamed an evil spirit. Because the West Bengal koro epidemic of the same year was also locally called jhin-jhini, the two are easily confused, but Nandi's team insisted that their epidemic was a distinct entity: the victims had no concern at all about their genitals. Today you would recognise it as a form of mass psychogenic illness (Section 7.8.9).

Four more syndromes complete the Indian list. The first, suchi-bai, described in Bengal, is an obsessional preoccupation with cleanliness and ritual purity (suchi means pure); Psycho Didi's notes call it "a condition like obsessional neurosis". It sits at the meeting point of custom and compulsion. Many Hindu households keep purity rules about bathing before cooking, separating clean from polluted spaces and washing after contact with certain people or objects, and following them is not a disorder; when the washing and purifying become excessive, time-consuming and distressing, go beyond what the family's own tradition requires and are resisted or regretted by the person, the presentation should be assessed for obsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 6.5). The second, the possession syndrome of rural India, is a state in which a person is taken over, usually by the spirit of a dead relative or by a local deity, speaks in a changed tone of voice and may even change gender if the possessing spirit is of the opposite sex. Most possession states are part of accepted religious practice and are not disorders; when they are involuntary, distressing, uncontrollable, in conflict with family or community and out of place and time, they fall under ICD-10's trance and possession disorders (F44.3), ICD-11's possession trance disorder or DSM-5-TR's possession-form dissociative identity disorder or other specified dissociative disorder (Section 7.6.7 draws these lines). The third, bhanmati sorcery (also spelt bhanamati), is a cultural explanation from South India: illness of almost any kind is attributed to sorcery worked by an envious or hostile person. In a clinical study of 209 patients who attended a special clinic in South India, Matcheri S. Keshavan, H. S. Narayanan and B. N. Gangadhar (1989) found that most had somatisation or conversion disorders, though several other psychiatric and medical disorders were also seen; Psycho Didi's notes add dysthymia, anxiety disorders and schizophrenia to the conditions found behind the explanation. The fourth, gilhari syndrome, reported from western Rajasthan, is the belief that a small swelling on the body is moving about under the skin like a squirrel (gilhari) or a lizard, travelling from the back towards the neck, where it will block the airway and kill the person unless it is destroyed; medically, the "swelling" is a strong contraction of a group of muscles under severe tension and stress.

Two last entries appear in Indian notes. One is suudu, described in Tamil culture (the Tamil word means heat), which presents with severe abdominal pain, dark yellow urine, painful and burning urination, headaches, fatigue, constipation and dry mouth. And mass hysteria, in the old term, describes short-lived epidemics in which hundreds or thousands of people behave in ways they normally would not; the koro and jhin-jhini outbreaks are Indian examples, and the modern concept of mass psychogenic illness is taught in Section 7.8.9. Table 1.28 brings the Indian syndromes together.
