Exam trap

Dhat, koro, suchi-bai, possession syndrome, bhanmati sorcery, gilhari syndrome and jhinjhinia are all reported from India; latah is not (it belongs to Malaysia and Indonesia, Section 1.10.5). Dhat belongs to the Indian subcontinent (with Sri Lanka, Pakistan, Nepal and Bangladesh), not to Malaysia, Latin America or the Arctic, and it was named by N. N. Wig in 1960. Koro is not only Chinese or Singaporean: Indian epidemics (1982 in Assam and West Bengal, 2010 in several states) affected women as well as men. Do not confuse jinjinia, a local name for koro in Assam, with jhinjhinia, the tingling-and-collapse epidemic of a West Bengal village.

Cultural syndromes around the world

The world list is long, and memorising it as an alphabet of strange names is the hardest way to learn it. It is easier to sort the syndromes by what they most resemble: the startle and running syndromes, the attacks of panic, anxiety and anger, the syndromes of social fear and withdrawal, the syndromes of weakness, fatigue and "thinking too much", the explanations built on fright, sorcery, the evil eye and spirits, the states of possession, trance and collapse, and the psychosis-like episodes. Keep two cautions in mind as you read. Resemblance is not identity: each syndrome overlaps with several DSM-5-TR disorders and maps onto none of them exactly. And many of these terms are explanations or idioms rather than syndromes, in the vocabulary of Section 1.10.2.

The startle and running syndromes. In a Malay village a middle-aged woman is poked in the ribs by a grinning neighbour. She jumps, shrieks a word she would never use in polite company, and then, to the delight of the onlookers, copies every gesture the neighbour makes and obeys whatever she is told to do, until the spell passes and she scolds them all. This is latah, described in Malaysia and Indonesia: an exaggerated, hypersensitive startle to sudden or minimal stimuli, followed by echolalia (repeating the words of others), echopraxia (imitating their movements), automatic obedience to commands and a dissociative or trance-like state, often with the blurting of obscene or sexual words (coprolalia). The DSM-IV glossary noted that in Malaysia it is more frequent in middle-aged women. Yap wrote one of the classic papers on it in 1952, and similar startle syndromes have been described in Siberia (myriachit), Thailand, among the Ainu of Japan (imu) and in the Philippines (mali-mali). A second Malay syndrome, amok, gave English the phrase "running amok". It is a dissociative episode in which a period of brooding, often after a perceived slight or insult, is followed by an outburst of violent, aggressive or even homicidal behaviour against people and objects, with persecutory ideas, automatic behaviour, amnesia for the episode and exhaustion afterwards, and then a return to the person's usual state. The DSM-IV glossary described it as seemingly prevalent only among men, and Barlow and Durand agree that most sufferers are male. Similar patterns have been reported from Laos, the Philippines, Polynesia, Papua New Guinea and Puerto Rico (mal de pelea), and some episodes occur during a brief psychotic episode or at the start of a chronic psychosis. Barlow and Durand place amok among the "running" syndromes, in which a person in a trance-like state runs or flees for a long time as if driven by a mysterious energy; apart from amok, these are somewhat more common in women.

The best-known running syndrome comes from the Arctic. The syndrome called pibloktoq (also written piblokto), or "Arctic hysteria", was described among the Inuit of the Arctic and subarctic, above all the Inughuit of north-west Greenland. After hours or days of withdrawal or mild irritability, the person, often a woman and usually in the long nights of winter, would be seized by an abrupt dissociative episode of extreme excitement lasting up to half an hour: tearing off her clothes, breaking furniture, shouting obscenities, eating faeces, or fleeing from shelter out into the snow and ice. Convulsive seizures and a stupor or coma of up to twelve hours could follow, and afterwards she remembered nothing. Many of the classic accounts came from the Greenland expeditions of the American explorer Robert Peary at the turn of the twentieth century, and that is the problem. The Canadian historian Lyle Dick argued in 1995, in Arctic Anthropology, that pibloktoq may be largely a phantom: the scholarly literature rested on a handful of cases, the word itself could not be found in the Inughuit language, and the episodes may say more about the experience and behaviour of the explorers than about Inuit psychology; some later scholars have read them as responses to colonial violence, including the sexual exploitation of Inuit women. Others have proposed physical causes, among them vitamin A poisoning from eating the livers of Arctic animals. Pibloktoq is now a lesson in method as much as a syndrome: a "cultural syndrome" can be partly a product of the observer's culture.

Attacks of panic, anxiety and anger. DSM-5-TR describes ataque de nervios ("attack of nerves") as a syndrome found in Latinx cultural contexts in the Caribbean, Latin America and the United States: intense emotional upset with acute anxiety, anger or grief, uncontrollable screaming and shouting, attacks of crying, trembling, heat in the chest rising into the head and verbal or physical aggression, sometimes with dissociative experiences, seizure-like or fainting episodes or suicidal behaviour. Its general feature is a sense of being out of control, and attacks usually follow a stressful family event, such as news of a death or a conflict with a spouse or children. In US community samples 7 to 15 percent of Latinx adults report an ataque; it is linked with suicidal thoughts and disability even after psychiatric diagnoses are taken into account, yet some ataques, at a funeral for example, are normal expressions of acute grief. It differs from a panic attack in its family trigger, its anger and aggression and its longer duration (Section 6.2). Its cousin nervios ("nerves") is a broader idiom of distress and causal explanation in the same communities, a general state of vulnerability to stress, with headaches and "brain aches", irritability, stomach upsets, poor sleep, nervousness, easy tearfulness, poor concentration, trembling, tingling and dizziness; it spans everything from no disorder at all to presentations resembling depressive, anxiety, dissociative, somatic symptom or psychotic disorders. Its relatives include nevra among Greeks in North America, "nerves" in Appalachia and Newfoundland, and "tension" in South Asia. Among Cambodians, khyâl cap ("wind attacks") combines the symptoms of a panic attack (dizziness, palpitations, breathlessness, cold hands and feet, tinnitus, neck soreness) with the catastrophic belief that khyâl, a wind-like substance, is rising in the body with the blood and may compress the lungs or enter the skull and cause a fatal collapse; attacks are set off by worrying thoughts, standing up, certain smells, crowds or riding in a car, and usually meet the criteria for a panic attack. Related ideas include pen lom in Laos and vata in Sri Lanka. A Vietnamese person who panics after walking into a windy street may attribute the attack to trúng gió ("hit by the wind"); DSM-5-TR uses this example to show that a cultural explanation can turn what looks like an unexpected panic attack into an expected one. The Korean hwa-byung (literally "fire illness", where hwa means fire and, by extension, anger; the DSM-IV glossary translated it "anger syndrome") is attributed to the suppression of anger and presents with insomnia, fatigue, panic, fear of impending death, low mood, indigestion, loss of appetite, breathlessness, palpitations, aches, a sensation of heat and a feeling of a mass in the upper abdomen; it is diagnosed most often in middle-aged women, and DSM-5-TR lists it as a relative of khyâl cap.

Social fear and withdrawal. The Japanese taijin kyofusho ("interpersonal fear disorder") is a syndrome of anxiety about and avoidance of social situations because of the thought, feeling or conviction that one's appearance or actions are inadequate or offensive to others. Notice the difference from Western social anxiety, which fears one's own embarrassment: in the "offensive type" the central worry is causing offence to others. DSM-5-TR describes a "sensitive type" and an "offensive type" and variants centred on blushing (sekimen-kyofu), an offensive body odour (jiko-shu-kyofu), an inappropriate gaze (jiko-shisen-kyofu) and a stiff or deformed appearance (shubo-kyofu). It is broader than social anxiety disorder, taking in features of body dysmorphic disorder, olfactory reference syndrome and delusional disorder, and a similar syndrome in Korea is called taein kong po (Section 6.3.7). Japan has also given psychiatry the word hikikomori (from Japanese words meaning to pull back and to seclude oneself): protracted, severe social withdrawal, typically in an adolescent or young adult man who does not leave his room in his parents' home and has no face-to-face contact with anyone, often while spending long hours online; a 2010 guideline of Japan's Ministry of Health, Labour and Welfare requires six months of withdrawal for the diagnosis. It may be "secondary" to an established disorder or "primary", and similar withdrawal has now been reported from many countries, India among them.

Weakness, fatigue and thinking too much. The Chinese shenjing shuairuo ("weakness of the nervous system" in Mandarin) joins the concepts of traditional Chinese medicine with the Western idea of neurasthenia. The second revised Chinese Classification of Mental Disorders (CCMD-2-R) defined it by three of five clusters: weakness (mental fatigue), emotions (feeling vexed), excitement (such as increased recollections), nervous pain (headache) and sleep problems. China adopted ICD-10 as its official system in 2011, and ICD-11 no longer contains neurasthenia; the diagnosis has declined sharply, replaced in the cities by idioms of depression and anxiety, though up to 45 percent of medical clinic patients with shenjing shuairuo met the criteria for no DSM-IV disorder at all (Kleinman's famous study of these patients is told in Section 5.2.7). Its close Chinese relative shen-k'uei ("kidney deficiency"; shenkui in mainland China) is marked anxiety or panic with dizziness, backache, fatigue, weakness, insomnia, frequent dreams and sexual complaints such as premature ejaculation and erectile failure, all attributed to excessive loss of semen, the vital essence, through intercourse, masturbation, night emissions or "white turbid urine"; you will recognise it as the Chinese cousin of dhat. The syndrome of brain fag was first described by the psychiatrist Raymond Prince in 1960 in a paper on "the 'brain fag' syndrome in Nigerian students". It is a West African condition of secondary school and university students, mostly young men, who complain that their brains are "fatigued" from study: they cannot concentrate, remember or think, and they report pain, pressure, tightness, heat, burning or crawling sensations in the head and neck and blurred vision. DSM-5-TR treats it as a cultural syndrome built around "thinking too much", attributed to excessive study, which is believed to damage the brain. The term itself is British: it was coined around 1850, in Victorian Britain, for the mental exhaustion of "brainworkers" who studied too hard, and it spread to the British colonies, West Africa among them, while it faded at home. Finally, kufungisisa ("thinking too much" in Shona) is an idiom of distress and a cultural explanation among the Shona of Zimbabwe. As an explanation it is held to cause anxiety, depression and bodily problems ("My heart is painful because I think too much"); as an idiom it signals interpersonal and social difficulties such as marital problems, unemployment or having no money for the children. In one random community sample, two-thirds of the cases identified by a general psychopathology measure included this complaint. "Thinking too much" turns out to be one of the commonest idioms on earth, reported from Africa, Asia, the Caribbean, Latin America and the Middle East, and you will hear it in Indian clinics too.

Fright, sorcery, the evil eye and spirits. The Latin American susto ("fright") is a cultural explanation for distress and misfortune in Latinx communities of North, Central and South America: a frightening event makes the soul leave the body, causing unhappiness, sickness and difficulty in carrying out one's social roles. Symptoms can appear days or even years after the fright and include disturbed appetite and sleep, troubled dreams, sadness, low self-worth or a sense of being dirty, interpersonal sensitivity, lack of motivation, aches, cold hands and feet, pallor, headache, stomach ache and diarrhoea; in extreme cases susto is believed to cause death. It is called espanto in the Andes, is not recognised as an illness among Latinx people from the Caribbean, and has three syndromic forms that resemble major depressive disorder, posttraumatic stress disorder and somatic symptom disorder respectively. The phrase mal de ojo, the "evil eye", is the Spanish name for a belief found across the Mediterranean, Latin American and Muslim worlds, and in India as nazar: an envious or admiring gaze causes illness, especially in infants and children, who sleep fitfully, cry without apparent cause and develop diarrhoea, vomiting and fever. In Haitian communities maladi dyab ("devil illness", also called "sent sickness") explains illness of almost any kind, from psychosis and depression to academic failure, as sent by sorcerers at the request of envious enemies; because visible success invites attack, the attractive, the clever and the newly prosperous are thought most at risk. DSM-5-TR warns that this explanation may lead a clinician to misdiagnose delusional disorder or schizophrenia. In the southern United States and the Caribbean, rootwork likewise explains illness as the result of hexing, witchcraft, voodoo or the influence of an evil person (mal puesto and brujería are Latin American equivalents). How powerful such beliefs can be was the subject of a famous 1942 paper on "voodoo death" by the American physiologist Walter Bradford Cannon (1871 to 1945), who suggested that a sentence of death pronounced by a healer could produce an intolerable state of autonomic arousal, made worse when family and friends, assuming the victim already as good as dead, withdrew their support. Among some American Indian groups, ghost sickness is a preoccupation with death and the dead, sometimes linked to witchcraft, with bad dreams, weakness, a sense of danger, loss of appetite, fainting, dizziness, fear, anxiety, hallucinations, confusion, feelings of futility and a sense of suffocation.

Possession, trance and collapse. The word zar is the general term in Ethiopia, Somalia, Egypt, Sudan, Iran and other North African and Middle Eastern societies for the experience of being possessed by spirits. Possessed people may have dissociative episodes of shouting, laughing, singing, weeping or hitting the head against a wall, may become apathetic and withdrawn, refusing to eat or work, or may develop a long-term relationship with the spirit; locally such behaviour is not necessarily considered pathological. Korea's shin-byung begins with anxiety and bodily complaints (weakness, dizziness, fear, loss of appetite, insomnia, digestive problems) and ends in dissociation and possession by ancestral spirits. In the southern United States a spell is a trance in which a person "communicates" with dead relatives or spirits; it is not considered a medical event in the folk tradition but may be mistaken for a psychotic episode in a clinic. The episode called falling out (southern United States) or blacking out (the Caribbean) is a sudden collapse, sometimes after dizziness or a "swimming" feeling in the head: the eyes are usually open but the person says he cannot see, and he hears and understands what is going on but feels powerless to move. DSM-5-TR lists falling out and blacking out as relatives of ataque de nervios and warns that these terms must not be confused with the blackouts of alcohol or drug use. India's possession syndrome (Section 1.10.4) belongs to this family.

Psychosis-like episodes. The French term bouffée délirante (a "puff" of delirium) names a syndrome described in West Africa and Haiti: a sudden outburst of agitated and aggressive behaviour, marked confusion and psychomotor excitement, sometimes with visual and auditory hallucinations or paranoid ideas; it resembles brief psychotic disorder (Section 4.5.1). A qi-gong psychotic reaction is an acute, time-limited episode of dissociative, paranoid or other psychotic or non-psychotic symptoms after taking part in qi-gong, a Chinese folk practice of exercising one's vital energy; people who become over-involved in the practice are especially vulnerable. Locura, in Latin America, is a severe chronic psychosis attributed to an inherited vulnerability, to the accumulated difficulties of life, or to both. And then there is windigo (also wendigo or witiko), the most famous syndrome that may never have existed. Among the Algonquian-speaking peoples of the Great Lakes region and the northern forests of Canada (the Ojibwe, Cree and Innu among them), the windigo is a cannibal monster, and windigo psychosis was described as an obsessive fear of turning into one, or a craving for human flesh, supposedly brought on in times of famine. In 1982 the anthropologist Lou Marano argued in Current Anthropology, in a paper subtitled "the anatomy of an emic-etic confusion", that there were probably never any windigo psychotics in the behavioural sense: the "psychosis" was an artefact of research that confused what people believed with what people did, and accusations that someone was turning windigo may have served to justify killing a person whom a hungry group saw as a threat to its survival. Windigo was not included in the DSM-IV glossary. Its nearest clinical neighbour is clinical lycanthropy, the delusional belief that one has been transformed into an animal, which is a symptom rather than a cultural syndrome (delusions are taught in Chapter 2).

These descriptions also dispose of the wrong options in a favourite question, "What is the characteristic belief associated with koro?" The right answer is the belief that the genitals are retracting into the body and that this may cause death. The belief that the body has been possessed by evil spirits belongs to possession syndrome, zar or shin-byung. The belief that one is being transformed into a mythical creature echoes windigo or clinical lycanthropy. And "a sense of impending doom associated with atmospheric changes" matches no standard syndrome exactly; the nearest are the folk "wind and cold illnesses" of Asian and Latin American traditions, which Psycho Didi's notes describe as a fear of cold and wind with a feeling of weakness and susceptibility to illness, Vietnam's trúng gió (DSM-5-TR itself links trúng gió attacks to fear of "atmospheric wind"), and the wind attacks of khyâl cap. Table 1.29 gathers the world's syndromes in their groups, and Figure 1.18 shows the groups at a glance.

Exam trap

Match the core feature to the name. An exaggerated startle to minimal stimuli with echolalia, echopraxia, automatic obedience and blurted sexual or obscene words is latah (Malaysia and Indonesia), not koro, dhat or susto. Brooding followed by a violent outburst and amnesia is amok. A sudden dissociative frenzy with screaming, tearing off clothes and running into the snow is pibloktoq, among Arctic and subarctic Inuit, not South Asian women, Malay men or West Africans. Soul loss after a fright is susto; screaming, crying and heat rising to the head after a family shock is ataque de nervios; the fear of offending others by one's looks, gaze or odour is taijin kyofusho; suppressed anger with heat in the chest is hwa-byung; and the conviction that the genitals are retracting and that death will follow is koro. Possession by evil spirits points to zar, shin-byung or possession syndrome, and transformation into a mythical creature to windigo or clinical lycanthropy.

Exam trap

Pibloktoq, brain fag and dhat are cultural concepts of distress (formerly culture-bound syndromes). They are not DSM-5 anxiety disorder subtypes (the anxiety disorders of Chapter 6, such as panic disorder and generalised anxiety disorder), not substance-induced disorders (conditions caused by the direct effects of a drug, medication or toxin, Chapter 10) and not ICD-11 dissociative disorders (such as trance disorder and possession trance disorder, Section 7.6.7), although a person who uses one of these cultural terms may also have any of these disorders. Brain fag belongs to West African students, first described in Nigerian students by Raymond Prince in 1960; some Indian exam notes list it under "African Americans", which is wrong. Windigo was never in the DSM-IV glossary, and DSM-5-TR's own glossary holds only ten concepts: the nine marked with an asterisk in Table 1.29, plus dhat.

From cultural concept to diagnosis

Finding the cultural concept is half the work. The other half is deciding what to do with it, and DSM-5-TR is clear about the rule. When a person presents with a cultural concept of distress, the assessment must decide whether the presentation meets the criteria for a specific DSM-5-TR disorder or is instead best classified as an other specified diagnosis. Once a disorder is diagnosed, the cultural terms and explanations belong in the case formulation, where they can clarify symptoms and causal beliefs that might otherwise confuse the clinician. And people whose symptoms meet the criteria for no mental disorder may still expect and need treatment, which must be judged case by case. Put together with what Section 1.10.1 said about culturally accepted experiences, the clinician's reasoning runs in five steps.

  1. Elicit the concept, ideally with the CFI, and work out what kind it is: an idiom, an explanation, a syndrome, or more than one of these.
  2. Judge the experience against the person's own cultural norms. A possession trance at a temple festival, a voice heard during prayer, or a belief in sorcery shared by the whole family is not, by itself, a symptom of disorder.
  3. If something beyond the culturally expected is present, ask whether it meets the criteria for a specific disorder, using the glossary's lists of "related conditions in DSM-5-TR" as a guide to the differential diagnosis. If it does, make the diagnosis and record the cultural concept in the formulation.
  4. If there is clinically significant distress or impairment but no named disorder fits, use an other specified diagnosis and state the reason: other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder for koro, for instance, or other specified dissociative disorder for a dissociative trance (Section 1.8.4 explains other specified and unspecified diagnoses).
  5. Record the problems that need attention even when there is no disorder. A CFI often uncovers concerns that correspond to DSM-5-TR's other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention, such as acculturation difficulty (Z60.3), when adjusting to a new culture after migration is the focus or affects treatment, and religious or spiritual problem (Z65.8), such as a loss or questioning of faith or difficulties after conversion; educational and economic problems are coded in the same chapter (Section 1.8.7).

Two dangers lie on either side of this path. The first is over-pathologising: turning a normal, culturally sanctioned experience into a disorder. DSM-5-TR's examples are pointed. Socially warranted suspicion, in a person from a community that has really been harassed, may be misread as paranoia; an unfamiliar presentation may be misdiagnosed as psychosis; a possession state or a religious vision may be called dissociative or psychotic. The manual adds that the risk of misdiagnosis is greater when clinicians assess people from socially oppressed or marginalised groups, and that racial and other biases can affect assessment. The second danger is under-diagnosing: accepting the cultural explanation at face value and missing the disorder behind it. A young man who says "dhat" may have a depressive or anxiety disorder; a woman who says "weakness" may be severely depressed, as Section 5.2.7 shows. The cultural concept is the door, not the room.

Two cases show both sides. Yusuf, 46, a power-loom weaver in a small town in western Uttar Pradesh, is brought by his brothers after four months of decline. The family is sure that a cousin, angry over a property dispute, has had sorcery done on him; they have taken him to a dargah twice, and Yusuf agrees with them. A trainee who heard only the sorcery story might write "persecutory delusion". But the belief is shared by his whole family and community, it is the kind of explanation DSM-5-TR warns can be mistaken for delusional disorder, and Yusuf holds it no more firmly than his brothers do. What the trainee should not miss is everything else: Yusuf wakes at three in the morning, has lost weight, has stopped going to his loom, says he is useless to his children and wishes he would not wake up. He has a major depressive episode. The diagnosis is made on those symptoms; the sorcery explanation goes into the formulation; the treatment plan is explained to the brothers in terms they can accept, and the family's visits to the dargah, which do him no harm, can continue alongside it. Had Yusuf instead insisted, alone and against his family's view, that neighbours were inserting thoughts into his head through the loom, the picture would be quite different (Chapter 4).

Charu, 23, a postgraduate student in Kolkata, lives with her grandmother, whose household keeps strict rules of ritual purity. The family jokingly calls her "our suchi-bai". But Charu now spends three hours a day washing after touching anything that has come from outside, washes her clothes again and again, has stopped attending classes because the bus "contaminates" her, and knows that her washing far exceeds anything her grandmother's tradition requires; she hates it and cannot stop. Here the cultural label is an idiom her family uses, and the presentation meets the criteria for obsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 6.5). The diagnosis is OCD; the theme of purity, and the family's own customs, which are not pathological, are part of the formulation and will shape how exposure and response prevention is explained to her and to her grandmother.

And Dinesh? His Cultural Formulation Interview (Section 1.10.3) explains why he wanted a tonic and feared a "mental" label. The clinician explains the physiology of semen to him respectfully, links his weakness to worry, poor sleep and anaemia, and treats his generalised anxiety disorder (Sections 6.4 and 6.9; cognitive behavioural therapy is taught in Book 2, Chapter 5).

Clinical link

Working within a person's explanatory model does not mean agreeing with it, and it does not mean dismissing it. Show respect: ask about the belief with curiosity, use the person's own words, and never ridicule a healer the family trusts. Give accurate information in terms that fit the person's frame, as the clinician did for Dinesh. Involve the family where the person agrees, because in India decisions about treatment are usually family decisions. Cooperate with traditional healers who do no harm, and draw a firm line at practices that do, such as chaining, beating or stopping medicines. And adapt the treatment itself, whether a medicine or a psychotherapy, to the person's language, values and expectations (Book 2, Section 8.11.3, describes how therapies are culturally adapted).

The lesson of this section reaches far beyond its syndromes. Every disorder described in Chapters 2 to 11 is diagnosed by one person about another, inside a culture, and the text for most DSM-5-TR disorders carries its own heading on culture-related diagnostic issues (Section 1.2.8), as every main disorder in ICD-11 carries a section on culture-related features. The men of Singapore, the women of Assam who feared for their breasts, Mohan in his skin clinic and Dinesh with his tonic were not exotic specimens. They were ordinary people using the ideas their world gave them to make sense of fear, and the clinician who understands those ideas will diagnose more accurately and treat more successfully than one who does not.

Exam trap

A cultural concept of distress is not itself a diagnosis. The DSM-5-TR rule is: diagnose a specific disorder if criteria are met and put the cultural terms in the case formulation; otherwise consider an other specified diagnosis; and remember that a culturally accepted experience is not a disorder, while a person who meets no criteria may still need help. A belief in sorcery or the evil eye shared by the person's family and community is not a delusion. The other conditions that a CFI often leads to include acculturation difficulty (Z60.3) and religious or spiritual problem (Z65.8).

Scan Your Brain

  1. Since DSM-5 (2013), the term culture-bound syndrome has been replaced by cultural concepts of , of three kinds: idioms, explanations and syndromes.
  2. Applying a diagnostic category developed in one culture to people of another culture, for whom it lacks coherence, is what Arthur Kleinman called the fallacy.
  3. The Hong Kong psychiatrist Pow Meng Yap proposed a classification of the culture-bound syndromes in 1967.
  4. The core Cultural Formulation Interview has questions in four domains, beginning with the cultural definition of the problem.

The case of the Singapore koro epidemic, revisited

Go back to the autumn of 1967, to the frightened men who crowded into the emergency departments of Singapore holding on to their genitals, and put the chapter's three questions to them again. Were they mentally ill? Run them through Section 1.1 and the answer is far from simple. Their distress was intense and their dysfunction real, but their belief was shared by their neighbours, printed in their newspapers and rooted in a medical tradition that had a name for it, suo yang, and in almost every case the terror was gone within hours or a few days: in the vocabulary of Section 1.2, an acute onset and a time-limited course. Barlow and Durand's third component, a response that is atypical or not culturally expected, is exactly what the epidemic lacked, because for a few weeks fear about pork and the genitals was what a man in that city might be expected to feel. The facts were not in doubt; whether they added up to a disorder was a judgement of value, the mixture that Section 1.1.1 described. What caused it? Not one thing, as Section 1.3 would predict. A rumour about the meat of vaccinated pigs was the stressor, a culture's ideas about the yang and the genitals gave the fear its shape, ordinary anxiety did the rest, and the diathesis-stress model would look for some vulnerability in the particular men who fell ill to explain why their neighbours stayed well. The epidemic ended in the same many-sided way. Once Gwee Ah Leng's panel of the Singapore Medical Association, and then the Ministry of Health, told the city on television and in the newspapers that the cause was fear and rumour, the cases seen in a day at Singapore General Hospital fell from 38 to 17 within two days, and within a month the epidemic was over.

And what should it be called? A psychiatrist armed only with a checklist could have written "panic attack" on every case sheet and missed almost everything that mattered. Section 1.10 traced the better answers. Pow Meng Yap had called koro "a culture-bound depersonalization syndrome" in 1965, and in 1967, the year of the epidemic, he proposed his classification of the culture-bound reactive syndromes: psychogenic reactions to stress, shaped by local beliefs, that are local forms of disorders psychiatry already knew. DSM-IV listed koro in its glossary of culture-bound syndromes; DSM-5 retired that term in favour of the cultural concepts of distress; and DSM-5-TR names koro as an example of other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder, lists it as a related condition of dhat in its glossary, and separates it from body dysmorphic disorder by its focus on death rather than on ugliness. The koro epidemic in lower Assam in 1982, the key study of Section 1.10.4, was a different event in a different culture, with 19 women among the 83 people studied, yet it ran the same course: it spread by rumour and fear, and it ended, with no deaths, after the public was reassured through the mass media. Dinesh, sure that his weakness came from losing dhat, stood in the same place on the scale of one person. The rule of Section 1.10.6 serves them all: diagnose a disorder only if its criteria are met, write the cultural concept into the formulation, and give accurate information in terms that fit the person's own frame. In effect, Singapore's doctors did that for a whole city.

Chapter summary

1.1 What Counts as Abnormal, and What Counts as Normal. Lacking a laboratory test, the line between normal and abnormal rests on criteria that mix judgements of fact and value. The statistical criterion calls abnormal whatever deviates from typical patterns, but rare talent is just as deviant; the social-norm criterion suffers from cultural relativity; distress and dysfunction fail alone. Comer's four Ds (deviance, distress, dysfunction, danger; duration is a fifth) combine them, and Jahoda (1958) defined ideal mental health by six criteria. Wakefield (1992) called a disorder a harmful dysfunction; DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 define it as a clinically significant disturbance reflecting a dysfunction, usually with distress or impairment, and DSM-5-TR excludes expectable responses to loss and socially deviant behaviour; the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 sets a higher legal bar. Offer and Sabshin (1966) described normality as health, utopia, average and process; the subjective model means no distress, disability or help-seeking. Normal and abnormal lie on a continuum.

1.2 The Vocabulary of Psychopathology: Onset, Course, Prognosis, Prevalence and Comorbidity. Psychopathology is the scientific study of mental disorders; Barlow and Durand divide its work into clinical description, aetiology and treatment and outcome, and Jaspers founded descriptive psychopathology (1913). A symptom is reported, a sign observed; a syndrome is a cluster that runs together, a disease has a known cause. Onset is acute or insidious; course is chronic, episodic or time-limited, waxing and waning or progressive; prognosis is the forecast of course and outcome, good or guarded. Epidemiology counts prevalence (point, period and lifetime: all existing cases) and incidence (new cases in the population at risk), and prevalence is roughly incidence multiplied by duration. The Epidemiologic Catchment Area study and the National Comorbidity Survey (Kessler, 1994) supply the figures, and India's National Mental Health Survey found a treatment gap of 70 to 92 percent. Comorbidity (Feinstein, 1970) is the co-occurrence of two or more differently defined disorders in one person.

1.3 Explaining Disorder: Models, Causes and the Diathesis-Stress Idea. One-dimensional models trace a disorder to a single cause; the multidimensional integrative model treats biological, behavioural, emotional, cognitive, social and developmental influences as one system with feedback loops, as Irfan's panic disorder shows. Each classic model blames a different level: unconscious conflict rooted in childhood trauma (psychodynamic), maladaptive learning (behavioural), disturbed thought patterns (cognitive), a failure to live up to one's potential (humanistic-existential), and poverty, stigma or family interaction (sociocultural and family systems). The language of cause separates necessary, sufficient and contributory causes, distal and proximal causes, the four Ps, reciprocal causation (Hammen's stress generation) and equifinality from multifinality. The diathesis-stress model holds that a predisposition is set off by a stressor (Meehl's schizotaxia, 1962; Zubin and Spring, 1977). Engel's biopsychosocial model (1977) says which levels to examine, and protective factors and resilience (Werner and Smith on Kauai) complete the picture.

1.4 Genes and Mental Disorder: Family, Twin, Adoption and Molecular Studies. The common disorders are polygenic, and Falconer's liability-threshold model (1965) explains how many small influences yield a disorder that appears only beyond a threshold. Family studies begin with a proband but cannot separate genes from a shared home; twin studies compare MZ with DZ concordance and estimate heritability (Falconer's formula, the ACE model) under the equal environments assumption; adoption studies (Heston, 1966; Kety, Rosenthal and Wender in Denmark; cross-fostering; Tienari in Finland) separate genes from rearing. Heritability is a population statistic, about 90 percent for bipolar disorder in some studies and about 80 for schizophrenia, yet identical-twin concordance stays far below 100 percent. Linkage (the Amish chromosome 11 finding of 1987, which collapsed on re-analysis), association studies, GWAS, polygenic risk scores and missing heritability follow, and endophenotypes are measurable, heritable traits on the path from genes to symptoms (John and Lewis, 1966; Gottesman and Gould, 2003).

1.5 Genes Meet the World: Interaction, Correlation, Epigenetics and the Brain. In gene-environment interaction the effect of a gene depends on the environment: in Caspi and Moffitt's Dunedin study (2002), maltreated boys with low-activity MAOA were the most likely to become antisocial, while the serotonin transporter finding of 2003 failed to replicate (Risch, 2009; Border, 2019). In gene-environment correlation, passive, evocative or active, genes shape the environments a person meets. Epigenetics changes gene expression without changing the DNA sequence, through DNA methylation and histone modification, as Meaney's rats, Suomi's monkeys, the Dutch Hunger Winter and McGowan's study of suicide victims show. Transmitter, hormone and brain findings are correlates rather than proof of cause (ex juvantibus reasoning misleads), and the neurodevelopmental model (Weinberger; Murray and Lewis, 1987) links them for schizophrenia. Bipolar disorder and schizophrenia are highly heritable, yet stress, sleep loss, family climate and cannabis shape them: genes set the diathesis, the environment times and shapes the illness.

1.6 Approaches to Classification: Syndromes, Categories and Diagnostic Error. Classification serves communication, prediction and treatment. Hippocrates named mania, melancholia and phrenitis, and Kraepelin classified by course and outcome, separating dementia praecox from manic-depressive insanity. The syndromal approach classifies by clusters of signs and symptoms, the aetiological or "historical" approach by known cause; the modern manuals are syndromal at the core and aetiological at the edges. Classification may be categorical, dimensional or prototypical (polythetic); Meehl's taxometrics tests for true categories, and HiTOP (Kotov, 2017) builds dimensions from the data. The diagnostic hierarchy survives as the "better explained" rule. Reliability is measured by Cohen's kappa (1960) and validity by Robins and Guze's five phases (1970). A false positive labels a well person disordered (low specificity), a false negative misses a case (low sensitivity), and base rates (Meehl and Rosen, 1955) decide what a positive result means. A label brings public stigma, self-stigma and diagnostic overshadowing, and benefits too.

1.7 The DSM from DSM-I to DSM-5-TR, and the Multiaxial System. The "statistical" in the DSM records its origin in counting: the census of 1840, whose errors Jarvis exposed, the seven categories of 1880 and the Statistical Manual of 1918. DSM-I (1952) spoke of Meyer's "reactions"; DSM-II (1968) kept the neuroses and followed ICD-8. DSM-III (1980), directed by Robert Spitzer and built on the Feighner and Research Diagnostic Criteria, brought explicit criteria, an atheoretical descriptive approach, field trials, PTSD and the multiaxial system. DSM-III-R (1987) relaxed the exclusion rules; DSM-IV (1994, Allen Frances) added the clinical significance criterion and the cultural appendix; DSM-IV-TR (2000) revised the text. Its axes recorded clinical disorders, personality disorders and mental retardation, general medical conditions, psychosocial and environmental problems and the GAF (0 to 100), as Dinesh's record shows. DSM-5 (2013; Kupfer and Regier) abolished the axes and took an Arabic numeral, and DSM-5-TR (2022) is the edition in use.

1.8 Inside DSM-5-TR: Sections, Specifiers, Conditions for Further Study and Z Codes. DSM-5-TR has three sections: Section I (basics), Section II (the only official criteria and codes, in 22 chapters, 20 of them mental disorders in a lifespan order beginning with the neurodevelopmental disorders) and Section III (emerging measures and models). Criteria guide clinical judgement and are not a cookbook. Subtypes are mutually exclusive ("Specify whether"), specifiers can be combined ("Specify if"), and severity ratings and ICD-10-CM codes complete the record, as Dinesh's record shows. "Other specified" records why a presentation falls short and "unspecified" does not; both replaced "not otherwise specified". Section III holds the cross-cutting measures, the WHODAS 2.0, the Cultural Formulation Interview, the Alternative Model for Personality Disorders and seven conditions for further study, among them non-suicidal self-injury disorder; persistent complex bereavement disorder became prolonged grief disorder. The other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention, the Z codes, include malingering (Z76.5).

1.9 The ICD: From ICD-6 to ICD-11. The ICD grew from lists of causes of death (Farr, d'Espine, Bertillon) and passed to the WHO, whose ICD-6 (1948) was the first with a section on mental disorders. Stengel's survey (1959) led to ICD-8 and its glossary. ICD-10 (endorsed 1990, in use from 1994) holds mental disorders in Chapter V (F), with the blue book (1992) and the research criteria (1993); its eleven blocks run from F00-F09, the organic disorders such as delirium and dementia, and F10-F19, substance use, to F99. ICD-11 (adopted 2019, in force 2022) uses Chapter 6, codes 6A00 to 6E8Z, in 21 groupings; it moved sleep-wake disorders to Chapter 7 and sexual dysfunctions and gender incongruence to Chapter 17, added complex PTSD, prolonged grief disorder and gaming disorder, and grades one personality disorder by severity. The DSM, the American Psychiatric Association's, is sold; the WHO's ICD covers all of medicine and is free.

1.10 Culture and Diagnosis: Culture-Bound Syndromes and Cultural Concepts of Distress. Culture shapes the content of symptoms, the words for distress and the path to help, and Kleinman warned against the category fallacy and separated disease from illness. Pow Meng Yap classified the culture-bound reactive syndromes (1967); DSM-IV's appendix held an Outline for Cultural Formulation and a glossary of 25 culture-bound syndromes; DSM-5 replaced the term with three cultural concepts of distress: idioms, explanations and syndromes. The core Cultural Formulation Interview has 16 questions in four domains. India's syndromes include dhat (named by Wig in 1960, a cultural explanation in DSM-5-TR), koro and its epidemics, jhinjhinia, suchi-bai, bhanmati sorcery and gilhari syndrome; latah, from Malaysia and Indonesia, is not reported in India. Around the world come amok, pibloktoq, ataque de nervios, taijin kyofusho, brain fag, susto and zar. A cultural concept is not a diagnosis: diagnose a disorder if its criteria are met and put the concept in the formulation.

From the past papers

  1. According to statistical approaches, what defines 'abnormal' behavior? (RML 2025)

    (a) Deviation from typical behavioral patterns (b) Experiencing intense anxiety (c) Loss of contact with reality (d) Withdrawal and depression

    Answer: (a) Deviation from typical behavioral patterns. The statistical criterion cares only about frequency: behaviour is abnormal when it is rare, far from the average of the population (Section 1.1.2). Intense anxiety and withdrawal and depression are symptoms, evidence of distress, and loss of contact with reality describes psychosis and the old legal idea of insanity; none of them says anything about rarity.

  2. The definition of abnormality which includes unhappiness is the ______ aspect. (Amity 2023)

    (a) Deviance (b) Dysfunction (c) Danger to self or others (d) Distress

    Answer: (d) Distress. Distress is the person's own suffering and unhappiness. Deviance is difference from society's norms, dysfunction is interference with daily life, and danger, the least common of Comer's four Ds, is risk to self or others.

  3. Society's explicit and implicit norm violations can be viewed as not normal in the sense that the violation is: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Distressful (b) Dysfunctional (c) Deviant (d) A danger to oneself or others

    Answer: (c) Deviant. Deviance covers both statistical rarity and the breaking of norms, explicit ones such as laws and written rules and implicit ones such as unwritten expectations. Distress is judged by the person's own suffering and dysfunction by what the behaviour costs in work, self-care and relationships, so neither is defined by norms.

  4. Which of the following best defines "prognosis"? (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) History of the illness (b) Likely outcome or course of the disorder (c) Immediate symptoms (d) Diagnostic criteria

    Answer: (b) Likely outcome or course of the disorder. Prognosis is the forecast, the anticipated course and likely outcome, which clinicians call good or guarded (Section 1.2.5). The history looks back at how the illness developed, the immediate symptoms are today's presenting complaints, and the diagnostic criteria are the rules for making a diagnosis: only prognosis looks forward.

  5. Comorbidity refers to: (RML 2020)

    (a) the frequency of mental illness (b) two or more causes of death indicated for patients (c) the occurrence of 2 or more disorders at the same time (d) the degree of suffering of mentally ill patients

    Answer: (c) The occurrence of 2 or more disorders at the same time. Comorbidity is the co-occurrence of two or more differently defined disorders in the same person, concurrent when they are present together, a word Alvan Feinstein coined in general medicine in 1970. The frequency of mental illness is prevalence (or incidence), several causes on a death certificate belong to mortality coding, and the degree of suffering is distress or severity.

  6. Comorbidity means: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) People suffering with the same psychopathology die at the same time (b) Sufferers exhibit a chronic fear of death (c) Depression always happens in the winter (d) Differentially defined disorders can co-occur

    Answer: (d) Differentially defined disorders can co-occur. This is the same definition seen from the other side, and in psychiatry such co-occurrence is the rule rather than the exception. Option (a) is a nonsense distractor, (b) describes thanatophobia or death anxiety, and (c) garbles the seasonal pattern specifier, which needs a regular seasonal link that is usually, but not always, winter.

  7. Psychodynamic therapists believe that disorder stems from: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Disturbed thought patterns (b) Emotional trauma experienced in childhood (c) A failure to live up to one's full potential (d) Inappropriate learning patterns

    Answer: (b) Emotional trauma experienced in childhood. The psychodynamic model traces disorder to unresolved, unconscious conflict rooted in emotional trauma in childhood, with fixation and defences. Each other option is another model's answer: disturbed thought patterns is the cognitive model, a failure to live up to one's full potential the humanistic-existential model, and inappropriate learning the behavioural model.

  8. To describe a predisposition towards and risk triggers for a mental disorder, modern theorists use a (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Diathesis-Stress model (b) Neurodevelopmental model (c) Clinical-Reductionist model (d) Syndrome-Sign model

    Answer: (a) Diathesis-Stress model. The diathesis is the predisposition (genetic, biological or psychological) and the stress is the trigger that sets it off (Section 1.3.4). The neurodevelopmental model is real but narrower, a theory that schizophrenia begins with a disturbance of early brain development (Weinberger; Murray and Lewis, 1987); "clinical-reductionist" and "syndrome-sign" are not established models.

  9. 'Psychological disorders develop when a biological predisposition is set off by a stressful situation', is proposed by (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Humanistics existential models (b) Socio-cultural model (c) Diathesis-stress model (d) Cognitive model

    Answer: (c) Diathesis-stress model. A predisposition set off by a stressor is the definition of the diathesis-stress model, whose history runs from Meehl's schizotaxia (1962) to Zubin and Spring's vulnerability model (1977). The humanistic-existential model blames a failure to fulfil one's potential, the sociocultural model poverty, culture, stigma and labelling, and the cognitive model disturbed thought patterns.

  10. The diathesis-stress model of psychopathology suggests that mental disorders result from : (RML 2023)

    (a) Genetic factors only (b) Environmental factors only (c) A combination of genetic predisposition and environmental stressors (d) Neurobiological abnormalities only

    Answer: (c) A combination of genetic predisposition and environmental stressors. Neither the diathesis nor the stress usually produces a disorder on its own, and the two trade off: the more vulnerable the person, the less stress it takes. Every wrong option says "only", the one-dimensional thinking that the model replaced.

  11. In abnormal psychology, the diathesis-stress model posits that mental disorders result from: (RML 2025)

    (a) Purely biological causes (b) Learned experiences alone (c) A combination of vulnerability and stress (d) Supernatural influences

    Answer: (c) A combination of vulnerability and stress. Vulnerability is another word for diathesis, which is why the model is also called the vulnerability-stress or stress-vulnerability model. Purely biological causes and learned experiences alone are single-cause explanations, and supernatural influences belong to the oldest of the one-dimensional traditions.

  12. The genetic mechanisms that ultimately contribute to the symptoms and difficulties experienced by people with psychological disorders are known as: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Endophenotypes (b) Phenotypes (c) Genotypes (d) Molecular genetics

    Answer: (a) Endophenotypes. The stem copies Barlow and Durand's definition: an endophenotype is a measurable, heritable trait, such as the working-memory or eye-tracking deficits of schizophrenia, that lies on the path between the genes and the disorder (Section 1.4.8). The phenotype is the observable symptoms and disorder at the end of that path, the genotype is the genetic make-up at its start, and molecular genetics is a research method, not a mechanism in the person.

  13. For which type of mental illness is there considerable evidence that it is caused by biological factors and little to no evidence that is caused by psychosocial factors? (RML 2020)

    (a) Bipolar disorder (b) Bulimia nervosa (c) Schizophrenia (d) (a) and (c)

    Answer: (d) (a) and (c). This is the keyed answer and the best of the options: bipolar disorder (heritability about 90 percent in some twin studies) and schizophrenia (about 80 percent) are the most heritable of the major adult disorders and respond most clearly to drugs, while bulimia nervosa is the most strongly shaped by culture and dieting. But the premise of the stem is false (Section 1.5.6): identical-twin concordance is only 40 to 70 percent for bipolar disorder and about 33 to 48 percent for schizophrenia, sleep loss and life events trigger manic episodes, high expressed emotion predicts relapse in schizophrenia, and bulimia nervosa is itself about half heritable.

  14. A high false positive rate in diagnosis means : (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Diagnosis is too strict (b) Normal people are diagnosed as disordered (c) Diagnosis has low reliability (d) Disordered people go undetected

    Answer: (b) Normal people are diagnosed as disordered. A false positive is a well person who is diagnosed anyway, so a high rate means over-diagnosis: low specificity and criteria that are too loose, not too strict. Disordered people going undetected is a high false-negative rate (low sensitivity), and low reliability means that raters disagree with each other, which is a different problem.

  15. Which version of the DSM is currently in use (as of 2025)? (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) DSM-IV (b) DSM-V (c) DSM-III (d) DSM-6

    Answer: (b) DSM-V. Examiners often write the fifth edition with a Roman numeral, so "DSM-V" means DSM-5 (2013), and the text now in use is its text revision, DSM-5-TR (March 2022), which you should choose whenever it is offered. DSM-III (1980) and DSM-IV (1994) are superseded, and there is no DSM-6.

  16. Which of the following is a proposed criterion set in the current DSM that involves cutting, burning, or otherwise intentionally hurting one's own body without the intent to kill oneself? (RML 2022)

    (a) Internet gaming disorder (b) Attenuated psychosis syndrome (c) Persistent complex bereavement (d) Non-suicidal self-injury

    Answer: (d) Non-suicidal self-injury. Non-suicidal self-injury disorder is one of the seven conditions for further study in DSM-5-TR Section III: intentional damage to the surface of one's own body on 5 or more days in the past year, without suicidal intent. The other options were also Section III proposals, but internet gaming disorder is excessive gaming, attenuated psychosis syndrome is sub-threshold psychosis, and persistent complex bereavement disorder, DSM-5's proposal for severe and lasting grief, became prolonged grief disorder in DSM-5-TR.

  17. Which of the following is a culture-bound syndrome where the patient responds to minimal stimuli with exaggerated startles, often exclaiming normally inhibited sexually denotative words? (RML 2022)

    (a) Latah (b) Koro (c) Dhat (d) Susto

    Answer: (a) Latah. Latah, described in Malaysia and Indonesia, is an exaggerated startle to sudden or minimal stimuli followed by echolalia, echopraxia, automatic obedience and the blurting of obscene or sexual words (coprolalia). Koro is the terror that the genitals are retracting, with fear of death; dhat is distress attributed to the loss of semen; and susto is soul loss after a fright, in Latinx communities of the Americas.

  18. Which of the following culture-bound syndromes is NOT reported in India? (Amity 2023)

    (a) Dhat Syndrome (b) Koro (c) Suchi-bai (d) Latah

    Answer: (d) Latah. Latah belongs to Malaysia and Indonesia. Dhat, named by N. N. Wig in 1960, is a syndrome of the Indian subcontinent; koro has struck India in epidemics, as in lower Assam in 1982; and suchi-bai, an obsessional preoccupation with cleanliness and ritual purity, was described in Bengal.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Diathesis-stress model: a disorder develops when a predisposition (the diathesis: genetic, biological or psychological) is set off by a stressor; neither usually suffices alone, and the more vulnerable the person, the less stress it takes. Also called the vulnerability-stress or stress-vulnerability model; roots in Meehl's schizotaxia (1962) and Zubin and Spring (1977). Options saying "only" or "alone" are wrong. asked 5 times
  2. Comorbidity is the co-occurrence of two or more differently defined disorders in the same person, at the same time or over the lifetime; Alvan Feinstein coined the word in general medicine (1970). It is not the frequency of illness (prevalence), not two causes of death and not the degree of suffering. Dual diagnosis is a mental disorder plus a substance use disorder. asked twice
  3. Comer's four Ds: deviance (statistical rarity and violation of explicit and implicit norms), distress (the person's own unhappiness or suffering), dysfunction (interference with daily life) and danger (to self or others, the least common). The fifth D is duration. asked twice
  4. Latah (Malaysia and Indonesia): an exaggerated startle to minimal stimuli with echolalia, echopraxia, automatic obedience and blurted obscene or sexual words. It is the one classic syndrome not reported in India. asked twice
  5. Dhat syndrome (Indian subcontinent, also Sri Lanka, Pakistan, Nepal and Bangladesh): weakness, fatigue, anxiety and sexual worries blamed on loss of semen in urine or night emissions. Named by N. N. Wig in 1960; DSM-5-TR calls it "a cultural explanation of distress"; ICD-10 lists it under F48.8. asked 3 times
  6. Koro: sudden terror that the penis (in women the breasts, nipples or vulva) is retracting into the body and that death will follow; epidemics in Singapore (1967) and India (lower Assam, 1982: 83 cases, 19 of them women). In DSM-5-TR it is an example of other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder. Dhat, koro and suchi-bai (Bengal: obsessional cleanliness) are all reported in India. asked twice
  7. Statistical criterion: abnormal means deviation from typical behavioural patterns, rare and far from the average (often beyond two standard deviations). Its flaws: rare talent counts as abnormal, common disorders are missed and the cut-off is arbitrary. Intense anxiety and withdrawal are symptoms, not rarity. asked once
  8. Endophenotype: a measurable, heritable trait on the pathway between genotype and disorder, "the genetic mechanisms that ultimately contribute to the symptoms" (Barlow and Durand). Coined by John and Lewis (1966), brought into psychiatry by Gottesman and Shields (early 1970s), revived by Gottesman and Gould (2003). The phenotype is the observable symptoms and disorder; the genotype the genetic make-up. asked once
  9. Psychodynamic model: disorder stems from emotional trauma in childhood and unconscious conflict. Cognitive model: disturbed thought patterns. Humanistic-existential model: failure to live up to one's full potential. Behavioural model: inappropriate learning. asked once
  10. Bipolar disorder and schizophrenia are the keyed answer to "biological, little to no psychosocial evidence" (RML 2020): heritability about 90 percent in some twin studies and about 80 percent. But identical-twin concordance is only 40 to 70 and 33 to 48 percent, and sleep loss, life events, expressed emotion and cannabis still shape the illness. asked once
  11. Non-suicidal self-injury disorder is a DSM-5-TR Section III condition for further study: intentional damage to the body's surface (cutting, burning, hitting) on 5 or more days in a year, without suicidal intent. Persistent complex bereavement disorder (DSM-5) became prolonged grief disorder in DSM-5-TR. asked once
  12. DSM-V means DSM-5 (2013), which uses an Arabic numeral; the text in use is DSM-5-TR (American Psychiatric Association, March 2022); there is no DSM-6. The string: 1952, 1968, 1980, 1987, 1994, 2000, 2013, 2022. asked once
  13. Prognosis is the anticipated course and likely outcome of a disorder, a forecast ("good", "guarded"). History and aetiology look back; the presenting symptoms and the diagnosis describe the present. asked once
  14. A false positive is a normal person diagnosed as disordered: over-diagnosis, low specificity, criteria too loose. A false negative is a disorder missed (low sensitivity). Low reliability means raters disagree. asked once
  15. Pibloktoq ("Arctic hysteria"): Arctic and subarctic Inuit, especially north-west Greenland; an abrupt dissociative frenzy with screaming, tearing off clothes and running into the snow, then amnesia. Not South Asian women, Malay men or West Africans. asked twice
  16. DSM-IV-TR axes: I clinical disorders, II personality disorders and mental retardation, III general medical conditions, IV psychosocial and environmental problems, V Global Assessment of Functioning (0 to 100). Introduced by DSM-III (1980); abolished by DSM-5 (2013), which lists I to III together, turns IV into Z codes and replaces the GAF with the WHODAS 2.0. asked twice
  17. The biopsychosocial model is George L. Engel's (Science, 1977), an alternative to the biomedical model. Kraepelin classified by course (dementia praecox versus manic-depressive insanity), Beck built the cognitive model and Bleuler coined the word schizophrenia. asked once
  18. Subjective model of normality: absence of distress, disability or help-seeking. Ideal (utopian) model: optimal functioning. Medical (health) model: absence of psychopathology. Offer and Sabshin's four perspectives (1966): health, utopia, average and process. asked once
  19. The syndromal (descriptive) approach classifies by clinical signs and symptoms that cluster; the aetiological ("historical") approach by known cause. The DSM and ICD are mainly syndromal. asked once
  20. ICD-10 F00-F09, organic, including symptomatic, mental disorders, holds delirium and dementia; F10-F19 substance use; F20-F29 schizophrenia, schizotypal and delusional; F30-F39 mood; F40-F48 neurotic, stress-related and somatoform. asked once
  21. DSM-5 and ICD-11 are classification and diagnostic systems (nosologies), not tests, therapies or intelligence scales. The DSM is the American Psychiatric Association's; the ICD is the WHO's, and ICD-11 (adopted 2019, in force 1 January 2022) puts mental disorders in Chapter 6 (6A00 to 6E8Z). asked once
  22. DSM-5 (2013) replaced culture-bound syndromes (DSM-IV glossary) with three cultural concepts of distress: cultural idioms of distress, cultural explanations (perceived causes) and cultural syndromes. Pibloktoq, brain fag (West African students, Prince 1960) and dhat are examples. asked once
